# Languages, Scripts, Literature and Manuscripts - Live Learning Session

> **Learner-first lock:** This session trains one habit above all others - hearing a sentence about an Indian language and immediately splitting it into six separate claims: a **family** (the ancestry a language is descended from), a **language or register** (the actual speech or written variety being used), a **script** (the marks used to write it, which can be swapped without changing the language), a **corpus** (the body of texts and the genre they belong to), a **material witness** (the inscription, copper plate, palm leaf, birch bark or paper sheet on which the words physically survive), and a **status** (what a named authority has formally said about the language, and when). Almost every error in this topic is one of these six being silently substituted for another. The session runs from language classification to the institutions that catalogue manuscripts today, and it keeps four disciplines throughout: never draw a single-parent line from one Indian language to another, never let a script stand in for a language, never attach a confident date or author to a layered corpus, and never quote a status total without the date on which it was true. Constitutional doctrine on the Eighth Schedule and Article 343 belongs to the polity papers; the political history of the dynasties that patronised literature belongs to the history papers; doctrine inside religious texts belongs to the religion and philosophy topic; heritage administration and site recognition belong to the heritage topic. What is owned here is the **classification of Indian languages, the registers and their social work, the scripts and their descent, the literary corpora and their evidentiary value, the manuscript as a physical object, and the recognition vocabulary that describes all of it**.

## How to read this session

Work down the lessons in order. Each opens with the question a learner actually has at that point, draws or tabulates before it names, and releases the technical word only after the idea it names is already in place. Four habits are built deliberately, because they are the four places this topic is lost.

1. **Split the claim before you evaluate it.** "Ashokan edicts were in Sanskrit written in Devanagari" contains three separate assertions - about the language, about the register, and about the script - and all three are wrong in different ways. A candidate who evaluates the sentence as one unit cannot say *which* part failed, and cannot answer the statement-based item built on it.
2. **Refuse the family tree drawn as a ladder.** Indian language history is layered coexistence, not succession. Sanskrit did not stop when Prakrit started; Prakrit did not stop when Apabhramsha started; Persian did not stop when the regional languages rose. The single most expensive sentence in this topic is any sentence with the shape "X replaced Y".
3. **Keep the dates apart.** Oral formation, written composition, redaction, earliest surviving manuscript, first inscription in that language, critical edition, constitutional listing and international recognition are eight different dates. The Rigveda is ancient; its documentary-heritage inscription is from 2007. Both facts are true and neither dates the other.
4. **Do not decorate.** Where a source gives a hedge - "probably", "perhaps", "according to some scholars" - the hedge is part of the fact and must be carried into your answer. Where this session does not supply a date, a count or an author, that silence is deliberate and should be reproduced rather than filled in.

Every lesson ends the same way: a **concept check** with a model answer and a misconception note, and then an **original lesson-local Mains question** with a model answer written to a stated word ceiling and a scoring note that sets out where the marks actually sit. Everything is drawn in text. No plate, facsimile or script chart is needed to follow any lesson here.

```

**Boundary map.** The polity papers own the constitutional doctrine of Article 343, the Eighth Schedule and language-policy litigation; this session states what each status *is*, so that a cultural answer does not misdescribe it, and stops there. The ancient and medieval history topics own dynastic chronology and the political history of patronage, and they own inscriptions as a *source-criticism* problem; this session owns the language, script and literary content of those inscriptions and the distinction between what stone and what palm leaf can prove. The religion-and-philosophy topic owns doctrine inside the Vedas, the Upanishads, the Tripitaka and the Agamas; this session owns those texts as *corpora* - their language, structure, layering and evidentiary limits. The theatre topic owns dramaturgy and performance; this session owns the play as a text and the language convention printed inside it. The heritage topic owns World Heritage, Intangible Cultural Heritage and conservation administration; this session states exactly one current record, in Lesson 21, and asserts nothing beyond it.

Read the row of "prevents" labels rather than the row of headings. Five of the six columns catch a specific, repeatedly examined error. The sixth carries no marks at all, and it is placed last and labelled so, because an answer that opens "Sanskrit is the mother of all Indian languages and the most perfect language in the world" has spent its opening sentence in the only column an examiner cannot reward.

### 1. The exact scope, stated once

This topic owns the **classification of Indian languages, the registers in which they were written and what those registers signalled socially, the scripts and their descent, the literary corpora and what each can be used to prove, the manuscript as a physical and institutional object, and the vocabulary of formal recognition**. Stated as a checklist of what must be answerable by the end of this session:

- the four major language families of India, their internal groups and the cautions that attach to the classification itself;
- the three Indo-Aryan layers - Old, Middle and New - and why they are layers and not a queue;
- Sanskrit as Vedic and as Classical, its grammarians, and the gap between its literary antiquity and its inscriptional record;
- the Prakrits by name, Pali, Apabhramsha, and the documented convention that assigned registers to characters on stage;
- the Dravidian group in its northern, central and southern divisions, its grammatical contrast with Indo-Aryan, and the four major languages;
- the dialect layer, which is where the "how many languages does India have" question actually lives;
- the Eighth Schedule, Article 343, the Official Languages Act of 1963, the state-level position, and the fact that no national language exists;
- classical-language status: the year the category was created, the three successive sets of criteria and which set is in force, the eleven languages with their dates, and the benefits attached;
- endangerment, extinction, the four-level scale, India's position on it, and the promotion and revival machinery;
- scripts: the undeciphered Indus signs, Brahmi and Kharosthi in full, their decipherment, and the whole descent tree down to the modern scripts;
- inscription against manuscript as two different kinds of witness;
- the Vedic corpus with its branches and schools, and the oral machinery that transmitted it;
- epics, Puranas, Upapuranas, the law-book tradition and its commentaries and digests;
- Classical Sanskrit drama, poetry, poetics and science, with author-text pairs treated as things to verify rather than recall;
- the Buddhist, Jain, Zoroastrian and Sikh corpora, each with its language and school;
- the Sangam corpus in full, with the structure of its anthologies and the exact limit of what it can prove;
- the Persianate court literature and the translation projects that connected it to Sanskrit;
- the formation stories of Urdu and of Hindi, and the genres each developed;
- the regional literary publics of the east, west, north and south, and the modern and nationalist turn;
- the manuscript as material - palm leaf, birch bark, paper - and the scribes, copying and illustration that produced it;
- manuscript survey, cataloguing, conservation and digitisation, and the institutions that do it;
- documentary-heritage recognition, its named Indian entries, and the discipline that separates a text's age from its recognition year;
- the attribution, chronology, region and terminology controls that keep an answer defensible.

### 2. The six questions, each with its technical vocabulary

**Question one - family.** What ancestry does the language descend from? A **language family** is a group of individual languages related through a common ancestor that existed before recorded history. The word *ancestor* is doing the work: a family claim is a claim about descent, not about similarity, borrowing, prestige or geography. Two languages spoken in the same district can belong to different families; two languages separated by three thousand kilometres can belong to the same one.

**Question two - language or register.** Which variety is actually in use? Here a second term is needed. A **dialect** is a form of a language spoken in a local area, and several dialects can derive from one language - Braj, Awadhi and Haryanvi are all dialects of Hindi. A **register** is something else again: a variety chosen for a social situation rather than a place. Sanskrit and Prakrit are the standing example - one codified and rule-bound, one natural and common, both in use at the same time by the same society for different purposes. Confusing dialect with register produces the claim that Prakrit was "the Hindi of its day", which is wrong in both directions.

**Question three - script.** Which marks are used? A **script**, also called a writing system or orthography, is a standard for representing the parts of a spoken language by making specific marks on a medium - paper, rock, birch bark. Notice what that definition does *not* say: it does not tie a script to one language. One script can write many languages and one language can be written in many scripts, and Lesson 10 supplies four separate proofs of that.

**Question four - corpus.** Which body of texts, and in which genre? A corpus has a structure - anthologies, books, recensions, commentaries - and a genre, and the genre controls what the text is evidence *for*. A didactic text is trying to change your conduct; a narrative text is trying to hold your interest. Treating a fable collection as a chronicle, or a devotional anthology as a census, is the commonest literature error.

**Question five - material.** On what physical thing do the words survive? An inscription is cut into rock, pillar or copper plate. A manuscript is written on palm leaf, birch bark or paper and survives only because somebody copied it, then somebody copied that. The two witnesses have different strengths and different failure modes, and Lesson 11 is devoted to the difference.

**Question six - status.** Who has formally said what, and when? Five separate statuses appear in this topic - Eighth Schedule listing, official-language use under Article 343, classical-language recognition, documentary-heritage inscription, and state-level official adoption. They are granted by different authorities under different criteria and none of them implies any other. This is developed in Lessons 6, 7 and 22; fix now only that every one of them is **dated** and **revisable**.

### 3. The vocabulary fixed here

These terms recur constantly and each is developed in its own lesson. Fix the working sense now so later lessons can use them without pausing.

| Term | Working definition, fixed here |
|---|---|
| **Language family** | A group of individual languages related through a common ancestor that existed before recorded history |
| **Dialect** | A form of a language spoken in a local area; several dialects can derive from one language |
| **Register** | A variety selected by social situation rather than by locality - the Sanskrit/Prakrit contrast is the standing case |
| **Script** | A writing system: a standard for representing parts of a spoken language by marks on a medium such as paper, rock or birch bark |
| **Abugida** | A writing system in which each unit is based on a consonant and the vowel notation is secondary, except where a vowel begins a word - the category to which Brahmi, Kharosthi, Devanagari, Vatteluttu, Modi and Nandinagari all belong |
| **Lingua franca** | A bridge, common, trade or vehicular language used systematically between people who do not share a native language, typically a third language distinct from both |
| **Shruti and Smriti** | The two divisions of the older Sanskrit corpus: Shruti is the revealed body - Vedas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas, Upanishads; Smriti, "that which is remembered", is the post-Vedic classical body |
| **Recension (*sakha*) and school (*charana*)** | A recension is a transmitted version of a Vedic text; a *charana* is the school or lineage that preserved and taught that recension |
| **Inscription** | Text cut into a durable public medium, usually datable and locatable |
| **Manuscript** | A hand-written text on a perishable medium, surviving through repeated copying |

### 4. The three habits that make the difference between a describing answer and an analysing one

**Habit one - name the level.** Do not write "Indian languages"; write "the Indo-Aryan languages", or "the southern group of the Dravidian family", or "the Middle Indo-Aryan registers". The general noun is the sign of a candidate who has not read the classification.

**Habit two - carry the hedge.** Sources in this topic hedge constantly and deliberately: "probably the first Hindi book"; "Buddha and Mahavira *perhaps* spoke in Ardhamagadhi"; "according to some scholars". A hedge is evidence about the state of knowledge, and removing it to sound confident converts a defensible sentence into a false one.

**Habit three - date the status.** Every recognition total in this topic has a date on which it was true, and every one of them can change. The safe construction is always "as recognised in October 2024, eleven languages hold classical status", never "India has eleven classical languages" floating free of time.

### 5. The boundaries, stated so they never have to be re-argued

- **The polity papers own** the constitutional doctrine of the Eighth Schedule, Article 343 and the litigation around language policy. This session states what each status is and what it is not, because a cultural answer that misdescribes the status is wrong on the facts; it does not argue constitutional law.
- **The ancient and medieval history topics own** dynastic chronology and the political history of patronage. When this session names Krishnadevaraya or Harshavardhana it does so as a patron with a named poet and a named text, not as a reign to be narrated.
- **The religion and philosophy topics own** doctrine. The Upanishads appear here as a layered textual corpus with recensions and schools; what they argue about the self belongs elsewhere.
- **The theatre topic owns** performance, staging and dramaturgy. The play appears here as a text with a language convention printed inside it.
- **The heritage topic owns** conservation administration, World Heritage and Intangible Cultural Heritage. This session owns documentary heritage as it bears on manuscripts, and states exactly one current record.
- **The sculpture, painting and music topics own** their own objects. Where a text is the *evidence* for another art - a Tamil epic mentioning painting, a treatise on music - the text is owned here and the art is routed there.

### Examination use

This lesson is not directly examinable and no question will ask you to list six columns. Its value is diagnostic: every objective item in this topic can be classified by which column it attacks, and knowing the column tells you which lesson holds the answer. A pairing item attacks family or region. A "which script" item attacks column three. A "who wrote" item attacks column four. A "how many/which was added recently" item attacks column six, and column six items are time-stamped, so the answer that was correct three years ago may now be wrong.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating "language", "script" and "religion" as a single package - the error behind "Urdu is a Muslim language" and "Devanagari is the Hindu script", both of which fail on the sources' own classification.
- Using the word "dialect" as an insult or a rank. In this topic it is a neutral descriptive term about locality.
- Answering an identification question with a shared feature rather than a discriminating one.
- Filling a silence. If a source does not give the date, the count or the author, the correct answer says so.

### Revision notes

1. Six separable columns: family, language/register, script, corpus, material, status. The seventh - value - carries no marks.
2. Language family = descent from a common ancestor predating recorded history.
3. Dialect = local form of a language; Braj, Awadhi and Haryanvi are dialects of Hindi.
4. Register = variety chosen by social situation; Sanskrit against Prakrit is the model case.
5. Script = a standard for representing a spoken language by marks on a medium; it is not tied to one language.
6. Abugida = consonant-based unit with secondary vowel notation; the category of most Indic scripts.
7. Shruti = Vedas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas, Upanishads. Smriti = the remembered, post-Vedic classical body.
8. *Sakha* = recension; *charana* = the school that preserved it.
9. Five distinct statuses: Eighth Schedule, official-language use, classical recognition, documentary-heritage inscription, state adoption. None implies another.
10. Every status total is dated and revisable; write the date.
11. Every hedge in a source is part of the fact and must be carried.
12. Boundaries: polity owns constitutional doctrine; history owns dynastic chronology; religion owns doctrine; theatre owns performance; heritage owns conservation administration.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Ashokan inscriptions prove that Sanskrit was the administrative language of the Mauryan empire and that Devanagari was already in use." Using the six-column split, identify exactly how many separate claims are being made and which column each belongs to.

**Model answer:** Three claims. The first - that the language was Sanskrit - belongs to the language/register column; the Ashokan record is overwhelmingly in Prakrit, not Sanskrit. The second - that the script was Devanagari - belongs to the script column; the edicts are in Brahmi, with Kharosthi in the north-west and Greek and Aramaic at Kandahar, and Devanagari develops only in the seventh century AD from the eastern Gupta variants called Nagari. The third - that inscriptions prove administrative language use - belongs to the material column; an inscription proves what was publicly displayed in that medium, which is a narrower claim than what an administration used internally. Separating the three lets you mark each one right or wrong independently, which is precisely what a statement-based item requires.

**Misconception to avoid:** That refuting one part of a compound sentence refutes the whole. A statement-based item is scored clause by clause, and a candidate who collapses three claims into one "wrong" cannot choose between response options that differ only in which clause they accept.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Identify the boundary between the cultural study of Indian languages and the polity and history papers, and explain why the boundary affects answer quality. (10 marks, 150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

**The boundary.** Polity owns the **doctrine** of Article 343, the Eighth Schedule and language-policy litigation. History owns **dynastic chronology** and the political history of patronage, and owns inscriptions as a **source-criticism** problem. Religion and philosophy own **doctrine inside texts**. The cultural topic owns **classification, register, script, corpus, materiality and recognition vocabulary**.

**Why it affects quality.** First, a misdescribed status is a **factual error**, not a stylistic one: writing "national language" for "official language of the Union" fails on the facts. Second, crossing the line **wastes words**: narrating a reign in a literature answer displaces the named author and text that would have earned the mark. Third, the boundary tells you **what to route**: for the Pallavas, supply Dandin and the Pallava-Grantha script here, and send the dynasty elsewhere.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 10: **4** for naming four owners and what each holds, at 1 each; **4** for the three consequences at roughly 1.3 each, of which the *status-misdescription-is-a-factual-error* point is compulsory; **2** for the routing illustration with a named author and a named script. An answer that lists boundaries without showing a consequence caps at 5.

   |            |   areas of Bengal and Odisha), Kurukh (Bengal, Odisha,
   |            |   Bihar, Madhya Pradesh)
   |            +-- CENTRAL group (11): Gondi, Khond, Kui, Manda, Parji,
   |            |   Gadaba, Kolami, Pengo, Naiki, Kuvi, TELUGU
   |            +-- SOUTHERN group (7): Kannada, Tamil, Malayalam, Tulu,
   |                Kodagu, Toda, Kota
   |
   +-- OLD INDO-ARYAN      (from c. 1500 BC)    -> Lesson 3
   +-- MIDDLE INDO-ARYAN   (c. 600 BC - AD 1000) -> Lesson 4
   +-- NEW / MODERN        (after AD 1000)       -> Lesson 5
```

The diagram is drawn deliberately without a single vertical line running from one family into another. That absence is the lesson. The four families are **parallel**, not stacked, and the residual category on the right exists because classification is incomplete, not because those languages are lesser.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The classification looks like something to memorise, so candidates memorise it and then produce sentences that the classification itself forbids - "Sanskrit is the mother of all Indian languages", "Dravidian came out of Prakrit", "the north-eastern languages are dialects". The problem is not the list. The problem is that a family tree *looks like* a hierarchy, and a hierarchy invites ranking and descent claims that the evidence does not support. So this lesson does two things at once: it gives the classification completely, and it marks the three sentences that must never be produced from it.

### 2. Indo-Aryan, stated exactly

Indo-Aryan is a branch of the larger **Indo-European** family - a family native to the overwhelming majority of Europe, the Iranian plateau and the northern Indian subcontinent. It is the largest language group of India, and around **74 per cent** of Indians speak languages belonging to it. It is divided into three sub-groups **by time period of origin**, and that phrase matters: the division is chronological, not geographical and not evaluative.

| Sub-group | Period | What it contains |
|---|---|---|
| **Old Indo-Aryan** | development around 1500 BC | Sanskrit - the ancient form found in the Vedas; the Upanishads, Puranas and Dharmasutras are also in Sanskrit |
| **Middle Indo-Aryan** | between 600 BC and AD 1000 | the Prakrits in the broad sense, including Pali, Ardhamagadhi, Shauraseni, Maharashtri, and then Apabhramsha |
| **Modern / New Indo-Aryan** | developed gradually after AD 1000 | Hindi, Assamese, Bengali, Gujarati, Marathi, Punjabi, Rajasthani, Sindhi, Odia, Urdu and others, mainly in northern, western and eastern India |

One striking entry sits outside India entirely: **Romani**, an Indo-Aryan language that is part of the Balkan *sprachbund* and is the only New Indo-Aryan language spoken exclusively outside the Indian subcontinent. It is worth holding because it is the cleanest available demonstration that family membership is a claim about descent and not about present geography.

### 3. Dravidian, stated exactly

The Dravidian group mainly comprises the languages of southern India and covers around **25 per cent** of the population. **Proto-Dravidian gave rise to 21 Dravidian languages**, divided into three groups:

- **Northern group - three languages.** Brahui, spoken in Baluchistan; Malto, in the tribal areas of Bengal and Odisha; Kurukh, in Bengal, Odisha, Bihar and Madhya Pradesh. Note what this row does to a mental map: a Dravidian language is spoken in Baluchistan, far outside the south.
- **Central group - eleven languages.** Gondi, Khond, Kui, Manda, Parji, Gadaba, Kolami, Pengo, Naiki, Kuvi and **Telugu**. Of these, only Telugu developed as a major literary language; the rest are tribal languages.
- **Southern group - seven languages.** Kannada, Tamil, Malayalam, Tulu, Kodagu, Toda and Kota, with Tamil the oldest among them.

Among the 21, four are the major languages: **Telugu**, numerically the largest of all Dravidian languages; **Tamil**, the oldest; **Kannada**; and **Malayalam**, the smallest and youngest of the group. One further language repays attention because it is repeatedly under-reported: **Tulu**, spoken mainly in Dakshina Kannada and Udupi in Karnataka and in the northern parts of Kasaragod district in Kerala, whose speakers are called Tuluva and whose area is unofficially called Tulu Nadu. Tulu is known for **oral literature in the form of epic poems called *pardana*** and for the traditional folk theatre **Yakshagana**. It is the cleanest single case in this session of a language whose literary weight is carried orally rather than in manuscripts, which is why it is easy to miss and easy to examine.

One nickname is worth carrying with its provenance attached: **Telugu is called the "Italian of the East"**, a description introduced by the fifteenth-century Italian traveller **Niccolò de' Conti**, who observed that Telugu words frequently end in vowel sounds as Italian words do. Cite it as a traveller's observation with a name and a century, which is evidence; do not cite it as a property of the language, which is not.

### 4. Sino-Tibetan and Austric, stated exactly

**Sino-Tibetan** languages belong to the Mongoloid family in the older descriptive usage and stretch across the Himalayas, north Bihar, north Bengal, Assam and up to the north-eastern frontiers. They are considered **older than the Indo-Aryan languages** and are referred to as **Kiratas** in the oldest Sanskrit literature. They are spoken by **0.6 per cent** of the population. The internal division is into Tibeto-Burman, itself in four groups - Tibetan, Himalayan, North Assam, and the Kuki-Garo-Bodo-Mikir-Naga group in which **Meitei**, spoken mainly in Manipur, is the most important - and **Siamese-Chinese**, whose Indian representative **Ahom is already extinct in the subcontinent**.

**Austric** languages belong to the Austro-Asiatic sub-family, represented by the **Munda or Kol** group spoken in central, eastern and north-eastern India, and by members of the **Mon-Khmer** group, namely **Khasi and Nicobarese**. These languages have been in existence long before the advent of the Aryans and were referred to as **Nisadas** in ancient Sanskrit literature. **Santhali** is the most important language of the group, spoken by Santhal tribals of Jharkhand, Bihar and Bengal. And then a sentence that should be memorised in full because it converts a classification into a policy fact: **with the exceptions of Khasi and Santhali, all the Austro-Asiatic languages in Indian territory are endangered.** That single line is the bridge from this lesson to Lesson 8.

Finally, an honest residual: the **"Others"** category holds several Dravidian tribal languages such as Gondi, Oraon and Praji which are very distinct and cannot be placed in the groups above. Leaving a residual category visible is a feature of a serious classification, not a defect, and an answer that reproduces it looks better informed than one that forces every language into a box.

### 5. The grammatical contrast that is worth more than the lists

The families differ not only in vocabulary but in how words are built, and this is the point at which a classification answer stops being a list.

| | **Indo-Aryan** | **Dravidian** |
|---|---|---|
| Root words | different from those of the other family | different from those of the other family |
| Grammatical structure | **inflected**: a word's ending or spelling changes according to its grammatical function in the sentence | **agglutinative**: root words are united in combinations that produce little or no change of form or loss of words |
| What that means in practice | grammatical information is fused into a changed word-form | grammatical information is added as separable pieces, leaving the root visible |

The two families are therefore distinguished on **two independent grounds** - different root vocabulary and different grammatical architecture - and citing both is what distinguishes an analytical answer from a memorised one.

### 6. The three unsafe sentences, and what to write instead

This is the most important section in the lesson, and it is the reason the classification is taught before anything else.

**Unsafe sentence one: "Sanskrit is the mother of many Indian languages."** The source itself uses this formulation, and it is defensible in a narrow sense: within the Indo-Aryan branch, the Old Indo-Aryan stage is ancestral to what follows. It becomes false the moment it is extended beyond that branch. Tamil, Telugu, Kannada and Malayalam are Dravidian. Santhali and Khasi are Austro-Asiatic. Meitei and Bodo are Sino-Tibetan. None of them descends from Sanskrit. **Safe formulation:** "Within the Indo-Aryan branch, the Old Indo-Aryan stage represented by Sanskrit is ancestral to the later Indo-Aryan layers; the Dravidian, Austro-Asiatic and Sino-Tibetan families have their own separate ancestries."

**Unsafe sentence two: "Dravidian emerged from Prakrit and Paisacha and blended with Negrito languages."** The source records this as the view of *some scholars*, and reproducing it without that attribution converts a minority nineteenth-and-early-twentieth-century speculation into a settled fact. It also contradicts the same source's own framing - that some Indian language groups are **indigenous to the subcontinent** and that Proto-Dravidian is the ancestor of the 21 Dravidian languages. **Safe formulation:** "Dravidian is treated as an independent family descending from Proto-Dravidian; older derivations of Dravidian from Middle Indo-Aryan are attributed views and are not the standard position." Writing it this way costs one clause and protects the whole answer.

**Unsafe sentence three: "Tamil is the oldest and purest form of language."** *Oldest among the seven southern Dravidian languages* is a defensible descriptive claim. *Purest* is an evaluative claim with no linguistic content, and the moment it enters an answer the answer has moved into the seventh column of Lesson 1 - the one with no marks. **Safe formulation:** "Tamil is the oldest of the southern Dravidian group", and then move to evidence.

The general rule underneath all three: **descriptive content from a source is usable; evaluative content from a source is quotable as an opinion but never as evidence.**

### 7. Comparative treatment - why the families are worth comparing at all

The reason to hold all four families rather than one is that each supplies a different kind of answer material.

| Family | What it supplies to an answer |
|---|---|
| Indo-Aryan | the layered-stage argument: three time-defined sub-groups showing coexistence rather than replacement |
| Dravidian | the independent-ancestry argument, and the geographical spread that breaks the "Dravidian = south" mental map (Brahui in Baluchistan) |
| Sino-Tibetan | the antiquity argument - described as older than Indo-Aryan - and the extinction case of Ahom |
| Austric | the endangerment argument in its sharpest form: every Indian Austro-Asiatic language except Khasi and Santhali is endangered |

A 15-mark answer on India's linguistic diversity that uses one point from each row is structurally complete before a single extra fact is added.

### Examination use

Classification items are usually built as pairings - a language against a family, or a language against a state - and they are marked pair by pair. The defences are: check the **family** claim against the group lists above; check the **region** claim separately, because a family can extend far outside its stereotyped region; and treat every *derivation* claim as suspect unless the source states it without attribution. For a Mains answer, the highest-value move is the comparison table in section 5, because the agglutinative-inflected contrast is a structural argument rather than a list, and it converts "India is linguistically diverse" into "India's linguistic diversity is architectural, not merely numerical".

### Traps introduced here

- Deriving one family from another, in any direction.
- Mapping a family onto a region without checking - Brahui is Dravidian and is spoken in Baluchistan; Meitei is Sino-Tibetan and is spoken in Manipur.
- Reproducing an attributed minority view as settled fact by dropping the words "according to some scholars".
- Importing an evaluative word - purest, richest, most perfect - from a source into your own argument.
- Treating the residual "Others" category as an error in the classification rather than an honest statement of its limits.
- Assuming the Sino-Tibetan share of speakers, at 0.6 per cent, means the group is small in the number of languages; it is not.

### Revision notes

1. Four major families: Indo-Aryan (~74%), Dravidian (~25%), Sino-Tibetan (~0.6%), Austric; plus a residual "Others".
2. Indo-Aryan is a branch of Indo-European, divided by time: Old (c. 1500 BC), Middle (600 BC-AD 1000), Modern (after AD 1000).
3. Romani is the only New Indo-Aryan language spoken exclusively outside the subcontinent.
4. Proto-Dravidian gave rise to 21 languages: Northern 3 (Brahui, Malto, Kurukh), Central 11 (incl. Telugu), Southern 7 (incl. Tamil, Kannada, Malayalam, Tulu, Kodagu, Toda, Kota).
5. Four major Dravidian languages: Telugu (numerically largest), Tamil (oldest), Kannada, Malayalam (smallest and youngest).
6. Tulu: Dakshina Kannada, Udupi and northern Kasaragod; oral epics called *pardana*; Yakshagana folk theatre.
7. Telugu called "Italian of the East" by Niccolò de' Conti, fifteenth century - cite as a traveller's observation.
8. Sino-Tibetan: called Kiratas in old Sanskrit literature; Tibeto-Burman in four groups; Meitei the most important of the fourth; Siamese-Chinese represented by Ahom, extinct in the subcontinent.
9. Austric: Munda/Kol plus Mon-Khmer (Khasi, Nicobarese); called Nisadas in old Sanskrit literature; Santhali the most important; all Indian Austro-Asiatic languages except Khasi and Santhali are endangered.
10. Grammar contrast: Indo-Aryan inflected; Dravidian agglutinative. Root vocabulary also differs.
11. Three unsafe sentences: Sanskrit as mother of all Indian languages; Dravidian derived from Prakrit; Tamil as "purest".
12. Descriptive source content is usable as evidence; evaluative source content is not.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it a stronger answer to say that the Indo-Aryan sub-groups are divided "by period of origin" than to say they are "three stages of one language"?

**Model answer:** Because "period of origin" describes when each layer *appeared* without claiming that the earlier layer *stopped*. The three sub-groups overlap in use: Sanskrit continued as a codified literary and ritual register long after the Middle Indo-Aryan registers were in everyday use, and Middle Indo-Aryan material continued to be written after modern languages had begun to form. "Three stages of one language" imports a relay-race model in which each runner hands over and leaves the track, and that model is exactly what generates the false claim that Prakrit succeeded Sanskrit or that vernaculars displaced Sanskrit. Saying "period of origin" keeps the chronology and drops the succession.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a chronological classification is automatically a genealogical one. Dating when a layer emerged is a weaker and safer claim than asserting that each layer is the direct single parent of the next, and the sources themselves note that transitions between one language or dialect and another were slow and cannot be divided into strict chronological periods.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Compare the Indo-Aryan and Dravidian families on ancestry, internal structure, grammar and spread, and assess what the comparison establishes about India's linguistic diversity. (15 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**Ancestry.** Indo-Aryan is a **branch of Indo-European**, spoken by around **74 per cent** of Indians. Dravidian descends from **Proto-Dravidian** and covers around **25 per cent**. The two ancestries are independent; older derivations of Dravidian from Middle Indo-Aryan are attributed views, not the standard position.

**Internal structure.** Indo-Aryan divides **by period** - Old from about 1500 BC, Middle from 600 BC to AD 1000, Modern after AD 1000. Dravidian divides **by geography and status** - Northern three (Brahui, Malto, Kurukh), Central eleven of which only **Telugu** became a major literary language, Southern seven including **Tamil**, the oldest.

**Grammar.** Indo-Aryan is **inflected**: endings change with function. Dravidian is **agglutinative**: roots combine with little change of form. Root vocabulary also differs, so the families are distinguished on **two independent grounds**.

**Spread.** Neither maps onto its stereotype. **Brahui**, Dravidian, is spoken in **Baluchistan**; **Romani**, Indo-Aryan, is spoken **exclusively outside** the subcontinent.

**Assessment.** The comparison establishes that Indian diversity is **architectural, not merely numerical**. Two families with different ancestries, different organising principles, different word-building systems and non-contiguous distributions coexist in one polity. That is a stronger claim than a count of languages, and it explains why a single classification - descriptive, constitutional or administrative - can never capture the whole field.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 15: **8** for the four comparison heads at 2 each, with the grammar head requiring both the agglutinative-inflected contrast **and** the separate root-vocabulary ground; **3** for the two counter-stereotype cases, Brahui and Romani, at 1.5 each; **4** for an assessment that converts the comparison into the *architectural* claim. A table without the assessment caps at 8.

## Lesson 3 - Old Indo-Aryan: what Sanskrit actually is, and when it first appears in stone

Progress: 3/23 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: Vedic and Classical Sanskrit, the grammarian sequence, the register's social function, and the gap between literary antiquity and inscriptional record

### Visual: two Sanskrits, three grammarians, two first inscriptions

```text
   OLD INDO-ARYAN develops around 1500 BC
                    |
        +-----------+------------+
        |                        |
   VEDIC SANSKRIT            CLASSICAL SANSKRIT
   the ancient form          "the chaste form", a refined version of
   found in the Vedas        Vedic Sanskrit, developed between
   (also Upanishads,         300 and 200 BC
   Puranas, Dharmasutras
   in Sanskrit)
                    |
   THE GRAMMAR SEQUENCE (three names, fixed order)
   PANINI --------> KATYAYANA --------> PATANJALI
   Ashtadhyayi      Varttika-sutra      Mahabhashya
   oldest book on   1500 verses on      "great commentary"
   Sanskrit         Panini, the         on Panini; the
   grammar;         Varttikas on the    Varttika survives
   grammar began    Sutras of Panini;   only through
   with him         he belonged to      references inside
   in 400 BC        a period BETWEEN    the Mahabhashya
   (source's date)  Panini and Patanjali (250 BC)
                    |
   THE INSCRIPTIONAL RECORD - a completely separate clock
   JUNAGARH / GIRNAR rock inscription of RUDRADAMAN I, c. AD 150
     -> one of the earliest pieces of evidence of the use of Sanskrit
   CHEBROLU inscription (Andhra Pradesh) of Satavahana king VIJAYA,
     issued AD 207, discovered in 2019
     -> earliest datable Sanskrit inscription from South India so far
                    |
   THE GAP IS THE POINT: literary antiquity (c. 1500 BC) and the
   earliest datable epigraphic use (2nd-3rd century AD) are
   DIFFERENT FACTS about DIFFERENT MEDIA
```

The diagram deliberately separates three clocks that examiners love to fuse: the clock of the language's development, the clock of its grammatical codification, and the clock of its appearance on stone. They run at different speeds and a well-built answer never lets one date stand in for another.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Everybody arrives knowing that Sanskrit is old and important. Almost nobody arrives able to say *which* Sanskrit, *codified by whom*, *in what order*, and *from what date it can be seen in a dated document*. That precision is exactly what the items in this area test - a chronology item asking which text is earliest, an identification item asking what Katyayana's position was, a statement item asking what an inscription proves. So this lesson supplies the two forms, the three grammarians in fixed order, and the two first inscriptions, and then insists on the gap between them.

### 2. The two forms of Sanskrit, stated exactly

Old Indo-Aryan **had its development around 1500 BC**, and Sanskrit was born out of this group. **The ancient form of Sanskrit is what is found in the Vedas.** The Upanishads, Puranas and Dharmasutras were also written in Sanskrit. It is described as the most ancient language of India and is one of the 22 Scheduled Languages listed in the Eighth Schedule of the Constitution - a status point developed in Lesson 6 and flagged here only so that the two facts are never fused.

The second form is the one candidates skip. **The chaste form of Sanskrit developed between 300 and 200 BC and was a refined version of Vedic Sanskrit.** That is the variety normally called Classical Sanskrit, and Lesson 14 treats its literature. Two consequences follow immediately. First, "Sanskrit" in an examination stem is ambiguous until you ask which form is meant. Second, the refinement happened *after* the beginning of grammatical codification, which is why the grammar sequence has to be held separately.

Sanskrit also crosses the religious boundary that candidates habitually draw around it. Some Buddhist literature of the **Mahayana** and **Hinayana** schools is written in Sanskrit. The **Mahavastu** of the Hinayana school is described as a treasure trove of stories written in a **mix of Sanskrit, Pali and Prakrit** - a single text that refutes the one-text-one-language assumption. The **Lalitavistara Sutra**, the most sacred Mahayana text, and **Ashvaghosha's Buddhacharita** were also written in Sanskrit.

### 3. The grammarian sequence, in the order that is actually examined

**Panini.** The development of Sanskrit grammar began with Panini, whose **Ashtadhyayi** is the oldest book on Sanskrit grammar and is described as a treatise on the rigid rules that bind the language. Most of Sanskrit literature is bound by the rules explained in it. The source places him at **400 BC**. Carry this date with a visible hedge: the conventional dating is to the middle of the first millennium BC, and a precise single year is not something a cultural answer needs to defend. What the answer *does* need is the function: Panini is where codification starts, and codification is what makes a register rule-bound rather than natural.

**Katyayana.** He wrote the **Varttika-sutra**, consisting of **1500 verses on Panini**, known as the Varttikas on the Sutras of Panini. Two facts about him are directly examinable and both are unusual. First, his **chronological position: he belonged to a period between Panini and Patanjali** - not a contemporary of either. Second, his **survival status: his work is at present available only through references in Patanjali's work.** That second fact is a small masterpiece of source criticism in a single line, and it belongs in any answer about how Indian textual traditions survive: a text can be historically central and physically lost, surviving only as quotation inside its own commentary.

**Patanjali.** He wrote the **Mahabhashya**, meaning "great commentary", dated in the source to **250 BC**. It is a book on grammar that refers to historical personalities only incidentally - a point worth holding because it controls how the text may be used as a historical source. A grammar that mentions a king in passing is evidence of a different and weaker kind than a chronicle written to record that king.

The three names therefore form a fixed order - **Panini, then Katyayana, then Patanjali** - and the order itself is examined.

### 4. Register as social function: the convention printed inside the plays

Here is the single most useful evidentiary line in the whole topic, and it comes from the description of Gupta-period drama. Many of the plays developed during the Gupta period were written in Sanskrit; **one of the features of these plays was the use of the Sanskrit language by the characters of high varna and the use of the Prakrit language by women and Shudras.**

Take the sentence apart before using it.

- It is a statement about a **dramatic convention**, that is, about how playwrights coded characters.
- It is therefore **direct textual evidence** that language register functioned as an on-stage marker of social position - not an inference a candidate has to construct unaided.
- It is **not** evidence that every high-varna person actually spoke Sanskrit in daily life or that no woman ever did. A stage convention records what a society found legible, which is a real historical fact of a different kind.
- It is stated for **Gupta-period plays**. Extending it, unqualified, to all Indian drama in all periods over-claims.

That four-line handling is how a single sentence becomes a defensible paragraph. It is also the bridge to Lesson 4, because the convention only works if Sanskrit and Prakrit were *simultaneously available* - the coexistence point.

### 5. The Gupta period as a literary high point, stated with its own vocabulary

It was the **Gupta period** to which the use of Sanskrit in poetry is traced, and the source calls it completely a period of creation of **pure literature**, evident in **Mahakavyas (epics)** and **Khandakavyas (semi-epics)**. The period is called one of unique creation because of the variety of literary works, and a second feature is recorded: the **ornate style** in literary works. Sanskrit was the court language and gained prominence in the Gupta period.

Hold the two genre words now - *mahakavya* and *khandakavya* - because Lesson 14 adds two more to complete the set of four styles, and a question asking you to distinguish them is asking about form, not about content.

### 6. The inscriptional record: a separate clock, with two dated anchors

This is where the lesson pays for itself. The *literary* antiquity of Sanskrit and the *epigraphic* record of Sanskrit are different facts, and only the second carries dates you can defend.

| Anchor | Exact record | What it proves | What it does not prove |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Junagarh / Girnar rock inscription of Rudradaman I**, present-day Gujarat, dated around **AD 150** | one of the earliest pieces of evidence of the use of Sanskrit | that Sanskrit was in public, prestige, inscriptional use by the mid-second century AD | that it was not used in writing earlier; absence of an earlier surviving inscription is not absence of earlier use |
| **Chebrolu inscription**, Andhra Pradesh, of the Satavahana king **Vijaya**, issued **AD 207**, **discovered in 2019** | the earliest datable Sanskrit inscription from South India **so far** | that dated Sanskrit epigraphy reaches South India by the early third century | that the record is closed - the words "so far" and the 2019 discovery date are the warning built into the fact itself |

The Chebrolu entry is the more instructive of the two, because it carries three separate dates in one row - the **issue date** (AD 207), the **discovery date** (2019), and the implicit **status date** ("so far"). Reproducing all three in an answer demonstrates exactly the discipline the topic rewards. Confusing the discovery year with the composition year is the single commonest dating error in this area, and it recurs in a different form in Lesson 22 with recognition years.

### 7. Comparative treatment: what codification does to a language

| | **A codified register (Sanskrit)** | **A natural common tongue (developed in Lesson 4)** |
|---|---|---|
| Rules | fixed, orthodox, explicitly stated in grammars | understood as natural, original and casual, without strict rules of usage |
| Users | learned people and elites, especially Brahmins | common people |
| Consequence for change | change is resisted and visible as deviation | change is continuous and unmarked |
| Consequence for the historian | a text's language can be dated by its conformity to rules | dating by language is harder and less precise |
| Consequence for writing | writing is prestigious and early | writing texts in it was a relatively late development compared with Sanskrit |

The final row is the one that surprises people: the *spoken* common registers were older in everyday use, but *written texts* in them came relatively late. That asymmetry is the reason the surviving record over-represents the codified register - a survival bias that returns in Lesson 21.

### 8. The limit of the analogy

It is tempting to describe Sanskrit as "the Latin of India". Resist it in an answer. The analogy captures one thing - a codified prestige register used across regions for learning and liturgy - and misrepresents two others: Sanskrit's continuous ritual and scholarly use, and the fact that Sanskrit is one of the 22 Scheduled Languages under the Constitution today and one of the eleven languages holding classical status, which has no European parallel. Use the comparison only if you immediately state its limit.

### Examination use

Three question families sit on this lesson. **Chronology items** ask you to order texts - the Panini-Katyayana-Patanjali sequence and the position of the Ashtadhyayi relative to later treatises are the standard material, and they are decided by the fixed order in section 3. **Identification items** ask what a named grammarian did or when he lived, and Katyayana's "between Panini and Patanjali" position is the classic trap because "contemporary of Panini" and "contemporary of Patanjali" are both plausible-looking and both wrong. **Statement items** ask what an inscription establishes, where the defence is the two-column table in section 6. For a Mains answer on Sanskrit as a cultural vehicle, the best spine is: codification (Panini sequence) - social register (the stage convention) - geographical reach (Junagarh in the west, Chebrolu in the south) - cross-religious use (Mahayana and Hinayana texts in Sanskrit) - and a closing qualification about the gap between literary and epigraphic clocks.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating "Sanskrit" as one thing when the sources distinguish the Vedic form from the chaste or classical form that developed between 300 and 200 BC.
- Making Katyayana a contemporary of Panini or of Patanjali; he belonged to the period between them.
- Forgetting that Katyayana's work survives only through references in Patanjali's Mahabhashya, and citing it as an extant text.
- Treating an inscription's discovery year as its issue year - the Chebrolu inscription was issued in AD 207 and discovered in 2019.
- Reading the earliest *datable* inscription as the earliest *use*.
- Assuming Sanskrit is exclusively a Brahmanical-Hindu vehicle, when Mahayana and Hinayana texts including the Lalitavistara Sutra and the Buddhacharita are in Sanskrit.
- Generalising the Gupta stage-language convention to all Indian drama of all periods.

### Revision notes

1. Old Indo-Aryan develops around 1500 BC; the ancient form of Sanskrit is that of the Vedas; Upanishads, Puranas and Dharmasutras are also in Sanskrit.
2. The chaste form - Classical Sanskrit - developed between 300 and 200 BC as a refined version of Vedic Sanskrit.
3. Grammar sequence, fixed: Panini (Ashtadhyayi, the oldest book on Sanskrit grammar, source date 400 BC) -> Katyayana (Varttika-sutra, 1500 verses on Panini) -> Patanjali (Mahabhashya, "great commentary", 250 BC).
4. Katyayana belonged to a period **between** Panini and Patanjali; his work survives only through references in Patanjali's.
5. The Mahabhashya is a grammar and refers to historical personalities only incidentally.
6. Gupta-period convention: high-varna characters speak Sanskrit; women and Shudras speak Prakrit - a dramatic convention, direct evidence of register as a social marker.
7. Gupta period: pure literary creation, Mahakavyas (epics) and Khandakavyas (semi-epics), ornate style; Sanskrit the court language.
8. Junagarh / Girnar rock inscription of Rudradaman I, c. AD 150 - among the earliest evidence of the use of Sanskrit.
9. Chebrolu inscription, Andhra Pradesh, Satavahana king Vijaya, issued AD 207, discovered 2019 - earliest datable South Indian Sanskrit inscription so far.
10. Three clocks: language development, grammatical codification, epigraphic appearance. Never substitute one date for another.
11. Sanskrit crosses religious boundaries: Mahavastu mixes Sanskrit, Pali and Prakrit; Lalitavistara Sutra and Buddhacharita are Sanskrit.
12. Sanskrit is both a Scheduled Language under the Eighth Schedule and a classical language - two separate statuses, neither implying the other.

### Concept check

**Question:** A statement reads: "The earliest datable Sanskrit inscription from South India was issued in 2019." Identify the error precisely and state the correct formulation.

**Model answer:** The error confuses the discovery year with the issue year. The Chebrolu inscription in Andhra Pradesh was **issued in AD 207** by the Satavahana king Vijaya and was **discovered in 2019**; it is described as the earliest datable Sanskrit inscription from South India **so far**. The correct formulation keeps all three elements: the issue date, the discovery date, and the provisional qualifier, because a new find can displace it.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a superlative in this topic is permanent. "Earliest so far" is a statement about the present state of the archaeological record, not about the past; it changed in 2019 and can change again, which is why the qualifier is part of the fact rather than decoration.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Trace the codification of Sanskrit grammar and explain what codification did to the language's social function. (10 marks, 150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

**The sequence.** **Panini's Ashtadhyayi** is the oldest book of Sanskrit grammar and the point at which codification begins. **Katyayana** follows, writing the **Varttika-sutra**, 1500 verses on Panini, and survives **only through references in Patanjali**. **Patanjali's Mahabhashya**, the "great commentary", completes the sequence and refers to historical persons only incidentally.

**What codification did.** It converted a language into a **rule-bound register**. Sanskrit became **orthodox, fixed and used by learned elites, especially Brahmins**, while the common tongues remained natural and rule-free. Three consequences follow. Deviation became **visible**, so texts can be placed by conformity. Writing in the codified register became **prestigious and early**, while writing in the common registers came comparatively late. And register became **socially legible**, which is what allows Gupta drama to assign Sanskrit to high-varna characters and Prakrit to women and Shudras.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 10: **3** for the three grammarians in the correct order, forfeited if Katyayana is made a contemporary of either; **1** for his survival only inside Patanjali; **3** for the codification-to-register argument; **3** for the three consequences at 1 each. Naming the texts without the social argument caps at 4.

## Lesson 4 - The Middle Indo-Aryan world: Prakrits, Pali, Apabhramsha, and language as a social marker

Progress: 4/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Prakrits named individually with their texts and regions, Apabhramsha, the Sanskrit-Prakrit social division, and the limits on reconstructing who actually spoke what

### Visual: the Middle Indo-Aryan field, by name, use and region

```text

 c BC     kind of    used to    predecessor Sinhala  language);   other than
 origin   Prakrit"   write      to Marathi  of Sri   regarded as  Sanskrit or
 in Ujjain court     dramas in  and Konkani Lanka;   an unimport- even Prakrit
 or Taxila language  medieval   official    similar  ant dialect;
 (per     of some    India;     language of to Pali  Gunadhya's   TRANSITION
 some     Maha-      predecessor Satavahanas         BRIHATKATHA  Middle ->
 scholars) janapadas  to Northern                    is in it     Modern

 to NE    rock edicts it; oldest GAUDAVAHO           Prakasha

 written  Assamese,  SHATKHAND-  the King of
 in       Odia,      GAMA        Gauda")
 BRAHMI   Maithili,
 script   Bhojpuri
```

The diagram is horizontal on purpose. These are not rungs. They are a field of simultaneously available varieties with different jobs - a canonical language here, a court language there, a stage language, a regional literary language - and the examiner's favourite error is to convert the field into a ladder.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

"Prakrit" is used in two different senses in the same paragraph of most sources, and until you see that, the section is unreadable. Sense one: **Prakrit is a broad term under which all the Middle Indo-Aryan languages are generally clubbed together.** Sense two: Prakrit is one named variety among several. The sentence "Ardhamagadhi, Pali and Apabhramsa find their origin from Prakrit" only makes sense in sense one, and it is not a claim that a single language called Prakrit gave birth to three daughters. Read it as "these belong to the Middle Indo-Aryan group", and the paragraph becomes coherent.

The second problem is the temptation to answer "which language did the Buddha speak?" with confidence. The sources hedge that question repeatedly, and the hedge is the answer.

### 2. What "Prakrit" means, and the register contrast that defines it

The period of development of the Middle Indo-Aryan sub-group is between **600 BC and AD 1000**, and it started with the development of Prakrit. **Prakrit is understood as being natural, original and casual, and was used as a common tongue without any strict rules of usage.** It was **associated with the common people**. On the other hand, **Sanskrit was orthodox, had fixed rules and was used by learned people or the elites, especially Brahmins.**

One further line completes the picture and is regularly missed: **the writing of texts in Prakrit was relatively a late development, as compared to Sanskrit.** So the ordering of *speech* and the ordering of *writing* run in opposite directions here, and that is a genuinely analytical point, not a curiosity: a common tongue can be older in use and later in writing, because writing is an institution with its own prestige gatekeeping.

And the caution that closes the section: **the period of transition from one language or dialect to another was slow and cannot be separated into strict chronological periods.** That sentence is your licence to refuse every "X replaced Y in year Z" formulation.

### 3. The Prakrits, one by one

**Pali.** Widely spoken in **Magadha**; popular during the **5th to 1st century BC**. According to some scholars it originated either in **Ujjain or Taxila**, but spread across the Vindhyas and into the north-eastern regions. It was **closely related to Sanskrit**, and texts in Pali were generally **written in the Brahmi script** - a clean, early demonstration that language and script are separable. The **Tripitaka** of Buddhism was written in Pali, and Pali **served as the lingua franca of Theravada Buddhism**. Then the hedge that must be carried: **it is believed that the Buddha himself did not speak in Pali but delivered his preachings in Ardhamagadhi.**

**Magadhi Prakrit or Ardhamagadhi.** Described as **the most important kind of Prakrit**. Its literary use **increased after the decline of Sanskrit and Pali**. **Buddha and Mahavira perhaps spoke in Ardhamagadhi** - note the word *perhaps*, which sits in tension with the flatter statement under Pali and must be handled, not smoothed over. It was the court language of a few **Mahajanapadas** and of the **Mauryan dynasty**. Several **Jain texts** and the **rock edicts of Ashoka** were written in it. It **later evolved into many languages of eastern India** - Bengali, Assamese, Odia, Maithili and Bhojpuri.

**Shauraseni.** **Widely used to write dramas in medieval India**, and therefore also referred to as **Dramatic Prakrit**. It was the **predecessor to Northern Indian languages**. **Jain monks mainly used this version of Prakrit while writing**, and the **oldest text of the Digambara Jains, the Shatkhandgama, is written in Shauraseni**.

**Maharashtri Prakrit.** **Spoken until the ninth century AD** and the **predecessor to Marathi and Konkani**. Used widely in western and southern India. It was the **official language of the Satavahana dynasty**. Two named works belong to it: **Gaha Kosha by King Hala** and **Gaudavaho**, meaning "slaying of the King of Gauda", **by Vakpati**.

**Elu.** An **ancient form of the Sinhala language of Sri Lanka**, described as similar to Pali. Its presence in the list is a reminder that the Middle Indo-Aryan field is not bounded by India's modern borders.

**Paishachi.** Also referred to as **Bhutabhasa**, meaning dead language. Often regarded as Prakrit, it is considered an **unimportant dialect** - again an evaluative description from the source, to be reported rather than adopted. **Gunadhya's Brihatkatha**, an ancient epic, is written in Paishachi. There is **one chapter dedicated to Paisachi Prakrit in the Prakrita Prakasha**, a grammar book on Prakrit **attributed to Vararuchi** - note *attributed*, another carried hedge.

### 4. Apabhramsha: the transition layer, and why two named critics matter

**Apabhramsha**, whose name means **corrupt or non-grammatical**, developed in the **6th-7th century AD**. It is also an **umbrella term for dialects other than Sanskrit or even Prakrit**, and it **represents the transition from the Middle to the Modern Indo-Aryan group**.

Two pieces of contemporary testimony establish that Apabhramsha was a recognised literary category rather than mere corruption, and both are quotable:

- **Bhamaha**, a renowned poet of Kashmir of the sixth or seventh century AD, **divided poetry into Sanskrit, Prakrit and Apabhramsha.** A three-way division by a poet-critic is direct evidence that the third category was taken seriously as literature.
- **Dandin said that Apabhramsha is the dialect of the common folk.** A second critic, describing its social location.

Apabhramsha **gradually became a literary language** used to write several texts and legends, and **developed its own identity by the seventh century AD**. Many **Jain monks and scholars wrote extensively in it**, thereby sustaining the language. Major works include **Pushpadanta's Mahapurana**, a Digambara Jain text, and **Dhanapala's Bhavisayattakaha**.

There is a lesson inside the name itself. A variety called "corrupt" by the prestige tradition became, within a century, a category in a critic's formal classification of poetry and the vehicle of a sustained religious literature. Naming is not the same as status, and the gap between the two is a Mains-quality observation.

### 5. The social-marker argument, assembled

Now the pieces from Lesson 3 and this lesson combine into an argument that can carry a fifteen-mark answer.

```text
  CLAIM: In classical Indian literary culture, language register
         functioned as a marker of social position.
                    |
  EVIDENCE 1: The documented Gupta-period dramatic convention -
              high-varna characters speak Sanskrit; women and
              Shudras speak Prakrit.
                    |
  EVIDENCE 2: The register descriptions themselves - Sanskrit
              orthodox, rule-bound, used by elites especially
              Brahmins; Prakrit natural, casual, associated with
              the common people.
                    |
  EVIDENCE 3: Dandin's description of Apabhramsha as the dialect
              of the common folk - a critic explicitly locating a
              register socially.
                    |
  EVIDENCE 4: The name "Apabhramsha" itself - "corrupt" - a value
              judgement embedded in a linguistic label.
                    |
  QUALIFICATION: Stage convention and critical labelling record how
              a society CODED language, not a transcript of how
              every person SPOKE. And the coding was not stable:
              the "corrupt" register acquired its own literature and
              its own place in Bhamaha's three-fold classification.
                    |
  VERDICT: Register was a social marker AND a contested one; the
           hierarchy is visible precisely because it was being
           renegotiated.
```

That qualification-then-verdict shape is what separates a fifteen-mark answer from a ten-mark one. The claim alone is worth little; the claim with its own counter-evidence built in is worth a great deal.

### 6. The hedge problem, handled openly

The sources in this area contain a genuine tension, and the honest move is to display it rather than pick a side.

| Statement | Where it sits | Status |
|---|---|---|
| "Buddha himself did not speak in Pali but delivered his preachings in Ardhamagadhi" | under Pali | stated as a belief |
| "Buddha and Mahavira **perhaps** spoke in Ardhamagadhi" | under Ardhamagadhi | explicitly hedged |
| The canonical-language labels - Pali for the Theravada canon, Ardhamagadhi for Jain texts | throughout | secure: these are statements about the **language of transmitted texts** |

**The safe examination position:** canonical and literary language labels describe **transmission histories** - which language a corpus was written and preserved in - and they are secure. The **spoken language of an individual historical teacher** is not recoverable with the same confidence, and both sources hedge it. Write the secure half; hedge the unrecoverable half in the same sentence the sources do.

### 7. Comparative treatment: four Prakrits, four different jobs

| Prakrit | Its distinctive job | The one text or fact that fixes it |
|---|---|---|
| Pali | canonical language of a religious tradition and its lingua franca | the Tripitaka; written in Brahmi script |
| Ardhamagadhi | court and administrative language, and a second religious canon | court language of Mahajanapadas and the Mauryas; Jain texts; Ashokan rock edicts |
| Shauraseni | stage and monastic writing language | called Dramatic Prakrit; the Digambara Shatkhandgama |
| Maharashtri | regional literary and dynastic official language | official language of the Satavahanas; Gaha Kosha by Hala; Gaudavaho by Vakpati |

Answering "what was Shauraseni?" with "a Prakrit" scores nothing. Answering with "the Dramatic Prakrit, used to write dramas and by Jain monks, in which the oldest Digambara text the Shatkhandgama is written" answers all four columns of the identification question at once.

### 8. A recorded internal inconsistency, and how to write around it

The source describes **Gaha Kosha by King Hala** among works in Maharashtri and says "several dramas were written in this language, such as 'Gaha Kosha'". Elsewhere the same work, as **Gatha Saptashati (Gaha Sattasai)**, is described as **a collection of 700 Prakrit love poems compiled by King Hala**, one of the earliest **secular anthologies** in Indian literature, portraying love, nature and daily life in **Maharashtri Prakrit**, and **including verses by women poets**.

A poetic anthology is not a drama. The safe answer therefore uses the description that the source states in detail and does not rely on the genre label given in passing: **Gaha Kosha / Gatha Saptashati is an anthology of 700 Prakrit love poems compiled by King Hala in Maharashtri Prakrit, one of the earliest secular anthologies in Indian literature, including verses by women poets.** The discrepancy is recorded in the ledger at the end of this session rather than resolved, and no argument here depends on the genre label.

### Examination use

Items in this area come in three shapes. **Attribution pairings** match a Prakrit to a text or a dynasty - Shatkhandgama to Shauraseni and the Digambaras, Gaha Kosha to Hala and Maharashtri, Brihatkatha to Gunadhya and Paishachi. **Descent claims** ask which later languages a Prakrit preceded, where Ardhamagadhi-to-eastern-languages and Maharashtri-to-Marathi-and-Konkani are the two stated lines. **Statement items** probe the Buddha's or Mahavira's language, where the correct behaviour is to prefer the hedged formulation. In a Mains answer, the assembled argument in section 5 is the single most transferable block in this session: it works for a question on language and social hierarchy, on classical drama, or on the relationship between elite and popular culture.

### Traps introduced here

- Reading "Ardhamagadhi, Pali and Apabhramsa find their origin from Prakrit" as descent from a single named language, rather than as membership of the Middle Indo-Aryan group.
- Assigning the Buddha or Mahavira a single certain spoken language.
- Treating Pali as simply a sub-branch of one named Prakrit with a settled position on a branch chart.
- Assuming a Prakrit must be written in a "Prakrit script" - Pali texts were generally written in Brahmi.
- Taking "Apabhramsha means corrupt" as a verdict on the literature rather than as a label from the prestige tradition, which Bhamaha's three-fold classification of poetry already contradicts.
- Calling Gaha Kosha a drama; the detailed description makes it an anthology of 700 love poems.
- Dating the Middle Indo-Aryan transition to sharp years, when the sources state the transition was slow and cannot be separated into strict chronological periods.
- Forgetting Elu and Paishachi because they are minor; both are named, and minor entries are exactly what pairing items use.

### Revision notes

1. Middle Indo-Aryan: 600 BC to AD 1000. "Prakrit" is both the group label and a named variety - read the sense from the sentence.
2. Register contrast: Prakrit natural, original, casual, common tongue, no strict rules, associated with common people; Sanskrit orthodox, fixed rules, learned elites especially Brahmins.
3. Writing texts in Prakrit was a relatively late development compared with Sanskrit.
4. Pali: Magadha; 5th-1st century BC; origin in Ujjain or Taxila per some scholars; spread across the Vindhyas to the north-east; closely related to Sanskrit; texts generally in Brahmi script; Tripitaka; lingua franca of Theravada Buddhism.
5. Ardhamagadhi: "most important kind of Prakrit"; court language of some Mahajanapadas and the Mauryas; Jain texts and Ashokan rock edicts; evolved into Bengali, Assamese, Odia, Maithili, Bhojpuri.
6. Shauraseni: Dramatic Prakrit; dramas of medieval India; Jain monks; oldest Digambara text Shatkhandgama; predecessor to Northern Indian languages.
7. Maharashtri: spoken to the 9th century AD; predecessor to Marathi and Konkani; official language of the Satavahanas; Gaha Kosha by Hala; Gaudavaho by Vakpati.
8. Elu: ancient form of Sinhala, similar to Pali. Paishachi: "Bhutabhasa"; Gunadhya's Brihatkatha; one chapter in the Prakrita Prakasha attributed to Vararuchi.
9. Apabhramsha: 6th-7th century AD; name means corrupt or non-grammatical; umbrella term; transition from Middle to Modern Indo-Aryan; own identity by the 7th century.
10. Bhamaha divided poetry into Sanskrit, Prakrit and Apabhramsha; Dandin called Apabhramsha the dialect of the common folk.
11. Apabhramsha works: Pushpadanta's Mahapurana (Digambara Jain); Dhanapala's Bhavisayattakaha.
12. Safe position on spoken language: canonical labels record transmission; the spoken language of an individual teacher is hedged in the sources and must be hedged in the answer.
13. Gatha Saptashati / Gaha Kosha: 700 Prakrit love poems in Maharashtri compiled by Hala; early secular anthology; includes verses by women poets.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why "Prakrit came after Sanskrit" is a weaker formulation than "Sanskrit and the Prakrits were simultaneously available registers with different social functions", using one piece of evidence from this lesson.

**Model answer:** "Came after" is a succession claim and the evidence contradicts it. The Gupta-period dramatic convention - high-varna characters speaking Sanskrit and women and Shudras speaking Prakrit **within the same play** - is only possible if both registers were current at the same time and both were legible to the same audience. A succession model cannot accommodate a single text that deploys both simultaneously as a coding device. The simultaneity formulation also absorbs the further fact that the sources describe the transition between varieties as slow and not separable into strict chronological periods.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a language's "period of development" is the period during which it was the only variety in use. The dating brackets mark when a layer emerged and was productive, not when the previous layer stopped; Sanskrit continued as a codified literary and court register through the entire Middle Indo-Aryan span and beyond.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Assess the claim that the Prakrits were the natural speech of the people while Sanskrit was the language of the elite, using the evidence of canon, court and drama. (15 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

The claim is well founded as a statement about **register and function**, but it must be qualified in three ways.

**The evidence for it.** Prakrit is described as **natural, original and casual, used as a common tongue without strict rules**, and **associated with the common people**; Sanskrit as **orthodox, rule-bound and used by learned elites, especially Brahmins**. Drama supplies the sharpest proof: in Gupta plays **high-varna characters speak Sanskrit and women and Shudras speak Prakrit**. Dandin separately calls **Apabhramsha the dialect of the common folk**.

**First qualification - the canon.** The Prakrits carried **elite institutional literature**, not only popular speech: the **Tripitaka** in Pali, the **Jain Agamas** in Ardhamagadhi, the **Shatkhandgama** in Shauraseni. A "people's language" does not normally hold two scriptural canons.

**Second qualification - the court.** **Ardhamagadhi** was the court language of several Mahajanapadas and of the **Mauryas**, and **Maharashtri** was the official language of the **Satavahanas**. These are state registers.

**Third qualification - the evidence type.** The drama convention records how a society **coded** language on stage, not a transcript of speech; and the writing of texts in Prakrit came **relatively late** compared with Sanskrit, so the written record under-represents common usage.

**Verdict.** Sanskrit and the Prakrits differed by **social function and codification**, not by a simple elite-popular split, since Prakrit registers served canon and chancery as well as everyday life.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 15: **4** for the evidence supporting the claim, including the drama convention; **6** for the three qualifications at 2 each, of which the canon and court qualifications are compulsory; **2** for the evidence-type caution distinguishing stage convention from speech; **3** for a verdict that refines rather than rejects the claim. Simply endorsing the elite-popular split caps at 6.

## Lesson 5 - Modern Indo-Aryan, the Dravidian literary languages and the dialect layer
