The diagram exists to make one point visible: the dialect layer is where the real complexity of Indian language sits, and it is also where two different classification systems - the descriptive and the administrative - openly disagree. That disagreement is a fact to be stated, not a contradiction to be resolved.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Candidates learn "22 scheduled languages" and think the counting is finished. It is not. Beneath every one of those 22 there is a dialect structure, some of whose members are themselves scheduled, and beneath that there are tribal varieties which the sources themselves say are "sometimes considered independent languages". A question that asks whether Maithili is a language or a dialect, or where Dakhini belongs, is testing whether you know that the two classifications are made by different authorities for different purposes.

### 2. Modern Indo-Aryan, stated exactly

The languages belonging to this group are **Hindi, Assamese, Bengali, Gujarati, Marathi, Punjabi, Rajasthani, Sindhi, Odia, Urdu** and others. They **developed gradually after AD 1000** and are mainly spoken in the northern, western and eastern parts of India. Note that **Rajasthani** appears in this list as a language of the group while also appearing in the dialect table as a *cluster of closely related dialects* within Hindi - the same double classification as Maithili, and for the same reason.

### 3. The dialect layer, language by language

This is a table to know by structure rather than by rote. The examinable pattern is: which dialect is the *standard* one, which is the *historically literary* one, and which is the *regionally named* one.

| Language | Dialect structure, as recorded |
|---|---|
| **Hindi** | Western (Khari Boli - the standard or formal variant, around Delhi and western UP; Braj Bhasha - historically important, Mathura and Agra, noted for melodious quality and literary significance; Bundeli - Bundelkhand in MP and UP; Haryanvi; Kannauji), Eastern (Awadhi - the Awadh region of UP; Bagheli - Baghelkhand in MP and UP; Chhattisgarhi), Rajasthani (Marwari, Mewari, Jaipuri, Mewati, Malwi), Bihari (Bhojpuri - eastern UP and western Bihar; Magahi - south-eastern Bihar; Maithili - eastern Bihar, Jharkhand and parts of Nepal), Pahari (Kumaoni and Garhwali), plus Bambaiya Hindi (a colloquial blend of Hindi and Urdu used in Mumbai) and Haflong Hindi (a distinct Hindi dialect spoken in Assam) |
| **Bengali** | Western, Central, Eastern and Hill dialects, with regional variation in Kolkata, rural West Bengal and Bangladesh. The linguist **Suniti Kumar Chatterji** categorised Bengali into four main dialects: **Rarhi, Bangali, Kamrupi and Varendri** |
| **Punjabi** | Majhi (the foundation of standard Punjabi), Doabi, Malwai, Pwadhi |
| **Marathi** | Varhadi (Vidarbha), Deshi, Ahirani, Konkani Marathi |
| **Tamil** | Central Tamil dialect (regarded as the standard spoken form), Kongu Tamil (Kongu Nadu), Madurai Tamil, Madras Bashai (Chennai), Nellai Tamil (Tirunelveli), Kumari Tamil (Kanyakumari) |
| **Telugu** | Telangana Telugu, Coastal Andhra, Rayalaseema Telugu |
| **Kannada** | Dharwad, Bengaluru, Mangalore and Gulbarga Kannada |
| **Malayalam** | northern, central and southern regional dialects, plus community-specific varieties (Christian, Muslim, Nair Malayalam) |
| **Odia** | coastal, western and south-western varieties. Major dialects: Baleswari, Kataki, Dhenkanalia, Anugulia, Ganjami, Sundargadi, Sambalpuri, Halbi, Desia. Minor dialects among tribal and community groups: Bhuyan, Kurmi, Sounti, Bathudi, Kondhan, Agharia, Bhulia, Matia |
| **Assamese** | Western, Central and Eastern |
| **Maithili** | various rural and urban dialects; **historically written in the Tirhuta script, now mainly in Devanagari** |
| **Gujarati** | Kathiawari, Surti, Gamadia |
| **Urdu** | **Dakhini** (the southern variant), **Rekhta** (an older or poetic form), and regional variations across the north and the Deccan |
| **Dravidian tribal dialects** | Gondi, Kui, Toda, in central and southern India - **some being considered independent languages** |

Four entries in that table are disproportionately examinable, and they are worth isolating.

- **Khari Boli** is the standard Hindi variant and, as Lesson 18 shows, the structural base on which Urdu was built - so it is the hinge between the Hindi and Urdu stories.
- **Braj Bhasha** is the dialect with literary significance, and Lesson 18 and Lesson 19 both return to it as the language of a major devotional literature.
- **Maithili's script change** - historically Tirhuta, now mainly Devanagari - is a complete script-versus-language demonstration in one line: the language persisted while the script was replaced.
- **Dakhini and Rekhta** as named Urdu varieties connect directly to Urdu's Deccan history in Lesson 18.

### 4. The Bengali Language Movement: language as politics, with dates

This is the one episode in this lesson with a full narrative, and it is worth holding in sequence because it explains an international observance that appears in objective items.

```text
  EARLY 1948  Bengalis in East Pakistan find that the post-office
              money-order form carries only Urdu, excluding Bangla
        |
  1948        DHIRENDRANATH DATTA raises the demand in the
              CONSTITUENT ASSEMBLY OF PAKISTAN that Bangla be one
              of the national languages, alongside Urdu
        |
  21 FEB 1952 Police open fire on rallies; lives are lost
        |
  1956        After years of conflict, the Central Government grants
              official status to the Bengali language
        |
  1999        UNESCO announces 21 FEBRUARY as INTERNATIONAL MOTHER
              LANGUAGE DAY, to promote awareness of linguistic and
              cultural diversity and to promote multilingualism
        |
  2002        The day is formally recognised by the UN
        |
  2008        The UN establishes the INTERNATIONAL YEAR OF LANGUAGES
```

Two precision points make the difference in an objective item. The body that announced the day in 1999 was **UNESCO**, not UNICEF - a substitution used in a past item. And the demand raised in 1948 was in the **Constituent Assembly of Pakistan**, not India's.

### 5. Why this matters beyond the facts

The Bengali episode is the clearest demonstration in this session that **language status is a political object**, not a neutral description. A form printed in one language and not another produced a mass movement, deaths, a constitutional concession in 1956, and eventually an international observance. When Lesson 6 discusses Article 343 and the protests that followed it, and when Lesson 7 discusses the creation of classical status in 2004, the same mechanism is visible: recognition is allocated by authorities, contested by communities, and dated.

### 6. Comparative treatment: two ways of counting India's languages

| | **Descriptive counting** | **Administrative counting** |
|---|---|---|
| Who does it | linguists and catalogues of languages | Parliament, through the Eighth Schedule, and state governments |
| What it counts | speech varieties distinguished by structure | languages listed for specified constitutional and administrative purposes |
| Typical answer | thousands of varieties; the world total is put near 7,000, of which only around 359 are spoken by millions and are "truly global", including Mandarin Chinese, English, Spanish and Hindi | 22 scheduled languages; state official languages which need not be scheduled at all |
| Where they disagree | Maithili and Rajasthani appear as dialect groups within Hindi and also as separately named languages; some Dravidian tribal dialects are "sometimes considered independent languages" | Tripura's Kokborok, Puducherry's French and Mizoram's Mizo are official at state level and not in the Eighth Schedule |
| Answer discipline | say "varieties" and cite the catalogue | say "scheduled" and cite the Schedule; never use one count to answer the other's question |

The **Linguistic Diversity Index** belongs here as the measuring instrument: it measures the diversity of languages spoken in a country on a scale from **0 to 1**, where **0 represents no linguistic diversity** - everyone speaks the same language - and **1 represents total diversity**, where no two people speak the same language. **No country has an index value of exactly 0 or 1.** The index is useful in an answer precisely because it converts "India is diverse" into a comparable measure.

### Examination use

The dialect table supports pairing items - a dialect against a language or a region - and the four isolated entries in section 3 are the ones most often used. The Bengali Language Movement supports statement items, where the two precision points (UNESCO not UNICEF; Constituent Assembly of Pakistan) are the discriminators. For Mains, the two-column comparison in section 6 is the highest-value block: a question on India's linguistic diversity answered with "22 scheduled languages" alone is a weak answer, while one that distinguishes descriptive from administrative counting, cites the Linguistic Diversity Index as the measuring instrument, and gives Kokborok or Mizo as a state-level official language outside the Schedule, has demonstrated command of the whole architecture.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating "dialect" as a lower rank. Maithili is a Bihari dialect group in the descriptive table and a scheduled language in the Constitution; both statements are true.
- Attributing International Mother Language Day to UNICEF. It was announced by **UNESCO** in **1999**, formally recognised by the UN in **2002**.
- Placing the 1948 Bangla demand in India's Constituent Assembly rather than Pakistan's.
- Assuming a state's official language must be in the Eighth Schedule - Kokborok, French and Mizo are counter-examples developed in Lesson 6.
- Treating Dakhini as a separate language rather than as a named southern variant of Urdu.
- Forgetting that Maithili's historical script was Tirhuta; the language-script separation is examinable in exactly this form.

### Revision notes

1. Modern Indo-Aryan: Hindi, Assamese, Bengali, Gujarati, Marathi, Punjabi, Rajasthani, Sindhi, Odia, Urdu and others; developed gradually after AD 1000.
2. Hindi dialect blocks: Western, Eastern, Rajasthani, Bihari, Pahari, plus contact forms Bambaiya Hindi and Haflong Hindi.
3. Khari Boli = standard/formal Hindi around Delhi and western UP; Braj Bhasha = Mathura-Agra, melodious and literary; Awadhi = Awadh region.
4. Bihari group: Bhojpuri, Magahi, Maithili. Pahari: Kumaoni, Garhwali.
5. Suniti Kumar Chatterji's four Bengali dialects: Rarhi, Bangali, Kamrupi, Varendri.
6. Punjabi's Majhi is the foundation of standard Punjabi; Tamil's Central dialect is the standard spoken form; Madras Bashai is the Chennai dialect.
7. Maithili was historically written in Tirhuta, now mainly Devanagari.
8. Urdu varieties: Dakhini (southern), Rekhta (older/poetic).
9. Some Dravidian tribal dialects - Gondi, Kui, Toda - are sometimes considered independent languages.
10. Bengali Language Movement: money-order form issue, early 1948; Dhirendranath Datta's demand in the Constituent Assembly of Pakistan, 1948; firing on 21 February 1952; official status to Bengali in 1956.
11. UNESCO announced 21 February as International Mother Language Day in 1999; the UN formally recognised it in 2002; the UN established 2008 as the International Year of Languages.
12. Linguistic Diversity Index: 0 to 1; 0 = no diversity, 1 = total diversity; no country is exactly 0 or 1. World total near 7,000 languages; around 359 spoken by millions.

### Concept check

**Question:** Maithili appears as a Bihari dialect of Hindi in the descriptive dialect table and as a separately listed language in the Eighth Schedule. Is one of the two classifications wrong?

**Model answer:** Neither is wrong, because they answer different questions. The descriptive classification groups speech varieties by structural affinity and places Maithili within the Bihari cluster. The constitutional classification lists languages for specified official purposes, and Maithili was added to the Eighth Schedule by the 92nd Amendment Act of 2003. A language can be structurally close to its neighbours and administratively distinct from them at the same time; the correct answer names both classifications, names the authority behind each, and does not force them to agree.

**Misconception to avoid:** That constitutional listing settles a linguistic question. The Schedule allocates status; it does not adjudicate descent, structure or mutual intelligibility, and treating it as linguistic evidence is the same category error as treating a script as a language.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Explain why India's linguistic diversity cannot be measured by the number of scheduled languages. (10 marks, 150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

**The Schedule is an administrative list, not a census.** It contains **22** languages after three amendment expansions, and its criteria are not demographic: **Sanskrit** is listed, **English** is not.

**Three reasons the count understates diversity.** First, the **dialect layer**: Hindi alone divides into Western, Eastern, Rajasthani, Bihari and Pahari blocks with named members such as Khari Boli, Braj Bhasha, Awadhi and Bhojpuri. Second, **classifications disagree**: Maithili is a Bihari dialect group descriptively and a scheduled language constitutionally, and some Dravidian tribal dialects - Gondi, Kui, Toda - are "sometimes considered independent languages". Third, **state practice runs outside the Schedule**: Tripura uses **Kokborok**, Puducherry **French**, Mizoram **Mizo**.

**The measure that does work.** The **Linguistic Diversity Index** runs from 0 to 1, and no country sits at either extreme - a comparative measure rather than a list.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 10: **2** for establishing that the Schedule is administrative, with the Sanskrit-and-English pair as proof; **6** for the three reasons at 2 each, each requiring a named example; **2** for offering the Linguistic Diversity Index as the appropriate instrument. Asserting that India has "many more languages" without the three structural reasons caps at 4.

## Lesson 6 - Constitutional and administrative status: what the Eighth Schedule is, and what it is not

Progress: 6/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Article 343, the fifteen-year clause and the protests, the Official Languages Act of 1963, the Eighth Schedule with its amendment history, the state-level position, and the non-existence of a national language

CA search: "Not required - settled constitutional status material; no current claim is made in this lesson"
CA found: None found; no genuine recent linkage for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four statuses that are constantly fused, separated once and for all

```text
 +------------------------+  +------------------------+
 | OFFICIAL LANGUAGE OF   |  | EIGHTH SCHEDULE        |
 | THE UNION              |  | 22 SCHEDULED LANGUAGES |
 | Article 343(1):        |  | A constitutional LIST  |
 | "Hindi in Devanagari   |  | Sanskrit is one of them|
 | script"                |  | English is NOT on it   |
 | English = "subsidiary  |  | Added by amendment     |
 | official language"     |  | over time              |
 | (Official Languages    |  |                        |
 | Act, 1963)             |  |                        |
 +------------------------+  +------------------------+
 +------------------------+  +------------------------+
 | STATE OFFICIAL         |  | NATIONAL LANGUAGE      |
 | LANGUAGE               |  |                        |
 | The Constitution does  |  |        DOES NOT EXIST  |
 | NOT specify one; States|  | Neither the Constitut- |
 | are free to adopt      |  | ion nor any Act defines|
 | Need NOT be in the     |  | the term. Hindi is NOT |
 | Eighth Schedule:       |  | a national language.   |
 | Tripura - Kokborok     |  |                        |
 | Puducherry - French    |  | Any sentence using the |
 | Mizoram - Mizo         |  | phrase about India is  |
 | Arunachal Pradesh and  |  | wrong before you read  |
 | Nagaland: English only |  | the rest of it.        |
 +------------------------+  +------------------------+

 NONE OF THESE FOUR BOXES IMPLIES ANY OTHER.
 (A fifth, classical-language status, is Lesson 7 and is also separate.)
```

Four boxes with no arrows between them. The absence of arrows is the content of the diagram.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

This is the highest-frequency error zone in the entire topic, and it is an error of *vocabulary*, not of knowledge. Candidates who know all the facts still write "Hindi is our national language" because the phrase is culturally ubiquitous. The cure is to hold the four boxes above as four separate objects with four different authorities, and to notice that an examination item in this area almost always works by swapping one label for another inside an otherwise correct sentence.

A boundary note first: the constitutional doctrine here belongs to the polity papers, which argue the law. This session states what each status **is**, because a cultural answer that misdescribes a status is factually wrong, and stops at the edge of the doctrine.

### 2. Article 343 and the fifteen-year clause

**Article 343(1)** states that **"The official language of the Union Government shall be Hindi in Devanagari Script."** Note that the provision fixes **both** a language and a **script**, which is itself an instructive example of the two being separable enough to need separate specification.

The Article carried a transitional design: **unless Parliament decided otherwise, the use of English for official purposes was to cease fifteen years after the Constitution came into effect, that is, on 26 January 1965**. Over that fifteen-year period Hindi was to replace English as the official language, and **Parliament could decide whether English could continue to be used**.

**This clause led to protests across the nation by non-Hindi-speaking communities** against the change from English to Hindi. The protest produced legislation: **the Official Languages Act, 1963**, which **declares Hindi in Devanagari script as the official language of the Union** and gives **English the status of "subsidiary official language" of the Union**.

Three things to carry from this. First, the term of art for English at Union level is **subsidiary official language** - not "second national language", not "associate language" in this formulation. Second, the outcome was produced by **protest followed by statute**, the same pattern as the Bengali episode in Lesson 5. Third, the date **26 January 1965** is the deadline that never operated as originally designed, and an item can be built on whether it did.

### 3. The Eighth Schedule, with its amendment history

The Schedule began with **fourteen** languages:

> Assamese · Bengali · Gujarati · Hindi · Kannada · Kashmiri · Malayalam · Marathi · Odia · Punjabi · Sanskrit · Tamil · Telugu · Urdu

It was then expanded four times:

| Addition | Amendment | Languages added | Running total |
|---|---|---|---:|
| First | **21st Amendment Act, 1967** | Sindhi | 15 |
| Second | **71st Amendment Act, 1992** | Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali | 18 |
| Third | **92nd Amendment Act, 2003** | Bodo, Maithili, Dogri, Santhali | 22 |
| Present position | - | - | **22** |

Two observations that earn marks. **Sanskrit is one of the 22 Scheduled Languages** - a point that surprises candidates who think of the Schedule as a list of currently spoken mother tongues, and a reminder that the Schedule's criteria are not demographic. And **English is not in the list of 22 scheduled languages**, despite being the subsidiary official language of the Union - the cleanest possible proof that the two statuses are independent.

The four-step growth is also worth holding as a pattern rather than as four isolated facts: the Schedule is an **amendable list**, which means it is a political instrument responding to claims, and a candidate who says "22 at present, after four expansions between 1967 and 2003" has said something more informative than one who says "22".

### 4. The state-level position

**The Constitution does not specify an official language for the States for the conduct of their official functions. States are free to adopt an official language.** And the crucial rider: **the language adopted by a State need not be one of those listed in the Eighth Schedule.** Several States have adopted an official language that is not listed.

The named examples are worth memorising exactly because each attacks a different assumption:

| State / UT | Official language | The assumption it breaks |
|---|---|---|
| **Tripura** | **Kokborok**, belonging to the Sino-Tibetan family | that state official languages must be scheduled, and that they must be Indo-Aryan |
| **Puducherry** | **French** | that a state official language must be an Indian language |
| **Mizoram** | **Mizo** | that a state language must be scheduled |
| **Arunachal Pradesh and Nagaland** | **English only** - the only states which have English as their **sole** official language | that English's position is uniform across India |

### 5. The sentence that must never be written

**There is no national language of India. Hindi is not a national language. Neither the Constitution nor any Act defines the word "national language".**

That is not a nuance; it is a flat statement of the position, and it is the single most useful line to carry from this lesson. An objective item in this area typically presents a list of features and asks which are **not** features of Article 343 - and the planted false feature is almost always the words "national language" substituted for "official language" in an otherwise accurate clause. Read the clause for the label before you evaluate the content.

### 6. Comparative treatment: five statuses, five authorities

Bringing Lesson 7's material forward one step, so that the whole status architecture is visible in one place:

| Status | Granted by | Governing instrument | What it actually confers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Official language of the Union | the Constitution, as modified by Parliament | Article 343(1); Official Languages Act, 1963 | the language of Union official business, with English as subsidiary official language |
| Eighth Schedule listing | Parliament, by constitutional amendment | the Eighth Schedule | listing for specified constitutional purposes; an amendable list, now 22 |
| State official language | the State itself | State decision; no constitutional specification | official use within the State; need not be scheduled |
| Classical language | the Government of India, from 2004 | the 2004 decision and its criteria (Lesson 7) | named academic and institutional benefits |
| Documentary-heritage inscription | UNESCO | the Memory of the World programme, at International or Asia-Pacific Regional level (Lesson 22) | recognition of a document at a stated register level, not of a language at all |

The last row is included deliberately. The most subtle version of this error is not "national versus official" but confusing a status held by a **language** with a status held by a **document** - and Lesson 22 shows how often that swap is made.

### Examination use

Items here are almost always **statement-evaluation** items with a swapped label, and the defence is mechanical: check the *label* first (national / official / scheduled / classical), then check the *authority*, then check the *content*. The state-level examples are pairing material. For Mains, this lesson supplies a precise qualification rather than a body of argument: any answer on Indian linguistic diversity that touches policy should state the four-box separation in one sentence, because it signals command and forecloses the commonest factual error an examiner looks for.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing or implying that India has a national language.
- Calling English an "associate national language"; at Union level the recorded term is **subsidiary official language** under the Official Languages Act, 1963.
- Assuming English is in the Eighth Schedule. It is not.
- Forgetting that **Sanskrit** is one of the 22 scheduled languages.
- Assuming a state official language must be scheduled - Kokborok, French and Mizo refute it.
- Forgetting that Arunachal Pradesh and Nagaland are the only states with English as their **only** official language.
- Misattributing the four expansions: Sindhi came by the 21st Amendment (1967); Konkani, Manipuri and Nepali by the 71st (1992); Bodo, Maithili, Dogri and Santhali by the 92nd (2003).
- Treating 26 January 1965 as a date on which English ceased to be used officially.

### Revision notes

1. Article 343(1): the official language of the Union shall be Hindi in Devanagari script - a language **and** a script are both specified.
2. English was to cease for official purposes fifteen years after commencement, i.e. on 26 January 1965, unless Parliament decided otherwise.
3. Protests by non-Hindi-speaking communities led to the Official Languages Act, 1963.
4. That Act declares Hindi in Devanagari script the official language of the Union and gives English the status of **subsidiary official language**.
5. Eighth Schedule began with 14: Assamese, Bengali, Gujarati, Hindi, Kannada, Kashmiri, Malayalam, Marathi, Odia, Punjabi, Sanskrit, Tamil, Telugu, Urdu.
6. 21st Amendment 1967: Sindhi (15). 71st Amendment 1992: Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali (18). 92nd Amendment 2003: Bodo, Maithili, Dogri, Santhali (22).
7. Sanskrit is scheduled; English is not.
8. The Constitution does not specify State official languages; States may adopt any language, scheduled or not.
9. Tripura - Kokborok (Sino-Tibetan); Puducherry - French; Mizoram - Mizo.
10. Arunachal Pradesh and Nagaland are the only states with English as their sole official language.
11. There is no national language of India; neither the Constitution nor any Act defines the term.
12. Five statuses, five authorities: Union official, Eighth Schedule, State official, classical, documentary-heritage inscription. None implies another.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the existence of Kokborok and French as state official languages matter for an answer about India's language policy, beyond being two pairing facts?

**Model answer:** Because they demonstrate that Indian language policy is **two-tier and asymmetric by design**, not a single centrally managed list. The Union tier is specified constitutionally - Hindi in Devanagari, with English as subsidiary official language - while the State tier is deliberately left unspecified, so that States may adopt a language from outside the Eighth Schedule entirely, including a non-Indo-Aryan language such as Kokborok, which belongs to the Sino-Tibetan family, or a European language such as French in Puducherry. An answer using these examples is arguing about the architecture of the policy rather than listing its outputs, and it also pre-empts the common assumption that the Eighth Schedule is the exhaustive universe of officially usable Indian languages.

**Misconception to avoid:** That the Eighth Schedule is a list of "recognised languages of India" in a general sense. It is a list for specified constitutional purposes; languages outside it are in official use at State level, and one language inside it - Sanskrit - is listed on grounds that are not demographic at all.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Distinguish the official language of the Union, a scheduled language and a state official language, and explain why the distinction matters in describing India's language policy. (10 marks, 150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

**Three distinct statuses.** **Article 343(1)** makes **Hindi in Devanagari script** the official language of the Union; the **Official Languages Act, 1963** gives English the status of **subsidiary official language**. The **Eighth Schedule** is a constitutional **list**, now of 22 languages, amended in 1967, 1992 and 2003. A **state official language** is adopted by the State itself, and the Constitution does not specify one.

**Why the distinction matters.** It is **descriptive accuracy**: the statuses come from different authorities under different instruments, and none implies another - English is subsidiary official but unscheduled, Sanskrit is scheduled without being in everyday official use. It also shows the policy is **two-tier and asymmetric by design**, because States may adopt languages from outside the Schedule entirely - **Kokborok**, a Sino-Tibetan language, in Tripura, **French** in Puducherry, **Mizo** in Mizoram - and Arunachal Pradesh and Nagaland use **English alone**.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 10: **4** for the three statuses with their instruments at 1.3 each, forfeited in full if "national language" appears; **3** for demonstrating non-implication using the English and Sanskrit pair; **3** for the two-tier asymmetry argument with at least two state examples. Listing the 22 languages earns nothing here.

## Lesson 7 - Classical-language status: a dated administrative decision, not a compliment

### Visual: the category, its gate, its holders and its consequences

```text
  2004: THE GOVERNMENT OF INDIA CREATES THE CATEGORY
        a new category of languages known as CLASSICAL LANGUAGES
                              |
   THE GATE HAS BEEN REVISED TWICE. THREE GENERATIONS OF CRITERIA.
   +--- 2004, the original three ------------------------------+
   | - high antiquity of early texts / recorded history OVER A  |
   |   THOUSAND YEARS                                           |
   | - a body of ancient literature / texts considered a        |
   |   valuable heritage by generations of speakers             |
   | - the literary tradition must be ORIGINAL AND NOT BORROWED |
   |   from another speech community                            |
   +------------------------------------------------------------+
                              |
   +--- NOVEMBER 2005, revised to four; Sanskrit then declared --+
   | - antiquity span raised to 1500-2000 YEARS                 |
   | - valuable-heritage criterion retained                     |
   | - ORIGINAL AND NOT BORROWED retained                       |
   | - NEW: classical language and literature DISTINCT FROM     |
   |   MODERN, with a possible DISCONTINUITY with later forms   |
   +------------------------------------------------------------+
                              |
   +--- 2024, THE CRITERIA NOW IN FORCE (four) -----------------+
   | 1. High antiquity of its early texts / recorded history    |
   |    over a period of 1500-2000 years                        |
   | 2. A body of ancient literature / texts which is           |
   |    considered A HERITAGE by generations of speakers        |
   | 3. KNOWLEDGE TEXTS, ESPECIALLY PROSE TEXTS in addition to  |
   |    poetry, EPIGRAPHICAL AND INSCRIPTIONAL EVIDENCE         |
   | 4. The Classical Languages and literature COULD BE         |
   |    DISTINCT from its current form or COULD BE              |
   |    DISCONTINUOUS with later forms of its offshoots         |
   +------------------------------------------------------------+
   -> the "ORIGINAL AND NOT BORROWED" test was DROPPED in 2024
   -> a KNOWLEDGE-TEXT-AND-EPIGRAPHY test was ADDED in its place
   -> criterion 2 softened from "valuable heritage" to "heritage"
   -> criterion 4 softened from "must be" to "could be"
                              |
   WHO REVISES IT: the LINGUISTIC EXPERTS COMMITTEE (LEC) under the
   Sahitya Akademi, set up by the Ministry of Culture on 1 NOV 2004;
   the 2024 LEC recommended Marathi, Pali, Prakrit, Assamese and
   Bengali as fulfilling the revised criteria; the UNION CABINET
   approved them on 3 OCTOBER 2024, taking the total to ELEVEN
                              |
   NEP 2020: in addition to Sanskrit, the teaching of all other
   classical languages and literature of India - including Tamil,
   Telugu, Kannada, Malayalam, Odia, PALI, PERSIAN and PRAKRIT -
   will also be widely available in schools as options
```

Read the third and fourth criteria of the 2024 set twice. The third is new and is the one candidates never quote, because it imports an **evidence requirement** - prose knowledge texts, epigraphy and inscriptions - into what looks like a purely literary category. The fourth concedes that a classical language may be **discontinuous** with whatever is spoken now, which is why a language with no modern speech community is not disqualified.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

"Classical language" sounds like a description of quality, so candidates treat it as a compliment and write sentences like "Tamil is a classical language because of its great beauty". It is nothing of the kind. It is an **administrative category created in 2004 by a government decision, with published criteria that have themselves been revised twice, a growing list and specified funding consequences**. Writing about it correctly means writing about a decision, its gate, its holders, its dates and its outputs - and never using it as a synonym for "old and important". It also means quoting the criteria **that are currently in force**, not the set a textbook printed before the 2024 revision.

### 2. The criteria now in force, read closely

The criteria were set in 2004, revised in November 2005 and revised again in 2024, each time on the recommendation of a **Linguistic Experts Committee (LEC) under the Sahitya Akademi**. The set below is the **2024 set, the one currently in force**; the earlier sets are set out in section 3 because an answer that quotes a superseded criterion is wrong on the law of the scheme even if it is quoting a textbook accurately.

| Criterion, 2024 set | What it actually requires | What it rules out |
|---|---|---|
| **1. High antiquity of its early texts / recorded history over a period of 1500-2000 years** | a documented span, not a claim of antiquity | languages with old speech but no long documented textual record |
| **2. A body of ancient literature / texts which is considered a heritage by generations of speakers** | a *corpus* plus a *community's continuing valuation* of it | isolated ancient inscriptions without a received literary body |
| **3. Knowledge texts, especially prose texts in addition to poetry, epigraphical and inscriptional evidence** | an **evidence base broader than verse**: expository prose, technical or knowledge literature, and a surviving epigraphic record | a tradition attested only by poetry, or only in manuscript, with no inscriptional footing |
| **4. The Classical Languages and literature could be distinct from its current form or could be discontinuous with later forms of its offshoots** | that a classical stage be identifiable; discontinuity with the modern form is **permitted**, not required | treating the modern standard language as automatically "the classical language" |

Two of these repay close reading.

**Criterion three is the new one, and it changes the character of the category.** It requires *knowledge texts, especially prose texts in addition to poetry, epigraphical and inscriptional evidence*. That imports the whole apparatus of Lessons 9 to 11 into a language-status question: a claim under this criterion is discharged by **prose knowledge literature plus an epigraphic record**, which is why the earliest-inscription anchors of Lesson 11 are not a separate curiosity but part of the evidence a classical-language case now rests on.

**Criterion four is permissive rather than mandatory.** Its 2024 wording - *could be* distinct, *could be* discontinuous - is weaker than the 2005 wording, which said the classical language and literature *being distinct from modern*, there *may also be* a discontinuity. Either way it disposes of a popular argument: that a language deserves classical status because it has been *continuously* spoken. Continuity is not the criterion, and a language with no modern speech community is not disqualified by that fact alone.

### 3. The three generations of criteria, and the eleven holders with their dates

**The criteria, as successively published.**

| Set | When | What it required |
|---|---|---|
| **Original** | **2004**, when the category was created | high antiquity of early texts or recorded history **over a thousand years**; a body of ancient literature or texts considered a **valuable heritage** by generations of speakers; the literary tradition **original and not borrowed** from another speech community. **Three criteria only** |
| **First revision** | **November 2005**, and Sanskrit was declared a Classical Language on that revision | antiquity span raised to **1500-2000 years**; valuable-heritage criterion retained; **original and not borrowed** retained; **added** - the classical language and literature being **distinct from modern**, there may also be a **discontinuity** between the classical language and its later forms or offshoots. **Four criteria** |
| **Second revision - the set in force** | **2024**, on the recommendation of the Linguistic Experts Committee | the four criteria set out in section 2: the 1500-2000-year span; **a heritage** by generations of speakers; **knowledge texts, especially prose texts in addition to poetry, epigraphical and inscriptional evidence**; and the classical language and literature **could be** distinct from its current form or **could be** discontinuous with later forms of its offshoots |

**What actually changed in 2024.** The *original and not borrowed* test was **dropped**. A **knowledge-text, prose and epigraphic-evidence** test was **added** in its place. Criterion two was softened from *valuable heritage* to *heritage*, and criterion four from a mandatory *must be distinct* formulation to a permissive *could be*. An answer that still lists *original and not borrowed* among the criteria is describing the 2004-2005 scheme, not the one under which the most recent recognitions were made.

**The eleven holders, with their official recognition dates.**

| Language | Date of recognition | Notified by |
|---|---|---|
| **Tamil** | **12 October 2004** | Ministry of Home Affairs |
| **Sanskrit** | **25 November 2005** | Ministry of Home Affairs |
| **Telugu** | **31 October 2008** | Ministry of Culture |
| **Kannada** | **31 October 2008** | Ministry of Culture |
| **Malayalam** | **8 August 2013** | Ministry of Culture |
| **Odia** | **1 March 2014** | Ministry of Culture |
| **Marathi** | **3 October 2024** (Union Cabinet approval) | Ministry of Culture |
| **Pali** | **3 October 2024** (Union Cabinet approval) | Ministry of Culture |
| **Prakrit** | **3 October 2024** (Union Cabinet approval) | Ministry of Culture |
| **Assamese** | **3 October 2024** (Union Cabinet approval) | Ministry of Culture |
| **Bengali** | **3 October 2024** (Union Cabinet approval) | Ministry of Culture |

Two administrative facts sit inside that table and are examinable in their own right. **The Ministry of Home Affairs granted the first two recognitions** - Tamil and Sanskrit - and **the Ministry of Culture took over responsibility for later implementation and recognitions**. And the **Linguistic Experts Committee was established by the Ministry of Culture on 1 November 2004**, that is, after Tamil had already been recognised, to assess future proposals.

**The total is eleven, and that total is dated.** The five additions of **October 2024** - Marathi, Pali, Prakrit, Assamese and Bengali - nearly doubled the list at one stroke, which is precisely why any figure learned before that date is now wrong and any figure learned now may be wrong later. A bounded verification undertaken for this session found **no further language granted classical status after the October 2024 decision** as on the date of this session; that verification is recorded in the ledger with its scope.

Two entries deserve separate attention because they are exactly where items are set.

- **Pali and Prakrit** hold classical status while being the canonical and literary registers of Lesson 4 rather than modern spoken languages. Criterion four of the 2024 set expressly permits a classical language to be discontinuous with later forms, so the absence of a modern speech community is not a disqualification. Note the limit of what can be said here: the Linguistic Experts Committee **recommended Marathi, Pali, Prakrit, Assamese and Bengali as fulfilling the revised criteria** and the Cabinet approved that recommendation; no criterion-by-criterion adjudication for any individual language has been published, so an answer should reason from the criteria to eligibility and stop short of asserting which criterion decided which case.
- **Sanskrit** now holds **three** separate statuses at once - Eighth Schedule listing, classical-language recognition (2005), and the status of the language in which a large part of the corpus in Lessons 12 to 14 is written. Naming the three separately is the discipline of Lesson 6 applied.

### 4. The benefits, as promised and as implemented

**As promised.** The Government of India's resolution states that the following benefits accrue to a language declared classical:

1. **Two major international awards** for scholars of eminence in Classical Indian Languages, to be awarded **annually**.
2. **A Centre of Excellence for Studies in the Classical Language** will be set up.
3. **The University Grants Commission will be requested** to create - and to start with, at least in the Central Universities - **a certain number of professional chairs in Classical Languages for scholars of eminence**.

Notice the verbs. The awards *will be awarded*; the centre *will be set up*; the UGC *will be requested*. The third is a request to an autonomous body, not a direction - a small but genuine distinction, and exactly the kind of clause an examiner uses to build a "which of the following is correct" item. Reproduce the verb.

**As implemented.** The official record of steps actually taken is more specific than the resolution, and citing it converts a promise into evidence:

- **three Central Universities were established in 2020 through an Act of Parliament to promote Sanskrit**;
- the **Central Institute of Classical Tamil** was created to translate ancient Tamil texts, promote research and offer courses for university students and scholars;
- **Centres for Excellence for studies in Classical Kannada, Telugu, Malayalam and Odia** were established under the **Central Institute of Indian Languages, Mysuru**;
- **National Awards for Classical Languages, university chairs and dedicated centres** are provided through the Ministry of Education.

The stated impact is employment in the **academic and research sectors**, and work in **archiving, translation, publishing and digital media** arising from the preservation, documentation and digitisation of ancient texts - which is the point at which this lesson joins the manuscript-institutions argument of Lesson 21.

### 5. The education-policy layer

The **National Education Policy (NEP) 2020** emphasises the study of classical languages in schools **as an additional option**. Its stated position: *"In addition to Sanskrit, the teaching of all other classical languages and literature of India, including Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, Malayalam, Odia, Pali, Persian, and Prakrit, will also be widely available in schools as options."*

That sentence contains a trap of its own. It names **Persian** alongside the classical languages available as school options - but Persian is **not** among the eleven languages holding classical-language status. The NEP's list of languages to be made available is not identical with the list of languages formally recognised as classical. Quote the policy sentence as a policy sentence, and keep the recognition list separate.

### 6. Comparative treatment: three ways a language can be "important" in Indian policy

| | **Eighth Schedule listing** | **Classical-language status** | **Union official-language use** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Created by | the Constitution, amended by Parliament | a Government of India decision, 2004 | Article 343(1) and the Official Languages Act, 1963 |
| Gate | political decision through amendment | published criteria, revised in 2005 and again in 2024 | constitutional specification |
| Current position | 22 languages | 11 languages | Hindi in Devanagari, with English as subsidiary official language |
| Typical output | listing for specified constitutional purposes | awards, a centre of excellence, university chairs | the conduct of Union official business |
| Overlap | Sanskrit, Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, Malayalam, Odia, Marathi, Assamese and Bengali are in both lists | Pali and Prakrit hold classical status and are **not** in the Eighth Schedule | neither list determines this |

The middle-bottom cell is the sharpest fact in the lesson: **two languages hold classical status without being scheduled**. Anyone who believes the statuses are nested now has a counter-example.

### 7. The date-discipline this status demands

Every sentence about this category should carry its date, for a reason that is structural rather than pedantic. **Two things move, not one.** The **list** has grown in 2004, 2005, 2008, 2013, 2014 and 2024, and it grew by five in a single year. The **criteria** have themselves been rewritten twice, in November 2005 and in 2024. So a sentence of the form "India has eleven classical languages, which must have an original and unborrowed literary tradition" is doubly stale: the total is undated and the criterion was dropped. The examinable form is: **"as on the Union Cabinet decision of 3 October 2024, eleven languages hold classical-language status under the criteria revised in 2024"**, with the verification date stated if the claim is being made in writing much later.

### Examination use

This area produces recurring objective items of three kinds: **which language was recently granted the status**, which is a pure date item and changes over time; **which of these languages holds the status**, which is a list item; and, increasingly, **which of the following is a criterion**, which is now a trap because the answer changed in 2024. All three are defended by the dated tables in section 3 plus the discipline in section 7. For Mains, the strongest use is as a **qualification block** inside a broader answer: a question about protecting India's linguistic heritage is well served by naming the category, the criteria in force, their revision history, the dated growth of the list and the concrete institutional outputs, because that converts a general appeal into a description of an actual instrument. The Pali-and-Prakrit case remains the best single illustration, and the 2024 knowledge-text-and-epigraphy criterion is the best single detail, because almost no candidate quotes it.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating classical status as a statement about beauty, purity or antiquity in the abstract rather than as an administrative recognition against published criteria.
- **Quoting the superseded criterion.** *"The literary tradition must be original and not borrowed from another speech community"* belonged to the 2004 and 2005 sets and was **dropped in the 2024 revision**. It is not a criterion in force.
- **Omitting the criterion that replaced it** - *knowledge texts, especially prose texts in addition to poetry, epigraphical and inscriptional evidence*.
- Quoting a pre-October-2024 total. The five additions of 2024 changed the figure.
- Assuming classical languages are a subset of scheduled languages; Pali and Prakrit are not scheduled.
- Assuming every language named in the NEP sentence holds classical status; Persian is named there and is not among the eleven.
- Rendering the third benefit as a direction to the UGC; the resolution says the UGC "will be requested".
- Forgetting that discontinuity with later forms is **permitted** by criterion four of the 2024 set, whose wording is *could be*, not *must be*.
- Asserting which criterion decided a particular recognition; the Committee recommended the five languages as fulfilling the revised criteria as a whole, and no per-language adjudication is published.
- Confusing the year a language was recognised with the antiquity of its literature; Odia's 2014 recognition says nothing about the age of its texts.
- Attributing all recognitions to one ministry; the **Ministry of Home Affairs** notified Tamil and Sanskrit, and the **Ministry of Culture** has handled the rest.

### Revision notes

1. The category was created in 2004 by a Government of India decision; the criteria were revised in November 2005 and again in 2024, each time on a Linguistic Experts Committee recommendation.
2. **2024 criterion 1:** high antiquity of early texts / recorded history over a period of 1500-2000 years.
3. **2024 criterion 2:** a body of ancient literature / texts which is considered **a heritage** by generations of speakers.
4. **2024 criterion 3 (new):** knowledge texts, especially **prose texts in addition to poetry**, **epigraphical and inscriptional evidence**.
5. **2024 criterion 4:** the Classical Languages and literature **could be** distinct from the current form or **could be** discontinuous with later forms of its offshoots.
6. **Dropped in 2024:** "the literary tradition must be original and not borrowed from another speech community", which belonged to the 2004 and 2005 sets.
7. 2004 original set: antiquity **over a thousand years**; valuable heritage; original and not borrowed - three criteria only. November 2005 set: span raised to 1500-2000 years, distinctness and possible discontinuity added - four criteria; Sanskrit declared on that revision.
8. The eleven with official dates: Tamil 12 October 2004 and Sanskrit 25 November 2005, both notified by the Ministry of Home Affairs; Telugu and Kannada 31 October 2008, Malayalam 8 August 2013, Odia 1 March 2014, all Ministry of Culture; Marathi, Pali, Prakrit, Assamese and Bengali by Union Cabinet approval on 3 October 2024, taking the total to eleven.
9. The Linguistic Experts Committee was established by the Ministry of Culture on 1 November 2004; the 2024 Committee recommended the five languages as fulfilling the revised criteria, and no per-language criterion-by-criterion adjudication is published.
10. Resolution benefits: two annual international awards for scholars of eminence; a Centre of Excellence for Studies in the Classical Language; the UGC to be *requested* to create professional chairs, at least in Central Universities to begin with.
11. Implementation record: three Central Universities for Sanskrit established in 2020 by an Act of Parliament; the Central Institute of Classical Tamil; Centres for Excellence in Classical Kannada, Telugu, Malayalam and Odia under the Central Institute of Indian Languages, Mysuru; National Awards, university chairs and centres through the Ministry of Education.
12. NEP 2020 makes classical languages available in schools as an additional option and names Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, Malayalam, Odia, Pali, Persian and Prakrit alongside Sanskrit; **Persian is named there but does not hold classical-language status**.
13. Pali and Prakrit hold classical status without being in the Eighth Schedule; Sanskrit holds both, and the two statuses are separate.
14. Date both moving parts: the **list** and the **criteria**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate lists the criteria for classical-language status as antiquity of 1500-2000 years, a valued body of ancient literature, an original and unborrowed literary tradition, and distinctness from the modern form. What is wrong with that list, and what does the correction change?

**Model answer:** The list is the **November 2005 set, which was superseded in 2024**. In the 2024 revision the **original-and-not-borrowed** criterion was **dropped** and replaced by a new one - **knowledge texts, especially prose texts in addition to poetry, epigraphical and inscriptional evidence** - while criterion two was softened from "valuable heritage" to "heritage" and criterion four from a mandatory to a permissive formulation. The correction changes the character of the category: the old set tested the **independence** of a literary tradition, whereas the set in force tests its **evidentiary breadth**, requiring expository prose and an epigraphic record alongside poetry. It also changes what an answer should cite, since the five recognitions of 3 October 2024 were made on the recommendation that those languages fulfilled the **revised** criteria.

**Misconception to avoid:** That published criteria are fixed once announced. In this scheme the gate has been rewritten twice - in November 2005 and in 2024 - so an answer must date the criteria it quotes just as carefully as it dates the total.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Classical-language recognition has been described both as a cultural honour and as an administrative instrument. Assess which description the record supports. (15 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

The record supports **both**, but the instrument is prior: the honour is delivered *through* an instrument, and every feature of the scheme is administrative in form.

**The instrument.** A category was **created by government decision in 2004**. A standing body evaluates claims: the **Linguistic Experts Committee**, established by the Ministry of Culture on **1 November 2004** under the Sahitya Akademi. Its **criteria are published, and have been rewritten twice** - in **November 2005** and in **2024**. Recognition is **notified**: by the **Ministry of Home Affairs** for Tamil and Sanskrit, by the **Ministry of Culture** thereafter. It is **dated**: Tamil 12 October 2004, Sanskrit 25 November 2005, Telugu and Kannada 31 October 2008, Malayalam 8 August 2013, Odia 1 March 2014, and five more by **Cabinet decision on 3 October 2024**.

**The honour.** The resolution attaches **two annual international awards**, a **Centre of Excellence** and a request to the UGC for **professional chairs**; implementation has produced **three Central Universities for Sanskrit (2020)**, the **Central Institute of Classical Tamil** and Centres for Excellence under the **Central Institute of Indian Languages, Mysuru**.

**The decisive test.** The **2024 revision** settles it. A purely honorific category would not need to drop a criterion - *original and not borrowed* - and replace it with an evidentiary one requiring **prose knowledge texts and epigraphical evidence**. Changing the test changes who can qualify, which is what instruments do.

**Verdict.** An administrative instrument that confers honour, not an honour with administrative trappings.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 15: **5** for the instrument features - created, committee, published criteria, notified, dated - at 1 each; **3** for the honour side, requiring at least two **implemented** institutions rather than only the promised benefits; **4** for using the 2024 revision as the decisive test; **3** for a verdict that orders the two descriptions rather than declaring both true. An answer without the revision argument caps at 8.

## Lesson 8 - Language death, endangerment and the revival machinery

Progress: 8/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: how a language dies, the four-level endangerment scale, India's position, the five extinct languages, the 10,000-speaker thresholds, and the promotion and translation initiatives

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the critically endangered and extinct languages section in full, the Atlas of the World Languages in Danger and the World Atlas of Languages, the four endangerment levels with India's numbers, the five extinct languages, the death-of-a-language box with the post-1971 threshold, the lingua franca box, and the government initiatives section
CA search: "Not required here - the initiatives are described from the source's own record and the session's single current linkage is reserved for Lesson 21"
CA found: None found; no genuine recent linkage for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the mechanism of language death, and the scale that measures it

```text
  THE MECHANISM - how a language actually dies
  native speakers migrate, for social and economic needs
          |
  they stop using the native language ALTOGETHER
          |
  in the INTERGENERATIONAL period, transmission fails
          |
  LANGUAGE DEATH -> LANGUAGE EXTINCTION -> a socio-cultural loss
  (no speaker remains; usage stops)

  THE SCALE - UNESCO's four levels between SAFE and EXTINCT
  SAFE -> 1. VULNERABLE -> 2. DEFINITELY ENDANGERED ->
          3. SEVERELY ENDANGERED -> 4. CRITICALLY ENDANGERED -> EXTINCT

  INDIA ON THAT SCALE (World Atlas of Languages)
  +-------------------------+-------+
  | Vulnerable              |    81 |
  | Definitely endangered   |    62 |
  | Severely endangered     |     7 |
  | Critically endangered   |    42 |
  | Extinct                 |     5 |
  | TOTAL                   |   197 |  <- the highest number of
  +-------------------------+-------+     languages in danger for
                                          any country in the world
  TWO SEPARATE 10,000-SPEAKER RULES - do not merge them
  (a) After the 1971 Census, the Indian government stated that any
      indigenous language spoken by FEWER THAN 10,000 people would
      no longer be considered in the List of Official Languages of India.
  (b) According to UNESCO, any language spoken by FEWER THAN 10,000
      people is POTENTIALLY ENDANGERED.
  -> (a) is an Indian enumeration rule; (b) is an international
     risk classification. Same number, different instruments.
```

The two rules at the bottom share a number and nothing else. An item that pairs the threshold with the wrong authority is trivially easy to set and trivially easy to fail.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Endangerment is usually learned as a list of sad numbers. The numbers are examinable, but the mechanism is what makes an answer analytical, and the mechanism is stated plainly in the sources: **migration for social and economic needs, complete abandonment of the native language, failure of transmission in the intergenerational period, then death and extinction.** Hold the chain and the numbers become evidence for an argument rather than a list to recite.

### 2. The monitoring instruments

**UNESCO has been releasing an Atlas of the World Languages in Danger since 1996** to identify languages in danger and preserve them from extinction. The **World Atlas of Languages (WAL)** is an interactive and dynamic online tool documenting aspects and features of language status in countries and languages around the world, aiming to provide a detailed record of languages **as communicative tools and knowledge resources in their socio-cultural and socio-political contexts**.

That last phrase is worth keeping. It states the premise on which the whole preservation case rests: a language is not only a communication system but a **knowledge resource**, and losing it loses the knowledge encoded in it. That is the sentence to build a Mains paragraph around, because it supplies a reason rather than a sentiment.

### 3. India's position, with the numbers

The UNESCO World Atlas uses **four levels of endangerment between "Safe" (not endangered) and "Extinct"**: **Vulnerable, Definitely Endangered, Severely Endangered, and Critically Endangered.**

As per WAL, **India has 197 languages that are endangered, vulnerable or extinct** on the list. Among these: **5 extinct, 42 critically endangered, 7 severely endangered, 62 definitely endangered and 81 vulnerable.** This is **the highest number of languages in danger for any country in the world.**

Two facts about the distribution repay attention. First, the numbers are **not** a smooth curve: the severely-endangered figure, at 7, is far lower than the critically-endangered figure of 42, which tells you the categories are assigned by criteria rather than by a progression. Second, **most dying languages are from the indigenous tribal groups spread across the country**, and a few are **the languages spoken by the nomadic people in Maharashtra, Karnataka and Telangana** - naming the nomadic case is what turns "tribal languages are at risk" into a located claim.

### 4. The five extinct languages, each with its story

| Language | Family / affiliation | Recorded detail |
|---|---|---|
| **Aka-Bo** | an Andamanese language of the Andaman Islands | the **last native speaker died in 2010** |
| **Aka-Cari** | another Great Andamanese language of the Andaman Islands | the **last speaker passed away in 2020** |
| **Ahom** | a **Tai** language historically spoken in **Assam** | also listed in the language classification as the Indian representative of the Siamese-Chinese group, already extinct in the subcontinent |
| **Pazeh** | an **Austronesian** language historically spoken in India | the **last speaker died in Taiwan in 2010** |
| **Konda-Dora** | a **Dravidian** language spoken by the Konda-Dora tribe in **Andhra Pradesh** | - |

Three of the five entries carry a precise death date or place, and those specifics are the examinable material. The **Pazeh** entry is the strangest and therefore the most memorable: the language was historically spoken in India and its last speaker died **in Taiwan**, which is a compressed illustration of the migration mechanism in section 1.

### 5. The two 10,000-speaker rules

**After the 1971 Census, the Indian government stated that any indigenous language spoken by fewer than 10,000 people would no longer be considered in the List of Official Languages of India.** Separately, **according to UNESCO, any language spoken by fewer than 10,000 people is potentially endangered.**

These are different instruments doing different work - one an Indian enumeration and listing rule after a particular census, the other an international risk threshold - and they happen to share a number. Attributing the endangerment threshold to the Indian government, or the census rule to UNESCO, is the standard swap.

### 6. The revival and promotion machinery

The sources record a set of initiatives with a clear technological emphasis. They are grouped here by what each actually does, because an answer that groups them is stronger than one that lists them.

| Function | Initiative | Recorded detail |
|---|---|---|
| **Translation between languages, including tribal ones** | **Adi Vaani** | **launched in 2025**; an **AI tool** facilitating real-time text and speech translation between Hindi, English and several **tribal languages**; developed by a consortium **led by IIT Delhi**; part of a broader effort to digitise and preserve tribal linguistic heritage |
| **Translation across Indian languages at scale** | **BHASHINI** | an **AI-powered translation mission launched in 2022** to bridge language barriers and ensure digital inclusion; services include **Anuvaad** (text), **Vaanianuvaad** (speech-to-speech) and **Chitraanuvaad** (video) |
| **Access to reference material** | **Bharatavani Portal** | a multilingual knowledge portal and mobile app managed by the **Central Institute of Indian Languages (CIIL)**, giving digital access to dictionaries and other resources in **over 100 Indian languages** |
| **Education delivery** | **DIKSHA and SWAYAM** | digital education platforms offering courses and materials in multiple Indian languages; **DIKSHA has content in 33 languages and Indian Sign Language**; AICTE and UGC have used tools such as **Anuvaadini** to translate technical course books |
| **Schooling policy** | **NEP 2020** | teaching in the home language or mother tongue **up to at least Class 5, and preferably up to Class 8**; promotes integration of Indian language learning through school and higher education |
| **Exposure and awareness** | **Bhasha Sangam** | exposes school students to **all 22 Eighth Schedule languages**; schools introduce simple dialogues from a new language each day **for 22 working days** |
| **Higher-education knowledge access** | **National Translation Mission (NTM)** | a Government of India scheme to facilitate higher education by making knowledge texts accessible in Indian languages; aims to disseminate knowledge **in all 22 Eighth Schedule languages**; presently engaged in translating all pedagogic material related to higher education into 22 Indian languages |

The National Translation Mission also belongs to Lesson 17, where translation appears as a historical practice rather than a policy instrument. Holding both ends of that thread - Akbar's translation bureau and the NTM - is one of the most effective closing moves available in a Mains answer on multilingual exchange.

### 7. One concept that keeps recurring: lingua franca

A **lingua franca**, also called a bridge language, common language, trade language or vehicular language, is a language or dialect **systematically used to make communication possible between persons not sharing a native language**, particularly when it is a **third language distinct from both native languages**. Lingua francas have developed throughout human history for commercial reasons but also for **cultural, religious, diplomatic and administrative convenience**, and as a means of exchanging information between scholars of different nationalities. **English is the best present example; Latin was the lingua franca of the Roman empire.**

The term has already appeared twice in this session and will appear again: **Pali served as the lingua franca of Theravada Buddhism** (Lesson 4), and Persian functioned as the language of the court across the Sultanate and Mughal periods (Lesson 17). Carrying the definition - especially the "third language distinct from both" clause - lets you use it precisely rather than as a loose synonym for "widely spoken".

### 8. Comparative treatment: two ways to save a language

| | **Documentation-led** | **Transmission-led** |
|---|---|---|
| Aim | record the language before it is lost | restore intergenerational use |
| Instruments here | WAL and the Atlas of Languages in Danger; Bharatavani's dictionaries; Adi Vaani's tribal-language models | NEP 2020's mother-tongue instruction to Class 5 or 8; Bhasha Sangam's daily exposure |
| Strength | works even when the speech community is tiny | addresses the actual mechanism of death in section 1 |
| Limit | a documented language with no speakers is a record, not a living language | requires a community large and stable enough to transmit |
| Answer use | cite as preservation of a **knowledge resource** | cite as intervention at the **intergenerational break** |

A Mains answer that names both approaches, and points out that documentation cannot by itself reverse a transmission failure, is making an argument. One that lists six schemes is not.

### Examination use

Objective items here attack the numbers (the 197 total and its five-way split), the named extinct languages with their dates and places, and the two 10,000-speaker rules with their authorities. They also attack the initiative names - matching Anuvaad, Vaanianuvaad and Chitraanuvaad to BHASHINI, or Bharatavani to CIIL. For Mains, this lesson is the natural evidence base for any question on protecting linguistic heritage: the mechanism in section 1 supplies causation, the WAL numbers supply scale, the extinct-language table supplies named evidence, the initiative table supplies the policy response, and the documentation-versus-transmission comparison supplies the qualification and the verdict.

### Traps introduced here

- Attributing the "fewer than 10,000 speakers" enumeration rule to UNESCO, or the potential-endangerment threshold to the Indian government.
- Reading the four endangerment levels as a smooth progression; India's severely-endangered figure (7) is far below its critically-endangered figure (42).
- Treating the 197 figure as a count of India's languages; it is the count of those endangered, vulnerable or extinct on the list.
- Forgetting that Ahom appears twice in the sources - as the Siamese-Chinese representative already extinct in the subcontinent, and among the five extinct languages as a Tai language of Assam.
- Assuming the last speaker of an Indian language must have died in India; Pazeh's last speaker died in Taiwan in 2010.
- Confusing Bhasha Sangam (exposure to all 22 scheduled languages over 22 working days) with the National Translation Mission (translating higher-education knowledge texts into the 22 languages).
- Using "lingua franca" loosely; the definition specifies a language used between people not sharing a native language, typically a third language distinct from both.

### Revision notes

1. Mechanism of death: migration for social and economic needs -> complete abandonment of the native language -> failure of transmission in the intergenerational period -> language death and extinction.
2. UNESCO has released an Atlas of the World Languages in Danger since 1996; the World Atlas of Languages (WAL) is the interactive tool documenting language status as communicative tools and knowledge resources.
3. Four levels between Safe and Extinct: Vulnerable, Definitely Endangered, Severely Endangered, Critically Endangered.
4. India: 197 languages endangered, vulnerable or extinct - 81 vulnerable, 62 definitely endangered, 7 severely endangered, 42 critically endangered, 5 extinct. Highest number in danger of any country.
5. Most dying languages are of indigenous tribal groups; nomadic peoples of Maharashtra, Karnataka and Telangana are named.
6. Five extinct: Aka-Bo (Andamanese; last speaker died 2010); Aka-Cari (Great Andamanese; last speaker died 2020); Ahom (Tai, Assam); Pazeh (Austronesian; last speaker died in Taiwan in 2010); Konda-Dora (Dravidian, Andhra Pradesh).
7. After the 1971 Census: languages with fewer than 10,000 speakers no longer considered in the List of Official Languages of India.
8. UNESCO: fewer than 10,000 speakers means potentially endangered. Same number, different instrument.
9. Adi Vaani (2025, AI, tribal-language translation, consortium led by IIT Delhi); BHASHINI (2022, AI translation mission; Anuvaad, Vaanianuvaad, Chitraanuvaad).
10. Bharatavani: CIIL-managed portal, over 100 Indian languages. DIKSHA: content in 33 languages plus Indian Sign Language; Anuvaadini used by AICTE and UGC.
11. NEP 2020: home language or mother tongue to at least Class 5, preferably Class 8. Bhasha Sangam: all 22 scheduled languages over 22 working days.
12. National Translation Mission: higher-education knowledge texts into all 22 Eighth Schedule languages.
13. Lingua franca: bridge/common/trade/vehicular language between people not sharing a native language, typically a third language distinct from both. English now; Latin in the Roman empire; Pali for Theravada Buddhism.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is documentation alone an incomplete response to language endangerment, on the mechanism described in this lesson?

**Model answer:** Because the mechanism of death is a failure of **transmission**, not a failure of record. Speakers migrate for social and economic reasons, abandon the native language entirely, and the break occurs in the intergenerational period when the language is not passed to children. Documentation - atlases, dictionaries, translation models - preserves the language as a knowledge resource and makes it recoverable, but it does not restore the household and community use in which transmission happens. That is why the policy set includes transmission-directed instruments as well: mother-tongue instruction to at least Class 5 and preferably Class 8 under NEP 2020, and structured exposure through Bhasha Sangam. A complete answer names both arms and states that they address different points in the same causal chain.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a language is "saved" once it is recorded. A fully documented language with no remaining speakers is classified as extinct; the record preserves the knowledge, not the language's life, and conflating the two overstates what documentation achieves.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Account for India having the largest number of languages in danger of any country, and identify the groups most affected. (10 marks, 150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

**The position.** On the World Atlas of Languages India has **197** languages that are endangered, vulnerable or extinct - **81** vulnerable, **62** definitely endangered, **7** severely endangered, **42** critically endangered and **5** extinct - the highest figure for any country.

**Why.** Partly **base effect**: a country with four families and a long tail of small speech communities has more candidates for endangerment. Mainly **mechanism**: speakers migrate for **social and economic needs**, abandon the native language entirely, and transmission fails in the **intergenerational period**.

**Who is affected.** **Indigenous tribal groups** across the country, and specifically the **nomadic peoples of Maharashtra, Karnataka and Telangana**. By family, the **Austro-Asiatic** languages are worst placed: **every one in Indian territory except Khasi and Santhali is endangered**. The extinct five - Aka-Bo, Aka-Cari, Ahom, Pazeh and Konda-Dora - are Andamanese, Tai, Austronesian and Dravidian, so no family is exempt.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 10: **3** for the 197 figure disaggregated across the five levels; **3** for separating base effect from the transmission mechanism, the mechanism being compulsory; **4** for the affected groups at 2 for the tribal-and-nomadic identification and 2 for the Austro-Asiatic exception clause. Quoting 197 as a single undifferentiated number caps at 5.

## Lesson 9 - Scripts, part one: what a script is, the undeciphered one, and the two ancient ones

### Visual: three ancient writing systems, compared on the things that are actually examined

 | witnesses    | inscriptions        | OF ASHOKA in north- | legends;      |

 |              |                     | dated 250-232 BCE   | manuscripts   |
 +--------------+---------------------+---------------------+---------------+
