```text
 | by           |                     |                     | (again)       |
 +--------------+---------------------+---------------------+---------------+
```

One name appears twice in the bottom row, and that is the single most examined fact in this lesson.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Two problems, and they pull in opposite directions. The first is that "script" and "language" are used interchangeably in ordinary speech, so a question about a script feels like a question about a language and gets the wrong answer. The second is that the Indus signs are routinely described as "the Indus script", which makes people assume it has been read. It has not, and the honest statement of what is unknown is itself examinable.

### 2. What a script is, and the three media named in the definition

**A script is also known as a writing system or orthography. It is a standard for representing the parts of a spoken language by making specific marks on a medium - paper, rocks, birch-bark, and so on.**

Three features of that definition do work later in this session. It is a **standard**, so it can be adopted, replaced or reformed by decision - which is exactly what happens with Vatteluttu and Pallava-Grantha in Lesson 10, and with Modi and Balbodh Devanagari for Marathi. It represents **the parts of a spoken language**, so it is downstream of the language and not identical to it. And it requires a **medium**, and the definition names rock, birch bark and paper in a single breath - the exact three-way material distinction that Lesson 21 develops into the manuscript story.

One further structural fact belongs here: **most of the ancient and modern scripts of India developed from the Brahmi script** - Devanagari, Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, Odia, Assamese, Bengali and others - so that **Brahmi can be called the mother of scripts**. Two named exceptions prove that the descent is not universal: **Urdu is written in a script derived from Arabic**, and a few minor languages such as **Santhali use independent scripts**.

Notice the care required here. "Brahmi is the mother of scripts" is a defensible claim about *scripts*. It says nothing whatever about languages, and pairing it with "Sanskrit is the mother of languages" - the unsafe sentence of Lesson 2 - produces a double error in which two separate descent claims are fused into one imaginary family tree.

### 3. The Indus script: what is actually known

**The Indus Script is a corpus of symbols produced by the Indus Valley Civilisation. Most inscriptions are extremely short and it is unclear if these symbols constitute a script used to record a language.**

That sentence is the whole of the responsible position, and both halves matter. The **shortness** of the inscriptions is a technical obstacle to decipherment - there is very little sequential text to analyse. And the **open question** of whether the symbols encode language at all is not a rhetorical hedge; it is a live scholarly possibility that they may be a non-linguistic sign system.

One positive structural observation is recorded: **the Indus Script is an example of the boustrophedon style, because it is written from left to right in one line and then continued from right to left in the other.** The word is worth learning because it describes a direction pattern that can be observed **without** reading the text - which is precisely why it is one of the few things confidently sayable about an undeciphered system.

The examination behaviour that follows is: when an item makes a claim about what the Indus script *says*, or about the language it records, the claim is unsupportable. When it makes a claim about direction, sign count or the objects on which the symbols appear, it is about observable features and may be correct.

### 4. Brahmi, in full

**Brahmi is the oldest writing system used in the Indian subcontinent and in Central Asia during the final centuries BCE and the early centuries AD.** Its origins are recorded as contested: **some believe that Brahmi was derived from a contemporary Semitic script, or perhaps from the Indus Script.** Carry that as a live disagreement, not as a settled derivation - it is the same discipline as the classification cautions in Lesson 2. **All surviving Indic scripts in Southeast Asia are descendants of Brahmi**, which is the fact that connects this lesson to India's cultural reach beyond its borders.

**The best-known Brahmi inscriptions are the rock-cut edicts of Ashoka in north-central India, dated 250-232 BCE.**

**Brahmi was mainly used to write Prakrit, Sanskrit, and even Tamil-Brahmi languages, making it a versatile script across cultures and languages.** That single sentence is the strongest available proof that one script serves many languages, and it should be the first thing quoted whenever the script-language distinction has to be defended.

**Brahmi is usually written from left to right and is an abugida**, meaning that each unit is based on a consonant and the vowel notation is secondary, except when vowels commence a word.

Its **stages are labelled by dynasty**: **Ashokan Brahmi, Kushana Brahmi, and Gupta Brahmi or Northern Brahmi**. **In the late sixth century, Gupta Brahmi evolved into a script known as Siddhamatrika or Kutila**, and **the modern North Indian scripts such as Bengali, Assamese, Odia and Maithili gradually emerged out of the Siddhamatrika script**. **This is also the time when the Sarada script emerged in Kashmir.** Finally, a simple visual contrast that is easy to remember and easy to examine: **the Northern Brahmi script is a bit angular and the Southern Brahmi script is more circular.**

### 5. The decipherment, and the second discovery that completed it

**The script was deciphered in 1837 by James Prinsep.** But decipherment produced a puzzle rather than a conclusion: **even though Prinsep managed to read these inscriptions, he had no idea about the identity of the king Piyadasi mentioned therein.**

**The answer came soon enough, when George Turnour identified the king as Ashoka on the basis of references in the Pali chronicle, the Dipavamsa.**

Pause on the shape of that episode, because it is the best single illustration in this session of how the different parts of this topic depend on each other.

```text
  A SCRIPT is deciphered (Brahmi, Prinsep, 1837)
        |
        v
  The TEXT can now be READ - and it names a king, "Piyadasi"
        |
        v
  But reading is not IDENTIFYING. Who is Piyadasi?
        |
        v
  A LITERARY TEXT in a different language supplies the answer:
  the Pali chronicle DIPAVAMSA
        |
        v
  George Turnour identifies Piyadasi as ASHOKA
        |
        v
  EPIGRAPHY + PALAEOGRAPHY + A PALI CHRONICLE together produce a
  historical identification that none of them could produce alone
```

That chain is worth reproducing in a Mains answer on sources, because it shows script, inscription and literature functioning as one evidential system rather than three separate topics.

### 6. Kharosthi, in full

**The Kharosthi script, of the third century BCE to the third century CE, is an ancient script used in ancient Gandhara - present-day Afghanistan and Pakistan - to write the Gandhari Prakrit and Sanskrit.** It is described as a **sister script of Brahmi** and was **also deciphered by James Prinsep**.

Prinsep **named it Bactrian Pahlavi because it was found on various Bactrian coins.** The naming is itself instructive. **Bactrian is an extinct Eastern Iranian language formerly spoken in the Central Asian region of Bactria, in present-day Afghanistan, and it was the official language of the Kushan empire.** So the original name attached the script to the *language of the coins on which it was first noticed* rather than to the language it actually wrote - a historical instance of exactly the script-language confusion this session is built to prevent.

**Kharosthi is also an abugida, like Brahmi.** **Kharosthi includes a set of numerals that are similar to Roman numerals, such as I and X.** And the direction fact, stated with its own qualification: **the Kharosthi script is mostly written right to left, but some inscriptions also show the left-to-right direction.** Carry the qualification. "Kharosthi is written right to left" is the standard answer; "mostly, with some inscriptions in the other direction" is the accurate one, and an item can be built on either.

Kharosthi also survives as **manuscript** material, not only as inscriptions - which matters for Lesson 11 and Lesson 21, because it means a right-to-left north-western Prakrit tradition existed on perishable media as well as on stone.

### 7. Comparative treatment: why two ancient scripts rather than one

| | **Brahmi** | **Kharosthi** |
|---|---|---|
| Geographical role | the subcontinent broadly and Central Asia | the north-west, Gandhara |
| Descent | ancestral to most Indian scripts and to all surviving Indic scripts in Southeast Asia | leaves no comparable line of descendants in the sources used here |
| Direction | usually left to right | mostly right to left |
| Languages carried | Prakrit, Sanskrit, Tamil-Brahmi | Gandhari Prakrit and Sanskrit |
| Time span | final centuries BCE to early centuries AD | 3rd century BCE to 3rd century CE |
| Shared features | abugida; deciphered by Prinsep | abugida; deciphered by Prinsep |

The comparison carries an argument, not just a contrast: the same empire could issue its message in **two scripts running in opposite directions** because each suited a different regional readership - which is why Ashokan material appears in Brahmi across most of the subcontinent, in Kharosthi in the north-west, and in Greek and Aramaic at Kandahar. Multi-script state practice is the norm in this period, not an exception.

### Examination use

The decipherment fact - **Brahmi and Kharosthi both deciphered by James Prinsep** - is one of the most frequently set single facts in this entire syllabus area, and the distractors are typically other nineteenth-century figures associated with Indian archaeology. The **Turnour-Dipavamsa** identification is the second-order version of the same question. Direction facts are set as statement items, and both need their qualifications: Brahmi *usually* left to right, Kharosthi *mostly* right to left. Claims about Harappan writing are set as "which of the following is **not** correct" items, and the defence is section 3. For Mains, section 5's chain is the highest-value block, because it converts a decipherment anecdote into an argument about how sources corroborate each other.

### Traps introduced here

- Saying the Indus script has been deciphered, or naming the language it records.
- Attributing the decipherment of Brahmi or Kharosthi to anyone other than James Prinsep.
- Crediting Prinsep with identifying Piyadasi as Ashoka; that identification was made by George Turnour, on the basis of the Pali chronicle Dipavamsa.
- Treating Kharosthi as a southern script; it belongs to Gandhara in the north-west.
- Stating the direction facts without the sources' own qualifications.
- Fusing "Brahmi is the mother of scripts" with "Sanskrit is the mother of languages" into a single descent claim.
- Assuming Brahmi wrote only Prakrit; it wrote Prakrit, Sanskrit and Tamil-Brahmi.
- Presenting Brahmi's origin as settled; the sources record both a Semitic derivation and a possible Indus derivation as beliefs.

### Revision notes

1. Script = a standard for representing parts of a spoken language by marks on a medium such as paper, rock or birch bark.
2. Most ancient and modern Indian scripts derive from Brahmi - "the mother of scripts"; exceptions are the Arabic-derived Urdu script and independent scripts such as Santhali's.
3. Indus script: corpus of symbols; most inscriptions extremely short; unclear whether the symbols constitute a script recording a language; boustrophedon direction.
4. Brahmi: oldest writing system of the subcontinent and Central Asia in the final centuries BCE and early centuries AD; origin debated between a Semitic script and the Indus script.
5. All surviving Indic scripts in Southeast Asia are descendants of Brahmi.
6. Best-known Brahmi inscriptions: the rock-cut edicts of Ashoka in north-central India, 250-232 BCE.
7. Brahmi wrote Prakrit, Sanskrit and Tamil-Brahmi; usually left to right; an abugida.
8. Stages: Ashokan, Kushana, Gupta / Northern Brahmi. Late sixth century: Gupta Brahmi evolves into Siddhamatrika or Kutila; Bengali, Assamese, Odia and Maithili emerge from it; Sarada emerges in Kashmir at the same time.
9. Northern Brahmi is angular; Southern Brahmi is more circular.
10. Deciphered in 1837 by James Prinsep; George Turnour identified Piyadasi as Ashoka using the Pali chronicle Dipavamsa.
11. Kharosthi: 3rd century BCE to 3rd century CE; ancient Gandhara; Gandhari Prakrit and Sanskrit; sister script of Brahmi; also deciphered by Prinsep; named by him Bactrian Pahlavi after Bactrian coins.
12. Bactrian: extinct Eastern Iranian language of Bactria; the official language of the Kushan empire.
13. Kharosthi is an abugida, has numerals similar to Roman numerals such as I and X, and is mostly right to left though some inscriptions run left to right.

### Concept check

**Question:** Prinsep named Kharosthi "Bactrian Pahlavi". What error does that name contain, and which distinction in this session does it illustrate?

**Model answer:** The name attaches the script to the language associated with the coins on which it was first noticed - Bactrian, an extinct Eastern Iranian language and the official language of the Kushan empire - rather than to the languages the script actually writes, which are **Gandhari Prakrit and Sanskrit**. It illustrates the script-versus-language distinction: a writing system is a set of marks that can serve several languages, so identifying a script by the first language a scholar associates with its find-context is unreliable. The same distinction is positively demonstrated by Brahmi, which wrote Prakrit, Sanskrit and Tamil-Brahmi.

**Misconception to avoid:** That an early scholarly name for a script is evidence about the script's linguistic affiliation. Names in palaeography are often provisional and reflect the find-context; the later, corrected designation reflects the languages actually recorded.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Compare Brahmi and Kharosthi on origin, direction, type, the languages they carried and their descent, and state what the comparison shows about early Indian writing practice. (15 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**Origin.** Both are ancient and **both were deciphered by James Prinsep in 1837**. Brahmi's own origin is recorded as **debated** - derived either from a contemporary Semitic script or perhaps from the Indus script. Kharosthi is called a **sister script** of Brahmi and was first named by Prinsep **"Bactrian Pahlavi"**, after the coins it was found on rather than the languages it wrote.

**Direction.** Brahmi runs **usually left to right**; Kharosthi **mostly right to left**, though some inscriptions run the other way. Both qualifications belong in the answer.

**Type.** Both are **abugidas** - each unit consonant-based, vowel notation secondary except word-initially. Kharosthi additionally has **numerals resembling Roman numerals**.

**Languages carried.** Brahmi wrote **Prakrit, Sanskrit and Tamil-Brahmi**; Kharosthi wrote **Gandhari Prakrit and Sanskrit**.

**Descent and range.** Brahmi is ancestral to most Indian scripts and to **all surviving Indic scripts in Southeast Asia**, passing through Ashokan, Kushana and Gupta stages to Siddhamatrika and beyond. Kharosthi leaves no comparable line in this record, and belongs to **Gandhara**, roughly present-day Afghanistan and Pakistan, in the third century BCE to third century CE.

**What it shows.** Writing practice was **regionally plural and deliberately multi-script**: the same imperial message could appear in Brahmi across most of the subcontinent, Kharosthi in the north-west, and **Greek and Aramaic at Kandahar**. Script choice tracked readership, not language.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 15: **5** for the five comparison heads at 1 each; **3** for carrying both direction qualifications and the abugida definition; **2** for the Bactrian Pahlavi misnaming as evidence of the script-language confusion; **5** for the concluding multi-script argument, of which 2 require the Kandahar Greek-and-Aramaic evidence. A two-column table with no concluding argument caps at 8.


### 1. The learner's actual problem

There are roughly fifteen named scripts here and they blur. The cure is to stop memorising them as a list and start filing each one under the **single distinguishing feature** that an item would use to identify it - a date, a named creator, a language, a structural oddity, or a replacement event. The table in section 3 does exactly that.

### 2. The descent, stated as a sequence

The **Gupta script** belongs to the Gupta Empire and was **used to write Sanskrit**. It **descended from Brahmi and gave rise to the Nagari, Sarada and Siddham scripts**. These in turn gave rise to many of the most important scripts of India, **including the Devanagari script, the Gurmukhi script for Punjabi, the Assamese script, the Bengali script and the Tibetan script**.

The **Siddhamatrika script** - often called **Siddham** - is a historic Indic writing system that **thrived between the sixth and thirteenth centuries AD**. **Originating from the Gupta script**, it laid the groundwork for **Nagari, Eastern Nagari, Tirhuta, Odia and Nepalese**. Its **title translates as "perfected mother" or "perfected form"**, signifying its application in inscribing **Sanskrit mantras and sacred writings**, and it is **closely associated with Buddhist texts, particularly those related to Vajrayana Buddhism**.

Notice that the Tirhuta entry closes a loop opened in Lesson 5: Maithili was **historically written in the Tirhuta script** and is **now written mainly in Devanagari**. Both Tirhuta and Devanagari descend from the same Gupta-Siddhamatrika complex, so the language changed script *within* a single descent family - which is a more interesting fact than a simple replacement.

### 3. Every named script, filed by its distinguishing feature

| Script | The one feature an item would use | Full record |
|---|---|---|
| **Gupta** | the Sanskrit script of an empire that seeds three families | Gupta Empire; used to write Sanskrit; descended from Brahmi; gave rise to Nagari, Sarada and Siddham, and through them to Devanagari, Gurmukhi, Assamese, Bengali and Tibetan |
| **Siddhamatrika / Siddham** | "perfected mother"; Vajrayana association | 6th-13th centuries AD; from the Gupta script; gave rise to Nagari, Eastern Nagari, Tirhuta, Odia and Nepalese; used for Sanskrit mantras and sacred writings |
| **Kadamba** | the birth of a dedicated script for **Kannada** | descendant of Brahmi, developed under the Kadamba dynasty in the 4th-6th century AD; later became the Kannada-Telugu script |
| **Vatteluttu** | the script that was **replaced** | abugida originating in South India; developed from Southern Brahmi; one of three main alphabet systems for Tamil and Malayalam; used for centuries in inscriptions and manuscripts of South India; **replaced by the Pallava-Grantha script in the Pallava court** |
| **Grantha** | named for the *granthas* it wrote | widely used between the 6th and 20th centuries AD by Tamil speakers in Tamil Nadu and Kerala to write **Sanskrit** and the classical language **Manipravalam**; still in restricted use in traditional Vedic schools; named because it was used for writing Sanskrit *granthas*; the **Malayalam script is a direct descendant**, as are **Tigalari** and **Sinhala** |
| **Sarada / Sharada** | named after a goddess; a community's ceremonial survival | named after Goddess Sarada or Saraswati, Goddess of Learning; abugida of the Brahmic family developed around the 8th century AD; used for **Sanskrit and Kashmiri**; once more widespread, later restricted to Kashmir; now rarely used except by the **Kashmiri Pandit community for ceremonial purposes** |
| **Gurmukhi** | standardised by a named person in a known century | developed **from the Sarada script**; standardised during the 16th century AD by the **second Sikh Guru, Guru Angad**; the whole of the **Guru Granth Sahib** is written in it; most commonly used by Sikhs and Hindus for Punjabi; has unique orthographic features because it developed **outside the ambit of the Sanskrit language and its cultural tradition**, unlike other scripts |
| **Devanagari** | a character count | abugida of India and Nepal; written left to right; developed during the **7th century AD** from the eastern variants of the Gupta script known as **Nagari**; composed of **47 primary characters - 14 vowels and 33 consonants** |
| **Modi** | invented by **scribes**, for **speed** | used to write **Marathi**; developed in the **14th century AD** as a **shorthand variant of Devanagari** by scribes unable to write original Devanagari during rapid writing; official for Marathi until the 20th century, when the **Balbodh** style of Devanagari was promoted as the standard; **also used to write Urdu, Kannada, Gujarati, Hindi and Tamil**; an abugida |
| **Urdu script** | Perso-Arabic, right to left, Nastaliq | a modification of the Persian alphabet, itself derived from the Arabic alphabet; origins in the **13th century**; closely related to the development of the **Nastaliq** style; up to **40 distinct letters** with **no distinct letter cases**; extended form known as **Shahmukhi**, used for **Punjabi and Saraiki** |
| **Nastaliq** | a calligraphic hand, not a script in itself | one of the main calligraphic hands for the Perso-Arabic script in Persian and Urdu, and the predominant style in Persian calligraphy; developed in Persia in the **14th and 15th centuries**; widely used in Iran, Afghanistan, Pakistan and elsewhere for poetry and as an art form; among the most fluid calligraphy styles for the Arabic script - short verticals with no serifs and long horizontal strokes |
| **Ol Chiki** | **not** an abugida | created in **1925** by **Pandit Raghunath Murmu** for **Santali**; *Ol* means write and *Chiki* means script; a **true alphabet**, giving vowels equal representation with consonants; the Constitution of India has been translated into it |
| **Kaithi** | a script of **records**, not of literature | a historical Brahmic script widely used in parts of northern and eastern India, primarily in present-day Uttar Pradesh, Jharkhand and Bihar; used for **legal, administrative and private records**; widely used during the **Mughal period** |
| **Nandinagari** | **no connecting headline** | Brahmic family, evolved from the Nagari script; in use in **South India from the 7th to the 19th centuries**; mainly for **Sanskrit manuscripts and inscriptions on philosophy, science and the arts**; unique for the **absence of a connecting headline** and variations in letter forms, especially in conjunct consonants; an abugida; concentrated in southern Maharashtra, Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh and Tamil Nadu; used extensively under the **Vijayanagara Empire**, particularly for **Sanskrit copper-plate inscriptions** |
| **Bharati** | a modern unification proposal | developed by researchers at **IIT Madras**; a unified script for **nine languages** - Devanagari, Bengali, Gurmukhi, Gujarati, Odia, Telugu, Kannada, Malayalam and Tamil - on the analogy of the Roman script as a common script for many European languages; objective is to reduce barriers of communication among the states |

### 4. Four documented proofs that script is not language

This is the analytical core of the lesson. Each proof is a specific, citable case, and together they close the question permanently.

**Proof one - one script, many languages.** **Brahmi was used to write Prakrit, Sanskrit and Tamil-Brahmi.** **Modi**, developed for Marathi, was also used to write **Urdu, Kannada, Gujarati, Hindi and Tamil**. A script is therefore a technology, not an identity.

**Proof two - one language, changing scripts.** **Maithili** was historically written in **Tirhuta** and is now written mainly in **Devanagari**. **Marathi** was written officially in **Modi** until the twentieth century and then in the **Balbodh** style of Devanagari. In both cases the language continued unchanged while the writing system was replaced.

**Proof three - a court decision replacing a script.** **Vatteluttu was replaced by the Pallava-Grantha script in the Pallava court.** This is a *decision*, taken in an institution, affecting court usage. It is the clearest evidence that script choice is an administrative and cultural act rather than a natural growth - and it is also a directly usable piece of evidence for any answer on Pallava cultural patronage.

**Proof four - one document, several scripts and languages at once.** Two inscriptions make the point unanswerable:

- **The ninth-century Kollam Copper Plate is mostly in Tamil and inscribed in the Vatteluttu script, with a few words in Grantha script. The sixth plate contains signatures of witnesses in three languages and scripts: Arabic written in Kufic script; Pahlavi; and Judaeo-Persian written in Hebrew script.**
- **A twelfth-century inscription from Kurgod in Karnataka is in three languages - Sanskrit, Prakrit and Kannada.**

The Kollam plate is the single richest object in this session. One document carries **two scripts for the main text** and **three further language-script combinations in the witness signatures**, including a Semitic script used for an Iranian language. If an answer needs one piece of evidence for India's multilingual documentary culture, this is it.

### 5. Comparative treatment: three ways a script changes

| Mechanism | Case | What drove it |
|---|---|---|
| **Replacement by decision** | Vatteluttu replaced by Pallava-Grantha in the Pallava court; Modi replaced by Balbodh Devanagari for Marathi in the 20th century | institutional or standardising choice |
| **Evolution by descent** | Gupta Brahmi -> Siddhamatrika -> Bengali, Assamese, Odia, Maithili | gradual palaeographic change |
| **Deliberate creation** | Ol Chiki created in 1925 by Raghunath Murmu for Santali; the Bharati script proposed by IIT Madras researchers | a designed response to a specific need - a community without a fitting script, or a wish for a common script |

The third row carries the most interesting argument. **Ol Chiki was designed, and designed differently**: unlike most Indic scripts it is a **true alphabet** rather than an abugida, giving vowels equal representation. A created script can therefore break with the structural convention of the family around it - which is the strongest possible refutation of the idea that a region's scripts must share a type.

### 6. A qualification about "mother of scripts"

Brahmi's descent is extensive but not total, and the exceptions are named: the **Urdu script** derives from the Arabic alphabet through Persian, and **Santhali** and a few other minor languages use **independent scripts** - to which Ol Chiki, created in 1925, must now be added as a designed independent system. State the generalisation and its exceptions together; a claim with its exceptions attached is stronger than a claim without them.

### Examination use

This lesson's facts are set as pairing and identification items: a script against its language, its century, its creator, or its distinguishing structural feature. The highest-frequency single facts are **Gurmukhi standardised by Guru Angad in the 16th century**, **Ol Chiki created in 1925 by Raghunath Murmu for Santali**, **Devanagari's 47 characters**, **Modi as a shorthand for Marathi created by scribes**, and **Vatteluttu replaced by Pallava-Grantha**. For Mains, section 4 is the transferable block: an answer on India's documentary and literary culture that cites the Kollam Copper Plate and the Kurgod trilingual inscription has replaced an assertion about pluralism with two dated objects.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating a script as evidence of a language, an ethnicity or a religion.
- Forgetting that Modi was used for several languages besides Marathi.
- Assuming all Indic scripts are abugidas; Ol Chiki is a true alphabet by design.
- Confusing Nastaliq - a calligraphic hand - with the Urdu script itself.
- Confusing Shahmukhi (the extended Urdu script, used for Punjabi and Saraiki) with Gurmukhi (from Sarada, standardised by Guru Angad).
- Assigning Grantha to Tamil alone; it was used to write Sanskrit and Manipravalam.
- Forgetting Nandinagari's two identifying features: the absence of a connecting headline, and its extensive use under Vijayanagara for Sanskrit copper-plate inscriptions.
- Treating Kaithi as a literary script; it was used for legal, administrative and private records.
- Stating that Brahmi is the source of all Indian scripts without the named exceptions.

### Revision notes

1. Gupta script: Gupta Empire, used for Sanskrit, descended from Brahmi, gave rise to Nagari, Sarada and Siddham, and through them to Devanagari, Gurmukhi, Assamese, Bengali and Tibetan.
2. Siddhamatrika / Siddham: 6th-13th centuries AD; "perfected mother"; from the Gupta script; source of Nagari, Eastern Nagari, Tirhuta, Odia and Nepalese; Sanskrit mantras; Vajrayana association.
3. Kadamba: 4th-6th century AD, Kadamba dynasty, dedicated script for Kannada; became the Kannada-Telugu script.
4. Vatteluttu: from Southern Brahmi; one of three main alphabet systems for Tamil and Malayalam; replaced by Pallava-Grantha in the Pallava court.
5. Grantha: 6th-20th centuries AD; Tamil Nadu and Kerala; wrote Sanskrit and Manipravalam; still restricted use in traditional Vedic schools; Malayalam, Tigalari and Sinhala descend from it.
6. Sarada: c. 8th century AD; named after Goddess Sarada/Saraswati; Sanskrit and Kashmiri; now largely ceremonial among Kashmiri Pandits.
7. Gurmukhi: from Sarada; standardised in the 16th century by Guru Angad, the second Sikh Guru; the Guru Granth Sahib is written in it; developed outside the ambit of Sanskrit tradition.
8. Devanagari: 7th century AD, from the eastern Gupta variants called Nagari; left to right; 47 primary characters - 14 vowels and 33 consonants.
9. Modi: 14th century AD; shorthand variant of Devanagari created by scribes for rapid writing; official for Marathi until the 20th century, replaced by Balbodh Devanagari; also used for Urdu, Kannada, Gujarati, Hindi and Tamil.
10. Urdu script: right to left; modification of the Persian alphabet from Arabic; 13th-century origins; up to 40 letters, no letter cases; Nastaliq style; extended form Shahmukhi for Punjabi and Saraiki.
11. Nastaliq: calligraphic hand developed in Persia in the 14th-15th centuries; short verticals without serifs, long horizontal strokes.
12. Ol Chiki: 1925, Pandit Raghunath Murmu, for Santali; Ol = write, Chiki = script; a true alphabet, not an abugida; the Constitution has been translated into it.
13. Kaithi: Brahmic; UP, Jharkhand, Bihar; legal, administrative and private records; widely used in the Mughal period.
14. Nandinagari: 7th-19th centuries; South India; from Nagari; Sanskrit manuscripts and inscriptions; no connecting headline; Vijayanagara Sanskrit copper-plate inscriptions.
15. Bharati script: IIT Madras; unified script for nine languages on the Roman-script analogy.
16. Kollam Copper Plate, 9th century: mostly Tamil in Vatteluttu with a few Grantha words; sixth plate has witness signatures in Arabic in Kufic script, Pahlavi, and Judaeo-Persian in Hebrew script.
17. Kurgod inscription, Karnataka, 12th century: three languages - Sanskrit, Prakrit and Kannada.

### Concept check

**Question:** Which single document in this lesson most completely disproves the assumption that one language implies one script, and why?

**Model answer:** The ninth-century **Kollam Copper Plate**. Its main text is **mostly in Tamil but inscribed in the Vatteluttu script, with a few words in the Grantha script** - so one language is already carried by two scripts within one document. Its **sixth plate** then adds witness signatures in **three further language-script combinations**: Arabic written in Kufic script, Pahlavi, and Judaeo-Persian written in Hebrew script. The last of those is decisive, because an Iranian language is there written in a Semitic script, which no one-language-one-script assumption can accommodate. The Kurgod inscription of the twelfth century reinforces the point from the language side, carrying Sanskrit, Prakrit and Kannada in a single record.

**Misconception to avoid:** That multi-script documents are curiosities. They record ordinary administrative practice in a trading and multilingual society; the witnesses signed in the scripts they used, which is why a single legal instrument preserves several writing traditions at once.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Describe the descent of the modern North Indian scripts from Brahmi, and identify the principal exceptions to Brahmi's ancestry. (10 marks, 150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

**The line of descent.** **Brahmi** passes through dynastic stages - **Ashokan, Kushana, Gupta** or Northern Brahmi. In the **late sixth century** Gupta Brahmi evolved into **Siddhamatrika**, also called Kutila, which thrived from the sixth to the thirteenth centuries and gave rise to **Nagari, Eastern Nagari, Tirhuta, Odia and Nepalese**; from it the modern **Bengali, Assamese, Odia and Maithili** scripts emerged. **Sarada** appeared in Kashmir at the same period and produced **Gurmukhi**, standardised in the sixteenth century by **Guru Angad**. **Devanagari** developed in the **seventh century** from the eastern Gupta variants called Nagari, and **Modi** from Devanagari in the fourteenth.

**The exceptions.** The **Urdu script** derives from the **Persian alphabet**, itself from Arabic. **Santhali** and a few minor languages use **independent scripts**, to which **Ol Chiki**, created in **1925**, must be added - a designed **true alphabet**, not an abugida.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 10: **6** for the descent chain, at 1.5 each for the Siddhamatrika step, the eastern scripts, the Sarada-Gurmukhi line and the Devanagari-Modi line; **3** for the two named exception families; **1** for noting that Ol Chiki breaks the abugida convention by design. A list of script names without the Siddhamatrika step caps at 4.

## Lesson 11 - Inscription against manuscript: two witnesses, two different things they can prove

Progress: 11/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the evidentiary properties of each medium, the earliest datable inscriptions language by language, the internal contradictions in that record, and the boundary with the history papers
