Book context: Queried - the "other notable facts" epigraphy block in the Dravidian section, the Junagarh and Chebrolu statements, the Pallava bilingual inscription record, the Kannada and Telugu inscription statements in two separate chapters, the Kollam and Kurgod records, and the manuscript references attached to Kharosthi, Sarada, Modi and the Tamil epic
CA search: "Not required - source-criticism lesson resting on dated epigraphic material"
CA found: None found; no genuine recent linkage for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the two witnesses, tested on the same six questions

```text
                       INSCRIPTION            |       MANUSCRIPT
 ----------------------+-----------------------+----------------------------
 Medium                | rock, pillar,         | palm leaf, birch bark, paper
                       | copper plate          |
 ----------------------+-----------------------+----------------------------
 How it survives       | durability of the     | REPEATED COPYING by scribes
                       | material itself       | - every surviving copy is a
                       |                       | copy of a copy
 ----------------------+-----------------------+----------------------------
 Date                  | often stated or       | the COPY has a date; the
                       | datable; fixed at the | TEXT usually does not
                       | moment of cutting     |
 ----------------------+-----------------------+----------------------------
 Place                 | fixed - it is where   | mobile - it travels, and
                       | it was put            | is found far from where it
                       |                       | was written
 ----------------------+-----------------------+----------------------------
 Who speaks            | usually a ruler, a    | an author, a compiler, a
                       | donor or an           | commentator - and a scribe
                       | institution           | who may alter as he copies
 ----------------------+-----------------------+----------------------------
 What it proves well   | that this text was    | the CONTENT of a tradition,
                       | publicly displayed,   | its argument, its literary
                       | in this language and  | form, its internal debates
                       | script, at this place |
                       | and time              |
 ----------------------+-----------------------+----------------------------
 Its failure mode      | SELECTION: only what  | TRANSMISSION: text changes,
                       | power chose to        | grows and is interpolated
                       | inscribe survives     | across centuries of copying
 ----------------------+-----------------------+----------------------------
```

A single conclusion follows from the table and should be written into any answer that uses either witness: **an inscription is strong on date, place and language and weak on content breadth; a manuscript is strong on content and weak on date and place.** They are complements, and the Prinsep-Turnour chain of Lesson 9 is the model of the two working together.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Both are "old writing", so both feel like the same kind of evidence. They are not, and the difference is the whole of source criticism in this topic. A candidate who has internalised the table above can answer, without looking anything up, why "the earliest Sanskrit inscription is from AD 150" does **not** mean "Sanskrit was not written before AD 150", and why "the Rigveda was compiled around 1500-1000 BCE" does **not** mean a manuscript from that date exists.

### 2. The earliest datable inscriptions, language by language

Assembled from across the sources, with the internal disagreements shown rather than smoothed:

| Language | Earliest datable epigraphic anchor recorded | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Sanskrit** | **Junagarh / Girnar rock inscription of Rudradaman I**, present-day Gujarat, around **AD 150** | described as one of the earliest pieces of evidence of the use of Sanskrit |
| **Sanskrit in South India** | **Chebrolu inscription**, Andhra Pradesh, of Satavahana king **Vijaya**, issued **AD 207**, discovered **2019** | "the earliest datable Sanskrit inscription from South India **so far**" |
| **Tamil** | Tamil became an important language of South Indian inscriptions **under the Pallava dynasty**; **bilingual Tamil-Sanskrit Pallava inscriptions from the 7th century onwards**; **Chola and Pandya kings also issued Tamil and bilingual Sanskrit-Tamil inscriptions** | the earliest Tamil epigraphy as such is not dated in the sources used here; this is recorded as a gap rather than filled |
| **Kannada** | **two different statements** in the sources: (i) "the earliest Kannada inscriptions belong to the late 6th / early 7th century AD"; (ii) "the oldest Kannada inscription is the **Halmidi inscription** from the **5th century AD**" | an unresolved internal discrepancy - handled in section 4 |
| **Telugu** | **two different statements**: (i) "the late 6th century epigraphs of early Telugu Chola kings mark the beginning of Telugu as a language of inscriptions"; (ii) "the oldest Telugu inscription is the **Tippalar inscription** and **Bhodampudu inscription** from the **7th century AD**" | a second unresolved internal discrepancy |
| **Multilingual** | **Kurgod inscription**, Karnataka, **12th century**, in **Sanskrit, Prakrit and Kannada**; **Kollam Copper Plate**, **9th century**, mostly Tamil in Vatteluttu with Grantha words and witness signatures in Arabic-Kufic, Pahlavi and Judaeo-Persian-Hebrew | the multilingual evidence is the strongest in the set because it is internally consistent |

### 3. What each anchor licenses, and what it does not

Take the Pallava row, because it is the one most often over-read. The record says: Tamil became an important language of South Indian inscriptions **under the Pallava dynasty**, and **bilingual Tamil-Sanskrit Pallava inscriptions appear from the seventh century onwards**.

- **Licensed:** that by the seventh century the Pallava administration was issuing records in two languages simultaneously, and that this was a sustained practice rather than an isolated act.
- **Licensed:** that Tamil had acquired epigraphic prestige in that period - it was chosen for public, durable, official display.
- **Not licensed:** that Tamil was not written before, or that Sanskrit was in decline, or that the population was bilingual. An inscription tells you what an institution chose to display, not what people spoke.
- **Best use in an answer:** as evidence of an **administratively institutionalised two-language literary culture**, which is a far more precise claim than "the Pallavas patronised Tamil and Sanskrit".

The same discipline applied to the Junagarh anchor produces: licensed - Sanskrit in public prestige use by the mid-second century AD; not licensed - the claim that Sanskrit writing began then.

### 4. The two internal discrepancies, handled openly

Two languages have conflicting earliest-inscription statements in the sources used here, and the correct response is neither to pick one silently nor to omit the fact.

**Kannada.** One statement places the earliest Kannada inscriptions in the **late sixth or early seventh century AD**; another names the **Halmidi inscription of the fifth century AD** as the oldest Kannada inscription. **How to write it:** "The Halmidi inscription, of the fifth century AD, is named as the oldest Kannada inscription; a broader statement in the same body of material places the earliest Kannada inscriptions in the late sixth or early seventh century. The specific named attribution is the more usable, and the discrepancy is on the record."

**Telugu.** One statement places the beginning of Telugu as a language of inscriptions with the **late sixth century epigraphs of early Telugu Chola kings**; another names the **Tippalar and Bhodampudu inscriptions of the seventh century AD** as the oldest Telugu inscriptions. **How to write it:** name both, and note that the earlier date refers to the *beginning of Telugu as an inscriptional language* while the later refers to *specific named oldest inscriptions* - which is a plausible reconciliation but is offered as such, not asserted as a resolution.

Both discrepancies are carried into the ledger at the end of this session. What matters for examination purposes is the behaviour: **when named specific evidence and a general statement conflict, cite the named evidence and record the conflict.** That is worth more than a confident wrong date.

### 5. The manuscript side: what the sources actually give

Manuscripts appear across the sources as attachments to scripts and texts rather than as a separate section, and gathering them is worthwhile because they are the material base of Lesson 21.

- **Kharosthi manuscripts** are recorded, establishing that the north-western right-to-left tradition existed on perishable media as well as on stone.
- **Vatteluttu was used for centuries in inscriptions *and manuscripts* of South India** - one script serving both witnesses.
- **A Kashmiri Sarada manuscript** is recorded, matching the script's regional restriction.
- **A Modi manuscript** is recorded, matching the script's administrative and rapid-writing function.
- **Silappadikaram** is described as the earliest Tamil epic and specifically as a **palm leaf manuscript** - the clearest single link in the sources between a named literary work and its physical medium.
- **Nandinagari** was used mainly for **Sanskrit manuscripts** as well as inscriptions, and specifically for **Sanskrit copper-plate inscriptions** under Vijayanagara - so a single script appears on both a perishable and a durable medium.
- The **Hamzanama** is recorded as an **illustrated manuscript commissioned by Akbar**, which introduces the third dimension of manuscript production - illustration - developed in Lesson 21.

### 6. The boundary with the history papers, stated precisely

Inscriptions are examined in two different syllabus places and the division is clean.

| Question about an inscription | Owned by | Example |
|---|---|---|
| What can it tell us about administration, chronology, dhamma, society, geography, and what are the limits of royal and uneven evidence? | the history papers, as source criticism | a Mains question on the importance of Ashokan inscriptions for reconstructing Mauryan history |
| What language and script is it in, what does that choice signal, how does it fit the history of that language's writing, and what is the earliest such record? | **this topic** | Junagarh and Chebrolu as Sanskrit anchors; Pallava bilingualism; the Kollam plate's multiple scripts |

An answer that stays on its own side of that line is more focused; an answer that crosses it without noticing produces a history answer to a culture question or the reverse. Where a question genuinely spans both - a Pallava art-and-literature question, for example - the correct move is to supply the language, script and text material from here and route the dynastic narrative to the history owner.

### 7. Comparative treatment: what each medium makes visible about literacy

| | Inscription | Manuscript |
|---|---|---|
| Whose literacy is visible | the state's, the temple's, the donor's - institutional | the scholar's, the monastery's, the scribe's - textual-community |
| What is over-represented | the powerful, the pious, the enduring stone | traditions with institutional copying networks |
| What is invisible | everyday writing, accounts, letters, teaching | everything a community chose not to recopy |
| Consequence for the historian | public language policy is recoverable | intellectual traditions are recoverable |
| Consequence for the answer | use inscriptions for dated, located, official claims | use manuscripts for claims about content, argument and literary form |

The final row is the practical rule. The row above it is the analytical one, and it is the beginning of the **survival-bias** argument completed in Lesson 21: the record we have is the record that institutions chose to make durable or to recopy, and neither choice was neutral.

### Examination use

Direct items here attack dates - Junagarh, Chebrolu, Halmidi, Tippalar and Bhodampudu - and language-script pairings in inscriptions. Statement items attack what an inscription proves, and the defence is section 3. For Mains, this lesson supplies the qualification layer that lifts an answer: any claim about a language's antiquity, patronage or spread should be followed by a sentence identifying **which kind of witness** supports it and **what that witness cannot show**. A 15-mark answer that does this twice reads as source-critical rather than descriptive.

### Traps introduced here

- Reading "earliest datable inscription" as "earliest use of the language in writing".
- Treating a manuscript's date as the text's date; the surviving copy is a copy of a copy.
- Silently choosing one side of the Kannada or Telugu earliest-inscription discrepancy without recording the conflict.
- Reading the Pallava bilingual inscriptions as evidence about popular speech rather than about administrative practice.
- Forgetting that Vatteluttu and Nandinagari each appear on both inscriptions and manuscripts.
- Answering a source-criticism question about Ashokan inscriptions as if it were a language question, or the reverse.
- Treating the absence of an early inscription as evidence that a language was unwritten.

### Revision notes

1. Inscription: rock, pillar, copper plate; durable; fixed in place; usually official; strong on date, place, language and script; weak on breadth of content; failure mode is selection.
2. Manuscript: palm leaf, birch bark, paper; survives through repeated copying; mobile; strong on content and literary form; weak on original date and place; failure mode is transmission change.
3. Sanskrit anchors: Junagarh / Girnar, Rudradaman I, c. AD 150; Chebrolu, Satavahana king Vijaya, AD 207, discovered 2019, earliest datable South Indian Sanskrit inscription so far.
4. Tamil: important as an inscriptional language under the Pallavas; bilingual Tamil-Sanskrit Pallava inscriptions from the 7th century onwards; Chola and Pandya kings issued Tamil and bilingual Sanskrit-Tamil inscriptions.
5. Kannada, conflicting statements: earliest inscriptions in the late 6th / early 7th century AD; and the Halmidi inscription of the 5th century AD as the oldest.
6. Telugu, conflicting statements: late 6th century epigraphs of early Telugu Chola kings as the beginning; and the Tippalar and Bhodampudu inscriptions of the 7th century AD as the oldest.
7. Kurgod inscription, Karnataka, 12th century: Sanskrit, Prakrit and Kannada.
8. Kollam Copper Plate, 9th century: mostly Tamil in Vatteluttu with Grantha words; witness signatures in Arabic-Kufic, Pahlavi and Judaeo-Persian in Hebrew script.
9. Manuscript witnesses recorded: Kharosthi manuscripts; Vatteluttu in manuscripts as well as inscriptions; a Kashmiri Sarada manuscript; a Modi manuscript; Silappadikaram as a palm leaf manuscript; Nandinagari Sanskrit manuscripts and Vijayanagara copper plates; the Hamzanama as an illustrated manuscript commissioned by Akbar.
10. Boundary: the history papers own inscriptions as historical source criticism; this topic owns their language, script and literary-historical significance.
11. Rule for conflicts: cite the specific named evidence, record the discrepancy, do not resolve it silently.
12. Survival bias: the record consists of what institutions chose to inscribe or to recopy, and neither choice was neutral.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Because the earliest datable Sanskrit inscription is from about AD 150, Sanskrit literature cannot be older than the second century AD." Identify the error in terms of the two witnesses.

**Model answer:** The sentence uses an **inscriptional** limit to date a **manuscript-transmitted** literature, and the two witnesses have different survival logics. An inscription survives because its medium is durable, so the earliest surviving one marks the earliest **datable public display** in that language, not the earliest use. Literary texts survive by repeated copying on perishable media, so their antiquity is established by internal, linguistic and traditional evidence rather than by the age of any surviving physical copy: Old Indo-Aryan develops around 1500 BC and the Vedic corpus is placed by historians at around 1500-1000 BCE, many centuries before the Junagarh inscription. The correct formulation keeps the two clocks separate and says that the earliest datable epigraphic use is about AD 150 while the literary tradition is far older.

**Misconception to avoid:** That the earliest surviving physical object sets an upper limit on a tradition's age. In a manuscript culture, absence of an early physical copy is expected rather than significant, because the medium does not survive centuries without recopying.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Compare what an inscription and a manuscript can each establish, and apply the comparison to dating the earliest written use of an Indian language. (15 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**The two witnesses.** An **inscription** is cut into rock, pillar or copper plate; it survives by **durability**, is **fixed in place**, is usually **official**, and is strong on **date, place, language and script**. Its failure mode is **selection**: only what power chose to inscribe survives. A **manuscript** is written on **palm leaf, birch bark or paper**; it survives only by **repeated copying**, is **mobile**, and is strong on **content, argument and literary form**. Its failure mode is **transmission**: the copy's date is not the text's date.

**Application.** Sanskrit's earliest datable epigraphy is the **Junagarh or Girnar inscription of Rudradaman I, about AD 150**, with the **Chebrolu inscription of the Satavahana king Vijaya, issued AD 207 and discovered in 2019**, the earliest datable South Indian instance **so far**. Those dates establish **public, prestige use by the second century AD**. They do **not** establish when Sanskrit was first written, because absence of an earlier surviving inscription is not absence of earlier use, and the literary tradition is centuries older.

**The discipline this forces.** Name the **witness** before the date; carry the **qualifier**, since "so far" changed in 2019 and can change again; and where sources conflict - Kannada is given both as late sixth or early seventh century and as the **Halmidi inscription of the fifth century**, Telugu as late sixth century and as the **Tippalar and Bhodampudu inscriptions of the seventh** - cite the named evidence and record the conflict rather than choosing silently.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 15: **6** for the two-witness comparison, requiring **both** failure modes, at 3 each; **4** for the Sanskrit application with the licensed and unlicensed inferences separated; **3** for carrying the "so far" and discovery-year qualifiers; **2** for handling the Kannada or Telugu conflict by citing named evidence and recording the variance. Giving dates without naming the witness caps at 6.

## Lesson 12 - The Vedic corpus and the machinery of oral transmission

Progress: 12/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Shruti and Smriti, the four Vedas with their contents and reciters, recensions and schools, Brahmanas, Aranyakas, Upanishads, the Vedangas, the Upavedas, and what the corpus can and cannot date

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the literature-in-ancient-India section in full: the Shruti-Smriti division, the Veda etymology and Apaurusheya note, the sakha and charana system, the complete Rig Veda mandala table, the Yajur, Sama and Atharva sections with their surviving recensions, the six Vedangas, the Brahmana table, the Aranyaka passage, the Upanishad passage with the Muktika canon and the principal ten, and the Upaveda list
CA search: "Not required - textual-structure lesson; the session's single current linkage is reserved for Lesson 21"
CA found: None found; no genuine recent linkage for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the corpus as a branching structure, not a bookshelf

```text
   VEDIC LITERATURE
        |
   +----+--------------------------------+
   |                                     |
 SHRUTI (the revealed)            SMRITI ("that which is remembered")
   |                              the collection of post-Vedic
   |                              Classical Sanskrit literature:
   |                              Vedanga | Smritis-Dharmashastras |
   |                              Shad darshana | Puranas | Itihasa |
   |                              Upavedas | Mahakavyas
   |
   +-- VEDAS (Samhitas)  ---- RIG | YAJUR | SAMA | ATHARVA
   +-- BRAHMANAS              each Veda has its own
   +-- ARANYAKAS              each Veda has its own
   +-- UPANISHADS             more than 200 known

   THE TRANSMISSION MACHINERY - this is the part candidates skip
   Each Veda has multiple RECENSIONS (sakhas), linked to schools
   (charanas) that preserved, studied and interpreted them ORALLY.
        |
   Each SAKHA typically includes:
        - its own version of the SAMHITA
        - an associated BRAHMANA
        - an ARANYAKA (sometimes)
        - one or more UPANISHADS
        |
   A CHARANA = a school or tradition of Vedic study preserving a
   particular sakha; FAMILY-BASED or GURU-BASED lineages, often
   tied to specific regions or Brahmin communities; each charana
   specialised in one sakha and had the DUTY TO TRANSMIT IT ORALLY,
   ensuring accuracy in PRONUNCIATION, INTONATION and MEMORISATION.
        |
   SURVIVAL IS PARTIAL AND COUNTABLE:
   Rig Veda:    21 shakhas traditionally -> only SHAKALA survives
                completely; parts of BASHKALA survive
   Sama Veda:   over 1000 original shakhas -> only KAUTHUMA,
                JAIMINIYA (Talavakara) and RANAYANIYA survive
   Atharva Veda: 9 shakhas -> only SHAUNAKA and PAIPPALADA survived
```

The bottom block is the most important thing in this lesson and the least often learned. Over a thousand branches of one Veda are recorded as having existed; three survive. That is a loss rate, and it is the single most powerful piece of evidence available for the survival-bias argument that runs through this session.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The Vedic corpus is usually met as a list of four names plus three attachments, which makes it impossible to answer a structural question - what a *sakha* is, why one Veda has several Brahmanas, why the Upanishads number more than two hundred but only ten are principal. The structure is the content here. Doctrine belongs to the religion and philosophy topic; what is owned here is the corpus as an **organised, transmitted, partially surviving body of text**.

### 2. Shruti and Smriti, and what "Veda" means

**Vedic literature is broadly divided into two categories, Shruti and Smriti.** **Shruti comprises the sacred texts: Vedas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas and Upanishads.** **Smriti, meaning "that which is remembered", is the collection of post-Vedic Classical Sanskrit literature**, comprising **Vedanga, Smritis-Dharmashastras, Shad darshana, Puranas, Itihasa, Upavedas and Mahakavyas**.

**Veda is derived from the root *vid*, meaning "to know".** The word signifies **knowledge**, and the texts are about providing knowledge for the conduct of life. **Veda is also believed to be *Apaurusheya*, meaning not a creation of human.** That claim is a **traditional theological claim about the texts' status**, and the correct handling is to report it as such - it tells you how the tradition regarded the corpus, which is a cultural fact, and it is not a historical claim about authorship.

**The Vedas are written in a highly stylised poetic form and the language is full of symbols and myths.** **They were initially handed down orally by generations of Brahmin families**, and **it is estimated by historians that they were compiled around 1500-1000 BCE.** Note the exact shape of that sentence: **oral handing down** first, **compilation** later, and the date attaches to the **compilation** and is an **estimate by historians**. Three qualifications in one line, all of which should survive into an answer.

They were **mostly composed by Vedic seers and poets called *rishis***, and **all the Vedas or Samhitas give prominence to *yagna* (sacrifice)**. **The Brahmanas, the Upanishads and the Aranyakas accompany each Veda.**

### 3. The four Vedas, each with what distinguishes it

| Veda | Name and focus | Structure and recensions | Reciter and distinctive facts |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Rig Veda** | the **oldest existing Veda**; **1028 individual Sanskrit hymns**; said to be one of the first extensive compositions in an Indo-European language to have survived; historians argue it was compiled around **1500-1200 BC**; focus on **worldly prosperity and natural beauty** | organised in **10 books called Mandalas** of varying age and length; each mandala has several **Suktas** or hymns, usually for sacrificial purposes; traditionally **21 shakhas**, of which only **Shakala** survives completely and parts of **Bashkala** survive; key charanas Shakala, Bashkala and **Ashvalayana**, the last known for its **Grhya Sutras** | hymns recited by **Hotri** sages; chief deity **Indra**; other deities Agni (fire), Varuna (water), Rudra (wind/storm), Aditya (a form of the Sun God), Vayu (air), the Ashwini twins; goddesses **Usha** (dawn), **Prithvi** (earth) and **Vak** (speech); **Rigarthadipika by Venkata Madhava** is an early commentary |
| **Yajur Veda** | "**Yajus**" signifies **sacrifice**; concentrates on the **rites and mantras** of the different types of sacrifice; **predominantly a ritual Veda**, a guidebook for the priests conducting sacrificial rituals | two major recensions: **Shukla** (white/pure), including the **Madhyandina** and **Kanva** shakhas, and **Krishna** (black/dark), including **Taittiriya, Kathaka, Maitrayani and Kapishthala**; major charanas **Taittiriya (Krishna), Kanva (Shukla) and Maitrayani**, each with distinct ritual systems and texts | **the Yajur Veda seems to know the metal iron** - the single most examined line in this row, because it is a dateable material marker inside a text |
| **Sama Veda** | named after **Saman**, melody; concentrates on **melody or songs**; also called the **"book of chants"**; shows how Indian music had developed in the Vedic period | entire text has **1875 hymns**, of which **historians argue 75 are original** and the rest are taken from the **Sakala branch of the Rig Veda**; consists of hymns, detached verses and **16,000 ragas and raginis**; of over **1000 original shakhas**, only **Kauthuma, Jaiminiya (Talavakara) and Ranayaniya** survive; the Kauthuma and Jaiminiya charanas emphasised **musical chanting** | hymns recited by **Udgatri** sages; the **Chandogya Upanishad**, a major Vedantic text, belongs to it |
| **Atharva Veda** | also known as the **Brahma Veda**; attributed to two rishis, **Atharvan and Angiras**, whence the older name **Atharvangirasa**; concerned with the peace and prosperity of human society and all aspects of daily life; specifically focused on **the treatment of ailments** and known to prescribe treatment for **almost 99 diseases**; deals with healing and **black and white magic**, and is also known as the **book of magical formula**; speculates on changes in the universe and touches on everyday problems of a householder | had **9 shakhas**, of which only **Shaunaka and Paippalada** survived, preserved by the corresponding charanas; includes hymns on healing, protection and domestic rituals | gives Vedic literature a **practical and magical dimension** |

One further grouping is examined directly: **the Rig Veda, Sama Veda and Yajur Veda are collectively known as *Traji* or *Trayi Vidya*, meaning Triple Science.** The Atharva Veda stands outside that triad, which is itself a fact about how the tradition ranked its own texts.

### 4. The Rig Veda's ten Mandalas, in detail

This table is examined at the level of individual mandalas, and the contents of Mandala X in particular.

| Mandala | Hymns | Age and length | Detail |
|---|---:|---|---|
| I | 191 | youngest and longest | mainly Agni and Indra, Varuna, Mitra, the Ashvins |
| II | 43 | oldest and shortest | mainly Agni and Indra; chiefly attributed to Rishi **Gritsamada Saunohotra** |
| III | 62 | oldest and shortest | mainly Agni, Indra and the Visvedevas; **contains the Gayatri Mantra** |
| IV | 58 | oldest and shortest | Agni and Indra, the Rbhus, Ashvins, Brhaspati, Vayu, Usas; most hymns attributed to **Vamadeva Gautama** |
| V | 87 | oldest and shortest | Agni, Indra, Visvedevas, the Maruts, Mitra-Varuna, the Asvins; most hymns attributed to the **Atri clan** |
| VI | 75 | oldest and shortest | Agni, Indra, all the Gods, Pusan, Ashvin, Usas |
| VII | 104 | oldest and shortest | Agni, Indra, Visvadevas, Maruts, Mitra-Varuna, Asvins, Ushas, Indra-Varuna, Varuna, Vayu; two each to **Saraswati** and **Vishnu**; most attributed to **Vasistha Maitravaruni** |
| VIII | 103 | mixed ages | attributed to the **Kanva** clan and other **Angirasa** poets |
| IX | 114 | mixed ages | **entirely devoted to Soma Pavamana**, the cleansing of the sacred potion |
| X | 191 | youngest and largest | Agni, Indra and various deities; contains **Nadistuti Sukta** (praise of rivers), **Purush Sukta** (Vedic sociology), **Nasadiya Sukta** (speculations about the creation of the universe), **Surya Sukta** (marriage hymns), and death hymns |
| **Total** | **1028** | | |

**The six Mandalas from the second to the seventh are called the Gotra or Vamsa Mandalas, also collectively known as Kula Grantha.**

Two examination-relevant observations. **Mandala IX is the single-subject book** - entirely Soma Pavamana - which makes it the easiest to identify. **Mandala X is the content-rich one**, holding four named Suktas including the creation hymn and the marriage hymns; items asking which Mandala contains a named Sukta are almost always about Mandala X, with the Gayatri Mantra in Mandala III as the standard alternative.

A recorded oddity should be noted rather than smoothed: the printed table labels **both Mandala I and Mandala X as "youngest"** while giving the intervening mandalas a uniform "oldest and shortest" label, and both I and X carry 191 hymns. The labels are best read as a broad three-way grouping - the family books in the middle, the later books at either end - rather than as a fine-grained ranking. No argument in this session depends on the label.

### 5. Brahmanas, Aranyakas, Upanishads: three attachments, three functions

**Brahmanas.** Part of the Shruti literature. **Each Veda has a Brahmana attached to it**, essentially a collection of texts with commentaries on that Veda - **a mixture of legends, facts, philosophy and detailed explanations of Vedic rituals**. They contain **instructions on how to conduct rituals properly**, enunciate the **science of sacrifice**, and **explain the symbolic significance of the sacred words used in rituals**. **Historians disagree on their dating**, which is usually pegged **between 900 and 700 BC**.

| Veda | Associated Brahmanas |
|---|---|
| Rig Veda | **Aitareya** Brahmana; **Kaushitaki** (or Shankhayana) Brahmana |
| Yajur Veda | **Taittiriya** Brahmana (Krishna Yajur Veda); **Shatapatha** Brahmana (Shukla Yajur Veda) |
| Sama Veda | **Tandya Mahabrahmana**, also called **Panchavimsha** Brahmana; **Jaiminiya** Brahmana |
| Atharva Veda | **Gopatha** Brahmana |

**The Shatapatha Brahmana is the largest and most philosophically rich of all Brahmanas.**

**Aranyakas.** The term means **"belonging to the forest"**. They are texts attached to the Vedas describing **the philosophy behind the rituals and sacrifices from various perspectives**, and are said to be compilations of ritualistic information on **the birth and death cycles** and **the complexity of the soul**. It is argued that **holy and learned men called *Munis*, who preferred to dwell within the limits of the forests, taught them**. **Each Veda has an accompanying Aranyaka.** And the functional sentence that fixes their place: **the Aranyakas bridge the way of work (*Karma Marga*) postulated by the Brahmanas and the way of knowledge (*Gyan Marga*) advocated by the Upanishads.**

That bridging formula is the best available answer to "what are the Aranyakas for?", and it also supplies the sequence Brahmana - Aranyaka - Upanishad as a movement from ritual action to knowledge.

**Upanishads.** The **etymology is the definition**: *u* (at), *pa* (foot), *ni* (down), *s(h)ad* (to sit) - **to sit down near the teacher**. **More than 200 Upanishads are known**, and the teacher **usually passed them down verbally to his students in the forest while they sat in front of him**; **this tradition was part of the guru-shishya parampara.** They are treatises in Sanskrit giving an account of the Vedas in **predominantly monastic and mystical terms**, and because they are generally the **last part of the Vedas** they are also known as **Vedanta**, "end (*anta*) of the Veda".

**Out of the more than 200, a set of 108 Upanishads is called the Muktika Canon.** **Only 10 of these are the principal Upanishads: Isha, Kena, Katha, Prashan, Mundaka, Mandukya, Tattiriya, Aitareya, Chandogya and Brihadaranyaka.** The canon is said to be important because **the number 108 equals the number of beads on a Hindu rosary or *mala***.

Their Vedic attachments:

| Veda | Upanishads |
|---|---|
| Shukla Yajur Veda | Brihadaranyaka, Isha |
| Krishna Yajur Veda | Tattiriya, Katha, Shvetashvatara |
| Sama Veda | Chandogya, Kena |
| Rig Veda | Aitareya |
| Atharva Veda | Mundaka, Mandukya, Prashan |

**The oldest Upanishads are the Brihadaranyaka and Chandogya.** And one fact that crosses into civic life: **the phrase *Satyameva Jayate*, the national motto of India, is taken from the Mundaka Upanishad.**

### 6. The Vedangas and the Upavedas

**To fully understand the Vedas it is necessary to read the Vedangas**, the branches or limbs of the Veda. **There are six auxiliary disciplines**, acting as supplements to the original Veda:

| Vedanga | Subject |
|---|---|
| **Siksha** | education (phonetics and pronunciation) |
| **Nirukta** | etymology, the origin of words |
| **Chhanda** | metrics in Sanskrit grammar |
| **Kalpasutra** | sacrificial practice and systematic sutras |
| **Jyotisha** | astronomy |
| **Vyakarana** | grammar |

**In the later period several authors wrote treatises on these subjects, called *Sutra*** - written in the form of a precept or a text defining the general rule that regulates thought and behaviour. **One of the most striking examples is Panini's Ashtadhyayi**, which defines the rules of Sanskrit grammar - the link back to Lesson 3, and the reason the grammarians belong to the Vedic apparatus rather than standing outside it.

**The Upavedas** - the term means **applied knowledge** - refer to traditional literature on technical subjects:

| Upaveda | Subject | Associated with |
|---|---|---|
| **Dhanurveda** | the science of warfare | Yajur Veda |
| **Gandharvaveda** | aesthetics, including music, dance, poetry, sculpture and erotica | Sama Veda |
| **Ayurveda** | the science of health and life | Atharva Veda |
| **Arthasastra** | public administration, governance, economy and polity | Atharva Veda |
| **Sthapatyaveda** | engineering and architecture; **considered the fifth Upaveda** | Yajur Veda |

The Upaveda table is the point at which this topic touches almost every other art topic in the syllabus: **Gandharvaveda** is the Vedic affiliation claimed for music, dance and poetry, which is why the performing-arts topics begin with a Vedic derivation of their own.

### 7. The machinery of oral transmission, assembled as an argument

```text
  PROBLEM: preserve a large body of text, exactly, for centuries,
           without relying on a written copy
                          |
  SOLUTION 1 - DIVIDE THE LOAD
  Multiple RECENSIONS (sakhas) of each Veda, each a complete
  transmitted version
                          |
  SOLUTION 2 - ASSIGN CUSTODY
  Each sakha is held by a CHARANA - a school or tradition of Vedic
  study, family-based or guru-based, often tied to a region or a
  Brahmin community
                          |
  SOLUTION 3 - SPECIFY THE DUTY
  Each charana specialises in ONE sakha and has the DUTY to transmit
  it ORALLY, ensuring accuracy in PRONUNCIATION, INTONATION and
  MEMORISATION
                          |
  SOLUTION 4 - BUNDLE THE TEXTS
  Each sakha carries its own Samhita, its Brahmana, sometimes an
  Aranyaka, and one or more Upanishads - so the custodian holds a
  COMPLETE working set, not a fragment
                          |
  SOLUTION 5 - TEACH FACE TO FACE
  The Upanishads are passed verbally, teacher to student, seated
  together, within the GURU-SHISHYA PARAMPARA
                          |
  RESULT: extraordinary fidelity - AND extraordinary fragility.
  Rig Veda: 21 shakhas -> 1 complete survivor + parts of a second
  Sama Veda: over 1000 shakhas -> 3 survivors
  Atharva Veda: 9 shakhas -> 2 survivors
                          |
  CONCLUSION: the system protected ACCURACY by distributing custody,
  but each lost lineage took its recension with it. What survives is
  not the corpus; it is the part whose custodians survived.
```

That conclusion is a genuinely analytical statement about Indian textual history and it is reusable in three different answers: on oral tradition, on manuscript conservation, and on the limits of literary evidence.

### 8. Dating discipline for this corpus

Four different dates are attached to this material and they must not be merged.

| Date | What it refers to |
|---|---|
| around **1500 BC** | the development of Old Indo-Aryan, out of which Sanskrit was born |
| **1500-1000 BCE** | the historians' estimate for the **compilation** of the Vedas, after an initial period of oral handing down |
| **1500-1200 BC** | the historians' argument for the compilation of the **Rig Veda** specifically |
| **900-700 BC** | the usual dating of the **Brahmanas**, on which historians are recorded as disagreeing |

Every one of these is an **estimate or an argument**, and the sources say so. The examinable behaviour is to quote the range with the attribution - "historians estimate" - rather than to state a year.

### Examination use

Objective items in this area attack: the Mandala contents, especially the four Suktas of Mandala X and the Gayatri Mantra in Mandala III; the reciter names, Hotri for the Rig Veda and Udgatri for the Sama Veda; the surviving recension counts; the Brahmana-to-Veda pairings, with the Shatapatha as the largest; the principal ten Upanishads and their Vedic attachments; the six Vedangas; the Upaveda affiliations; and the Yajur Veda's knowledge of iron. A recurring statement item concerns the presence of parables in the Upanishads and their chronological position relative to the Puranas, which the sequence Shruti-then-Smriti in section 2 and the Purana material in Lesson 13 together support. For Mains, section 7 is the strongest block in this lesson.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating *Apaurusheya* as a historical statement about authorship rather than as the tradition's own claim about the texts' status.
- Merging the four dates in section 8, or dropping the words "estimated by historians".
- Assigning the Chandogya Upanishad to the wrong Veda; it belongs to the Sama Veda.
- Forgetting that the Sama Veda's hymns are largely drawn from the Sakala branch of the Rig Veda, with historians arguing only 75 are original.
- Confusing the Muktika canon of 108 with the principal ten, or with the more than 200 known Upanishads.
- Attributing the national motto to the wrong text; *Satyameva Jayate* is from the Mundaka Upanishad.
- Treating the Aranyakas as a kind of Brahmana; their recorded function is to bridge Karma Marga and Gyan Marga.
- Forgetting Sthapatyaveda as the fifth Upaveda, or misassigning Arthasastra, which is attached to the Atharva Veda.
- Listing Vedangas as texts; they are six auxiliary disciplines, on which Sutras were later written.

### Revision notes

1. Shruti = Vedas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas, Upanishads. Smriti = Vedanga, Smritis-Dharmashastras, Shad darshana, Puranas, Itihasa, Upavedas, Mahakavyas.
2. Veda from *vid*, "to know"; believed *Apaurusheya*; orally handed down by Brahmin families; compiled, historians estimate, around 1500-1000 BCE.
3. Rig Veda: oldest existing; 1028 hymns; 10 Mandalas; compiled c. 1500-1200 BC per historians; hymns recited by Hotri; chief deity Indra; commentary Rigarthadipika by Venkata Madhava.
4. Mandalas II-VII = Gotra or Vamsa Mandalas, collectively Kula Grantha. Mandala III has the Gayatri Mantra. Mandala IX is entirely Soma Pavamana. Mandala X has Nadistuti, Purush, Nasadiya and Surya Suktas and death hymns.
5. Yajur Veda: "Yajus" = sacrifice; ritual guidebook; Shukla (Madhyandina, Kanva) and Krishna (Taittiriya, Kathaka, Maitrayani, Kapishthala) recensions; **seems to know the metal iron**.
6. Sama Veda: named after Saman, melody; 1875 hymns of which historians argue 75 are original, the rest from the Sakala branch of the Rig Veda; 16,000 ragas and raginis; book of chants; recited by Udgatri; Chandogya Upanishad belongs to it.
7. Atharva Veda: Brahma Veda; attributed to Atharvan and Angiras; Atharvangirasa; treatment for almost 99 diseases; black and white magic; book of magical formula.
8. Rig, Sama and Yajur together = Traji / Trayi Vidya, Triple Science.
9. Recension survival: Rig 21 shakhas -> Shakala complete, parts of Bashkala; Sama over 1000 -> Kauthuma, Jaiminiya (Talavakara), Ranayaniya; Atharva 9 -> Shaunaka and Paippalada.
10. Charana = school or tradition preserving a sakha; family- or guru-based; duty of oral transmission with accurate pronunciation, intonation and memorisation.
11. Brahmanas: one per Veda; legends, facts, philosophy and ritual explanation; usually dated 900-700 BC with historians disagreeing; Shatapatha the largest and most philosophically rich.
12. Brahmana pairings: Aitareya and Kaushitaki (Rig); Taittiriya and Shatapatha (Yajur); Tandya Mahabrahmana/Panchavimsha and Jaiminiya (Sama); Gopatha (Atharva).
13. Aranyakas: "belonging to the forest"; taught by Munis; bridge Karma Marga of the Brahmanas and Gyan Marga of the Upanishads.
14. Upanishads: "to sit down near"; more than 200 known; Muktika canon of 108; principal ten - Isha, Kena, Katha, Prashan, Mundaka, Mandukya, Tattiriya, Aitareya, Chandogya, Brihadaranyaka; also called Vedanta; oldest are Brihadaranyaka and Chandogya; *Satyameva Jayate* from Mundaka.
15. Six Vedangas: Siksha, Nirukta, Chhanda, Kalpasutra, Jyotisha, Vyakarana; later treatises on them called Sutras, e.g. Panini's Ashtadhyayi.
16. Five Upavedas: Dhanurveda (warfare, Yajur), Gandharvaveda (aesthetics, Sama), Ayurveda (health, Atharva), Arthasastra (governance, Atharva), Sthapatyaveda (engineering and architecture, Yajur; the fifth).

### Concept check

**Question:** The Sama Veda is recorded as having had over a thousand recensions, of which three survive. What does that single fact establish about the nature of the surviving Indian textual record?

**Model answer:** It establishes that the surviving record is a **custodial residue**, not a representative sample. Each recension was held by a *charana* - a family- or guru-based school whose duty was to transmit its own recension orally with accurate pronunciation, intonation and memorisation - and each sakha carried a complete working set including its Samhita, Brahmana, sometimes an Aranyaka and one or more Upanishads. When a school failed, its entire bundle went with it. So what survives reflects which lineages persisted, not which texts were most important, and any argument built on "what the Vedic corpus contains" is really an argument about what three surviving Sama Vedic schools and one complete Rig Vedic school happened to preserve.

**Misconception to avoid:** That oral transmission is inherently unreliable and written transmission reliable. The oral system was engineered for exactness - specialised custody, prescribed phonetic accuracy, bundled complete sets - and it achieved remarkable fidelity. Its weakness was not corruption but **catastrophic loss when a lineage ended**, which is a different failure mode from the gradual textual drift of a copying tradition.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Assess the Vedic corpus as a source for early Indian society and knowledge systems, distinguishing what its structure can and cannot show. (20 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**What the structure shows.** The corpus is **organised, not miscellaneous**. Shruti comprises Vedas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas and Upanishads; Smriti the post-Vedic classical body. Each Veda carries **recensions (sakhas)** held by **schools (charanas)**, each school holding a complete working set - Samhita, Brahmana, sometimes Aranyaka, and Upanishads. That architecture is itself social evidence: knowledge was held by **family- or guru-based lineages** with a **duty of exact oral transmission** in pronunciation, intonation and memorisation.

**What its content shows.** Ritual and cosmology from the Samhitas, with **yagna** central; ritual science and symbolism in the Brahmanas; the forest-taught bridge from **Karma Marga to Gyan Marga** in the Aranyakas; and a teaching relation - *to sit down near* - in the Upanishads, transmitted within **guru-shishya parampara**. Material markers appear: the **Yajur Veda seems to know the metal iron**; the **Purush Sukta** of Mandala X supplies a social scheme; the **Sama Veda** records musical development; the six **Vedangas** and five **Upavedas** show an organised knowledge taxonomy from phonetics to architecture.

**What it cannot show.** Not a **chronology**: compilation is estimated by historians at 1500-1000 BCE and Brahmana dating at 900-700 BC is itself disputed. Not a **complete picture**: over a thousand Sama Vedic recensions survive as three, so the corpus is a **custodial residue**. Not **lived practice**: prescriptive ritual texts record what should be done.

**Verdict.** Unmatched on ritual, cosmology and the organisation of knowledge; structurally incapable of supplying chronology or a representative social survey.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 20: **5** for the structural argument that custody architecture is itself evidence; **7** for content evidence, at 1 each for seven named items including at least two material markers; **5** for the three limits at 1.7 each, the custodial-residue point being compulsory; **3** for a verdict that separates what the corpus is unmatched on from what it structurally cannot do. Retelling the contents without the limits caps at 10.

### Visual: two ways of classifying the same shelf

```text
  CLASSIFICATION BY PURPOSE                CLASSIFICATION BY DEVICE
  (what the text is trying to do)          (what the story uses)

  DIDACTIC TEXT                            PARABLE
  also called DIRECTIVE TEXT               short story, prose or verse,
  tries to INFLUENCE the reasoning,        illustrating a SPIRITUAL,
  thinking and conduct of the reader       MORAL or RELIGIOUS lesson
  writer intends to PERSUADE, COAX and         |
  COMPEL the reader into thinking in a     usually features a HUMAN
  particular way                           character
  usually used for POLITICAL or MORAL
  issues, specifically in SERMONS and      FABLE
  RELIGIOUS TREATISES                      short story, prose or verse,
                                           illustrating a MORAL through a
  NARRATIVE TEXT                           pithy maxim or clever story
  provides all the essential information       |
  about the topic so that what is          usually features ANIMALS,
  discussed makes sense to the reader      INANIMATE OBJECTS, MYTHICAL
  writer intends to ENHANCE and SUSTAIN    CREATURES and PLANTS given
  interest and inquisitiveness             HUMAN-LIKE QUALITIES
  the MOST COMMON type of prose, used
  mostly in STORY WRITING and NOVELS       -> PANCHATANTRA is a DIDACTIC
                                              FABLE: both classifications
  -> the two classifications are               apply at once
     INDEPENDENT. A text has a purpose
     AND a device, and an item can test
     either one.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

This block of material looks like an undifferentiated mass of Sanskrit titles, and candidates respond by memorising names without knowing what kind of text each is. But the questions set here are overwhelmingly **genre** questions - is this a law-book or a commentary, a parable or a fable, a Purana or an Upapurana, a didactic text or a narrative one. Getting the category right is usually enough to eliminate three response options, and getting it wrong makes the title useless.

### 2. The epics, positioned

The **two epics - Mahabharata and Ramayana - are part of the classical category** but are discussed separately because of their **religious importance**. And then the sentence that positions them for this topic: **irrespective of their centrality to the Hindu religion, these epics can also be considered to be the precursors of Sanskrit *kavya* (epic poetry), *nataka* (classical drama) and other treatises on medicine, statecraft, grammar, astronomy, mathematics and so forth.**

Read as a literary-historical claim, that is a strong statement: the epics are positioned as the **formal ancestors** of the classical genres of Lesson 14, not merely as their thematic quarry. It also explains why so many classical dramas and poems take their plots from epic episodes - Bhasa's Mahabharata plays, Bhavabhuti's Ramayana plays, Bharavi's Kiratarjuniya, Magha's Shishupalavadha.

The epics also belong to the layered-corpus problem of Lesson 23: they have long accretion histories, so core composition, redaction and surviving manuscripts are separate dates, and no single "date of the Mahabharata" can be given.

### 3. The Puranas

**As the word "Purana" suggests, these texts talk about "that which renews the old".** They are **ancient Indian mythological texts consisting of narrative stories about the creation of the universe, illustrating its history till the supposed destruction of the universe**. They cover **folk tales, pilgrimages, astronomy, grammar and theology**. They contain **stories of kings, heroes, sages and demigods**, but focus on the **Hindu trimurti or trinity: Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesh**.

**There are 18 major Puranas (Mahapuranas), and each gives prominence to a particular deity** and expounds the philosophical and religious concepts related to it. **Prominent ones: Bhagavata, Brahma, Vayu, Agni, Garuda, Padma, Vishnu and Matsya.** They **contain anecdotes about the social, cultural and religious life of post-Vedic India and provide historians with critical information about geography, history and dynastic genealogies.** **The Skanda Purana is the longest and the Markandeya Purana is the shortest.**

They are **written in the form of stories combining myths, legends and sermons about the deities**, and **this easy form of story writing made them very popular among the masses, who did not always understand the complex Vedas.** **The Puranas were translated and distributed in various vernacular languages.**

That last sentence is important and easy to skip. It is the earliest **translation-and-vernacularisation** fact in this session, and it prefigures both the Bhakti vernacular turn of Lesson 18 and the translation economy of Lesson 17. The Puranic corpus is where the movement from a restricted learned register towards a broad popular readership first becomes visible.

**Upapuranas.** The Puranas were so popular that they gave rise to a sub-genre called **Upapuranas or minor Puranas**. **There are about 19 minor Puranas**, based on **five major subjects as dictated by the Sanskrit lexicographer of the Gupta period, Amarasimha**:

| Subject | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Sarga** | the creation of the universe |
| **Pratisarga** | the periodic cycle of destruction and re-creation |
| **Manvantara** | the periods of Manu's lifetime |
| **Vamsa** (Chandra and Surya) | genealogies of the solar and lunar dynasties of gods and sages |
| **Vamshanucharita** | dynastic histories of kings |

The five-subject scheme is examinable as a set, and the attribution to **Amarasimha**, the Gupta-period lexicographer, is the pairing fact. Amarasimha returns in Lesson 14 as the compiler of the **Amarakosha** in the court of Chandragupta II - one person, two separate contributions, and items sometimes attach the wrong one.

### 4. The law-book tradition: four layers, not one

This is the most structurally interesting block in the lesson, because it is a four-layer textual tradition and each layer has its own name.

```text
  STAGE 1 - DHARMASUTRA
  emerged during the VEDIC ERA; BASED UPON THE VEDAS
        |
  STAGE 2 - DHARMASASTRA
  a collection of ancient Sanskrit texts containing codes of conduct
  and moral principles (DHARMA) for Hindus; between 18 and 100 texts;
  believed to have ORIGINATED IN THE DHARMASUTRA TEXTS;
  offers commentaries on responsibilities, duties and ethics towards
  oneself, one's family and one's community;
  WRITTEN IN POETIC VERSES; MAINLY BASED ON THE PURANAS
  (while the Dharmasutras are based upon the Vedas)
        |
  STAGE 3 - BHASYAS (commentaries)
  interpret and explain the Dharmasastra, accept or reject ideas
  WITH REASONS WHY
  Prominent Bhasya authors on the Manusmriti: BHARUCI, MEDHATITHI
        |
  STAGE 4 - NIBANDHAS (digests)
  arose because of CONFLICT AND DISAGREEMENT on a subject ACROSS
  the various Dharma texts; attempted to RECONCILE, BRIDGE or
  SUGGEST A COMPROMISE GUIDELINE
  Oldest surviving: KRITYAKALPATARU, early 12th century, by
  LAKSHMIDHARA OF KANNAUJ in present Uttar Pradesh
```

The four named Dharmashastra texts, with their dates and their distinguishing characterisation:

| Text | Date | Characterisation |
|---|---|---|
| **Manusmriti** | about the **2nd to 3rd century AD** | **most often studied**; particular influence on **medieval Buddhism and Hinduism in Cambodia and Indonesia**; lays down important legal and social norms |
| **Yajnavalkya Smriti** | about the **4th to 5th century AD** | considered the **best crafted text** of the Dharmasastra tradition |
| **Naradasmriti** | about the **5th to 6th century AD** | a **juridical text**, as opposed to a text about righteous conduct |
| **Visnusmriti** | about the **7th century AD** | **details the bhakti tradition** rather than dharma directly |

The four characterisations are the examinable content, because they differ from each other in kind: most-studied, best-crafted, juridical, bhakti-oriented. Note also that the **Manusmriti's recorded influence extends to Cambodia and Indonesia**, which is a cross-regional transmission fact of the same family as the Southeast Asian script descent in Lesson 9.

**The recorded property provision.** In the context of the distribution of wealth and property, **the paternal estate was to be divided equally among sons after the death of the parents. Women could not claim a share of these resources. *Stridhana*, literally "a woman's wealth", were the gifts they received on the occasion of their marriage. Women were allowed to retain this Stridhana. This could be inherited by their children without any claim of the husband on this property. These norms about access to property and wealth caused various social differences between men and women.**

Note precisely what the last clause of the Stridhana rule says: it could be inherited by the woman's children **without any claim of the husband**. That specific negation is exactly the kind of clause that statement items are built on, and dropping it produces a wrong answer.

**Named Nibandha authors and their subjects**, which is a pairing set:

| Author | Subject written on |
|---|---|
| **Lakshmidhara of Kannauj** | the **Krityakalpataru**, the oldest surviving Nibandha, early 12th century |
| **Jimutavahana** and **Raghunandana** | inheritance |
| **Nanda-pandita** | adoption |
| **Todar Mal** | the king's duties |
| **Candesvara, Kamalakara-bhatta, Nilakantha, Mitra-misra** | judicial process |
| **Lakshmidevi** | *Vivadachandra* |
| **Mahadevi Dhiramati** | *Danavakyavali* |

**A few notable Nibandhas were written by women** - Lakshmidevi's *Vivadachandra* and Mahadevi Dhiramati's *Danavakyavali*. That is a directly usable piece of named evidence for any answer touching women's participation in Sanskrit learned literature, and it is far stronger than a general claim, because it supplies two names and two titles in a technical legal genre.

### 5. Didactic and narrative, parables and fables

**Didactic text.** Also known as **directive text**, because it tries to **influence the reasoning, thinking and conduct of the reader**. The writer intends to **persuade, coax and compel the reader into thinking in a particular way**. It is usually used for writing about **political or moral issues, specifically in sermons and religious treatises**.

**Narrative text.** Provides **all the essential information about the topic** so that what is discussed in the narration is explained or makes sense to the reader. The writer intends to **enhance and sustain the interest and inquisitiveness of readers**. It is **the most common type of prose**, used mostly in **story writing and novels**.

**Parables** are short stories, in prose or verse, that illustrate a **spiritual, moral or religious lesson**, and they **usually feature a human character**. **Fables** are short stories, in prose or verse, that illustrate a **moral through a pithy maxim or clever story**, and they **usually feature animals, inanimate objects, mythical creatures and plants that are given human-like qualities**.

The discriminator is therefore **the kind of character**, not the presence of a moral: both carry morals; the parable's carrier is human, the fable's is not.

**The two named works.**

- **Panchatantra** by **Vishnu Sharma**. A **didactic fable** comprising several stories with morals and knowledge about the world **through animals**. An **ancient non-religious text** of interrelated animal fables **in Sanskrit**, believed to be dated around **200 BC-300 AD**. It **deals with human conduct, particularly the wise conduct of life**. It is also described elsewhere as a collection of animal fables **teaching political science, moral lessons and statecraft**.
- **Hitopadesha**, believed to be written by **Narayan Pandit** around the **12th century**. Of the same genre, with **human and animal elements imparting worldly wisdom and advice on political affairs**.

The Panchatantra is the single most travelled Indian text in this session. Its onward journey - **translated into old Persian in the sixth century, into Arabic in the eighth century as *Kalila wa Dimna*** - belongs to Lesson 17, and its documentary-heritage recognition belongs to Lesson 22, where a fifteenth-century manuscript of it was inscribed on the **Asia-Pacific Regional Register** in 2024. One text, three different lessons, three different kinds of significance: that is the clearest available demonstration that a literary work has a composition history, a transmission history and a recognition history, and that they are separate.

### 6. Comparative treatment: three narrative corpora, three evidentiary values

| Corpus | What it is | What it is good evidence for | What it cannot support |
|---|---|---|---|
| Epics | classical-category texts of religious importance; precursors of kavya, nataka and technical treatises | literary genealogy; the plot stock of later drama and poetry | a single date, or a straightforward historical narrative |
| Puranas | mythological narrative from creation to destruction, deity-focused, 18 major and about 19 minor | social, cultural and religious life of post-Vedic India; geography; dynastic genealogies; popular religious transmission and vernacular translation | precise chronology; they are narrative, not chronicle |
| Didactic fables | Panchatantra, Hitopadesha; non-religious; conduct, statecraft, worldly wisdom | the existence of a secular advisory literature in Sanskrit; cross-cultural transmission | religious doctrine, for which they are explicitly not the vehicle |

The middle row's phrase **"provide historians with critical information about geography, history and dynastic genealogies"** is the sanctioned historical use of the Puranas, and pairing it with the qualification that they are **narrative mythological texts** is what makes the citation defensible.

### Examination use

Genre items dominate here: whether a named text is a Purana or an Upapurana, a Dharmasutra or a Dharmashastra, a Bhasya or a Nibandha, a parable or a fable, a didactic or a narrative text. Pairing items attach an author to a text - Vishnu Sharma to the Panchatantra, Narayan Pandit to the Hitopadesha, Lakshmidhara to the Krityakalpataru, Amarasimha to the five Upapurana subjects. Statement items attack the Smriti property provisions, where the Stridhana clause about inheritance by children without the husband's claim is the discriminator, and the Upanishad-Purana chronology, which is settled by the Shruti-then-Smriti structure of Lesson 12. For Mains, the four-layer law-book diagram is the best available illustration that Indian textual traditions are **self-correcting** - commentaries that give reasons, and digests that exist because the primary texts disagreed.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating Dharmasutra and Dharmashastra as synonyms; the first emerged in the Vedic era and is based on the Vedas, the second originated in them and is mainly based on the Puranas and written in poetic verses.
- Confusing a Bhasya (commentary, interpreting and giving reasons) with a Nibandha (digest, reconciling disagreements between texts).
- Dropping the Stridhana clause that the property could be inherited by the woman's children without any claim of the husband.
- Making the Panchatantra a religious text; it is described as an ancient non-religious text.
- Distinguishing parable from fable by the presence of a moral; both have morals, and the discriminator is the human versus non-human character.
- Forgetting that the Skanda Purana is the longest and the Markandeya the shortest.
- Confusing Amarasimha's two contributions - the five-subject scheme for Upapuranas, and the Amarakosha of Lesson 14.
- Treating the epics as datable single-author works.

### Revision notes

1. The two epics are part of the classical category, discussed separately for religious importance, and are precursors of Sanskrit kavya, nataka and technical treatises.
2. Purana = "that which renews the old"; narrative from creation to destruction; folk tales, pilgrimages, astronomy, grammar, theology; focused on Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesh.
3. 18 Mahapuranas; prominent ones Bhagavata, Brahma, Vayu, Agni, Garuda, Padma, Vishnu, Matsya; Skanda longest, Markandeya shortest.
4. Puranas provide historians with information on geography, history and dynastic genealogies; popular with the masses; translated and distributed in vernacular languages.
5. About 19 Upapuranas, based on five subjects dictated by Amarasimha, the Gupta-period lexicographer: Sarga, Pratisarga, Manvantara, Vamsa, Vamshanucharita.
6. Dharmasutra: Vedic era, based upon the Vedas. Dharmashastra: between 18 and 100 texts, originated in the Dharmasutras, written in poetic verses, mainly based on the Puranas.
7. Manusmriti (2nd-3rd c AD, most studied, influence in Cambodia and Indonesia); Yajnavalkya Smriti (4th-5th c AD, best crafted); Naradasmriti (5th-6th c AD, juridical); Visnusmriti (7th c AD, details the bhakti tradition).
8. Smriti property norms: paternal estate divided equally among sons; women could not claim a share; Stridhana retained by women and inheritable by their children without any claim of the husband.
9. Bhasyas interpret and explain, accepting or rejecting ideas with reasons; Bharuci and Medhatithi wrote on the Manusmriti.
10. Nibandhas arose from conflict across Dharma texts and sought to reconcile them; oldest surviving is the Krityakalpataru, early 12th century, by Lakshmidhara of Kannauj.
11. Nibandha subjects: Jimutavahana and Raghunandana on inheritance; Nanda-pandita on adoption; Todar Mal on the king's duties; Candesvara, Kamalakara-bhatta, Nilakantha and Mitra-misra on judicial process. Women authors: Lakshmidevi's Vivadachandra and Mahadevi Dhiramati's Danavakyavali.
12. Didactic = directive; persuade, coax, compel; political or moral issues, sermons and religious treatises. Narrative = provides essential information; sustains interest; most common prose type, story writing and novels.
13. Parable: human character, spiritual/moral/religious lesson. Fable: animals, inanimate objects, mythical creatures, plants with human-like qualities; moral through a pithy maxim or clever story.
14. Panchatantra by Vishnu Sharma: didactic animal fables in Sanskrit, c. 200 BC-300 AD; ancient non-religious text; wise conduct of life; political science and statecraft. Hitopadesha by Narayan Pandit, c. 12th century.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Nibandhas exist because the primary Dharma texts disagreed with one another. What does that tell you about the character of the Indian legal-textual tradition, and how would you use it in an answer?

**Model answer:** It shows the tradition was **plural and self-consciously reconciliatory rather than monolithic**. The Dharmashastra corpus runs to between eighteen and a hundred texts; they conflict on particular subjects; so a distinct genre developed whose declared purpose was to **reconcile, bridge or suggest a compromise guideline** across those disagreements, the oldest surviving example being Lakshmidhara of Kannauj's *Krityakalpataru* of the early twelfth century. Alongside it the **Bhasyas** interpret and explain, expressly **accepting or rejecting ideas with reasons why**. In an answer this supports a precise claim - that normative authority in this tradition was constructed through commentary and digest rather than asserted by a single code - and it is supported by named genres, named authors and a dated oldest survivor rather than by assertion.

**Misconception to avoid:** That the Manusmriti functioned as a single authoritative code. It is described as the most often studied text within a large and internally divergent corpus; the very existence of the digest genre is evidence that no single text settled the questions on its own.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Distinguish the Dharmasutra, the Dharmashastra, the Bhasya and the Nibandha, and explain what the sequence shows about textual authority in India. (10 marks, 150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

**The four.** **Dharmasutras** emerged in the **Vedic era** and are **based upon the Vedas**. **Dharmashastras** - between 18 and 100 texts - originated in them, are **written in poetic verses** and are **mainly based on the Puranas**; Manusmriti, Yajnavalkya Smriti, Naradasmriti and Visnusmriti are the named four. **Bhasyas** are **commentaries** that interpret and explain, **accepting or rejecting ideas with reasons why**; Bharuci and Medhatithi wrote on the Manusmriti. **Nibandhas** are **digests** that arose because the primary texts **conflicted**, and that sought to reconcile or compromise; the oldest surviving is **Lakshmidhara of Kannauj's Krityakalpataru**, early twelfth century.

**What the sequence shows.** Authority was **constructed, not decreed**. It accumulated through reasoned commentary and negotiated digest, and the digest genre exists only because no single text settled the questions - which is why Manusmriti is described as the most studied rather than the authoritative code.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 10: **4** for the four genres correctly distinguished at 1 each, with Bhasya-Nibandha confusion forfeiting 2; **3** for named exemplars including the dated oldest Nibandha; **3** for the authority argument, of which 2 require the point that the digest genre presupposes disagreement. Listing the four Smriti texts alone caps at 4.
