```text
                              |
   THE POETICS VOCABULARY - the terms a poetry answer must define
   ALANKARA figures of speech - similes, metaphors - that beautify poetry
   RASA     the emotion a poem evokes, such as love or heroism
   DHVANI   the suggested meaning beyond the words
   RITI     the poet's style of expression
   VAKROKTI beauty in indirect or creative expression
   GUNAS    qualities of good poetry - clarity, sweetness, vigour
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

There are perhaps sixty author-text pairs in this area, and no candidate can hold them all with confidence. The realistic goal is different: **hold the framework securely, hold the high-frequency pairs exactly, and treat every other pair as something to be verified rather than guessed.** The framework - period, four styles, six poetics terms, the distinction between drama and kavya - is small, stable and heavily rewarded. The pairs are large, unstable and punish pattern-matching. This lesson is organised accordingly.

### 2. The period and its grammatical foundation

**The period of Classical Sanskrit Literature in India is from the 4th century AD to the 12th century AD.** Most Sanskrit literature is divided into the **Vedic** and the **Classical** categories.

**Most of the Sanskrit literature is bound by the rules of grammar explained in Panini's Ashtadhyayi.** The other two famous works on Sanskrit grammar of ancient India are **Patanjali's Mahabhashya**, "great commentary", of **250 BC**, and **Katyayana's Varttika-sutra** - the sequence established in Lesson 3, repeated here because the classical literature is written under its rules.

### 3. Drama: purpose, authors and works

**One of the most popular genres of lyric poetry and prose is the popular romantic tale, whose sole purpose was to offer diversion or entertainment - *Lokaranjana* - to the public.** These were usually written as **elaborate dramas with distinct stories and plots, yet giving a unique perspective on life.**

Hold *Lokaranjana* as a term. It is a statement about **purpose**, and it is the cleanest available contrast with the *didactic* purpose of Lesson 13: a literature that exists for diversion is a different cultural object from one that exists to persuade.

| Author | Major works | What distinguishes them |
|---|---|---|
| **Bharata Muni** | **Natyashastra** | foundational text on Indian drama, music and dance; known as the **Fifth Veda**; introduces **Rasa, Bhava and stagecraft** |
| **Sudraka** | **Mrichchhakatika**, Vinavasavadatta, Padmaprabhritaka | Mrichchhakatika features a **courtesan heroine and a Brahmin lover**; **not based on mythology**; known for **realism, urban setting and an early reference to Holi** |
| **Vishakhadatta** | **Mudrarakshasa**, **Devi-Chandraguptam** | Mudrarakshasa is a **political drama** on Chandragupta Maurya, highlighting **Chanakya's diplomacy**; Devichandraguptam depicts the exploits of **Chandragupta II Vikramaditya** and **survives only in fragments** |
| **Bhavabhuti** | **Uttararamacharita**, **Malatimadhava**, **Mahaviracharita** | based on the Ramayana; explores **emotion, ethics and duty**; Uttararamacharita covers **Rama's life after his coronation and his reunion with Sita** |
| **Bhasa** | **Pancharatra**, **Svapnavasavadattam**, **Urubhanga**, **Charudatta**, **Dootaghatotkacha**, **Dootavakyam** | among the **earliest Sanskrit dramatists**; **Urubhanga presents a sympathetic view of Duryodhana**; **Svapnavasavadattam was rediscovered in 1912 by T. Ganapati Sastri** |
| **Kalidasa** | **Abhijnanashakuntalam**, **Malavikagnimitram**, **Vikramorvashiyam** | known as the **Shakespeare of India**; Abhijnanashakuntalam **praised by Goethe**; combines romance, nature and philosophy; reflects Gupta-era court culture |
| **Trivikrama Bhatta** | **Damayanti-Katha** | classical Sanskrit narrative literature based on the **Nalopakhyana episode from the Mahabharata** |
| **Harshavardhana** | **Ratnavali**, **Priyadarsika**, **Nagananda** | **Ratnavali mentions Holi**; **Nagananda reflects Buddhist influence and the theme of self-sacrifice**; Priyadarsika is a romantic comedy on the love between King **Udayana** and **Priyadarsika**, daughter of King Dridhavarman |
| **Maharana Kumbha** | **Sangita Raja**, **Sangita Mimamsa**, **Sudha Prabandha** | Sangita Raja is a treatise on music and dance; Sangita Mimamsa a philosophical study of music theory; Sudha Prabandha a poetic work on devotion and aesthetics |

**Bhasa's fuller list**, recorded separately, is worth holding because it is the most heavily examined single author in this area: **Pratima-nataka** (a Ramayana play in which Rama's story is told through statues placed in a temple), **Yagna-Phalam** (the fruits of sacrifice, from the Ramayana), **Abhisheka-nataka** (the coronation of Rama, symbolising the restoration of dharma), **Panch-ratra** (from the Mahabharata, "Five Nights"), **Madhyama-vyayoga** (the identity of the "Middle Son" in a Mahabharata episode), **Duta-Ghattotkacha** (Ghatotkacha as an envoy), **Duta-Vakya** ("The Message of the Envoy"), **Urubhanga** ("The Broken Thigh", Duryodhana's moral downfall and tragic defeat), **Karnabharam** ("Karna's Burden", the conflict between duty and loyalty), **Harivamsa or Bala-charita** (legends of Krishna and his early deeds), and **Daridra Charudatta** (poor Charudatta and the courtesan Vasantasena, **a precursor to Mrichchhakatika**).

That last entry deserves a sentence of its own, because it resolves a pair that looks like a contradiction: **Bhasa's Daridra Charudatta is the precursor of Sudraka's Mrichchhakatika**, which is why both concern Charudatta and Vasantasena. Two different authors, two different plays, one shared story - and an item that pairs the wrong author with the wrong title is trivially easy to set.

**Maharana Kumbha** also carries a separate recorded attribution set: he is credited with a **commentary on Jayadeva's Gita Govinda** and with **four dramas said to have used four provincial languages** - a multilingual dramatic practice that belongs with the register material of Lesson 4.

### 4. Kavya: the four styles and the poets

**This genre is also called *kavya* or poetry. Unlike drama, where the story is the main focus of the text, poetry concentrates more on form, style, figure of speech and the like.**

| Style | Description |
|---|---|
| **Mahakavya** | epic poetry with formal structure: invocation, court scenes, nature and so on |
| **Khandakavya** | shorter standalone lyric poems, focused on a single theme or emotion |
| **Gadya Kavya** | prose-poetry, such as **Kadambari** and **Dashakumaracharita** |
| **Champu** | mixed style of prose and verse, for example **Vasavadatta by Subandhu** |

The poets, with what each is known for:

| Author | Works | What distinguishes them |
|---|---|---|
| **Kalidasa** | **Kumarasambhavam**, **Raghuvamsha**, **Meghaduta**, **Ritusamhara**, **Malavikagnimitram**, **Vikramorvashiyam** | Kumarasambhavam - the birth of Kumara (Kartikeya), son of Shiva and Parvati, glorifying Shaivism, rich in similes and rasa; Raghuvamsha - the Raghu dynasty from **King Dileepa to Agnivarna**, showcasing ideal kingship (*rajarshi*) and dharma-centric rule; Meghaduta - a **Yaksha, exiled by Kubera**, sends a message to his wife via a cloud, celebrated for romantic imagery, *shringara rasa* and geographic description; Ritusamhara - the **six Indian seasons**; Malavikagnimitram - the Shunga king **Agnimitra** and Malavika, mentioning **Pushyamitra's Rajasuya Yajna**; Vikramorvashiyam - **Pururavas and Urvashi**, based on a Vedic legend |
| **Jayadeva** | **Gita Govinda** | devotional poem on **Radha-Krishna's love**; blends bhakti, romance and music; a key text for **Odissi dance** |
| **Dandin** | **Kavyadarsha**, **Dashakumaracharita** | Kavyadarsha is a **foundational text on Sanskrit poetics and *alankara***; Dashakumaracharita narrates the adventures of **ten princes**, reflecting court politics, diplomacy and ornate prose |
| **Banabhatta** | **Kadambari**, **Harshacharita** | Kadambari is a romantic prose classic; **Harshacharita is the first historical biography in India**, based on Harsha's life |
| **Harisena** | **Allahabad Pillar Inscription** | eulogy of **Samudragupta**, inscribed on a pillar; a major source of Gupta political history; **written in poetic prose** |
| **Bharavi** | **Kiratarjuniyam** | Arjuna's battle with **Shiva in the form of a hunter (*Kirata*)**; famous for linguistic depth, heroic rasa and philosophical undertones |
| **Magha** | **Shishupalavadha** | Krishna kills Shishupala; known for **complex Sanskrit, ornate style and verbal artistry** |
| **Sriharsa** | **Naishadhiyacharita** | the romantic tale of **Nala and Damayanti**; celebrated for linguistic virtuosity and ornamentation |
| **Nayachandra Suri** | **Hammira Mahakavya** | legendary biography of **King Hammira**; reflects **Jain historical storytelling in epic form** |
| **Somadeva Suri** | **Upasakadyayana**, **Nitivakyamrita** | **Jain** works on spiritual stories and political ethics; important in the Digambara tradition and medieval polity |
| **Subandhu** | **Vasavadatta** | romantic prose involving **Udayana and Vasavadatta**; written in **Champu** style, rich in metaphors |
| **Bhatti** | **Bhattikavya (Ravanavadha)** | narrates the killing of Ravana **while simultaneously illustrating Sanskrit grammar** |
| **Bhartrhari** | **Vakyapadiya**, **Nitishataka** | Vakyapadiya is a treatise on **grammar and the philosophy of language**, introducing ideas of **semantic unity**; Nitishataka is **100 verses** of ethical, philosophical and practical wisdom |
| **Vatsyayana** | **Kamasutra**, **Nyaya Sutra Bhashya** | Kamasutra is a foundational treatise on human sexual behaviour and the arts of living, part of the **Kama Shastra** tradition; the Nyaya Sutra Bhashya is an early commentary on **Gautama's Nyaya Sutras** |

Two entries in that table are cross-topic hinges. **Harisena's Allahabad Pillar Inscription** is simultaneously a **kavya** and an **inscription** - the champu-style **Prayag Prashasti** - which makes it the single object in this session that belongs to Lesson 11 and Lesson 14 at once. And **Jayadeva's Gita Govinda** is a Sanskrit devotional poem that reappears in Lesson 19 as a central text of Odia literary and temple tradition.

### 5. The scientific and technical corpus

**Sanskrit was the court language and gained prominence in the Gupta period.** Some of the scientific texts written during the ancient period:

| Author | Work | Subject |
|---|---|---|
| **Pingala** | Chandashastra | mathematics |
| **Charak** | Charaka Samhita | medicine |
| **Sushruta** | Sushruta Samhita | surgery |
| **Madhava** | Madhava Nidana | pathology |
| **Varahamihira** | **Pancha-Siddhantika** | mathematical astronomy |
| **Varahamihira** | **Brihat-Samhita** | wide-ranging subjects: planetary movements, geology, architecture; elsewhere described as covering astrology, natural sciences, architecture, weather and eclipses |
| **Aryabhata** | **Aryabhatiya** | astronomy and mathematics; described as introducing **zero, place value, trigonometry and planetary models** |
| **Lagadha** | Vedanga Jyotisa | astronomy |
| **Bhaskara II** | Siddhanta Shiromani | mathematics and astronomy |
| **Chanakya** | **Arthashastra** | statecraft; the text mentions that **"Kautilya" or "Vishnugupta"** wrote it, and historians argue both names were aliases for Chanakya, a learned scholar at the court of **Chandragupta Maurya** |

Two further items complete the technical field: the traditional **Surya Siddhanta**, an important astronomical text calculating planetary positions and celestial phenomena, and the commentary **Yuktidipika**, elaborating astronomical and mathematical concepts.

The **Aryabhatiya** entry is directly relevant to a recent objective demand on the **place-value system**, handled honestly in the PYQ section: this session records the Aryabhatiya's association with place value, but holds **no epigraphic record of place-value notation**, and therefore does not supply the discriminating material for that item.

### 6. The Gupta high point and the medieval continuation

**The Gupta period is known as the Golden Age of art and literature**, with an extensive range of religious and secular texts. **Prominent Puranas from this period: Vishnu, Vayu, Matsya, Shiva, Varaha, Vamana and Narasimha.** Several **Smritis** were compiled, notably the **Narada Smriti**, reflecting the era's social and economic customs. **Sanskrit was the dominant language of literary expression.** The fifth-century poet **Kalidasa, who flourished in Chandragupta II's court, is celebrated as the greatest poet of the time**, and his works have been translated into many European languages. The period also saw the development of Sanskrit grammar based on Panini and Patanjali, and it is **particularly memorable for the compilation of the *Amarakosha* by Amarsimha, a luminary in the court of Chandragupta II**.

Gupta literature also included scientific and technical texts such as **Aryabhata's Aryabhatiya** and **Varahamihira's Brihat Samhita**. **The Allahabad Pillar Inscription or Prayag Prashasti is a mixture of prose and verse, the style known as champu**, and **the Bhittari pillar inscription, Junagadh inscription, Udaygiri inscription and many others of the period show the glory of Gupta literature** - a direct statement that inscriptions of this period are read as *literature* and not only as records, which is the strongest support in this session for treating epigraphy as a literary form.

**Medieval Sanskrit.** **Although literature in Sanskrit was not so predominant in the medieval period, some excellent works were composed in Rajasthan and Kashmir.** The two most notable Kashmiri works are **Kalhana's Rajatarangini**, giving a detailed account of the kings of Kashmir, and **Somadeva's Kathasaritsagara**, an eleventh-century Sanskrit collection of Indian legends, histories and folk tales by **Somadeva Bhatta**, a writer from Kashmir. **Vikramankadevacharita** is a Sanskrit epic on the life of the Western Chalukya king **Vikramaditya VI**, written by the Kashmiri poet **Bilhana** in the eleventh century.

The **Yoga Vasistha Samhita** belongs here as a long philosophical text with a recorded structure. Popularly known as **Maha-Ramayana, Arsha Ramayana and Vasistha Ramayana**, it is dated around the **7th-15th century AD**, contains around **29,000 verses**, and is named after the sage **Vasistha**, mentioned and revered in the seventh book of the Rigveda. It is structured as a **discourse of Vasistha to Prince Rama** in six books: **Vairagya-prakaranam** (Rama's frustration with life and human suffering); **Mumukshuvayahara-prakaranam** (the desire for liberation and the need for self-effort); **Utpatti-prakaranam** (the birth of creation and of Rama's spiritual side); **Sthiti-prakaranam** (the nature of the world and non-dualism through stories); **Upashama-prakaranam** (meditation and the dissolution of false dualism); and **Nirvana-prakaranam** (the state of an enlightened Rama; also discusses Yoga). Its philosophical foundation is similar to **Advaita Vedanta**, particularly the **drsti-srsti** sub-school holding that "the whole world of things is the object of mind". **It was translated many times in the later period** - which is the thread picked up in Lesson 17, where its Persian translation is recorded.

### 7. The attribution problem, and the rule that solves it

This area is where attribution items are set, and the reason is structural: **many authors share a name-shape, many works share a plot, and several works exist in more than one version by different hands.** Four documented confusion sets:

| Confusion | The correct separation |
|---|---|
| **Charudatta / Mrichchhakatika** | Bhasa wrote **Daridra Charudatta**, described as a precursor; **Sudraka** wrote **Mrichchhakatika** |
| **Kiratarjuniyam** | by **Bharavi**; frequently offered against Magha, Bhasa and Bhavabhuti in items |
| **Vasavadatta** | **Subandhu** wrote the prose romance **Vasavadatta** in champu style; **Bhasa** wrote **Svapnavasavadattam**; and **Sudraka** is credited with a **Vinavasavadatta** |
| **Somadeva** | **Somadeva Bhatta** of Kashmir wrote the **Kathasaritsagara**; **Somadeva Suri** wrote the Jain **Upasakadyayana** and **Nitivakyamrita** |

**The rule:** never answer a work-author item by recognising a famous name. Recall the work's **content**, and check that the content matches the author's recorded profile. Bharavi's Kiratarjuniyam is about Arjuna and Shiva as a hunter; if the content does not match, the pairing is wrong regardless of how familiar the name looks.

### Examination use

Attribution pairings dominate, and this is the single highest-frequency area of the whole topic in objective terms. The defences are the content-first rule in section 7 and the four confusion sets. Chronology items ask which of several texts is earliest, typically across the Natyashastra, the Ashtadhyayi, the Sushruta Samhita, the Manusmriti, the Charaka Samhita and Sangam literature; the relevant anchors are distributed across Lessons 3, 12, 13, 14 and 16 and are assembled in the master chronology strip of Lesson 23. Identification items attack the poetics vocabulary and the four kavya styles. For Mains, this lesson supplies named textual evidence for almost any question on classical Indian culture, and the highest-value single observation is the Gupta statement that inscriptions of the period are themselves read as literature.

### Traps introduced here

- Pairing Mrichchhakatika with Bhasa; Bhasa wrote its precursor, Daridra Charudatta, and Sudraka wrote Mrichchhakatika.
- Confusing Subandhu's Vasavadatta, Bhasa's Svapnavasavadattam and Sudraka's Vinavasavadatta.
- Confusing Somadeva Bhatta of Kashmir with the Jain Somadeva Suri.
- Assigning Kiratarjuniyam to Magha or Shishupalavadha to Bharavi.
- Forgetting that Harshavardhana was himself an author - Ratnavali, Priyadarsika, Nagananda - and not only a patron.
- Treating the Harshacharita as a general history; it is described as the first historical biography in India.
- Forgetting that the Allahabad Pillar Inscription is a kavya in champu style as well as an inscription.
- Extending the classical period beyond 4th-12th century AD.
- Missing that Devichandraguptam survives only in fragments.
- Treating Maharana Kumbha only as a patron; three works and a commentary on the Gita Govinda are attributed to him.

### Revision notes

1. Classical Sanskrit Literature: 4th to 12th century AD; Sanskrit literature divides into Vedic and Classical.
2. Drama's stated purpose: **Lokaranjana** - diversion or entertainment for the public.
3. Four kavya styles: Mahakavya (formal epic), Khandakavya (short lyric on a single theme), Gadya Kavya (prose-poetry: Kadambari, Dashakumaracharita), Champu (mixed prose and verse: Vasavadatta by Subandhu).
4. Poetics vocabulary: alankara (figures of speech), rasa (emotion evoked), dhvani (suggested meaning), riti (style), vakrokti (indirect or creative expression), gunas (clarity, sweetness, vigour).
5. Bharata Muni's Natyashastra: foundational on drama, music and dance; known as the Fifth Veda; introduces Rasa, Bhava and stagecraft.
6. Sudraka's Mrichchhakatika: courtesan heroine, Brahmin lover, not mythological, realism and urban setting, early reference to Holi. Bhasa's Daridra Charudatta is its precursor.
7. Vishakhadatta: Mudrarakshasa on Chandragupta Maurya and Chanakya's diplomacy; Devichandraguptam on Chandragupta II, surviving only in fragments.
8. Bhavabhuti: Uttararamacharita (Rama after his coronation and reunion with Sita), Malatimadhava, Mahaviracharita.
9. Bhasa: among the earliest Sanskrit dramatists; Urubhanga's sympathetic Duryodhana; Svapnavasavadattam rediscovered in 1912 by T. Ganapati Sastri; also Pratima-nataka, Abhisheka-nataka, Panch-ratra, Madhyama-vyayoga, Duta-Ghattotkacha, Duta-Vakya, Karnabharam, Harivamsa/Bala-charita, Yagna-Phalam.
10. Kalidasa: Abhijnanashakuntalam (praised by Goethe), Malavikagnimitram (Agnimitra; mentions Pushyamitra's Rajasuya Yajna), Vikramorvashiyam (Pururavas and Urvashi), Kumarasambhavam, Raghuvamsha (Dileepa to Agnivarna), Meghaduta (Yaksha exiled by Kubera), Ritusamhara (six seasons).
11. Harshavardhana wrote Ratnavali (mentions Holi), Priyadarsika (Udayana and Priyadarsika, daughter of Dridhavarman) and Nagananda (Buddhist influence, self-sacrifice).
12. Banabhatta: Kadambari; Harshacharita, the first historical biography in India.
13. Harisena: the Allahabad Pillar Inscription / Prayag Prashasti, a eulogy of Samudragupta in champu style.
14. Bharavi - Kiratarjuniyam; Magha - Shishupalavadha; Sriharsa - Naishadhiyacharita; Bhatti - Bhattikavya (Ravanavadha), narrating Ravana's killing while illustrating grammar.
15. Bhartrhari - Vakyapadiya (grammar and philosophy of language, semantic unity) and Nitishataka (100 verses). Vatsyayana - Kamasutra and the Nyaya Sutra Bhashya.
16. Jain kavya: Nayachandra Suri's Hammira Mahakavya; Somadeva Suri's Upasakadyayana and Nitivakyamrita.
17. Scientific texts: Pingala-Chandashastra; Charak-Charaka Samhita; Sushruta-Sushruta Samhita; Madhava-Madhava Nidana; Varahamihira-Pancha-Siddhantika and Brihat-Samhita; Aryabhata-Aryabhatiya; Lagadha-Vedanga Jyotisa; Bhaskara II-Siddhanta Shiromani; plus Surya Siddhanta and the Yuktidipika commentary.
18. Arthashastra: the text names "Kautilya" or "Vishnugupta"; historians argue both were aliases for Chanakya at Chandragupta Maurya's court.
19. Gupta period: Golden Age; Kalidasa in Chandragupta II's court; Amarakosha by Amarsimha; Narada Smriti; Prayag Prashasti in champu; Bhittari, Junagadh and Udaygiri inscriptions show the glory of Gupta literature.
20. Medieval Sanskrit in Kashmir and Rajasthan: Kalhana's Rajatarangini; Somadeva Bhatta's Kathasaritsagara (11th century); Bilhana's Vikramankadevacharita on Vikramaditya VI.
21. Yoga Vasistha Samhita: also Maha-Ramayana, Arsha Ramayana, Vasistha Ramayana; c. 7th-15th century AD; around 29,000 verses; six books; Advaita-like, drsti-srsti sub-school; translated many times later.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is "the Allahabad Pillar Inscription is a source for Gupta political history" an incomplete description of that object in this topic?

**Model answer:** Because it describes only its **documentary** function and omits its **literary** one. The inscription is **Harisena's eulogy of Samudragupta**, composed as **a mixture of prose and verse in the champu style**, and it is listed among Harisena's works in the classical poetry corpus. The broader statement in the same source - that the Bhittari, Junagadh and Udaygiri inscriptions and many others **show the glory of the literature of the Gupta period** - makes the point general: Gupta epigraphy is read as a literary genre, not merely as a record. A complete description therefore says the object is simultaneously an inscription, a *prashasti* and a kavya in a named style, and notes that this is exactly the overlap Lesson 11 predicts between the two kinds of witness.

**Misconception to avoid:** That inscriptions and literature are separate categories of evidence. In this period the same composition can be commissioned as royal eulogy, executed as a poem in a named literary style, and cut into a pillar - so that classifying it under only one heading loses the feature that makes it distinctive.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Why do work-author attributions in classical Sanskrit literature generate so many errors, and what method reduces them? Answer with specific instances. (15 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**Four structural causes.**

**Shared plots.** Bhasa's **Daridra Charudatta** and Sudraka's **Mrichchhakatika** tell the same Charudatta-Vasantasena story, one being the precursor of the other. Two authors, one narrative.

**Shared name-elements.** Three separate works cluster around one heroine: Subandhu's prose romance **Vasavadatta** in champu style, Bhasa's **Svapnavasavadattam**, and the **Vinavasavadatta** credited to Sudraka.

**Shared personal names.** **Somadeva Bhatta** of Kashmir wrote the **Kathasaritsagara**; **Somadeva Suri** wrote the Jain **Upasakadyayana** and **Nitivakyamrita**. Likewise **Mahapurana** belongs to Pushpadanta in Apabhramsha and to Jinasena in Sanskrit, and **Adipurana** to Jinasena, to Gunabhadra and to Pampa in Kannada.

**Shared genre neighbourhoods.** Kiratarjuniyam and Shishupalavadha are both heroic mahakavyas, so **Bharavi** and **Magha** are interchangeable to a candidate who recalls only the type.

**The method.** Recall the **content first**, then test it against the author's profile. Kiratarjuniyam is Arjuna's encounter with **Shiva disguised as a hunter** - therefore Bharavi. Shishupalavadha is **Krishna killing Shishupala** - therefore Magha. Where four options are offered and three are a poetics treatise, a dramaturgical treatise and a grammatical commentary, the fourth is the only **play**, so a question about a playwright is settled by genre without recalling the title.

**The residual risk.** Some pairs cannot be resolved by content, only by record; there the honest answer names what is securely attributed and stops.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 15: **8** for the four causes at 2 each, each requiring a named instance; **4** for the content-first method demonstrated on two worked cases; **2** for the genre-elimination technique; **1** for acknowledging the residual class that content cannot resolve. Listing confusable pairs without the method caps at 8.

The right-hand column is the one candidates neglect. Each of these canons has a **sectarian fact** that determines what counts as scripture, and that fact is far more examinable than any individual title.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

These four corpora are usually learned as four lists of titles, which makes them interchangeable in memory. They are not interchangeable in structure. Each has a **canonical core**, a **language attached to that core**, a **non-canonical periphery in other languages**, and a **sectarian dispute about the core's authority**. Learn those four slots for each tradition and the titles attach themselves.

A boundary note: **doctrine** in these texts belongs to the religion and philosophy topics. What is owned here is the **corpus** - its divisions, languages, schools and transmission.

### 2. Buddhist literature

**During the post-Vedic period, apart from Sanskrit, literature was also composed in Prakrit and Pali.** **Gautama Buddha used Ardha Magadhi and Pali to preach his sermons** - a formulation that should be read alongside the hedges recorded in Lesson 4 rather than instead of them.

**Buddhist literature divides into canonical and non-canonical literature. The canonical literature consists of the *Tripitaka* or baskets of knowledge, written in Pali: the Vinaya Pitaka, the Sutta Pitaka and the Abhidhamma Pitaka.**

| Text | Details, school and language |
|---|---|
| **Vinaya Pitaka** | rules of **monastic discipline for monks and nuns**; divided into **Suttavibhanga, Khandhaka and Parivara**. School: Theravada. Language: Pali |
| **Sutta Pitaka** | over **10,000 discourses (*suttas*)** of the Buddha on ethics, meditation, philosophy and social duties; the **moral foundation** of Buddhism; divided into **five Nikayas**: **Digha Nikaya** (long discourses), **Majjhima Nikaya** (middle-length), **Samyutta Nikaya** (thematically grouped), **Anguttara Nikaya** (numerically arranged), **Khuddaka Nikaya** (shorter texts, including the **Dhammapada**, the **Jataka tales** and the **Sutta Nipata**). School: Theravada. Language: Pali |
| **Abhidhamma Pitaka** | highly analytical and philosophical; mental phenomena, consciousness, causality, ethics, epistemology; includes **7 books** such as **Dhammasangani** and **Patthana**. School: Theravada. Language: Pali |
| **Jataka Tales** | **550+ stories** of the Buddha's previous births as a Bodhisattva; illustrate truth, generosity and patience; **the last 10 Jatakas depict the 10 *paramitas*** (perfections). School: Theravada (core). Languages: Pali and Sanskrit. **Nidanakatha** is a narrative of the Jataka tales explaining the Buddha's previous lives and spiritual journey |
| **Buddhacharita** | epic biography of the Buddha by **Ashvaghosha**, an early Mahayana thinker; **poetic classical Sanskrit**, blending narrative and philosophy. School: early Mahayana. Language: Sanskrit |
| **Milinda Panho** | dialogue between the Greek king **Milinda (Menander)** and the monk **Nagasena**; explores non-self, rebirth and karma through **logical reasoning and parables**. School: Theravada. Language: Pali |
| **Dipavamsa** | the **oldest Sri Lankan chronicle**, about the 3rd-4th century CE; describes the Buddha's **three visits to Sri Lanka**, the spread of Buddhism and the arrival of the Buddha's tooth relic. School: Theravada. Language: Pali |
| **Mahavamsa** | **fifth-century epic by Mahanama**; chronicles Sri Lankan kings, the Buddha's visits and the introduction of Buddhism to Ceylon by **Mahinda**; **follows the Dipavamsa**. School: Theravada. Language: Pali |
| **Mahavastu** | text of the **Mahasamghika Lokottaravada** school; a mix of Jataka tales, Avadanas, miracles and divine elements; important for early Mahayana development. **Language: mixed - Sanskrit, Pali, Prakrit** |
| **Lalitavistara Sutra** | Mahayana text portraying the Buddha's life as a **divine play**; focuses on miracles and supernatural qualities; **ends with the first sermon at Sarnath**. School: Mahayana. Language: Sanskrit |
| **Udana** | among the earliest Theravada texts, part of the **Khuddaka Nikaya**; short inspirational verses including the famous **"Blind Men and the Elephant"** parable. School: Theravada. Language: Pali |
| **Bodhi-Vamsa** | **12th-century** poetic text narrating the journey of the **Bodhi tree from India to Sri Lanka**; composed by **Upatissa**. School: Theravada (Sri Lanka). Language: **Sanskritised Pali** |
| **Mulamadhyamakakarika** | central text of the **Madhyamika** school by **Nagarjuna**; focuses on **Shunyata (emptiness)**, rejecting extremes. School: Mahayana (Madhyamika). Language: Sanskrit |
| **Abhidharma Mahavibhasa Sastra** | written in **150 CE**; major scholastic work of the **Sarvastivada** tradition; basis of the **Vaibhasika** school; discusses phenomenology, causality and critiques other doctrines. Language: Sanskrit |
| **Visuddhimagga** | doctrinal and meditation manual by **Buddhaghosa**, **5th century**; focuses on **morality (sila), concentration (samadhi) and wisdom (prajna)**; still authoritative in Theravada. Language: Pali |
| **Mahavamsatika** | Theravada; a **Pali chronicle by Sri Lankan monks** detailing the island's history and the spread of Theravada Buddhism |
| **Divya Vadana** | compiled in the **2nd century CE** by **Dharmaraksita**; a collection of **miraculous stories (*avadanas*)** illustrating Buddhist morals and teachings |
| **Commentary on the Sarvastivada Vinaya** | **Sanghabhuti**, a **4th-century Indian monk of the Sarvastivada school**, wrote a commentary on the **Sarvastivada Vinaya** |

Three structural observations are worth more than the titles. First, **the Pali canon is Theravada**, and the great Sanskrit texts - Buddhacharita, Lalitavistara, Mulamadhyamakakarika, the Mahavibhasa - belong to Mahayana and Sarvastivada traditions. Language tracks school. Second, **the Mahavastu mixes Sanskrit, Pali and Prakrit in one text**, which is the single most useful counter-example to any one-text-one-language assumption in this whole lesson. Third, **the chronicle tradition is Sri Lankan** - Dipavamsa, then Mahavamsa following it, then Mahavamsatika, then the Bodhi-Vamsa - and the Dipavamsa is the text that supplied George Turnour with the identification of Piyadasi as Ashoka in Lesson 9. A Pali chronicle from Sri Lanka thus completed the decipherment of an Indian script, which is a striking closed loop worth stating in an answer.

### 3. Jain literature

**Apart from writing in Prakrit and Ardha Magadhi, Jain monks wrote in many other languages depending on the era, region and patrons who supported them. They wrote in Tamil during the Sangam Age in South India. They also wrote in Sanskrit, Shauraseni, Gujarati, Kannada and Marathi.**

The corpus divides into **canonical or religious texts called Jain Agamas or Agam**, and **non-canonical literary works**, which in Jainism include texts called the **Niryuktis and Samhitas**, written by **Bhadrabahu, the last acharya before the split in the Jain Sangha**.

**The Agamas.** Sacred texts said to be the teachings of the Jain tirthankaras, **originally compiled by the Ganadharas, the chief disciples of Mahavira**. **The present Angas are said to have been recompiled in a council of monks of the Svetambara sect held under the leadership of Acharya Shraman Devardhigani, in Vallabhi in Gujarat, during the mid-5th century AD. The Digambara sect believes that the original teachings were lost long ago, and they reject the authority of the Agamas compiled in Vallabhi.**

That last sentence is the sectarian fact for Jainism, and it is the most examinable single item in this section, because it determines what each sect counts as scripture.

**The Agamas comprise 45 texts**, structured as follows:

| Division | Number | Function |
|---|---:|---|
| **Angas** | 11 | the core; teach **reverence for all forms of life, strict codes of vegetarianism, asceticism, compassion and non-violence** |
| **Upangas** | 12 | **explanation of the Agamas** |
| **Prakirnaka-sutras** | 10 | texts on **miscellaneous subjects** |
| **Mulasutras** | 4 | **guiding texts for the early days of monks** |
| **Chedasutras** | 6 | texts on the **conduct and behaviour of monks and nuns** |
| **Chulikasutras** | 2 | **enhance the meaning of the Angas** |

They were **written in the Ardhamagadhi Prakrit language**. **The Digambaras gave sacred status to two works: the Karmaprabhrita (Discussion on Karma), also called the Shatkhandagama, and the Kashayaprabhrita** - the Shatkhandagama being the Digambara text recorded in Lesson 4 as written in Shauraseni.

The major Jain authors and texts:

| Author | Text(s) | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| **Bhadrabahu** | **Uvasaggaharam Stotra**, **Kalpa Sutra** | 3rd century BCE monk; **last Shrutkevali** (one with full knowledge of Jain scriptures); teacher of **Chandragupta Maurya**; migrated to **Shravanabelagola**; pioneer of the Digambara sect; the Uvasaggaharam Stotra is a sacred hymn to **Parshvanatha** believed to remove obstacles; the **Kalpa Sutra** contains **biographies of the Tirthankaras** and is recited during **Paryushana**, a Svetambara festival |
| **Acharya Kundkund** | **Samayasara**, **Niyamasara** | early Digambara philosopher; Samayasara on the purity of the soul and the distinction between **Jiva** and **Ajiva**; Niyamasara on ethical conduct and spiritual discipline for liberation |
| **Samantabhadra** | **Ratnakaranda Sravakachara**, **Aptamimansa** | 2nd-century Digambara thinker; Ratnakaranda outlines the ethical duties of Jain householders, emphasising **12 vows (*vratas*)**; Aptamimansa defends Jainism using logic, introduces **Syadvada** (conditional predication) and refutes rival schools |
| **Acharya Umaswami / Umasvati** | **Tattvarthasutra** | written between the **2nd and 5th century CE**; **the only Jain text accepted by both Digambara and Svetambara sects**; composed in **Sanskrit**; deals with logic, metaphysics, karma theory, ethics and cosmology; outlines the **Seven Tattvas** |
| **Jinasena** | **Mahapurana**, **Adipurana** | 8th-9th century CE Digambara monk, disciple of **Virasena**; Adipurana describes the life of **Rishabhanatha**, the first Tirthankara; Mahapurana presents Jain cosmology, philosophy and biographies of the Tirthankaras |
| **Haribhadra Suri** | **Saddarsanasamuccaya**, **Samaraiccakaha** | 5th-6th century CE Svetambara scholar, **pioneer in comparative philosophy**; Saddarsanasamuccaya compares Jainism with six other Indian schools; Samaraiccakaha uses didactic stories to explain karmic causality and rebirth, promoting **anekantavada** |
| **Hemachandra Suri** | **Parishishtaparvan** | 12th-century Svetambara polymath in Sanskrit and Prakrit grammar, lexicography, poetry, prosody and philosophy; adviser to the Chaulukya ruler **Kumarapala**; earned the title **Kalikalasarvajna**, omniscient of the Kaliyuga; key role in the Jain revival in Gujarat; Parishishtaparvan depicts the history of Jainism and royal dynasties. He is also credited with **Siddha-Hema-Sabdanushasana** and the **Dvyasraya Mahakavya** under Kumarapala |
| **Subhachandra** | **Pandavapurana** | a Jain version of the **Mahabharata**, portraying the Pandavas through a non-violent Jain ethical lens |
| **Tirutakkatevar** | **Civaka Cintamani** | written around the **10th century CE**; one of the **Five Great Epics of Tamil literature (*Aimperumkappiyam*)**; the life of the warrior-prince Jivaka from sensuality and conquest to renunciation and Jain liberation |
| **Jain monks** | **Nalatiyar** | ancient Tamil Jain moral text; **400 quatrains** with ethical maxims; reflects Jain impact on early Tamil didactic literature |
| - | **Valaiapathi** | one of the Five Great Tamil Epics; **now lost except for fragments**; narrates prince Valaiapathi's life and renunciation |
| - | **Vagavatisutra** | Jain scripture on conduct, discipline and philosophy for monks and lay followers |
| Traditional, based on Mahavira's teachings | **Nigantha Nikaya** | early centuries BCE; an early Jain scripture **within Buddhist Nikayas**, dealing with Jain doctrines and monks' conduct |

Two institutional facts close the section: **the universities of Vallabhi and Kalinga were important centres of learning for Jains before they began to decline**; and **between the 9th and 12th centuries Jain monks wrote extensively in Kannada - Pampa, Ponna and Ranna, the three gems of Kannada literature, are the famous writers related to Jainism.** With the revival of Hinduism and the spread of the Lingayats in Karnataka, **the popularity of Jainism declined and a reduction in literary works was seen after the 12th-13th century.**

The single most useful analytical observation available here: **Jain literature is the most multilingual corpus in this session.** Ardhamagadhi for the canon, Shauraseni for the oldest Digambara text, Sanskrit for the one text both sects accept, Apabhramsha for Pushpadanta's Mahapurana, Tamil for two of the five great epics and the Nalatiyar, and Kannada for the three gems. A single religious tradition writing across six languages over a millennium is the strongest possible evidence that in India religious affiliation did not determine literary language.

### 4. Zoroastrian literature

**Zoroastrianism refers to the religion developed from the teachings of the Persian prophet Zoroaster or Zarathushtra.** Its Zoroastrian name is **Mazdayasna**, combining **Mazda** with the Avestan word **yasna**, meaning worship or devotion. According to scholars, **Zoroastrianism was the first religion to believe in angels, a day of judgement, a demonic figure, and a battle between the forces of good and evil.** **During the reign of the Sassanid Empire in Iran, the religion went through reforms and many texts were written and re-interpreted.**

**The most important text is the Avesta**, a collection of texts dealing with religious beliefs, practices and instruction, written and compiled over a period of time. **It was written in the Avestan language, which is now extinct** and **is similar to Sanskrit**. It was **compiled in its final form during Sassanian rule, probably in the 4th century AD.**

Its structure:

| Part | Content |
|---|---|
| **Yasna** | a collection of texts with **72 chapters**, of great importance; **Yasna also refers to the most important ceremony of the faith** |
| **Gathas** | **five chapters containing 17 hymns**, within the Yasna; **the most revered**, and supposed to be **written by Zoroaster himself** |
| **Vendidad / Videvdad** | **laws against the demons** |
| **Yashts** | **praise as a form of worship** |
| **Khordeh Avesta** | a book on **daily prayers** |

Two important texts outside the Avesta: **Denkard**, a collection of books containing various aspects of the faith, regarded as the **Encyclopaedia of Zoroastrianism**; it **does not have a divine status** and was **written in the 10th century AD**. And **Bundahishn**, literally **"Primal Creations"**, providing details of the theory of creation, containing astronomical ideas and theories, and mentioning the battles of **Ahura Mazda** and **Angra Mainyu**; **most of its chapters were written in the 8th and 9th centuries.**

The detail that **Avestan is similar to Sanskrit** is the linguistically significant one: it places the Avestan corpus in the same broad Indo-Iranian neighbourhood as Vedic Sanskrit, which is why the Zoroastrian material sits in an Indian literature chapter at all.

### 5. Sikh literature

**Sikhism was established in the 15th century and is based on the teachings of Guru Nanak.** Its beliefs and philosophies are written in the **Guru Granth Sahib**. **Gurbani is the composition and hymns of the Sikh Gurus and the Guru Granth Sahib.**

| Text | Record |
|---|---|
| **Adi Granth** | **compiled by Bhai Gurdas under the aegis of the fifth guru, Guru Arjan Dev, in 1604**; a **predecessor to the Guru Granth Sahib**; contains the teachings of the Sikh Gurus and **15 Bhagats of the Bhakti and Sufi traditions** |
| **Guru Granth Sahib** | the Adi Granth **further expanded in 1678 under the 10th guru, Guru Gobind Singh**; regarded as the **11th and final spiritual authority of the Sikhs**; **written in the Gurmukhi script**, in a language called **Sant Bhasha**; contains the teachings of Bhakti saints called **Bhagats** - **Ramananda, Namdev, Ravidas, Parmanand, Sain and Surdas**, and **two Muslim Bhagats, Kabir and Baba Farid** |
| **Dasam Granth** | believed to compile the hymns written by **Guru Gobind Singh**, though **many do not agree with this**; contains fables and Puranic stories; some hymns are recited during the daily prayers called **Nitnem**; **mostly written in Braj Bhasha**, with some parts in **Avadhi, Punjabi, Persian and Hindi** |
| **Janamsakhis** | books containing the mythological and exaggerated stories of **Guru Nanak**; the most popular is **Bhai Bala Janamsakhi**; others are **Miharban Janam-sakhi** and **Adi Janam-sakhi** |
| **Ardas** | a set of prayers recited during daily rituals in the Gurudwara, such as the opening or closing of the Guru Granth Sahib; contains **three parts**, the first being the **virtues of the 10 Sikh gurus** |
| **Five Banis** | five prayers from the Guru Granth Sahib and Dasam Granth recited daily: **Japji Sahib, Jaap Sahib and Tav-Prasad Saaiye** for morning recitals, and **Benti Chaupai and Anand Sahib** for evening recitals |

**Sant Bhasha** is the most important single concept here, and it deserves to be stated as a definition rather than a label: it is a composite language **containing words from Punjabi, Apabhramsa, Hindi, Braj Bhasha, Sanskrit, Khariboli and Persian**. A scripture written in a deliberately composite language, in a script standardised by the second Guru, containing the compositions of Hindu and Muslim devotional poets alongside those of the Sikh Gurus, is the single most compact piece of evidence in this session for literary and religious synthesis. That is why it appears in items as "the language of the nirguna mystics" and why the composite character is the answer rather than any single constituent language.

### 6. Comparative treatment: four different relationships between religion and language

| Tradition | The relationship |
|---|---|
| **Buddhist** | **language tracks school**: Pali for the Theravada canon, Sanskrit for Mahayana and Sarvastivada, mixed registers in transitional texts such as the Mahavastu |
| **Jain** | **language tracks region and patronage**: Ardhamagadhi canon, Shauraseni, Sanskrit, Apabhramsha, Tamil, Kannada, Gujarati and Marathi across eras and courts |
| **Zoroastrian** | **language is fixed and then dies**: the Avesta in Avestan, now extinct; later works such as Denkard and Bundahishn written centuries afterwards |
| **Sikh** | **language is deliberately composite**: Sant Bhasha drawing on seven named sources, in a script standardised for the purpose |

Four traditions, four different answers to the same question, and none of them is "a religion has one language". This table is the analytical payoff of the lesson and the block to reuse in any answer on religion and literature.

### Examination use

Objective items here attack: the three Pitakas and the five Nikayas; which Nikaya holds the Dhammapada and the Jataka tales; the school and language of a named text, where Mulamadhyamakakarika-Nagarjuna-Madhyamika and Visuddhimagga-Buddhaghosa-Theravada are standard; whether a named text is Jain or Buddhist, where the Parishishtaparvan is the classic Jain entry; the Tattvarthasutra as the only text both Jain sects accept; the Vallabhi council and the Digambara rejection; the Avesta's parts; and the compilation dates and authorities of the Adi Granth and Guru Granth Sahib. For Mains, the section 6 comparison and the Sant Bhasha evidence are the two most transferable blocks.

### Traps introduced here

- Assuming all Buddhist literature is in Pali; the Mahayana and Sarvastivada corpus is largely Sanskrit, and the Mahavastu mixes three languages.
- Placing the Dhammapada or the Jataka tales outside the Khuddaka Nikaya.
- Treating the Dipavamsa and Mahavamsa as Indian chronicles; they are Sri Lankan, and the Mahavamsa follows the Dipavamsa.
- Assuming both Jain sects accept the Agamas; the Digambaras reject the authority of those recompiled at Vallabhi.
- Forgetting that the Tattvarthasutra is the only Jain text accepted by both sects, and that it is in Sanskrit.
- Confusing Jinasena's Mahapurana and Adipurana with Pushpadanta's Apabhramsha Mahapurana of Lesson 4.
- Treating the Gathas as the whole Avesta; they are five chapters of 17 hymns within the Yasna.
- Giving Denkard divine status; the source expressly denies it.
- Confusing the Adi Granth (compiled by Bhai Gurdas under Guru Arjan Dev, 1604) with the Guru Granth Sahib (expanded in 1678 under Guru Gobind Singh).
- Describing Sant Bhasha as a dialect of one language; it is composite, drawing on Punjabi, Apabhramsa, Hindi, Braj Bhasha, Sanskrit, Khariboli and Persian.

### Revision notes

1. Buddhist literature: canonical (Tripitaka, in Pali) and non-canonical. Tripitaka = Vinaya, Sutta and Abhidhamma Pitakas.
2. Vinaya Pitaka: monastic discipline; Suttavibhanga, Khandhaka, Parivara. Sutta Pitaka: over 10,000 suttas; five Nikayas - Digha, Majjhima, Samyutta, Anguttara, Khuddaka. Abhidhamma Pitaka: 7 books including Dhammasangani and Patthana.
3. Khuddaka Nikaya holds the Dhammapada, the Jataka tales and the Sutta Nipata; the Udana is also part of it and contains the "Blind Men and the Elephant" parable.
4. Jataka tales: 550+ stories of previous births; the last 10 depict the 10 paramitas; Nidanakatha is their narrative frame.
5. Sanskrit Buddhist texts: Buddhacharita (Ashvaghosha, early Mahayana); Lalitavistara Sutra (Mahayana; ends with the first sermon at Sarnath); Mulamadhyamakakarika (Nagarjuna, Madhyamika, Shunyata); Abhidharma Mahavibhasa Sastra (150 CE, Sarvastivada, basis of Vaibhasika).
6. Mahavastu: Mahasamghika Lokottaravada school; mixed Sanskrit, Pali and Prakrit.
7. Milinda Panho: Menander and Nagasena, Pali, Theravada. Visuddhimagga: Buddhaghosa, 5th century, sila-samadhi-prajna.
8. Sri Lankan chronicles: Dipavamsa (oldest, c. 3rd-4th century CE, Buddha's three visits, tooth relic); Mahavamsa (5th century, by Mahanama, follows Dipavamsa, Mahinda); Mahavamsatika; Bodhi-Vamsa (12th century, by Upatissa, Sanskritised Pali).
9. Divya Vadana: 2nd century CE, compiled by Dharmaraksita, avadana stories. Sanghabhuti: 4th-century Sarvastivada monk, commentary on the Sarvastivada Vinaya.
10. Jain canon: Agamas in Ardhamagadhi Prakrit; 45 texts - 11 Angas, 12 Upangas, 10 Prakirnaka-sutras, 4 Mulasutras, 6 Chedasutras, 2 Chulikasutras.
11. Agamas originally compiled by the Ganadharas; Angas recompiled by the Svetambaras under Acharya Shraman Devardhigani at Vallabhi in the mid-5th century AD; the Digambaras reject that authority and hold the Karmaprabhrita / Shatkhandagama and Kashayaprabhrita sacred.
12. Bhadrabahu: last Shrutkevali, teacher of Chandragupta Maurya, migrated to Shravanabelagola; Uvasaggaharam Stotra and Kalpa Sutra (Tirthankara biographies, recited at Paryushana).
13. Tattvarthasutra by Umaswami/Umasvati: 2nd-5th century CE; in Sanskrit; the only Jain text accepted by both sects; Seven Tattvas.
14. Haribhadra Suri: comparative philosophy; Saddarsanasamuccaya and Samaraiccakaha; anekantavada. Hemachandra Suri: Kalikalasarvajna; adviser to Kumarapala; Parishishtaparvan, Siddha-Hema-Sabdanushasana, Dvyasraya Mahakavya.
15. Jain Tamil and Kannada: Civaka Cintamani by Tirutakkatevar; Nalatiyar's 400 quatrains; Valaiapathi, lost except fragments; Pampa, Ponna and Ranna as the three gems of Kannada. Vallabhi and Kalinga were Jain centres of learning.
16. Avesta: in Avestan, now extinct and similar to Sanskrit; compiled finally under Sassanian rule, probably 4th century AD. Yasna (72 chapters, also the name of the chief ceremony); Gathas (5 chapters, 17 hymns, supposed to be by Zoroaster); Vendidad/Videvdad (laws against demons); Yashts (praise); Khordeh Avesta (daily prayers).
17. Denkard: Encyclopaedia of Zoroastrianism; no divine status; 10th century AD. Bundahishn: "Primal Creations"; creation theory and astronomy; Ahura Mazda against Angra Mainyu; mostly 8th-9th centuries.
18. Adi Granth: compiled by Bhai Gurdas under Guru Arjan Dev, 1604; 15 Bhagats of the Bhakti and Sufi traditions. Guru Granth Sahib: expanded 1678 under Guru Gobind Singh; 11th and final spiritual authority; Gurmukhi script; Sant Bhasha.
19. Bhagats in the Guru Granth Sahib: Ramananda, Namdev, Ravidas, Parmanand, Sain, Surdas, and the Muslim Bhagats Kabir and Baba Farid.
20. Dasam Granth: attributed to Guru Gobind Singh though disputed; mostly Braj Bhasha with Avadhi, Punjabi, Persian and Hindi; some hymns recited at Nitnem. Janamsakhis: stories of Guru Nanak; Bhai Bala, Miharban and Adi Janam-sakhi. Ardas: three parts, the first on the virtues of the ten Gurus. Five Banis: Japji Sahib, Jaap Sahib, Tav-Prasad Saaiye (morning); Benti Chaupai, Anand Sahib (evening).

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the Jain corpus alone, show that religious affiliation did not determine literary language in India.

**Model answer:** The Jain canon is in **Ardhamagadhi Prakrit**; the oldest Digambara text, the **Shatkhandagama**, is in **Shauraseni**; the one text accepted by **both** sects, Umaswami's **Tattvarthasutra**, is in **Sanskrit** - the register that the tradition's Brahmanical rivals also used; **Pushpadanta's Mahapurana** is in **Apabhramsha**; two of the Five Great Tamil Epics, the **Civaka Cintamani** and **Valaiapathi**, together with the **Nalatiyar**, are in **Tamil**; and between the ninth and twelfth centuries Jain monks wrote extensively in **Kannada**, producing **Pampa, Ponna and Ranna**, the three gems of Kannada literature. The sources also state directly that Jain monks wrote in many languages depending on **era, region and the patrons who supported them**. Six or more languages across two families, over a millennium, for one religious tradition, is decisive: the determinants were region and patronage, not creed.

**Misconception to avoid:** That the language of a text identifies the religion of its author, or the reverse. Sanskrit carries Buddhist, Jain and Brahmanical texts; Tamil carries Jain, Buddhist and Shaiva-Vaishnava devotional literature; and the Guru Granth Sahib is written in a deliberately composite language containing Persian and Sanskrit alike.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Compare the canonical languages of the Buddhist and Jain traditions, and explain why neither establishes the speech of its founder. (10 marks, 150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

**The canons.** The **Tripitaka** - Vinaya, Sutta and Abhidhamma Pitakas - is in **Pali**, the canonical language of **Theravada**, for which Pali served as **lingua franca**. The Jain **Agamas**, 45 texts in six divisions, are in **Ardhamagadhi Prakrit**; the oldest Digambara text, the **Shatkhandgama**, is in **Shauraseni**.

**Why neither settles founder-speech.** The sources hedge deliberately: the Buddha is believed **not** to have spoken Pali but to have preached in Ardhamagadhi, and the Buddha and Mahavira **perhaps** spoke Ardhamagadhi. Canonical labels record **transmission**, not speech - which school wrote and preserved a corpus. The pattern confirms it: Mahayana and Sarvastivada texts are in **Sanskrit**, the **Mahavastu** mixes Sanskrit, Pali and Prakrit, and the Jain canon was **recompiled at Vallabhi** in the mid-fifth century AD by one sect and rejected by the other.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 10: **4** for the two canons with their languages and one named sub-text each; **4** for the transmission-not-speech argument, of which 2 require carrying the sources' own hedges; **2** for a corroborating case, the Mahavastu or the Vallabhi recompilation. Asserting that the Buddha spoke Pali forfeits the whole second band.

## Lesson 16 - Sangam and the Tamil corpus: powerful evidence with a hard boundary

### Visual: the Sangam corpus, structurally
