### 1. The learner's actual problem

Sangam material is examined in two completely different ways and candidates prepare for only one. The first is **structural**: what the anthologies are called, how many poems and poets, who chaired which assembly, which text is a grammar. The second is **evidentiary**: what Sangam poetry can and cannot be used to reconstruct. The second is where the Mains marks are, and it depends on a single well-known qualification that must be stated exactly rather than paraphrased.

A boundary note: the Sangam **age** as political and economic history - the Chera, Chola and Pandya polities, the ports, the trade - belongs to the ancient history topic. What is owned here is the **corpus**: its structure, its languages, its genres, its authorship and its evidentiary value.

### 2. What the corpus is

**"Sangam" means fraternity**, and this literature was very popular among the masses. It is **a collection of works containing approximately 2381 poems attributed to 473 poets**, with **a further corpus written by 102 poets who remain anonymous**. It is **the earliest known literature of South India**. It is **mainly preserved as the Eight Anthologies (*Ettuthokai*) and the Ten Idylls (*Pattuppattu*)**, and in total **around 30,000 lines of poetry** are arranged in these.

**The poets included men and women from different classes of society.** The literary tradition was so popular that **the period between 300 BC and 300 AD, during which most of these were composed and compiled, is often called the Sangam period.**

Two of those facts are more useful than they look. The **102 anonymous poets** are evidence that the corpus was compiled from a broader poetic culture than the named tradition preserves. And the statement that the poets included **men and women from different classes** is the basis for using Sangam poetry as social evidence at all - it is not a court literature written by a single class.

**The Eight Anthologies (Ettuthokai)** deal with various aspects of **love, war, ethics and nature**: **Ainkurunuru, Akananuru, Purananuru, Kalittokai, Kuruntokai, Natrinai, Paripadal and Pathitrupathu.**

**The Ten Idylls (Pattuppattu)** are **longer poems praising kings and describing landscapes, trade and urban life**, and include **Tirumurukarruppatai** and **Pattinappalai**.

**The two schools.** **The first is the *Aham* or *Agam*, the "inner field", which concentrates on the discussion of abstract human aspects such as love and sexual relations. The second is the *Puram* or "outer field", which discusses human experiences and emotions such as social life, ethics, valour and customs.**

That division is the organising principle of the whole corpus and the single most examinable structural fact in this lesson.

### 3. The three Sangams, with the caution attached

**There were three Sangams organised over a period of 600-700 years. However, conclusive historical accounts of the first two Sangams are not available. The first and second Sangams are considered legends and myths by many scholars.**

The table in the visual gives the full record. Three details carry disproportionate weight: **Kapatapuram** as the place of the second Sangam - the only one not at Madurai; **Tolkappiyar**, disciple of Agastya, as the second Sangam's chairman and the author of the **Tolkappiyam**; and **Nakkirar** as the third Sangam's chairman, who also appears in the poets table as an important commentator and as the author of **Tirumurukarruppadai**.

And the caution is the content, not a footnote. The tradition of three assemblies is part of the corpus's **self-description**, and the first two are regarded by many scholars as legendary. So the correct formulation in an answer is: "the tradition records three assemblies organised by the Pandyas, of which the first two are widely regarded as legendary and the third is associated with the surviving corpus."

### 4. Tolkappiyam

**The Tolkappiyam was written to elaborate on the nuances of Tamil grammar and poetry.** It is **acknowledged as the earliest surviving Tamil literary work** and was **written by Tolkappiyar, one of the 12 disciples of saint Agastya.** It is **a work on the grammar of spoken and written Tamil**. It **talks about the two-fold walks of life, the *aham* and *puram***, and it **also deals with the cultural history and geographical studies of ancient Tamil land.**

A grammar that also contains cultural history and geography is an unusual object, and that breadth is what makes Tolkappiyam a source as well as a rule-book. Its two recorded classificatory schemes are examined directly:

**The fivefold division of lands, based on the occupations of the people living in them:**

| Land | Occupation |
|---|---|
| **Kurinji** | hunting and honey collection |
| **Mullai** | cattle-rearing |
| **Marudam** | agriculture |
| **Neydal** | fishing and salt manufacturing |
| **Palai** | robbery |

**The four castes mentioned in Tolkappiyam:** **Arasar** (the ruling class), **Anthanars** (related to Sangam polity and religion), **Vanigars** (who carried on trade and commerce) and **Vellalas** (agriculturists).

The second table is the decisive one for a recurring examination demand, because it establishes that **social classification was known to the Sangam poets** - the corpus itself records a fourfold social division. That is a documented statement inside the corpus, not an inference.

### 5. The five great Tamil epics

| Epic | Author | Content and affiliation |
|---|---|---|
| **Silappadikaram** | **Ilango Adigal** | the **earliest Tamil epic**, described as a **palm leaf manuscript**; a tragic love story of the ordinary couple **Kannaki** and her husband **Kovalan**, and Kovalan's later love **Madhavi**; the title means **the tale of an anklet**, the anklet belonging to Kannaki being central; Kannaki, having lost her husband to **a miscarriage of justice at the court of the King of the Pandyan dynasty**, wreaks revenge on his kingdom; the **River Cauvery, the Sun, the Moon and the city of Poompuhar** are praised; written around the **5th-6th century**; believed to have **Jain elements** |
| **Manimekalai** | **Satanar** | composed probably around the **6th century**; an **anti-love story**, a **sequel** to Silappadikaram; the story of **Manimekalai, the beautiful daughter of Kovalan and Madhavi**, who was **a nun of Mahayana Buddhism**; believed to have **Buddhist elements** |
| **Civaka Cintamani** (The Jewelled Garland of Civaka) | **Tirutakkatevar** | the life of **Civakan**, an ideal hero skilled in arts and warfare, who later renounces worldly pleasures for salvation; **Jain influence**; emphasises **ascetic ideals** |
| **Valayapathi** | **unknown author** | on **domestic virtue, chastity and ethics**; **Jain influence**; **now lost except for fragments** |
| **Kundalakesi** | **Nathakuthanar** | the story of **Kundalakesi**, a woman who **kills her husband, repents, and becomes a Buddhist nun**; **Buddhist influence** |

The affiliation column is the analytically important one. **Three of the five great Tamil epics carry Jain influence and two carry Buddhist influence.** The foundational epic tradition of Tamil is therefore substantially **Jain and Buddhist**, which is a fact many candidates find counter-intuitive and which supports a strong, evidenced statement about the religious plurality of early Tamil literary culture.

**Silappadikaram and Manimekalai** are also described together as the Tamil counterparts of the twin Sanskrit epics, both **written in the 5th-6th century AD**, focusing on **Tamil society and the economic and political changes it was experiencing** - which is the sentence that licenses their use as social-historical evidence.

### 6. The poets

| Poet | Major work | Significance |
|---|---|---|
| **Kapilar** | Purananuru, Akananuru (many poems) | the **largest contributor to the Sangam corpus**; celebrated for **Puram themes** - war and generosity |
| **Nakkirar** | Puram poems; commentaries; **Tirumurukarruppadai** | important commentator and poet; contributed to the grammar and poetics tradition; Tirumurukarruppadai is a Sangam-era poem guiding devotees to **Murugan's shrines** and is described as the **earliest Tamil devotional work** |
| **Avvaiyar** | didactic and moral poems | **famous female poet**; wrote on ethics, values and everyday life |
| **Thiruvalluvar** | **Tirukkural**, **1,330 couplets** | a classic of Tamil ethical literature on **virtue (*aram*), polity (*porul*) and love (*inbam*)**; **traditionally linked to Sangam but probably post-Sangam** |

Two further named women poets of the Sangam period are recorded: **Nachchellaiyar** and **Kakkaipadiniyar**.

The **Thiruvalluvar** entry carries its own qualification and it must be preserved: the Tirukkural is **traditionally linked to Sangam but probably post-Sangam**, and it is described elsewhere as having been contributed to Sangam literature and as being divided into three parts discussing the epics, polity-governance and love. When an answer cites the Tirukkural as Sangam literature, the safe formulation is "traditionally associated with the Sangam corpus, though probably post-Sangam".

### 7. Dynastic patronage

| Dynasty | Rulers named | Literary contribution |
|---|---|---|
| **Chera** | Uthiyan Cheralathan, Senguttuvan | supported many Sangam poets; **Silappatikaram was written by Ilango Adigal during this period**, and the stories of **Kannagi / Pattini** became popular under their patronage |
| **Chola** | Karikala Chola | **Karikala was praised in Sangam poems such as Pattinappalai and Porunaraatrupadai**; poets wrote about his bravery, administration and ports, which encouraged Tamil literature to flourish |
| **Pandya** | Neduncheliyan, Mudukudumi Peruvazhi | **organised the Sangam assemblies at Madurai**, where poets from all over the Tamil regions gathered; **sponsored the poems of the Ettuthokai and the Pattuppattu** |
| **Pallava** | Mahendravarman I, Narasimhavarman I, Nandivarman | **encouraged both Tamil and Sanskrit works**; supported **Bhakti saints and scholars**; **the Sanskrit prose Dashakumara Charita by Dandin is linked to their court** |

The Pallava row is the one with direct cross-topic value, because it supplies the literary half of a bilingual patronage argument: a court **encouraging both Tamil and Sanskrit**, supporting **Bhakti saints and scholars**, with a named Sanskrit prose work by a named author linked to it, and - from Lessons 10 and 11 - **replacing Vatteluttu with the Pallava-Grantha script** and issuing **bilingual Tamil-Sanskrit inscriptions from the seventh century onwards**. Those four elements together make a complete, evidenced paragraph on Pallava literary patronage.

### 8. Recorded Sangam-period cultural practices

These come from inside the corpus and are therefore evidence of the strongest available kind - the texts describing their own society.

- **Hereditary monarchy** was the form of government; **the imperial court was known as *avai*.**
- **Silappadikaram refers to two types of councils: *Aimperunkulu*, the council of five members, the council of the ministers; and *Enperayam*, the great assembly of eight members, government officers.**
- **Pattinappalai refers to the custom officials employed in the seaport of Puhar.**
- **Tolkappiyam records the fivefold division of lands** and **the four castes**, as above.
- **The primary deity of the Sangam period was Seyon or Murugan.**
- **Singing bards in royal courts were called *Panar* and *Viraliyar***, the latter skilled in folk songs and folk dances. **Dancing was performed by *Kanigaiyar*.** **Koothu**, folk art or informal dance, was the most popular entertainment of the people.
- ***Vatakkiruttal* was a Tamil ritual of fasting till death during the Sangam age**: the Tamil kings were prepared to meet their death **facing North**, to save their honour and prestige, and **would never turn their backs in battle after defeat.**

The **Panar and Viraliyar** entry belongs with the oral-transmission thread of this session: court poetry in the Tamil south was carried by named classes of **singing bards**, which is the Tamil parallel to the *charana* custodianship of Lesson 12 and the Rajasthani bardic tradition of Lesson 19.

### 9. The Bhakti turn in Tamil

**The final turn came during the early medieval period when Vaishnava Bhakti sentiments began to colour Tamil literature. Texts composed between the 7th and 12th centuries were highly devotional in nature.** **In Tamil-speaking regions, the 12 Alvars or saint poets, immersed in the devotion of Vishnu or his avatar Krishna, wrote several texts. One of the Alvar saints was a woman called Andal.** **Another important Bhakti group was the Nayanars, who sang the praises of Shaivism.**

| Work | Author / saint | Theme and significance |
|---|---|---|
| **Nalayira Divyaprabandham** | the **12 Alvars** | a collection of **4,000 Tamil hymns** praising Vishnu and his avatars; **regarded as the Tamil Veda** |
| **Tevaram** | **Appar, Sambandar, Sundarar** | devotional hymns expressing love and surrender to **Shiva**; foundation of **Shaiva Siddhanta** |
| **Tiruvachakam** | **Manikkavachakar** | mystical hymns of spiritual surrender and divine love towards Shiva |
| **Periya Puranam** | **Sekkizhar** | 12th-century AD text of the Chola period, under **Kulottunga II**; a poetic account of the lives of the **63 Nayanars**; a masterpiece of Tamil literature, **ranked as the fifth Veda in Tamil** |
| **Kamba Ramayanam (Ramavataram)** | **Kambar** | Tamil version of the Ramayana, composed under the patronage of **Thiruvennai Nallur Sadayappa Vallal**; **references the patron's name once in every 1,000 verses** |
| **Tiruppavai** | **Andal** | **30 hymns** expressing devotion and longing for Vishnu; recited during the month of **Margazhi** |
| **Tirumurukarruppadai** | **Nakkirar** | Sangam-era poem guiding devotees to Murugan's shrines; **the earliest Tamil devotional work** |
| **Tirukkural** | **Thiruvalluvar** | didactic text on **aram, porul and inbam**; embodies universal ethics |
| **Tirumandiram** | **Tirumular** | philosophical and yogic text emphasising spiritual discipline and union with the divine |
| **Sittar poetry / hymns** | **Thayumanavar (1705-44)** | philosophical and devotional hymns based on **Shaiva Siddhanta**; **protested the caste system and temple-entry restrictions** |

Two entries repay attention. **The Kamba Ramayanam's patron-naming convention** - the patron's name appearing once in every thousand verses - is a precise, unusual and directly citable fact about how patronage was inscribed *inside* a literary text. And **Thayumanavar's Sittar poetry**, recorded as protesting the caste system and temple-entry restrictions, is the evidence for treating Tamil devotional literature as socially critical rather than only devotional.

### 10. The evidentiary boundary, stated exactly

This is the section that a Mains answer on Sangam literature must contain, and it must be stated with its two halves in balance.

```text
  WHAT SANGAM LITERATURE DOES WELL
  - portrays the SOCIAL and ECONOMIC conditions of its time with
    REMARKABLE VIVIDNESS
  - records material culture: seaports (Puhar), customs officials,
    trade, urban life, landscapes
  - records social classification directly: the FOUR CASTES in
    Tolkappiyam, the fivefold land division by occupation
  - records political institutions: hereditary monarchy, the avai,
    the five-member Aimperunkulu, the eight-member Enperayam
  - records warrior ethic and ritual: vatakkiruttal, facing north,
    never turning the back after defeat
  - records the performing culture: Panar, Viraliyar, Kanigaiyar, Koothu
  - written by men AND women from DIFFERENT CLASSES of society
                              |
  WHAT IT DOES NOT DO
  - it is NOT VERY USEFUL from the point of view of a CONNECTED
    POLITICAL HISTORY of South India
  - it is poetry organised by theme (aham and puram), not annals
    organised by reign
  - its compilation period (300 BC - 300 AD) is a compilation window,
    not a date of composition for each poem
  - the three-Sangam framework is the tradition's self-description;
    the first two assemblies are widely regarded as legendary
                              |
  THE CORRECT VERDICT SHAPE
  "Rich and vivid on society, economy, material culture and values;
   bounded as a source for connected dynastic chronology."
```

The wording in the left-hand box is deliberately close to the standard formulation, because that formulation is precise: **vividness about social and economic conditions** on one side, **limited usefulness for connected political history** on the other. An answer that reproduces both halves and then evidences each with named material from sections 5 to 8 has done the whole job.

### 11. Comparative treatment: Sangam against the Sanskrit classical corpus

| | **Sangam Tamil** | **Classical Sanskrit** |
|---|---|---|
| Organising principle | thematic: *aham* (inner) and *puram* (outer) | formal: drama against kavya, and four kavya styles |
| Authorship | 473 named poets plus 102 anonymous; men and women of different classes | largely named individual authors, court-attached |
| Institutional setting | assemblies (*sangamas*) organised by a kingdom | royal courts and patronage of individual poets |
| Evidentiary strength | social, economic and material life | court aesthetics, poetics, genre and technical knowledge |
| Shared limitation | both are layered corpora whose compilation dates differ from composition dates | |

The shared bottom row is the point at which the two traditions converge on the same discipline, and it is the bridge to Lesson 23.

### Examination use

Objective items here attack: the identity of Tolkappiyam as a **work of grammar**; the authorship of Manimekalai (**Satanar**) and Silappadikaram (**Ilango Adigal**); the river praised in Silappadikaram (**Cauvery**); the religious affiliation of a named epic; the Nalayira Divyaprabandham as the **Alvars'** compilation and the **Tamil Veda**, against Tevaram as the **Nayanars'** Shaiva collection; the practice named *vattakirutal* or *vatakkiruttal*; and the presence of **varna or social classification** in Sangam poetry, which the Tolkappiyam four-caste record settles. Note the spelling variation between *vatakkiruttal* and *vattakirutal* across sources; both refer to the same recorded practice of ritual fasting till death, and the variant is recorded rather than resolved. For Mains, section 10 is the answer spine, and sections 5 to 8 are the evidence bank.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating Sangam literature as a dynastic chronicle; its recorded limitation is precisely its lack of use for a connected political history.
- Treating 300 BC-300 AD as the composition date of each poem rather than the period of composition and compilation of most of the corpus.
- Accepting the three-Sangam framework as established history; the first two are widely regarded as legendary.
- Assigning Tolkappiyam to the wrong genre; it is a work of grammar that also covers cultural history and geography.
- Confusing Silappadikaram (Ilango Adigal, Jain elements, Kannaki and Kovalan, the anklet) with Manimekalai (Satanar, Buddhist elements, Manimekalai as a Mahayana nun, a sequel and an anti-love story).
- Assuming all five great Tamil epics are Hindu; three carry Jain influence and two Buddhist.
- Presenting the Tirukkural as securely Sangam; it is traditionally linked to Sangam but probably post-Sangam.
- Swapping Alvars and Nayanars, or attributing the Nalayira Divyaprabandham to the Nayanars.
- Claiming Sangam poetry contains no reference to material culture or to social classification; the ports, customs officials, four castes and fivefold land division are recorded inside the corpus.
- Attributing the Sangam assemblies to a dynasty other than the Pandyas.

### Revision notes

1. Sangam means fraternity; the Pandya kingdom organised the assemblies (*sangamas*); around 2381 poems, 473 named poets, plus 102 anonymous poets; around 30,000 lines; mostly composed and compiled between 300 BC and 300 AD.
2. Poets included men and women from different classes of society.
3. Two schools: Aham/Agam, the inner field - love and sexual relations; Puram, the outer field - social life, ethics, valour and customs.
4. Ettuthokai, the eight anthologies: Ainkurunuru, Akananuru, Purananuru, Kalittokai, Kuruntokai, Natrinai, Paripadal, Pathitrupathu. Pattuppattu, the ten idylls: longer poems praising kings and describing landscapes, trade and urban life; include Tirumurukarruppatai and Pattinappalai.
5. Three Sangams over 600-700 years: First at Madurai under Agastya or Shiva, no book survived, used Agattiyam as grammar; Second at Kapatapuram under Agastya then Tolkappiyar, around 2000 poems compiled into Tolkappiyam; Third at Madurai under Nakkirar, most of the existing corpus. The first two are widely considered legendary.
6. Tolkappiyam: earliest surviving Tamil literary work; by Tolkappiyar, one of the 12 disciples of Agastya; grammar of spoken and written Tamil; covers aham and puram; also cultural history and geography.
7. Fivefold land division: Kurinji (hunting and honey), Mullai (cattle-rearing), Marudam (agriculture), Neydal (fishing and salt), Palai (robbery).
8. Four castes in Tolkappiyam: Arasar, Anthanars, Vanigars, Vellalas.
9. Five great Tamil epics: Silappadikaram (Ilango Adigal, Jain elements, palm leaf manuscript, Kannaki and Kovalan, the anklet, Cauvery, Poompuhar, 5th-6th century); Manimekalai (Satanar, Buddhist, sequel and anti-love story, Manimekalai a Mahayana nun); Civaka Cintamani (Tirutakkatevar, Jain, ascetic ideals); Valayapathi (unknown author, Jain, lost except fragments); Kundalakesi (Nathakuthanar, Buddhist).
10. Poets: Kapilar the largest contributor, Puram themes; Nakkirar commentator and poet, author of Tirumurukarruppadai; Avvaiyar the famous female didactic poet; also Nachchellaiyar and Kakkaipadiniyar. Thiruvalluvar's Tirukkural has 1,330 couplets on aram, porul and inbam and is traditionally linked to Sangam but probably post-Sangam.
11. Patronage: Cheras (Uthiyan Cheralathan, Senguttuvan; Silappatikaram; Kannagi/Pattini); Cholas (Karikala praised in Pattinappalai and Porunaraatrupadai); Pandyas (assemblies at Madurai; sponsored Ettuthokai and Pattuppattu); Pallavas (both Tamil and Sanskrit; Bhakti saints; Dandin's Dashakumara Charita linked to their court).
12. Cultural practices: hereditary monarchy; the court called *avai*; Aimperunkulu of five ministers and Enperayam of eight officers in Silappadikaram; customs officials at Puhar in Pattinappalai; primary deity Seyon or Murugan; Panar and Viraliyar bards; Kanigaiyar dancers; Koothu as popular entertainment; *vatakkiruttal*, fasting till death facing north.
13. Tamil Bhakti: 12 Alvars for Vishnu, including the woman saint Andal; Nayanars for Shiva. Nalayira Divyaprabandham - 4,000 hymns, the Tamil Veda; Tevaram by Appar, Sambandar and Sundarar; Tiruvachakam by Manikkavachakar; Periya Puranam by Sekkizhar on the 63 Nayanars, under Kulottunga II, ranked the fifth Veda in Tamil; Kamba Ramayanam by Kambar, patron named once every 1,000 verses; Tiruppavai by Andal, 30 hymns for Margazhi; Tirumandiram by Tirumular; Thayumanavar's Sittar poetry protesting caste and temple-entry restrictions.
14. Evidentiary boundary: vivid on social and economic conditions, material culture, institutions and values; not very useful for a connected political history of South India.

### Concept check

**Question:** A question asks you to assess Sangam literature as a historical source. Write the two-clause verdict and name three pieces of evidence for each clause.

**Model answer:** The verdict is: **Sangam literature portrays the social and economic conditions of its time with remarkable vividness, but it is not very useful from the point of view of a connected political history of South India.** For the first clause: the **Pattinappalai's record of customs officials at the seaport of Puhar**, which is direct evidence of trade administration; **Tolkappiyam's fivefold division of lands by occupation** - Kurinji, Mullai, Marudam, Neydal and Palai - which is evidence of an ecological-occupational classification; and **Tolkappiyam's four castes** - Arasar, Anthanars, Vanigars and Vellalas - which shows social classification was known to the Sangam poets. For the second clause: the corpus is organised **thematically by *aham* and *puram*, not chronologically by reign**; the **period 300 BC to 300 AD is a composition-and-compilation window** rather than a set of poem dates; and the **three-Sangam framework is the tradition's own account**, with the first two assemblies widely regarded as legendary, so even the corpus's institutional history is not securely datable.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a source's limitation for one purpose weakens it for all purposes. The bounded verdict is not a concession; it is the precise claim. Sangam poetry is among the richest available evidence for early South Indian society, economy and values, and stating the boundary is what makes that richness credible.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Examine the internal organisation of the Sangam corpus and explain what that organisation reveals about how the corpus was formed. (15 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**The organisation.** The corpus is **thematic before it is anything else**. Its two schools are **Aham**, the inner field, on love and sexual relations, and **Puram**, the outer field, on social life, ethics, valour and customs. Its transmitted shape is two compilations: the **Ettuthokai**, eight anthologies - Ainkurunuru, Akananuru, Purananuru, Kalittokai, Kuruntokai, Natrinai, Paripadal, Pathitrupathu - and the **Pattuppattu**, ten longer idylls praising kings and describing landscapes, trade and urban life. Around **2381 poems** by **473 named poets**, with a further body by **102 anonymous poets**, fill around **30,000 lines**.

**What that reveals.** First, **compilation, not composition, produced the object**: poems were gathered into anthologies by theme and length, so the corpus's order is editorial. Second, **the poetic culture was wider than the surviving names**: 102 anonymous poets means the compilers drew on a general practice rather than a closed school. Third, **the social base was broad**: poets included **men and women from different classes**. Fourth, the tradition supplied its **own institutional story** - three **Sangams** convened by the **Pandyas**, at Madurai, Kapatapuram and Madurai again, chaired by Agastya, Tolkappiyar and Nakkirar - but conclusive historical accounts of the first two are unavailable and they are widely considered legendary.

**Consequence.** The corpus must be read as an **edited anthology with a traditional origin-story attached**, which is exactly why its window of 300 BC to AD 300 marks composition **and** compilation rather than dating any single poem.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 15: **6** for the organisation - the two schools, the two compilations by name, and the poem, poet and line counts, at 2 each; **6** for the four inferences at 1.5 each, the anonymous-poets inference being the discriminating one; **3** for the closing consequence about the compilation window. Listing the eight anthologies without an inference caps at 6.

## Lesson 17 - The Persianate court and the translation economy

Progress: 17/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Persian's arrival and function, the Sultanate and Mughal chronicle tradition, Sufi and Indo-Persian poetry, women authors at court, and translation as an organised cultural project

### Visual: the Persianate literary system, by function

```text
  MEMOIR AND FEMALE AUTHORSHIP - a distinct and examinable strand

    ISLAMIC WORLD; early Mughal conquests and geography
  TUZUK-I-JAHANGIRI - Jahangir's autobiography; art, flora, fauna,
    justice and statecraft
  HUMAYUN NAMA - GULBADAN BEGUM, half-sister of Humayun; a RARE
    FEMALE-AUTHORED historical work
  MUNIS-AL-ARWAH - JAHANARA BEGUM, daughter of Shah Jahan; life of
    Shaikh Muinuddin Chishti

    POSTHUMOUSLY; she was imprisoned by her father for her last 20 years
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

This period is usually learned as a list of chronicles with similar names - four different *Tarikh*s, two different *Alamgirnama*s, a *Padshahnama* and a *Shahjahannama* about the same reign. The names are genuinely confusable and the sources warn about one pair explicitly. The way through is to file each work by **function** - chronicle, political theory, poetry, Sufi discourse, memoir, translation - and then by **patron reign**, so that two works about the same reign are distinguished by who wrote them and what kind of text they are.

A boundary note: the political history of these reigns belongs to the medieval history topic, and so does the broader question of what Persian sources reveal about the spirit of their age. This session owns the **literary system**: the languages, the genres, the authorship and the translation practice.

### 2. Persian's arrival and function

**Several different trends emerged in the medieval period which affected the languages and dialects that emerged. The major change was the emergence of Persian as the writing language of the Delhi Sultanate and the Mughal courts.** **Although the roots of the Persian language are as old as Sanskrit, it came to India with the coming of the Turks and Mongols in the 12th century. It is during their rule that Persian became the mode of communication of the court.**

Two precision points. Persian's **age** and its **arrival in India** are separate facts, and the sources state the first specifically to prevent the inference that a language new to India is a young language. And the function claimed is **the mode of communication of the court** - an institutional, administrative function, exactly parallel to the position Sanskrit held as court language in the Gupta period. Persian, in the terms of Lesson 8, operated as a **lingua franca** of governance across a multilingual territory.

### 3. The Sultanate corpus

| Author | Work | Period and patronage | Significance |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Hasan Nizami** | **Taj-ul-Ma'asir** | early Delhi Sultanate (Qutb-ud-Din Aibak) | **the first Persian history of the early Delhi Sultanate**; glorifies Aibak's rule |
| **Minhaj-i-Siraj** | **Tabaqat-i-Nasiri** | Delhi Sultanate (Nasir-ud-Din Mahmud) | detailed chronicle of Muslim rule; an important source for the **Slave Dynasty** |
| **Amir Khusrau Dehlavi**, called **"Tuti-e-Hind", the Parrot of India** | **Divan**, **Nuh Sipihr**, **Duval Rani-Khizr Khan** | Delhi Sultanate; **served seven rulers** | famous Indo-Persian poet; **pioneer of Qawwali and Persian poetry**; the Divan is a collection of Persian lyrical poems on love, devotion and mysticism; **Nuh Sipihr is a Persian *masnavi* of 1318 praising Alauddin Khalji and describing India's cultural diversity, climate, language, flora and fauna**; Duval Rani-Khizr Khan is a romantic masnavi narrating the love story of **Khizr Khan and Duval Rani of Gujarat**, described as a tragic love poem |
| **Ziauddin Barani** | **Tarikh-e-Firoz Shahi**, **Fatwa-i-Jahandari** | Delhi Sultanate, Tughlaq period | key historian and **political thinker**; Tarikh-e-Firoz Shahi is a Persian chronicle of the Sultanate's history till Firoz Shah Tughlaq; **Fatwa-i-Jahandari is a political treatise on ideal Islamic kingship and the moral duties of rulers** |
| **Shams-i-Siraj Afif** | **Tarikh-i-Firoz Shahi** | Firoz Shah Tughlaq | describes **social, economic and welfare policies**; a major source for Tughlaq administration |
| **Isami (Abdul Malik Isami)** | **Futuh-us-Salatin** | Muhammad bin Tughlaq / Bahmani period | a **poetic chronicle**; shows the decline of the Delhi Sultanate and the rise of the Deccan states |
| **Shaikh Nizamuddin Auliya / Amir Hasan Sijzi** | **Fawaid-ul-Fuwad** | Delhi Sultanate | a collection of **Sufi discourses**; reflects **Chishti mysticism** |
| **Malik Muhammad Jayasi**, a **Sufi poet** | **Padmavat** | Sur period, **1540 AD** | a **Sufi romantic epic blending history and legend**, concerning Rani Padmini and Alauddin Khilji |

**Two works with almost the same name, by different authors, about the same ruler:** Barani's **Tarikh-e-Firoz Shahi** and Afif's **Tarikh-i-Firoz Shahi**. The discriminator is content and reach: Barani's is a chronicle of the Sultanate's history **till** Firoz Shah Tughlaq and he is also the author of a political treatise; Afif's describes **social, economic and welfare policies** and is the administrative source. This is the first of two name-collision pairs in this lesson.

**Amir Khusrau** deserves a separate note because he is the most heavily examined single figure of the period. He is recorded as **serving seven rulers**, as **"Tuti-e-Hind", the Parrot of India**, and as a **pioneer of Qawwali**. The work that most often appears in items is **Nuh Sipihr**, the masnavi of **1318** in which he praises **India's climate, its languages - notably Sanskrit - its arts and music and even its animals**. That is a Persian court poet writing a celebration of Indian languages including Sanskrit, and it is therefore the best single piece of evidence in this session against the idea that the Persianate court was culturally sealed off from the Indian traditions around it. He is also recorded in Lesson 18 as writing texts in Urdu in its nascent stage.

### 4. The Mughal corpus

| Author | Work | Reign | Significance |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Babur** | **Baburnama (Tuzk-e-Babri)** | Babur | autobiography; **the earliest in the Islamic world**; details early Mughal conquests and geography |
| **Gulbadan Begum**, half-sister of Humayun | **Humayun Nama** | Humayun | a **rare female-authored historical work**; records Humayun's life and struggles |
| **Abul Fazl** | **Akbarnama**, including the **Ain-i-Akbari** | Akbar | among Akbar's **Navaratnas**; detailed administration, economy and culture; the **official court chronicle** |
| **Faizi**, elder brother of Abul Fazl | **Nal-Daman**; translation of **Lilavati** of Bhaskaracharya | Akbar | one of Akbar's Navaratnas; **translated Sanskrit classics** - Indo-Persian cultural synthesis |
| **Haji Ibrahim Sirhindi** | translated the **Atharva Veda into Persian** | Akbar | part of **Akbar's translation projects at Fatehpur Sikri** |
| **Mohammad Arif Qandhari** | **Tarikh-i-Akbari (Muzaffar Nama)** | Akbar | the **first chronicle of Akbar's early reign** |
| **Nizamuddin Ahmad** | **Tabaqat-i-Akbari** | Akbar | general history of Muslim rulers up to Akbar; a reliable record |
| **Abdul Qadir Badauni**, a **critic of Akbar** | **Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh**; translation of the **Mahabharata (Razmnama)** | Akbar | translated many Sanskrit works while **opposing Akbar's religious policies** including Din-i-Ilahi |
| **Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khana** | Dohas; **Persian and Hindi poetry** | Akbar | one of Akbar's Navaratnas; **wrote in both Persian and Hindi**; blended Sufi and Bhakti ideals |
| **(illustrated manuscript)** | **Hamzanama**, the story of the Persian hero Amir Hamza | Akbar | **illustrated manuscript commissioned by Akbar**; showcases Mughal art and narrative tradition |
| **Muhammad Qasim Firishta** | **Tarikh-i-Firishta / Gulshan-i-Ibrahimi** | Deccan Sultanates (Bijapur, c. 1600 AD) | comprehensive history of Muslim India; valuable for the Deccan and North India |
| **Jahangir** | **Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri** | Jahangir | autobiography; rich detail on **art, flora, fauna, justice and statecraft** |
| **Inayat Khan** | **Shahjahannama** | Shah Jahan | chronicles Shah Jahan's reign; politics, architecture and court life |
| **Abdul Hamid Lahori** | **Padshahnama** | Shah Jahan | the **official chronicle** of Shah Jahan's reign - **not to be confused with the Shahjahannama** |
| **Jahanara Begum**, daughter of Shah Jahan | **Munis-al-Arwah**, the life of **Shaikh Muinuddin Chishti** | Shah Jahan | a tribute to a Sufi saint; shows **female authorship and spiritual devotion** |
| **Khawas Khan** | **Alamgirnama** | Aurangzeb, early years | official chronicle of Aurangzeb's first decade |
| **Mirza Muhammad Kazim** | **Alamgirnamah (continuation)** | Aurangzeb | continuation of the official history of Aurangzeb's rule |
| **Jafar Zatalli**, a satirist | **Kulliyat**, satirical poetry | Aurangzeb | known for **satire and humour** in Persian literature; **unique for its time** |
| **Saqi Mustad Khan** | **Maasir-i-Alamgiri** | Aurangzeb | a further chronicle of Aurangzeb's reign |
| **Zeb-un-Nissa**, Aurangzeb's eldest daughter | **Diwan-i-Makhfi** | Aurangzeb | a great poet; her writings were **compiled posthumously**; she was **imprisoned by her father during the last 20 years of her life** |

**The second name-collision pair** is explicitly flagged in the sources: **Inayat Khan's Shahjahannama** and **Abdul Hamid Lahori's Padshahnama** are both about Shah Jahan's reign and are **not to be confused**. The discriminator is that the **Padshahnama is the official chronicle**.

**Four women authors** appear in this corpus - **Gulbadan Begum, Jahanara Begum, Zeb-un-Nissa**, and, from the Nibandha tradition of Lesson 13, **Lakshmidevi and Mahadevi Dhiramati**. Naming them together, across two religious and linguistic traditions and two genres - history, hagiography, poetry and legal digest - is a far stronger evidenced claim about women's authorship than any general statement.

### 5. The translation economy

This is the analytical heart of the lesson, and the material supports a clean argument.

```text
  TRANSLATION AS AN ORGANISED COURT PROJECT
  Akbar's translation projects at FATEHPUR SIKRI
        |
  +-----+------------------+----------------------+------------------+
  |                        |                      |                  |
 FAIZI                  BADAUNI            HAJI IBRAHIM        NIZAMUDDIN
 translated             translated the      SIRHINDI           PANIPATI
 Bhaskaracharya's       MAHABHARATA as      translated the     translated the
 LILAVATI               the RAZMNAMA        ATHARVA VEDA       YOGAVASISTHA
 (mathematics)          (epic)              (Vedic corpus)     into Persian
                        while OPPOSING                         during the
                        Akbar's religious                      reign of AKBAR
                        policies
        |
  WHAT THIS SHOWS
  1. The range is deliberate: MATHEMATICS, EPIC, VEDIC SCRIPTURE and
     PHILOSOPHY were all translated - not a single genre.
  2. Translation was INSTITUTIONAL, located at a named place, not the
     private enthusiasm of one scholar.
  3. Participation did NOT require agreement: Badauni translated the
     Mahabharata while opposing the emperor's religious policy.
  4. It ran in BOTH DIRECTIONS across time: the PANCHATANTRA had already
     travelled OUT - translated into OLD PERSIAN in the 6TH CENTURY and
     into ARABIC in the 8TH CENTURY as KALILA WA DIMNA, a collection of
     fables featuring two jackals, Kalila and Dimna, as narrators and
     protagonists.
        |
  AND IT DID NOT STOP: the NATIONAL TRANSLATION MISSION now translates
  higher-education knowledge texts into all 22 Eighth Schedule languages
  (Lesson 8) - the same function, a different instrument, 400 years later.
```

Point three is the one that earns marks, because it is counter-intuitive and fully evidenced: **Badauni translated the Mahabharata into Persian as the Razmnama while being a recorded critic of Akbar's religious policies, including Din-i-Ilahi.** Translation at this court was therefore an **institutional programme** that could absorb participants who disagreed with the emperor's religious direction - which is a much more interesting claim than "Akbar promoted syncretism".

Point four is the other high-value move. The **Panchatantra's** journey - Sanskrit, then **old Persian in the sixth century**, then **Arabic in the eighth century as Kalila wa Dimna** - establishes that Indian texts were circulating westward in translation a thousand years before the Mughal projects. Translation is therefore not a Mughal innovation but a long-running feature of Indian textual culture.

### 6. Comparative treatment: two court literary systems

| | **Sanskrit court literature (Lesson 14)** | **Persianate court literature (this lesson)** |
|---|---|---|
| Dominant genres | drama, kavya, poetics, technical sastra | dynastic chronicle, political treatise, memoir, masnavi, Sufi discourse |
| Relation to the ruler | the ruler as patron, sometimes as author (Harshavardhana, Maharana Kumbha, Krishnadevaraya) | the ruler as patron, as subject of official chronicles, and as **author of his own memoir** (Baburnama, Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri) |
| Historical writing | rare and late: Banabhatta's Harshacharita as the first historical biography; Kalhana's Rajatarangini | central and continuous: a chronicle for almost every reign |
| Women authors | Lakshmidevi and Mahadevi Dhiramati in the Nibandha tradition | Gulbadan Begum, Jahanara Begum, Zeb-un-Nissa |
| Attitude to the other tradition | Sanskrit texts translated into Persian at court | Persian court poets praising Indian languages, notably Sanskrit (Amir Khusrau's Nuh Sipihr) |

The bottom two rows are where an answer becomes an argument. The two systems are not sealed: the Mughal court translated Sanskrit scripture and mathematics, a Persian court poet wrote a celebration of Sanskrit, and a Navaratna such as Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khana **wrote in both Persian and Hindi and blended Sufi and Bhakti ideals**. That is the evidence for treating medieval Indian literary culture as interactive rather than parallel.

### Examination use

Objective items here attack author-work pairings, of which the two name-collision pairs are the highest-risk; the identification of the **first** Persian history of the Sultanate (Taj-ul-Ma'asir) and the **first** chronicle of Akbar's early reign (Tarikh-i-Akbari by Arif Qandhari); which work of Amir Khusrau praises India's climate, languages, arts, music and animals (Nuh Sipihr); which scholar translated Lilavati (Faizi); who authored Munis-al-Arwah (Jahanara); which royal woman wrote a history (Gulbadan Begum's Humayun Nama); and under whose reign the Yogavasistha was translated into Persian by Nizamuddin Panipati (Akbar). For Mains, the translation-economy diagram in section 5 and the two-system comparison in section 6 are the blocks to carry; a question about medieval literary culture answered with Badauni's dissenting participation and Khusrau's praise of Sanskrit is evidenced rather than asserted.

### Traps introduced here

- Confusing Barani's Tarikh-e-Firoz Shahi with Afif's Tarikh-i-Firoz Shahi.
- Confusing Inayat Khan's Shahjahannama with Abdul Hamid Lahori's Padshahnama, which is the official chronicle.
- Attributing the translation of Lilavati to Abul Fazl rather than to Faizi.
- Treating Badauni as a supporter of Akbar's religious policies because he translated the Mahabharata; he opposed them.
- Forgetting that Amir Khusrau served seven rulers and was a pioneer of Qawwali.
- Dating the Padmavat loosely; it is recorded at 1540 AD in the Sur period.
- Treating Persian as new in the twelfth century; its roots are recorded as as old as Sanskrit, and it is its **arrival in India** that dates to the twelfth century.
- Assuming translation ran only from Sanskrit into Persian; the Panchatantra reached old Persian in the sixth century and Arabic in the eighth as Kalila wa Dimna.
- Attributing the Alamgirnama to a single author; Khawas Khan wrote it and Mirza Muhammad Kazim continued it, and Saqi Mustad Khan separately wrote the Maasir-i-Alamgiri.

### Revision notes

1. Persian's roots are as old as Sanskrit; it came to India with the Turks and Mongols in the 12th century and became the mode of communication of the court.
2. Hasan Nizami's Taj-ul-Ma'asir: the first Persian history of the early Delhi Sultanate, under Qutb-ud-Din Aibak.
3. Minhaj-i-Siraj's Tabaqat-i-Nasiri: important source for the Slave Dynasty.
4. Amir Khusrau, "Tuti-e-Hind", served seven rulers; pioneer of Qawwali; Divan, Nuh Sipihr (masnavi of 1318, praising Alauddin Khalji and India's diversity, climate, languages, flora and fauna), Duval Rani-Khizr Khan.
5. Ziauddin Barani: Tarikh-e-Firoz Shahi and Fatwa-i-Jahandari on ideal Islamic kingship and rulers' moral duties. Shams-i-Siraj Afif: Tarikh-i-Firoz Shahi on social, economic and welfare policies.
6. Isami's Futuh-us-Salatin: poetic chronicle; decline of the Sultanate and rise of the Deccan states.
7. Fawaid-ul-Fuwad: Sufi discourses of Shaikh Nizamuddin Auliya recorded by Amir Hasan Sijzi; Chishti mysticism.
8. Jayasi's Padmavat, 1540 AD, Sur period: Sufi romantic epic blending history and legend.
9. Baburnama (Tuzk-e-Babri): the earliest autobiography in the Islamic world. Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri: art, flora, fauna, justice, statecraft.
10. Gulbadan Begum's Humayun Nama; Jahanara Begum's Munis-al-Arwah on Shaikh Muinuddin Chishti; Zeb-un-Nissa's Diwan-i-Makhfi, compiled posthumously after twenty years of imprisonment.
11. Abul Fazl's Akbarnama including the Ain-i-Akbari - the official court chronicle; Nizamuddin Ahmad's Tabaqat-i-Akbari; Arif Qandhari's Tarikh-i-Akbari, the first chronicle of Akbar's early reign; Badauni's Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh.
12. Akbar's translation projects at Fatehpur Sikri: Faizi on Lilavati; Badauni's Razmnama from the Mahabharata; Haji Ibrahim Sirhindi on the Atharva Veda; Nizamuddin Panipati's Yogavasistha into Persian.
13. Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khana wrote in both Persian and Hindi, blending Sufi and Bhakti ideals.
14. Hamzanama: illustrated manuscript commissioned by Akbar, the story of Amir Hamza.
15. Firishta's Tarikh-i-Firishta / Gulshan-i-Ibrahimi, Bijapur, c. 1600 AD.
16. Shah Jahan: Inayat Khan's Shahjahannama and Abdul Hamid Lahori's Padshahnama, the official chronicle - do not confuse.
17. Aurangzeb: Khawas Khan's Alamgirnama and Mirza Muhammad Kazim's continuation; Saqi Mustad Khan's Maasir-i-Alamgiri; Jafar Zatalli's satirical Kulliyat.
18. Kalila wa Dimna: Arabic fables featuring the jackals Kalila and Dimna; a translation of the Panchatantra, which went into old Persian in the 6th century and into Arabic in the 8th.

### Concept check

**Question:** What does Badauni's participation in Akbar's translation projects establish about the nature of those projects?

**Model answer:** It establishes that they were **institutional rather than ideological**. Badauni is recorded as a **critic of Akbar**, opposing the emperor's religious policies including Din-i-Ilahi, yet he **translated many Sanskrit works**, including the Mahabharata as the **Razmnama**. If the programme had been a vehicle for the emperor's personal religious project, a known opponent of that project would not have been one of its principal translators. The better description is that a **court translation bureau located at Fatehpur Sikri** commissioned work across mathematics (Faizi on Lilavati), epic (Badauni's Razmnama), Vedic scripture (Haji Ibrahim Sirhindi on the Atharva Veda) and philosophy (Nizamuddin Panipati on the Yogavasistha), and that scholars participated as professionals whose religious views were not a condition of employment.

**Misconception to avoid:** That cultural exchange at a court implies agreement among the participants. Translation here was an organised knowledge-transfer programme with a broad genre range, and its most productive contributors included a documented dissenter - which makes it stronger evidence of institutional practice than any statement of imperial intent would be.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Assess the Mughal translation programme as an institution, considering its location, range and participants. (20 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**Location.** The programme was **situated**, not ad hoc: Akbar's translation projects were carried on at **Fatehpur Sikri**, which gives it a place, a patron and a standing character.

**Range.** Its genre spread was deliberate and unusually wide. **Faizi** translated **Bhaskaracharya's Lilavati**, a work of **mathematics**. **Badauni** produced the **Razmnama** from the **Mahabharata**, an **epic**. **Haji Ibrahim Sirhindi** translated the **Atharva Veda**, a work of **scripture**. **Nizamuddin Panipati** rendered the **Yogavasistha** into Persian, a work of **philosophy**. Four genres, four translators: this is a knowledge-transfer programme, not a devotional gesture.

**Participants.** The decisive evidence. **Badauni was a recorded critic of Akbar**, opposing his religious policies including Din-i-Ilahi, and nonetheless translated many Sanskrit works. Participation therefore did **not require agreement** with the patron's religious direction - which is what distinguishes an institution employing professionals from a personal project.

**Context.** Translation was not a Mughal invention. The **Panchatantra** had already travelled into **old Persian in the sixth century** and into **Arabic in the eighth** as **Kalila wa Dimna**. Nor did it end: the **National Translation Mission** now renders higher-education knowledge texts into all **22** scheduled languages. The court programme sits in the middle of a long institutional series.

**Verdict.** An organised, located, multi-genre translation bureau whose staffing crossed religious-political lines - stronger evidence of institutionalised cultural exchange than any statement of imperial intent.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 20: **3** for locating the programme at a named place under a named patron; **8** for the four genres with their translators at 2 each, the genre label being required as well as the name; **5** for the Badauni dissent argument, which is the band most answers miss; **4** for placing the programme in the long series between the Panchatantra's westward journey and the National Translation Mission. Praising Akbar's tolerance without the staffing evidence caps at 9.

```text
 +------------------------------------+------------------------------------+
 | FORMATION                          | THE DECISIVE PUSH                  |
 | Linguists have DEBATED that it     | Its BIGGEST BOOST came with the    |
 | developed through the INTERACTION  | BHAKTI MOVEMENT, which SHUNNED     |
 | OF PERSIAN AND HINDI, especially   | Sanskrit as the language of the    |
 | in the BARRACKS OF THE TURKISH     | Brahmins because the common people |
 | ARMY                               | did not use it; writers started    |
 |                                    | using THE LANGUAGE OF THE PEOPLE   |
 +------------------------------------+------------------------------------+
 | COMPOSITION                        | THE WIDER TURN                     |
 | Follows the GRAMMAR OF HINDI and   | From the 12TH CENTURY ONWARDS a    |
 | the FORM AND SCRIPT OF PERSIAN     | SHARP RISE in regional languages:  |
 |                                    | BENGALI, HINDI, MARATHI, GUJARATI  |
 +------------------------------------+------------------------------------+
 | EARLY NAME                         | THE FIRST BOOK - with its hedge    |
 | Used by the BAHMANI STATES of      | PRITHVIRAJ RASO by CHAND BARDAI    |
 | AHMADNAGAR, GOLCONDA, BIJAPUR      | was "PROBABLY the first Hindi      |
 | and BERAR, so initially also       | book"; documents Prithviraj        |
 | called DAKKANI (southern)          | Chauhan and the challenges he faced|
 +------------------------------------+------------------------------------+
 | WHAT MUST NOT BE WRITTEN           | WHAT MUST NOT BE WRITTEN           |
 | "Urdu is a foreign / Muslim        | "Vernacular literature REPLACED    |
 | language."                         | Sanskrit."                         |
 | The sources class it as INDO-ARYAN | The recorded claim is an EXPANSION |
 | and BORN IN INDIA, on the          | of literary publics, and Sanskrit  |
 | structure of Khari Boli.           | and Persian continued alongside.   |
 +------------------------------------+------------------------------------+
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Both these languages have a formation story that is politically charged, and the charged version is the one people absorb first. The sources are unusually careful here, and the care is the content: Urdu is classified **linguistically**, its formation is stated as something **linguists have debated**, and the "first Hindi book" claim carries the word **probably**. An answer that reproduces those three qualifications is both more accurate and more impressive than one that does not.

### 2. Urdu: classification before narrative

**Linguists have debated that Urdu developed through the interaction of Persian and Hindi, especially in the barracks of the Turkish army. Linguistically it is Indo-Aryan, born in India and built on the same basic structure of Hindi (Khari Boli).**

Take that apart, because the order of the clauses matters. The **classification** comes first and is stated flatly: Indo-Aryan, born in India, on the structure of Khari Boli. The **formation account** comes second and is explicitly marked as debated. So the secure claim is the classification; the barracks account is a scholarly proposal, not a settled fact.

**The language mostly follows the grammar of Hindi and the form and script of Persian.** That single sentence is the most efficient description of Urdu available and should be memorised verbatim: **grammar from one source, form and script from another.** It is also a compact restatement of the whole script-versus-language argument of Lessons 9 and 10 - two languages can share a grammatical structure and differ in the writing system and the literary form they adopt.

**As it was used by the Bahmani states of Ahmadnagar, Golconda, Bijapur and Berar, it was initially also called Dakkani (southern).** That fact matters twice: it names the four Deccan states, and it explains the dialect entry of Lesson 5, where **Dakhini** appears as the southern variant of Urdu alongside **Rekhta**, the older or poetic form.

**Amir Khusrau also wrote several texts in Urdu, which was in its nascent stage during this period** - the link back to Lesson 17, and the reason Khusrau appears in both.

### 3. Urdu's poets, patrons and modern turn

| Figure | Record |
|---|---|
| **Mirza Ghalib (1797-1869)** | one of the greatest Urdu poets; composed a **Diwan**, a collection of poetry, in Urdu |
| **Sauda, Dard and Mir Taqi Mir** | other notable Urdu poets |
| **Iqbal** | a major figure in twentieth-century Urdu literary writing; wrote **Bang-e-Dara**; famous for **"Sare Jahan se Achcha"**, which has become a celebrated nationalistic song |
| **Bahadur Shah Zafar** | among the last Mughal emperors who patronised Urdu; himself **a noted poet** |
| **The Nawabs of Awadh** | patronised several scholars who composed in Urdu |
| **Sir Syed Ahmad Khan** | in the twentieth century **gave Urdu an uplift as a moderniser**; wrote several **didactic and nationalistic texts in Urdu and English**; published the journal ***Tahdhib-ul-Akhlaq***, which propagated progressive ideas such as **the improvement of the position of women through education**, and **opposed purdah and polygamy** |

The Sir Syed entry is the most useful in an answer, because it links a language to a **social reform programme with named positions** - women's education, opposition to purdah and polygamy - carried through a named periodical. That is evidence, not assertion.

**The three genres** named for this tradition should be learned with their defining feature, because they are set as identification items:

| Genre | Defining feature |
|---|---|
| **Qaseeda** | has **a single presiding subject that is logically developed and concluded**; generally written **in praise of a king or a nobleman**; recited in **Arabic, Persian or Tajik by the Ismailis in Central Asia** |
| **Ginans** | **devotional hymns recited by the Nizari Ismaili Muslim communities (Khojas) in South Asia**; cover divine love, cosmology, rituals, eschatology, ethical behaviour and meditation; **originally oral renditions mostly by the Pirs**, later composed as texts; **based on verses from the Quran**; composed in **Gujarati, Urdu, Punjabi and Sindhi** |
| **Shahr Ashob** | an **ancient Urdu poetic genre in South Asia**; a **narrative poem** describing the writer's emotional thoughts; **widely used by poets during the Mughal Empire to express anguish and sorrow over political and social shifts** |

The **Ginans** entry is quietly one of the richest in this session: a devotional genre that was **originally oral**, later written down, and composed **in four different languages**. It belongs simultaneously to the oral-transmission thread, the multilingual thread and the devotional-literature thread. The **Shahr Ashob** is the one to cite whenever a question touches literature as a response to political change, because its recorded function is exactly that.

### 4. Hindi: descent, the Bhakti boost, and the vernacular turn

**Hindi as we know it today evolved between the 7th and 14th century from Apabhramsa, which evolved from Prakrit.**

**The language received its biggest boost with the Bhakti movement, which shunned the use of Sanskrit as it was the language of the Brahmins and the common people did not use it. Hence writers started using the language of the people, and from the 12th century onwards a sharp rise in regional languages such as Bengali, Hindi, Marathi and Gujarati was seen.**

That passage does three separate things, and separating them improves any answer built on it. It gives a **cause** - a devotional movement's deliberate rejection of a restricted learned register. It gives a **mechanism** - writers choosing the language of the people. And it gives a **general consequence** - a sharp rise not in one language but in **four named regional languages from the twelfth century**. The Bhakti vernacular turn is therefore a **pan-regional literary event**, not a Hindi event, which is why Lesson 19 finds the same mechanism at work in Bengal, Assam, Odisha, Gujarat, Kashmir, Punjab and Maharashtra.

**For a long time Hindi literature was in the shadow of its Sanskrit antecedents. *Prithviraj Raso*, written by *Chand Bardai*, was probably the first Hindi book, and it documents the life of Prithviraj Chauhan and the challenges faced by him.** The word **probably** is the source's own and must be carried; and, as Lesson 23 notes, the dating of the *Raso* is itself debated.

**The bulk of the work is poetry by Bhakti writers.**

| Poet | Works and record |
|---|---|
| **Kabir** | famous for his **dohas (couplets)**, used by the common people of India even today |
| **Tulsidas** | a Vaishnava saint, a **devotee of Rama**, who "became immortal by writing **Ramcharitmanas** in the **Awadhi** language"; other texts: **Vinaya Patrika, Krishna Gitavali, Kavitavali, Gitavali, Sahitya Ratna, Dohavali, Vairagya Sandipani** |
| **Surdas** | wrote **Sur Sagar**, about Krishna's infancy and his adolescent affairs with the gopis |
| **Rahim, Bhushan and Raskhan** | also wrote about devotion to Lord Krishna |
| **Mirabai** | famous as the woman who **renounced the world for Lord Krishna** and wrote bhakti poetry and bhajans for him |
| **Narayana Bhatta Goswami** | in the 16th-17th century wrote **over 50 bhakti-granthas**; his famous Vraja Mandal Parikrama guide, the ***Vraja Bhakti Vilasa***, states that he **discovered 333 forests and holy places of Sri Krishna** |
| **Bihari** | early 17th-century Hindi poet; **Satsai**, based on devotion and love, in the **Braj Bhasha** dialect |
| **Ashta-Chhapa** | a group of **eight poet-disciples of Vallabhacharya** who composed devotional songs in praise of Krishna, forming a key part of the **Pushtimarg Bhakti tradition in Braj Bhasha** |

Two linkages are worth making explicit. **Ramcharitmanas is in Awadhi**, the eastern Hindi dialect of Lesson 5 - so the most famous work of Hindi devotional literature is in a named dialect rather than in the standard variety, which is a direct rebuttal of the idea that a literary language must be the standard one. And **Braj Bhasha** carries the Satsai, the Ashta-Chhapa's devotional songs, Mirabai's writings as recorded in Lesson 19, and most of the **Dasam Granth** of Lesson 15 - a dialect described in Lesson 5 as "noted for its melodious quality and literary significance", with four separate bodies of literature to prove it.

### 5. Comparative treatment: two languages, one region, different components

| | **Urdu** | **Hindi** |
|---|---|---|
| Family position | Indo-Aryan, born in India | Indo-Aryan, from Apabhramsha, from Prakrit |
| Structural base | the same basic structure as Hindi (Khari Boli) | Khari Boli as the standard variant |
| Grammar | follows the grammar of Hindi | its own |
| Form and script | the form and script of Persian; the Urdu script, right to left, in Nastaliq | Devanagari |
| Formation period | debated; the nascent stage is already visible with Amir Khusrau | evolved between the 7th and 14th centuries |
| Decisive push | court and army contact; Deccan use under the Bahmani states, hence Dakkani | the Bhakti movement's rejection of Sanskrit in favour of the people's language |
| Named genres | Qaseeda, Ginan, Shahr Ashob | doha, and the devotional forms of the Bhakti poets |
| Modern reform figure | Sir Syed Ahmad Khan, with *Tahdhib-ul-Akhlaq* | Bharatendu Harishchandra and the Adhunik kaal phases of Lesson 20 |

Read across the table and the conclusion writes itself: **two languages sharing a structural base and a region, differentiated by script, literary form, genre repertoire and patronage network.** That is a much more precise statement than any claim about which language belongs to whom, and it is the statement the sources support.

### 6. The qualification that must close any vernacular-rise answer

The rise of the regional languages from the twelfth century is an **expansion of literary publics**, not a replacement. The evidence is in this session already: Sanskrit continued to produce major works in medieval Kashmir and Rajasthan (Lesson 14); Persian became the court language and produced a continuous chronicle tradition into the eighteenth century (Lesson 17); Sanskrit was compiled into digests and commentaries through the twelfth century and beyond (Lesson 13); and the Guru Granth Sahib was written in a composite language drawing on Sanskrit, Persian, Braj Bhasha and four others (Lesson 15). The correct formulation is therefore: **the vernacular turn added readerships and registers rather than subtracting them.**

### Examination use

Objective items attack the genre identifications - **Ginan as the literary genre developed by the Khojas, a branch of the Ismaili sect**, is the standard one - and the author-work pairings for the Bhakti poets, where **Sursagar as Surdas's work** and **the Ashta-Chhapa as the successors of Surdas of the Braj region** recur, as does the list of Tulsidas's works, which is tested by asking which of a set of titles he wrote. The **Vraja Bhakti Vilasa by Narayana Bhatta** is another recurring identification. For Mains, this lesson supplies the causal account of the vernacular turn, the named-genre vocabulary, and the closing qualification in section 6, which is the single most reliable way to convert a narrative answer into an analytical one.

### Traps introduced here

- Describing Urdu as a foreign or a religious language; the sources classify it as Indo-Aryan and born in India, built on the structure of Khari Boli.
- Dropping the word "debated" from the account of Urdu's formation in the Turkish army barracks.
- Dropping the word "probably" from the claim that the Prithviraj Raso was the first Hindi book.
- Treating Dakkani as a separate language rather than as the early southern name for Urdu, used by the Bahmani states of Ahmadnagar, Golconda, Bijapur and Berar.
- Assigning Ramcharitmanas to standard Hindi; it is in Awadhi.
- Describing the vernacular rise as the replacement of Sanskrit or Persian.
- Confusing the three named genres: Qaseeda has one presiding subject and praises a king or noble; Ginans are Nizari Ismaili devotional hymns in four languages; Shahr Ashob is a narrative poem of anguish over political and social change.
- Attributing the Ashta-Chhapa to the wrong tradition; they were eight poet-disciples of Vallabhacharya in the Pushtimarg tradition, associated with the Braj region and with Surdas.

### Revision notes

1. Urdu: linguistically Indo-Aryan, born in India, built on the same basic structure as Hindi (Khari Boli); linguists have **debated** that it developed through the interaction of Persian and Hindi, especially in the barracks of the Turkish army.
2. Urdu follows the grammar of Hindi and the form and script of Persian.
3. Initially also called **Dakkani** (southern) from its use by the Bahmani states of Ahmadnagar, Golconda, Bijapur and Berar. Dakhini and Rekhta are its named varieties.
4. Amir Khusrau wrote texts in Urdu in its nascent stage.
5. Urdu poets: Mirza Ghalib (1797-1869), who composed a Diwan; Sauda, Dard, Mir Taqi Mir; Iqbal, who wrote Bang-e-Dara and "Sare Jahan se Achcha".
6. Patrons: later Mughals including the poet-emperor Bahadur Shah Zafar; the Nawabs of Awadh.
7. Sir Syed Ahmad Khan: moderniser; didactic and nationalistic texts in Urdu and English; the journal *Tahdhib-ul-Akhlaq*; women's education; opposed purdah and polygamy.
8. Qaseeda: single presiding subject, logically developed and concluded; generally in praise of a king or nobleman; recited in Arabic, Persian or Tajik by Ismailis in Central Asia.
9. Ginans: devotional hymns of the Nizari Ismaili Khojas; divine love, cosmology, rituals, eschatology, ethics, meditation; originally oral renditions by the Pirs; based on verses from the Quran; in Gujarati, Urdu, Punjabi and Sindhi.
10. Shahr Ashob: ancient Urdu narrative poetic genre; widely used under the Mughals to express anguish over political and social shifts.
11. Hindi evolved between the 7th and 14th centuries from Apabhramsa, itself from Prakrit.
12. Its biggest boost came from the Bhakti movement, which shunned Sanskrit as the Brahmins' language; from the 12th century a sharp rise followed in Bengali, Hindi, Marathi and Gujarati.
13. Prithviraj Raso by Chand Bardai was **probably** the first Hindi book; it documents Prithviraj Chauhan.
14. Kabir's dohas; Tulsidas's Ramcharitmanas in Awadhi plus Vinaya Patrika, Krishna Gitavali, Kavitavali, Gitavali, Sahitya Ratna, Dohavali and Vairagya Sandipani; Surdas's Sur Sagar; Rahim, Bhushan and Raskhan on Krishna; Mirabai's bhakti poetry and bhajans.
15. Narayana Bhatta Goswami: over 50 bhakti-granthas; Vraja Bhakti Vilasa, stating he discovered 333 forests and holy places of Sri Krishna.
16. Bihari's Satsai, early 17th century, in Braj Bhasha. Ashta-Chhapa: eight poet-disciples of Vallabhacharya; Pushtimarg Bhakti tradition in Braj Bhasha.
17. The vernacular rise expanded literary publics; it did not replace Sanskrit or Persian.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is "Urdu follows the grammar of Hindi and the form and script of Persian" a better opening sentence for an answer on Urdu than any account of where it was first spoken?

**Model answer:** Because it states the **secure, classificatory** facts before the **debated, narrative** ones. The classification - Indo-Aryan, born in India, on the structure of Khari Boli, with Hindi grammar and Persian form and script - is recorded flatly; the account of formation in the barracks of the Turkish army is expressly something linguists have **debated**. Opening with the classification also does analytical work immediately: it shows Urdu as a composite of components drawn from different sources, which is exactly the frame needed to explain why it shares a structural base with Hindi while looking entirely different on the page, and it forecloses the common error of treating it as a foreign or religious language.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a language's identity is settled by its script or by the community most associated with it. Script is a separate variable, as Lessons 9 and 10 establish repeatedly, and the sources here classify Urdu by structure and origin - Indo-Aryan and born in India - not by the writing system it adopted.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Explain the linguistic composition of Urdu and account for its early name Dakkani. (10 marks, 150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

**Composition.** Urdu is **linguistically Indo-Aryan, born in India**, and built on the **same basic structure as Hindi (Khari Boli)**. It **follows the grammar of Hindi and the form and script of Persian** - its script a modification of the Persian alphabet, itself from Arabic, written right to left in the **Nastaliq** hand. Its formation through **Persian-Hindi interaction, especially in the barracks of the Turkish army**, is expressly recorded as a **debated** account and must be reported as such. **Amir Khusrau** already wrote in it at its **nascent stage**.

**Dakkani.** The name is **geographical and institutional**: the language was used by the **Bahmani states of Ahmadnagar, Golconda, Bijapur and Berar**, so it was initially also called **Dakkani**, meaning southern. **Dakhini** survives as a named southern variety alongside **Rekhta**, the older or poetic form.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 10: **4** for the classification - Indo-Aryan, born in India, Khari Boli base - forfeited in full if Urdu is described as foreign or as a religious language; **3** for the grammar-from-one-source, form-and-script-from-another formula; **1** for carrying the word "debated"; **2** for the Dakkani account naming at least two of the four Deccan states. Describing the script without the structural base caps at 4.
