### Visual: one mechanism, repeating across eleven regions

```text
  THE SHARED PATTERN - visible in every region below
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
  | 1. An EARLY ANCHOR: an inscription, or a first named text, often   |
  |    religious or grammatical, often JAIN or BUDDHIST or BHAKTI      |
  |                                |                                   |
  | 2. A DEVOTIONAL TURN: Bhakti (or Sufism, or a monastic order)      |
  |    shifts composition into the LANGUAGE OF THE PEOPLE              |
  |                                |                                   |
  | 3. A COURT PHASE: a dynasty patronises poets; sometimes the RULER  |
  |    HIMSELF WRITES                                                  |
  |                                |                                   |
  | 4. A PRINT-AND-PROSE PHASE: press, grammar, dictionary, journal,   |
  |    novel - and with it a NATIONALIST turn                          |
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
        |               |               |               |
    MALAYALAM       TELUGU          KANNADA         BENGALI
    pattu genre     Nannayya        Jain scholars   Charyapada
    Ramacharitam    Vijayanagara    Kavirajamarga   Mangal-Kavyas
    Ezhuthachan     ashtadiggajas   Ratnatraya      Serampore press
    Vallathol       Veereshalingam  Sarvajna        Tagore
        |               |               |               |
    ASSAMESE        ODIA            GUJARATI        RAJASTHANI
    buranjis        Sarala Das      Narsinh Mehta   Dingal & Pingal
    Sankardev       Panchasakha     Narmad          Dhola Maru
    Bezbaroa        Fakir Mohan     K.M. Munshi     bardic virkavya
        |               |               |               |
     SINDHI         KASHMIRI        PUNJABI         MARATHI
    Shah Abdul     Lal Ded          Nath Yogis      Mahanubhav Panth
    Latif          Habba Khatoon    Waris Shah      Jnaneshwar
    Shah Jo Risalo Mahjoor          Amrita Pritam   Tukaram, Ramdas
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Eleven regional literatures, each with a dozen names, is unmemorable as a list. It becomes manageable the moment you see that the **same four-stage mechanism repeats** in every region, with local variation in which stage is strongest. That is also what makes it answerable at Mains level: the regional detail becomes evidence for a single structural claim rather than a catalogue.

This lesson is organised in four Parts. Parts A to C take the regions; Part D assembles the patronage ledger that underlies all of them.

### Part A - The southern literary publics

**Malayalam.** Spoken in Kerala and surrounding areas. Two major medieval works are **Kokasandisan** and **Bhasa Kautilya**, described as **a commentary on the Arthashastra**. **Ramacharitam**, an epic poem written by **Cheeraman in the 12th century**, is another major work; **Thirunizhalmala**, "Garland of the Sacred Shade", and **Ramacharitam** are **two epic poems written in old Malayalam**, belonging to the ***pattu* genre** of Malayalam poems and described as **the earliest literary works of Malayalam**. **Ezhuthachan**, a strong proponent of the Bhakti movement, is known as **the Father of the Malayalam Language**. In the twentieth century, **Vallathol Narayana Menon** was **one of the triumvirate poets of modern Malayalam poetry, along with Kumaran Asan and Ulloor S. Parameshwara Iyer**; his magnum opus is the **Mahakavya Chitrayogam**.

Note the pattern instantly: a commentary on a Sanskrit classic, a *pattu*-genre epic, a Bhakti figure named as the father of the language, and a modern triumvirate. Four stages in four sentences.

**Telugu.** **Linguists have argued that Nannayya, of the 11th century AD, was the first poet in Telugu; he translated the Mahabharata from Sanskrit to Telugu.** **The oldest Telugu inscriptions are the Tippalar inscription and the Bhodampudu inscription, from the 7th century AD** - to be read with the alternative statement recorded in Lesson 11.

**Telugu reached its zenith during the Vijayanagara period, which is known as the Golden Age of Telugu Literature. It is also called the Prabandha Age** because poets such as **Allasani Peddana** wrote kavyas popularly known as ***Prabandhas***. One of the most successful works of the period is **Uttaraharivamsam**, composed by **Nachana Somanatha**, a well-known court poet of **King Bukka I**. **Not only court poets but some kings composed exceptional poetry, such as Krishnadevaraya (1509-29), who wrote *Amuktamalyada*.**

**The ashtadiggajas.** Eight learned Telugu poets were attached to Krishnadevaraya's court and were called the **ashtadiggajas**. The sources name **seven** of them, introduced with the words that, out of the eight, "the following deserve special attention"; the eighth name is therefore **not recorded here** and is listed among the declared gaps. The seven, with their works:

| Poet | Work(s) |
|---|---|
| **Allasani Peddana**, also known as **Andhra Kavita Pitamahudu** | **Manu Charitra**; also recorded as **Swaarochisha Manu Sambhavam** |
| **Nandi Thimmana** | **Parijatapaharanamu**, based on a story of the Harivamsam; also **Vanivilasam** |
| **Tenali Ramakrishna**, a **court jester and poet**, said to have been a duo with the king, whose stories are circulated even in modern times | **Panduranga Mahatmayam**, **Ghatikachala Mahatyam** (a Vaishnava devotional text) and **Udbhataradhya Charitramu** (a Shaivite text) |
| **Ramaraja Bhushanudu**, also known as **Bhattumurti** | **Vasucharitram**, **Narasabhupaleeyamu**, **Harishchandra Nalopakhyanam**, **Kavyalankarasangrahamu** |
| **Madayyagari Mallana** | **Rajasekharacharitra**, about the love and war of King Rajasekhara of the kingdom of Avanti |
| **Ayyalaraju Ramabhadrudu** | **Ramabhudayamu** |
| **Pingali Surana** | **Raghava Pandaveeyam**, describing both the Ramayana and the Mahabharata simultaneously; and **Kalapurnodayam**, the **first original poetic novel in Telugu** |

**Krishnadevaraya** himself is recorded as **a scholar and patron of many languages - Telugu, Kannada, Tamil and Sanskrit**. He **patronised the Kannada poets Mallanarya, Chatu Vittal-anatha and Thimmana**, and the Kannada saint **Vyasatirtha was his Rajaguru**. **Krishnadevarayana Dinachari in Kannada** is one of his famous works. **While travelling via Vijayawada during his Kalinga campaign he himself wrote *Amuktamalyada*, a treatise stating the instance of Lord Vishnu in his dream.** **He also wrote treatises in Sanskrit: *Madalasa Charita*, *Satyavadu Parinaya*, *Rasamanjari* and *Jambavati Kalyana*.** **He patronised the Tamil poet Haridasa.**

That paragraph is the single best-evidenced patronage case in this session and it is directly examinable. **A ruler who wrote in Telugu and Kannada, composed treatises in Sanskrit, and patronised Kannada and Tamil poets** is a four-language cultural programme attested by named works - and it is exactly the kind of evidence a Mains answer on multilingual patronage requires. Note also the recorded caution from a past demand: **Dhurjati and Siddheshvara are names that appear near this court in examination material; the eight recorded here are the ones the sources name**, and an answer should use the recorded list rather than an expanded one.

**Twentieth-century modern Telugu literature was shaped by K. Veereshalingam Pantalu, who wrote the first Telugu novel, *Rajasekhara Charitramu*.**

**Kannada.** **The Jain scholars made the first foray into Kannada literature. The best example of a Jain-influenced text is *Dharmanathapurana* by Madhava, on the life of the 15th tirthankara.** **Kannada is the second oldest language in South India.** **The oldest Kannada inscription is the *Halmidi inscription* from the 5th century AD** - again to be read with the alternative statement in Lesson 11. Another Jain scholar, **Uritta Vilasa, wrote *Dharma Parikshe***.

**One of the first recorded texts in Kannada is *Kavirajamarga*, written by Amoghavarsha I in the 9th century**, who was **a very powerful Rashtrakuta king**; he also authored **Prashnottara Ratnamalika in Sanskrit**.

**The three gems or *Ratnatraya*:**

| Gem | Period | Works and record |
|---|---|---|
| **Pampa** | 9th-10th century AD | better known as the **Father of Kannada**; wrote **Adipurana**, in **Champu** style, and **Vikramarjuna Vijaya**, also known as **Pampa Bharatha**; renowned for his mastery over the **rasa** involved in poetic composition; attached to the court of **Chalukya Arikesari** |
| **Ponna** | 9th-10th century AD | wrote **Shanti Purana**, a biography of the 16th Jain Tirthankara Shantinath; attached to the court of the **Rashtrakuta king Krishna III**; bestowed with the title **Ubhaya Kavichakravarthi** for his command of both Sanskrit and Kannada |
| **Ranna** | 10th-11th century AD | wrote **Ajitha Purana**, on the life of the 2nd Jain Tirthankara Ajitanatha, and **Sahasa Bhima Vijaya**, also known as **Gada Yuddha**; also at Krishna III's court |

**Although Kannada became a full-fledged language by the 10th century, the growth of Kannada literature has been closely attributed to the patronage of the Vijayanagara Empire.** Once the literature flourished, grammatical works followed, such as ***Shabdamanidarpana* by Keshiraja**. Quasi-religious texts were composed: **Narahari composed *Torave Ramayana*, inspired by the Valmiki Ramayana and said to be the first story on Rama in Kannada**; and **Lakshmisha wrote *Jaimini Bharata***.

**What sets Kannada literature apart was its close association with the people who read it.** **The title of the people's poet was given to *Sarvajna* of the 16th century, who composed *tripadi*, three-line poems.** **Honnamma, the first poetess of some repute in Kannada, wrote *Hadibadeya Dharma*, the Duty of a devout wife.** And **Madana Vijaya, or Kabbigara Kava, was written by Andayya - the first pure Kannada text without any Sanskrit word.**

The Andayya entry is unusually interesting. A text defined by the **deliberate exclusion of Sanskrit vocabulary** is a conscious linguistic-political act inside a literary tradition, and it is the sharpest single illustration in this session that the relationship between Sanskrit and a regional language could itself become a literary subject.

### Part B - The eastern literary publics

**Bengali.** **Charyapada, a collection of mystic songs of the 10th and 11th centuries, is considered the oldest literary document of the Bengali language. This was discovered by the Bengali scholar Haraprasad Shastri in the Nepal Durbar Library in 1916.**

That single sentence is a complete manuscript-discovery story and belongs with Lesson 21: the oldest literary document of a major Indian language survived **outside India**, in a palace library in Nepal, and was **recovered in the twentieth century**. Survival is contingent, and recovery is an event with a date and a named scholar.

**Although a lot of ancient and medieval literary works existed in Bengali before the 19th century, such as the Mangal-Kavyas, they had not been widely published.** The **Mangalkavya tradition** included **Chandimangala by Mukundaram Chakraborty, Manasamangal by Bipradas Pipilai, Dharmamangal by Mayuramukha, Annadamangal by Bharatchandra Ray, and Shibamangal by Rupchand Pakhi**. **These poems praised deities such as Chandi, Manasa, Dharma, Annapurna and Shiva, reflecting devotion, morality and rural faith.** **Shivasankirttan by Rameshwar Bhattacharya** is a Bengali devotional work praising Shiva. **Chakrapanidatta, an 11th-century Ayurvedic scholar from Bengal, wrote *Bhanumati*, a renowned commentary on the Sushruta Samhita.** **Ramcharita was written by Sandhyakar Nandi, a 12th-century poet in Bengal, narrating the story of King Ramapala of the Pala dynasty using the Ramayana as an allegory** - a text that tells **two stories simultaneously**, which is the feature by which it is identified.

**The distribution of this literature was facilitated by the establishment of the Baptist Mission Press at Serampore, Bengal, by the Englishman William Carey in 1800. Carey was also responsible for writing a book about the grammar of Bengali and publishing an English-Bengali dictionary. His press inspired wealthy local Bengalis to open their own presses and disseminate literature in Bengali.**

| Figure | Record |
|---|---|
| **Raja Ram Mohan Roy (1772-1833)** | among the first to write in Bengali and English; his work was widely read; **laid the real foundation of Bengali prose** and **heralded the Indian Renaissance** |
| **Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar (1820-91)** and **Akshay Kumar Dutta (1820-86)** | social reformers whose writings **revealed the rich potential of the Bengali language** |
| **Bankim Chandra Chatterjee (1838-94)** | the **zenith of nationalistic Bengali literature**; his **Anandamath** was hugely popular, and **Vande Mataram, the national song, is an excerpt from that novel** |
| **Rabindranath Tagore (1861-1941)** | **the first Indian, and the first Asian, to win a Nobel Prize**, for his Bengali masterpiece **Gitanjali**, in **1913**; **both the national anthems of India and Bangladesh were written by him** |
| **Michael Madhusudan Dutt** | **successfully inculcated European forms into Bengali poetry**, as in his epic poem **Meghnad Badh Kavya**, based on an episode of the Ramayana concerning the demise of **Meghnad, son of Ravana**, published in **1861**; his **Tilottama Sambhava Kabya (1860) was the first Bengali poem written in blank verse** |
| **Sarat Chandra Chatterjee, Qazi Nazrul Islam and R.C. Dutt** | further important contributors |

**Assamese.** **In the medieval period Assamese literature was dominated by *buranjis*, court chronicles.** Apart from these official works, **Sankardev in the 15th-16th century composed devotional poetry in Assamese**, and **Kirtana Ghosha by Sankardeva praises Lord Krishna and spreads the message of Bhakti.** In modern Assamese literature the major names are **Padmanath Gohain Baruah, Lakshminath Bezbaroa, Jyoti Prasad Agarwalla, Arupa Patangia and Parismita Singh.**

The *buranji* entry is worth isolating: a regional literature whose **medieval dominant genre is the court chronicle** is unusual in this session, and it is the closest Indian parallel outside the Persianate tradition to the systematic historical writing of Lesson 17.

**Odia.** **The first major work came from Sarala Das in the 15th century.** **In the medieval period one of the outstanding writers was Upendra Bhanja, who wrote in the early 18th century.** **Jaydev was one of the best-known poets of Odia literature, most known for his Gita Govinda (Geet Govindam in Sanskrit). Every night in the Jagannatha temple of Puri, the Gita Govinda of Jayadeva is sung in the style of Odissi music, a tradition that has continued unbroken since the time of Jayadeva himself.** **Odia literature was enhanced by the contributions of the five poets popularly known as *Panchasakha*: Jagannath Das, Balaram Das, Achyutanda Das, Yasovanta Das and Ananta Das.** In the modern period, **Radhanath Ray and Fakir Mohan Senapati** made their mark with the nationalistic tenor of their work.

The Gita Govinda entry is the best available case in this session of a **text embedded in a living ritual and musical practice**: a Sanskrit devotional poem of Lesson 14, sung nightly in a temple in a named regional music style. Literature here is not only read; it is performed on a daily liturgical schedule.

### Part C - The western and northern literary publics

**Gujarati.** **The Bhakti movement was at its peak in Gujarat, and that affected the literature composed there. Narsinh Mehta of the 15th century is a very popular name in this area, as he combined devotional songs for Lord Krishna with local folk traditions.** The later period saw **poetry by Narmad in the 19th century** and **prose by Govardhanram Madhavram Tripathi (19th-20th century), who wrote the classic Gujarati novel *Saraswatichandra***. **The pinnacle of success in Gujarati literature was achieved by Dr K.M. Munshi**, who wrote both fiction and non-fictional historical work; **one of his finest novels is *Prithivivallabh* (1928).**

**Rajasthani.** **Medieval Rajasthani literature was ridden with several dialects and has two main forms of fictional writing called *Dingal* and *Pingal*.** **The most famous text in this context is *Dhola Maru*.** **The writings of Mirabai in Braj have been very famous for a long time**, and several other saints composed devotional poetry in the region. **The stories of Rajasthani writers were usually oral in nature and were spread by the bards who sang *virkavya*, victory poetic songs.**

The Rajasthani entry is the clearest western counterpart to the Tamil *Panar* and *Viraliyar* of Lesson 16: a literature carried by **bards, orally, in a named genre**. Two regions, two bardic classes, one transmission mode.

**Sindhi.** **The literature in Sindh has been deeply influenced by the two regions surrounding it, Rajasthan and Gujarat.** **As Sindh was always on the border of India, it was among the first regions to come into contact with Islamic settlers, which explains the influence of Islam and Sufism on Sindhi poetry. The poetry is very lyrical and meant to be sung.** **Shah Abdul Latif of the 17th century was a Sindhi Sufi mystic and poet, widely considered the greatest poet of the Sindhi language; his poems were compiled by his disciples in *Shah Jo Risalo*, published in 1866.** **Two prominent names in Sindhi literature of the 18th-19th century are Dewan Kauromal and Mirza Kalich Beg.** **Pir Nooruddin was also a famous name in Sindhi literature; his poetic verses are known as *ginans*** - the genre defined in Lesson 18, here attached to a named Sindhi figure.

Note the **compilation date** in the Shah Abdul Latif entry: a seventeenth-century poet whose works were **compiled by disciples and published in 1866**. Composition, compilation and publication are three dates, exactly as the discipline of Lesson 23 requires.

**Kashmiri.** **The use of the Kashmiri language was started by Rajanaka Shiti Kantha's work *Mahanaya-Prakasha*.** **One of the earliest texts from Kashmir was Kalhana's *Rajatarangini*, written in Sanskrit in the 12th century AD** - the language distinction is the point: the region's earliest famous text is in Sanskrit, and the Kashmiri-language tradition begins separately. **The local people used Kashmiri, which had influences from Persian and Hindi dialects.**

**In the early medieval period, when the Bhakti movement was at its peak, the Kashmiri language saw its first female poetess, a Shaivite mystic called Lal Ded.** **The major spread happened after the coming of Islam and Sufism to Kashmir, with major writers such as Sufi Ghulam Mohammad, Zinda Kaul and Mahjoor.** **Zainavilas was written by Shrivara, a 15th-century Kashmiri historian, describing the reign and achievements of Sultan Zain-ul-Abidin and the cultural prosperity of Kashmir under his rule.** **Habba Khatoon of the 16th century was a Kashmiri Muslim poet and ascetic known as the "Nightingale of Kashmir"; she composed songs in Kashmiri, and it is claimed that she introduced *loal* to Kashmiri poetry, which is equivalent to the English "lyric".** **Noor Din, also known as Nund Rishi, was purported to bring Hindi and Islamic elements together in his poetry.** **With political power passing to the Dogra family in Jammu in 1846, Kashmiri has been eclipsed by the Dogri language; recently some interest has been shown in reviving it.**

The Kashmiri section is the most complete single illustration of the four-stage mechanism plus a **fifth stage - eclipse**. A language with a named originating text, a female mystic poet, a Sufi expansion, a court historian, a lyric innovator and a syncretic poet is then **eclipsed by a neighbouring language after a political transfer in 1846**. Political change altering a language's literary fortunes is exactly the mechanism of Lesson 8, visible here at regional scale.

**Punjabi.** **The earliest writings in Punjabi belong to the Nath Yogi era, from the 9th to the 14th century AD.** **Punjabi literature was composed in two major scripts: Persian and Gurmukhi.** **One of the greatest texts of the world is the religious book of the Sikhs, the Adi Granth, a majority of which is in Gurmukhi; it also comprises the dohas of Kabir, Dadu and Nanak in Hindi or Braj, and is therefore a very composite text.** **Guru Gobind Singh, who contributed to the Adi Granth, also wrote *Savaiye*, poetry, in Punjabi.**

**The local literature took heavily to love stories and epics, for example *Sohni-Mahiwal*, *Sassi-Punnu* and the very famous *Heer-Ranjha*, composed by Waris Shah.** **The Sufi poetry of Baba Farid and Bulleh Shah became popular with the masses as well as with the rulers of this region; they composed many songs or classical compositions called *Kafi(s)*, usually sung by the common people.**

**Modern Punjabi literature was also affected by nationalist writing, and Bhagat Singh's legendary "Rang de Basanti Chola" is a classic example of this trend.** **There was a trend to remember past rulers who might have repelled the British, and Bhai Vir Singh wrote *Rana Surat Singh* with such sentiments in mind.** **Contemporary writers include Dr Mohan Singh and Puran Singh.** **The novels, short stories and poetry of Amrita Pritam are examples of the experience of women and the Partition of India; her most famous novel is *Pinjar*.**

**Marathi.** **The journey of Marathi literature began with the old Yadav dynasty.** **The *Mahanubhav Panth* and the *Warkari Sampradaya* laid the foundation of Marathi literature and were influenced by the *Nath Panth* of the 9th and 10th centuries.** **Mahanubhav Panth was founded by Sarvadnya Shri Chakradhar Swami in 1100-1200. This cult accepted all members irrespective of their caste.** These sects, through literature, **tried to describe incarnations of gods and explain the philosophies**, which enriched Marathi literature. **Leela Charitra is thought to be the first biography written in the Marathi language; it was written by Mhaimbhat in 1278 and is a biography of Chakradhar Swami of the Mahanubhava sect.**

**Bhakti saints like Jnyaneshwar and Vitthal sang in their local, colloquial language.** **The oldest known work in Marathi is from the 13th century by Saint Jnaneshwar, also known as Gyaneshwar. He is credited with beginning the *kirtan* in Maharashtra and has written a detailed commentary on the Bhagavad Gita in Marathi.** **Several saints such as Namdev, Sena and Gora were popular from the same period. The oldest known female writer is Janabai, of the 13th-14th century.** **Namdev wrote a great many *Abhangas* on devotion to God.** This is called **the first or early period of Marathi literature, extending from 1200 to 1350 AD.**

**There was no such development in Marathi literature from the middle of the 14th century till the beginning of the 16th century. Saint Eknath was born in 1518, and the seed of literary genius germinated again.** **The third period is the most brilliant in the history of Marathi literature; it extends from the beginning of the 17th century to the close of Peshwa rule.** Three great poets were born: **Ramdas, Tukaram and Mukteshwar**.

- **Saint Tukaram**, described as the greatest poet in Marathi literature, **preached asceticism, toleration and devotion to God** and composed **Abhanga** poetry; the **Tukaramgatha** is the Marathi compilation of his works. **He includes a discussion of the conflict between *Pravritti* and *Nivritti*** - between a passion for life, family and business, and the desire to renounce everything for individual liberation (*Moksha*).
- **Ramdas** wrote ***Dasbodh***, containing **sermons on abstract as well as practical topics**.
- **Mukteshwar**, by his **translation of the Mahabharata**, motivated the masses.

**The nationalist movement affected Marathi poetry and prose writing. Bal Gangadhar Tilak published his regional newspaper *Kesari* in Marathi, which criticised the British and their policies and published tracts by leading revolutionaries of the time.** **On 6 January 1832, Balshastri Jambhekar began *Darpan*, the first Marathi-English fortnightly magazine; he is known as the Father of Marathi Journalism.** **Marathi prose writing took a turn for the better with the works of Hari Narayan Apte, V.S. Chiplunkar and Kesavasut. Contemporary Marathi poets include M.G. Ranade, G.T. Madkholkar and K.T. Telang.**

The Marathi section is the only one in which the sources supply an explicit **periodisation with a recorded gap**: a first period from 1200 to 1350, then **no such development from the middle of the fourteenth century to the beginning of the sixteenth**, then a revival from Eknath's birth in 1518, then a third and most brilliant period from the seventeenth century to the close of Peshwa rule. A literature with a **documented two-century pause** is valuable evidence against any smooth narrative of continuous literary growth.

### Part D - The patronage ledger

Two tables, read together, show that court patronage of literature runs continuously from the post-Mauryan centuries to the nineteenth century, across every dynasty and both major court languages.

**Post-Mauryan dynasties and their literary contributions:**

| Dynasty | Rulers and scholars | Works | Contribution |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Sungas** (c. 185-73 BCE) | Patanjali, Bhasa | **Mahabhashya** (Sanskrit) by Patanjali; **Svapnavasavadatta**, **Pratijnayaugandharayana** (Sanskrit) by Bhasa | the Sungas **revived Sanskrit learning and classical drama**; Patanjali's Mahabhashya gave a **systematic commentary on Panini's grammar**, while Bhasa's plays show the **early development of Sanskrit theatre** and influenced later writers such as Kalidasa |
| **Satavahanas** (c. 1st BCE-2nd CE) | King Hala | **Gatha Saptashati (Gaha Sattasai)** (Prakrit) | a collection of **700 Prakrit love poems compiled by King Hala**; **one of the earliest secular anthologies in Indian literature**, portraying love, nature and daily life in **Maharashtri Prakrit**, **including verses by women poets** |
| **Indo-Greeks** (2nd BCE-1st CE) | Menander (Milinda), Nagasena | **Milindapanho** (Pali / Sanskrit) | a dialogue between King Menander and the monk Nagasena, **combining Greek rational inquiry with Buddhist philosophy**; a major Pali prose work reflecting **Indo-Greek cultural exchange** |
| **Kushan Empire** (1st-3rd CE) | Kanishka I, Ashvaghosha, Charaka | **Buddhacharita**, **Saundarananda** (Sanskrit) by Ashvaghosha; **Charaka Samhita (revised)** (Sanskrit) | under Kanishka the Kushan age saw a flourishing of **Sanskrit Buddhist and scientific literature**; Ashvaghosha's works are the **earliest Sanskrit Buddhist epics**, and the revised Charaka Samhita **established Ayurveda's classical form** |

**Famous rulers and their court poets, across the full span:**

| Dynasty | King | Court poet / scholar | Famous works |
|---|---|---|---|
| Maurya | Chandragupta Maurya | Kautilya (Chanakya) | Arthashastra, Chanakya Niti |
| Gupta | Samudragupta | Harisena | Prayaga Prashasti (Allahabad Pillar Inscription) |
| Gupta | Chandragupta II (Vikramaditya) | Kalidasa | Raghuvamsha, Kumarasambhava, Meghaduta, Abhijnanasakuntalam |
| Pushyabhuti (Vardhana) | Harshavardhana | Bana (Bana Bhatt) | Harshacharita, Kadambari |
| Kannauj | Yashovarman of Kannauj | Bhavabhuti | Mahaviracharita, Malatimadhava, Uttararamacharita |
| Chalukya | Pulakeshin II | Ravikirti | Aihole Inscription |
| Chalukya | Arikesari II | Adikavi Pampa | Vikramarjuna Vijaya (Pampa Bharata) |
| Kalyani Chalukya (Western Chalukya) | Vikramaditya VI (Tribhuvanamalla) | Bilhana | Vikramankadeva Charita |
| Rashtrakuta | Amoghavarsha I | Jinasena, Gunabhadra | Mahapurana (Jinasena), Adipurana (Gunabhadra) |
| Rashtrakuta | Krishna III | Sri Ponna | Shantipurana, Bhuvanaika-Ramabhyudaya |
| Pratihara (Gurjara) | Gurjara Pratiharas | Rajashekhara | Balabharata, Karpuramanjari, Balaramayana, Kavyamimamsa |
| Paramara | Raja Bhoja | Dhananjaya, Padmagupta | Dasharupa (Dhananjaya), Nava-sahasanka-charita (Padmagupta), Sarasvati-Kanthabharana (Bhoja himself) |
| Hoysala | Hoysala kings | Raghavanka | Harishchandra Kavya |
| Vijayanagara (Tuluva) | Sri Krishna Deva Raya | Ashtadiggajas | Manucharitramu (Allasani Peddana), Amuktamalyada (Krishna Deva Raya himself), Swaarochisha Manu Sambhavam (Peddana), Parijathapaharanam and Vanivilasam (Nandi Thimmana) |
| Mysore (Wodeyar) | King Wodeyar | Tirumalarya | Karna Vrittanta Kathe |
| Tanjore | Serfoji II | Vedanayagam Sastriar | Bethlehem Kuravanji, Gnanakummi |
| Delhi Sultanate | Alauddin Khilji and seven Delhi Sultans | Amir Khusro | Tuhfatus-Sighr, Qiranus-Sa'dain |
| Sen Dynasty (Bengal) | Lakshmana Sena | Jayadeva | Gita Govinda |
| Chaulukya | Kumarapala | Hemachandra | Siddha-Hema-Sabdanushasana, Dvyasraya Mahakavya |
| Maratha | Shivaji | Parmanand | Shivbharat |
| Sambalpur (Odisha) | Baliar Singh | Pandit Gangadhar Mishra | Kosalananda Mahakavya |
| Kamata (Assam) | Durlabh Narayan | Hema Saraswati | Prahalad Charita |
| Chola | Kulottunga Chola I | Jayamkondar | Kalingattu Parani |
| Mughal | Akbar | Abu'l Fazl | Akbarnama, Ain-i-Akbari |
| Mughal | Jahangir | Mutamad Khan, Muhammad Hadi | Iqbal-Nama-i-Jahangiri (Mutamad Khan); Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri (edited by Muhammad Hadi) |
| Mughal | Shah Jahan | Abdul Hamid Lahori | Padshahnama |
| Mughal | Aurangzeb | Saqi Mustad Khan | Maasir-i-Alamgiri |
| Sikh Empire | Ranjit Singh | Rattan Singh Bhangu | Sri Gur Panth Prakash |
| Awadh | Wajid Ali Shah | Agha Hasan Amanat | Inder Sabha |
| Nadia (Bengal) | Krishna Chandra of Nadia | Ramprasad Sen | Vidyasundar, Shaktigiti |

Three observations turn this ledger from a table into an argument. First, **the same institution - court patronage - operates continuously across roughly two thousand years and across Sanskrit, Prakrit, Pali, Persian, Kannada, Telugu, Tamil, Bengali and Urdu**. Second, **rulers frequently appear as authors, not only as patrons** - Amoghavarsha I, Raja Bhoja, Krishnadevaraya, Harshavardhana, Maharana Kumbha, Babur, Jahangir, Wajid Ali Shah's court sponsorship of Inder Sabha. Third, **a single inscription can be a court poet's commissioned work**: Harisena's Prayaga Prashasti and Ravikirti's Aihole Inscription both appear in a table of court poets, which closes the loop with Lesson 11 and Lesson 14.

### Examination use

This lesson is the densest pairing material in the session, and items are built directly on it: a regional language against its first poet, its oldest inscription, its father figure or its first novel; a Vijayanagara court poet against his work; a Kannada gem against his epic; a Bengali reformer against his text. The recurring high-frequency items concern **Amuktamalyada as Krishnadevaraya's Telugu work on statecraft**, **Chandimangala by Mukundaram Chakraborty**, **Kirtana-Ghosha by Srimanta Sankaradev**, **Ramcharita by Sandhyakara as a *dvyasraya-kavya* telling two stories simultaneously**, **Shivsankirttan by Rameshwar Bhattacharya**, **Bhanumati by Chakrapanidatta**, **Zainavilas by Shrivara**, and **Anandamath among Bankim's novels**. For Mains, Part D's three observations and the four-stage mechanism in the opening visual are the transferable blocks.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating Krishnadevaraya as a patron only; he wrote Amuktamalyada in Telugu, Krishnadevarayana Dinachari in Kannada, and four named treatises in Sanskrit.
- Assigning Amuktamalyada to Sanskrit or Kannada; it is in Telugu.
- Expanding the recorded list of ashtadiggajas with names the sources do not carry.
- Confusing Pampa's Adipurana with Jinasena's Adipurana, or Ponna's Shanti Purana with either.
- Forgetting that the oldest literary document of Bengali, the Charyapada, was discovered in the Nepal Durbar Library in 1916 by Haraprasad Shastri.
- Attributing Vande Mataram to a standalone poem; it is an excerpt from Bankim's Anandamath.
- Confusing Michael Madhusudan Dutt's Meghnad Badh Kavya (1861) with his Tilottama Sambhava Kabya (1860), the first Bengali poem in blank verse.
- Treating buranjis as literary epics; they are Assamese court chronicles.
- Forgetting the recorded gap in Marathi literature from the mid-14th to the early 16th century.
- Confusing Dingal and Pingal, the two Rajasthani forms of fictional writing, with dialects.
- Assigning Shah Jo Risalo to Shah Abdul Latif's own compilation; it was compiled by his disciples and published in 1866.
- Treating Rajatarangini as a Kashmiri-language text; it is in Sanskrit.

### Revision notes

1. Malayalam: Kokasandisan; Bhasa Kautilya, a commentary on the Arthashastra; Ramacharitam by Cheeraman, 12th century; Thirunizhalmala and Ramacharitam in old Malayalam, of the *pattu* genre, the earliest literary works; Ezhuthachan the Father of the Malayalam Language; Vallathol Narayana Menon with Kumaran Asan and Ulloor S. Parameshwara Iyer as the modern triumvirate; Mahakavya Chitrayogam.
2. Telugu: Nannayya, 11th century, first poet, translated the Mahabharata; oldest inscriptions Tippalar and Bhodampudu, 7th century AD; Vijayanagara period the Golden Age, also the Prabandha Age; Uttaraharivamsam by Nachana Somanatha, court poet of Bukka I.
3. Ashtadiggajas: eight were attached to the court and seven are named in the sources - Allasani Peddana (Andhra Kavita Pitamahudu) - Manu Charitra and Swaarochisha Manu Sambhavam; Nandi Thimmana - Parijatapaharanamu, Vanivilasam; Tenali Ramakrishna - Panduranga Mahatmayam, Ghatikachala Mahatyam, Udbhataradhya Charitramu; Ramaraja Bhushanudu/Bhattumurti - Vasucharitram, Narasabhupaleeyamu, Harishchandra Nalopakhyanam, Kavyalankarasangrahamu; Madayyagari Mallana - Rajasekharacharitra; Ayyalaraju Ramabhadrudu - Ramabhudayamu; Pingali Surana - Raghava Pandaveeyam and Kalapurnodayam, the first original poetic novel in Telugu.
4. Krishnadevaraya (1509-29): wrote Amuktamalyada in Telugu and Krishnadevarayana Dinachari in Kannada; Sanskrit treatises Madalasa Charita, Satyavadu Parinaya, Rasamanjari, Jambavati Kalyana; patronised Kannada poets Mallanarya, Chatu Vittal-anatha and Thimmana, with Vyasatirtha as Rajaguru, and the Tamil poet Haridasa.
5. K. Veereshalingam Pantalu wrote the first Telugu novel, Rajasekhara Charitramu.
6. Kannada: Jain scholars first; Dharmanathapurana by Madhava on the 15th tirthankara; second oldest language in South India; Halmidi inscription, 5th century AD; Uritta Vilasa's Dharma Parikshe; Kavirajamarga by the Rashtrakuta king Amoghavarsha I, 9th century, who also wrote Prashnottara Ratnamalika in Sanskrit.
7. Ratnatraya: Pampa, the Father of Kannada - Adipurana in Champu style and Vikramarjuna Vijaya (Pampa Bharatha), at the court of Chalukya Arikesari; Ponna - Shanti Purana, titled Ubhaya Kavichakravarthi; Ranna - Ajitha Purana and Sahasa Bhima Vijaya (Gada Yuddha); Ponna and Ranna at Krishna III's court.
8. Kannada later: Shabdamanidarpana by Keshiraja; Torave Ramayana by Narahari, the first story on Rama in Kannada; Jaimini Bharata by Lakshmisha; Sarvajna the people's poet, composing tripadi; Honnamma, first poetess of repute, Hadibadeya Dharma; Andayya's Madana Vijaya / Kabbigara Kava, the first pure Kannada text without a Sanskrit word.
9. Bengali: Charyapada, 10th-11th century mystic songs, the oldest literary document, discovered by Haraprasad Shastri in the Nepal Durbar Library in 1916.
10. Mangalkavya: Chandimangala (Mukundaram Chakraborty), Manasamangal (Bipradas Pipilai), Dharmamangal (Mayuramukha), Annadamangal (Bharatchandra Ray), Shibamangal (Rupchand Pakhi), praising Chandi, Manasa, Dharma, Annapurna and Shiva.
11. Shivasankirttan by Rameshwar Bhattacharya; Bhanumati by Chakrapanidatta, an 11th-century commentary on the Sushruta Samhita; Ramcharita by Sandhyakar Nandi, 12th century, on King Ramapala using the Ramayana as an allegory.
12. Baptist Mission Press at Serampore, founded by William Carey in 1800; Carey wrote a Bengali grammar and published an English-Bengali dictionary.
13. Raja Ram Mohan Roy laid the foundation of Bengali prose; Vidyasagar and Akshay Kumar Dutta; Bankim Chandra Chatterjee's Anandamath, from which Vande Mataram is an excerpt; Tagore, first Indian and first Asian Nobel laureate, for Gitanjali in 1913, author of both national anthems; Michael Madhusudan Dutt's Meghnad Badh Kavya (1861) and Tilottama Sambhava Kabya (1860), the first Bengali blank verse.
14. Assamese: buranjis as medieval court chronicles; Sankardev's Kirtana Ghosha; modern - Padmanath Gohain Baruah, Lakshminath Bezbaroa, Jyoti Prasad Agarwalla, Arupa Patangia, Parismita Singh.
15. Odia: Sarala Das, 15th century; Upendra Bhanja, early 18th century; Jayadeva's Gita Govinda sung nightly at the Jagannatha temple in Odissi music style; Panchasakha - Jagannath Das, Balaram Das, Achyutanda Das, Yasovanta Das, Ananta Das; modern Radhanath Ray and Fakir Mohan Senapati.
16. Gujarati: Narsinh Mehta, 15th century; Narmad, 19th century; Govardhanram Madhavram Tripathi's Saraswatichandra; K.M. Munshi's Prithivivallabh (1928).
17. Rajasthani: Dingal and Pingal; Dhola Maru; Mirabai in Braj; bards singing virkavya.
18. Sindhi: Shah Abdul Latif, 17th century, compiled by disciples as Shah Jo Risalo, published 1866; Dewan Kauromal and Mirza Kalich Beg; Pir Nooruddin's ginans.
19. Kashmiri: Rajanaka Shiti Kantha's Mahanaya-Prakasha; Kalhana's Rajatarangini in Sanskrit, 12th century; Lal Ded, the first female poetess; Sufi Ghulam Mohammad, Zinda Kaul, Mahjoor; Shrivara's Zainavilas on Zain-ul-Abidin; Habba Khatoon, "Nightingale of Kashmir", who introduced *loal*; Noor Din / Nund Rishi; eclipse by Dogri after 1846.
20. Punjabi: Nath Yogi era, 9th-14th century; composed in Persian and Gurmukhi scripts; the Adi Granth as a composite text with dohas of Kabir, Dadu and Nanak; Guru Gobind Singh's Savaiye; love epics Sohni-Mahiwal, Sassi-Punnu, Heer-Ranjha by Waris Shah; Kafis of Baba Farid and Bulleh Shah; Bhagat Singh's "Rang de Basanti Chola"; Bhai Vir Singh's Rana Surat Singh; Mohan Singh and Puran Singh; Amrita Pritam's Pinjar.
21. Marathi: Yadav beginnings; Mahanubhav Panth founded by Chakradhar Swami, 1100-1200, accepting all castes; Warkari Sampradaya; Nath Panth influence; Leela Charitra by Mhaimbhat, 1278, the first Marathi biography; Jnaneshwar, 13th century, began kirtan and wrote a Bhagavad Gita commentary; Namdev's Abhangas; Janabai the oldest known female writer; first period 1200-1350; a gap from the mid-14th to the early 16th century; Eknath born 1518; third period from the 17th century to the close of Peshwa rule - Ramdas's Dasbodh, Tukaram's Abhanga and Tukaramgatha with the Pravritti-Nivritti discussion, Mukteshwar's Mahabharata translation.
22. Marathi modern: Tilak's Kesari; Balshastri Jambhekar's Darpan, 6 January 1832, the first Marathi-English fortnightly, making him the Father of Marathi Journalism; Hari Narayan Apte, V.S. Chiplunkar, Kesavasut; M.G. Ranade, G.T. Madkholkar, K.T. Telang.
23. Post-Mauryan patronage: Sungas - Patanjali's Mahabhashya and Bhasa's plays; Satavahanas - Hala's Gatha Saptashati, 700 Prakrit love poems in Maharashtri including verses by women poets; Indo-Greeks - Milindapanho; Kushans under Kanishka - Ashvaghosha's Buddhacharita and Saundarananda, and the revised Charaka Samhita.
24. Court-poet pairings across the ledger, including Ravikirti's Aihole Inscription under Pulakeshin II and Harisena's Prayaga Prashasti under Samudragupta - two inscriptions appearing in a table of court poets.

### Concept check

**Question:** Name three separate pieces of evidence from this lesson showing that patronage of literature in India was routinely multilingual, and state what they establish together.

**Model answer:** First, **Krishnadevaraya** wrote **Amuktamalyada in Telugu** and **Krishnadevarayana Dinachari in Kannada**, composed four named treatises in **Sanskrit**, patronised the **Kannada** poets Mallanarya, Chatu Vittal-anatha and Thimmana with the Kannada saint Vyasatirtha as his Rajaguru, and patronised the **Tamil** poet Haridasa - four languages around a single court. Second, the **Pallava** kings **encouraged both Tamil and Sanskrit works**, supported Bhakti saints, had Dandin's Sanskrit prose linked to their court, issued **bilingual Tamil-Sanskrit inscriptions from the seventh century**, and **replaced Vatteluttu with the Pallava-Grantha script**. Third, **Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khana**, one of Akbar's Navaratnas, **wrote in both Persian and Hindi and blended Sufi and Bhakti ideals**, in a court that was simultaneously translating Sanskrit mathematics, epic, scripture and philosophy into Persian. Together they establish that multilingual production was the **normal operating condition of Indian literary patronage**, not an exceptional gesture of tolerance by particular rulers.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a court had "a" language. The evidence shows courts operating several languages at once for different purposes - one for administration, another for devotional composition, a third for scholarship - and rulers themselves crossing between them as authors.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Identify the common stages through which India's regional literatures passed, and test the pattern against two regions that do not fit it neatly. (15 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**The pattern.** Four stages recur. An **early anchor** - a first named text, an inscription, or a religious foundation, often Jain, Buddhist or Bhakti: the **Halmidi inscription** and Jain beginnings in Kannada, the **Charyapada** in Bengali, **Ramacharitam** in Malayalam. A **devotional turn** moving composition into the people's language: **Ezhuthachan** in Malayalam, **Narsinh Mehta** in Gujarati, **Jnaneshwar** and **Namdev** in Marathi, **Sankardev** in Assamese. A **court phase**, sometimes with the ruler writing: **Amoghavarsha I's Kavirajamarga**, **Krishnadevaraya's Amuktamalyada**, the **ashtadiggajas**. A **print-and-prose phase** with a nationalist edge: the **Serampore press of 1800**, **Tilak's Kesari**, **Fakir Mohan Senapati**.

**First region that resists it - Assam.** The medieval dominant genre is the **buranji**, the **court chronicle**, not devotional verse. Assam therefore leads with systematic historical writing, which is unusual outside the Persianate tradition, and its Bhakti stage sits alongside rather than before it.

**Second region that resists it - Kashmir.** The sequence runs but adds a fifth stage, **eclipse**. After a named originating work, a female mystic in **Lal Ded**, a Sufi expansion, a court historian in **Shrivara** and a lyric innovator in **Habba Khatoon**, political transfer to the **Dogras in 1846** left Kashmiri overshadowed by Dogri.

**Assessment.** The pattern is a useful default, not a law: genre dominance and political change can reorder or terminate it.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 15: **6** for the four stages, each supported by a named text or figure from a different region, at 1.5 each; **6** for the two resisting regions at 3 each, requiring the buranji point and the 1846 eclipse; **3** for an assessment treating the pattern as a default rather than a law. A region-by-region catalogue with no pattern caps at 6.

## Lesson 20 - Modern literature and the nationalist turn

Progress: 20/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: print as the enabling technology, the Adhunik kaal phases of Hindi, the nationalist mechanism, Subramania Bharati, women writers, and the declared limits of this session on Indian English writing

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the modern literature section: the eighteenth-century print statement, the Adhunik kaal four-phase table with its named writers and date ranges, Swami Dayanand's Satyartha Prakash, the Premchand and Chhayavaad material, the Subramaniya Bharathi box, and the headings for nationalist and Indian English literature
CA search: "Not required - modern literary-historical material; the session's single current linkage is reserved for Lesson 21"
CA found: None found; no genuine recent linkage for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the modern turn as a chain of causes

```text
  THE ENABLING TECHNOLOGY
  "The distribution and dissemination of language took a major step
   ahead in the 18TH CENTURY with the development of PRINT TECHNOLOGY,
   which led more and more people to READ AND APPRECIATE LITERATURE."
                          |
  THE INSTITUTIONAL INSTANCE (Lesson 19)
  BAPTIST MISSION PRESS, SERAMPORE, 1800, WILLIAM CAREY
  -> a grammar of Bengali; an English-Bengali dictionary
  -> inspires WEALTHY LOCAL BENGALIS to open THEIR OWN PRESSES
                          |
  THE FORM CHANGE
  PROSE becomes central: essay, novel, drama, journalism
  -> "the most common type of prose... used mostly in STORY WRITING
      AND NOVELS" (the narrative text of Lesson 13)
                          |
  THE CONTENT CHANGE
  "With the coming of the British, THE FOCUS OF LITERATURE CHANGED.
   This change occurred phenomenally in HINDI PROSE WRITING where there
   was a ZEST TO GO BACK TO THE CLASSICS and be INSPIRED BY SANSKRIT.
   This zest was COMBINED WITH NATIONALISTIC FERVOUR."
                          |
  THE OUTPUTS
  POLITICAL SATIRE (Andher Nagari) | NATIONALIST DRAMA (Bharat Durdasha)
  THE SOCIAL NOVEL (Godaan, Karmabhoomi) | NEO-ROMANTIC POETRY (Kamayani)
  THE NEWSPAPER AS LITERATURE (Kesari, Darpan, India, Tahdhib-ul-Akhlaq)
  THE PARTITION NOVEL (Pinjar) | THE NATIONALIST SONG (Sare Jahan se
  Achcha, Vande Mataram, Rang de Basanti Chola)
                          |
  AND THE CURRENT VERSION OF THE SAME MECHANISM: "Currently, ELECTRONIC
  LITERATURE has taken centre stage and far more people read through
  this medium."
```

The chain is the argument. A technology change enlarges the readership; an enlarged readership demands prose; prose in a colonial situation acquires political content; and the outputs are the genres listed at the bottom. Reproducing the chain converts a list of modern authors into an explanation.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Modern literature is where lists are longest and least memorable, and where candidates most often write a general sentence about "the freedom struggle inspiring writers". The material supports something much more precise: a **named technology**, a **named press with a date and a founder**, a **documented change in the dominant form**, a **four-phase periodisation with date ranges and named writers**, and a set of **specific outputs**. Precision here is entirely achievable, and this lesson supplies exactly what the sources record and nothing more.

### 2. Print, and what it changed

**The distribution and dissemination of language took a major step ahead in the 18th century with the development of print technology, which led more and more people to read and appreciate literature.** And the present-day continuation, recorded in the same place: **currently, electronic literature has taken centre stage and far more people read through this medium.**

The institutional instance is the Serampore press of Lesson 19 - **the Baptist Mission Press at Serampore, established by William Carey in 1800**, which produced a **Bengali grammar** and an **English-Bengali dictionary** and **inspired wealthy local Bengalis to open their own presses**. That last clause is the important one: the decisive effect was not the mission press itself but the **local presses it triggered**, which is what turned a technology into an indigenous publishing industry.

Print also explains a puzzle raised in Lesson 19: **a lot of ancient and medieval literary works existed in Bengali before the 19th century, such as the Mangal-Kavyas, but they had not been widely published.** Print did not create the literature; it created the **public** for it. That distinction is worth stating in an answer.

### 3. The Hindi case, in four dated phases

**With the coming of the British, the focus of literature changed. This change occurred phenomenally in Hindi prose writing, where there was a zest to go back to the classics and be inspired by Sanskrit. This zest was combined with nationalistic fervour.**

| Phase | Dates | Named writers |
|---|---|---|
| **Bhartendu Yug** | **1850-1900** | Radhakrishna Das, Pratapnarayan Mishra, Balkrishna Bhatta, Badrinarayan Chaudhuri, Sudhakar Dwivedi |
| **Dwivedi Yug** | **1900-18** | Nathuram Sharma Sankar, Ram Naresh Tripathi, Ayodhya Sinha Upadhyay, Maithalisharan Gupt |
| **Chhayavad Yug** | **1918-37** | Subhadrakumari Chauhan, Sumitranandan Pant, Mahadevi Verma, Jayashankar Prasad, Makhanlal Chaturvedi |
| **Contemporary Period** | **1937-present** | Ramdhari Singh Dinkar, Bhagvati Charan Varma, Harivansh Rai Bachchan |

**The period of modern literature has been called *Adhunik kaal*, a term used mostly in the context of Hindi.** Note that the first two phases are **named after individual writers** - Bharatendu and Dwivedi - which is itself a fact about how the tradition periodised itself: an entire phase of Hindi writing is named after **Mahavir Prasad Dwivedi**.

The named figures:

| Figure | Record |
|---|---|
| **Bharatendu Harishchandra** | wrote his most famous drama ***Andher Nagari*** (City of Darkness) in **1881**, which became **a major play on political satire**; his other very famous nationalist work is ***Bharat Durdasha***; he is called the **Father of Modern Hindu Literature** |
| **Mahavir Prasad Dwivedi** | another major writer of the period, **after whom an entire phase of Hindi writing has been named** |
| **Swami Dayanand** | **spearheaded the movement to make Hindi the national language, which would link all regions**, even though he wrote a great deal in Gujarati; his most famous work in Hindi is ***Satyartha Prakash*, written in 1875** |
| **Munshi Premchand** | among the Hindi authors who **questioned the orthodoxies in society**; wrote many anthologies **in Hindi and Urdu**; famous works include ***Godaan*** and ***Karmabhoomi*** |
| **Suryakant Tripathi 'Nirala'** and **Maithili Sharan Gupt** | likewise questioned social orthodoxies |
| **Harivansh Rai Bachchan** | wrote ***Madhushala*** and ***Madhukalash*** |
| **Mahadevi Verma** | **one of the most famous female writers of Hindi in the 20th century** |
| **Jaishankar Prasad** | ***Kamayani* (1936) is considered the magnum opus of the *Chhayavaad* - neo-romanticism - genre of Hindi literature** |

One qualification must be carried on the Swami Dayanand entry. The record is that he **spearheaded a movement** to make Hindi a national link language; it is **not** a statement that such a status was ever created. The constitutional position of Lesson 6 is unambiguous: **no national language exists**. A movement's aim and a constitutional outcome are different objects, and conflating them is the classic error in this corner of the syllabus.

### 4. Nationalist literature: the mechanism, and its named instances

The nationalist turn is not confined to any one language, and the strongest way to present it is as a **mechanism with instances across languages**, drawing on Lesson 19.

| Instrument | Instance | Language |
|---|---|---|
| **Political satire on stage** | Bharatendu Harishchandra's *Andher Nagari* (1881) | Hindi |
| **The nationalist novel** | Bankim Chandra Chatterjee's *Anandamath*, from which **Vande Mataram**, the national song, is an excerpt | Bengali |
| **The nationalist song** | Iqbal's **"Sare Jahan se Achcha"**; Bhagat Singh's **"Rang de Basanti Chola"** | Urdu; Punjabi |
| **The regional newspaper as a political instrument** | Tilak's ***Kesari***, which **criticised the British and their policies and published tracts by leading revolutionaries** | Marathi |
| **The reformist journal** | Sir Syed Ahmad Khan's ***Tahdhib-ul-Akhlaq***, propagating women's education and opposing purdah and polygamy | Urdu |
| **The pioneering periodical** | Balshastri Jambhekar's ***Darpan***, begun **6 January 1832**, the first Marathi-English fortnightly | Marathi and English |
| **Historical memory as resistance** | Bhai Vir Singh's ***Rana Surat Singh***, written to remember past rulers who might have repelled the British | Punjabi |
| **The Partition novel** | Amrita Pritam's ***Pinjar***, on the experience of women and the Partition of India | Punjabi |
| **Social-reform fiction** | Premchand's *Godaan* and *Karmabhoomi*, questioning orthodoxies | Hindi and Urdu |

Read down the instrument column and the point emerges without being asserted: nationalist literature worked through **at least nine distinct forms** across at least five languages, and several of them - the newspaper, the journal, the fortnightly - are **periodical** rather than literary in the narrow sense. An answer that names the press alongside the novel is describing the actual system.

### 5. Subramania Bharati: the fullest single case

**C. Subramaniya Bharathiyar, known as Bharathi and as Mahakavi Bharathiyar (1882-1921), was a poet, journalist, freedom fighter and social reformer from Tamil Nadu**, and is **considered one of India's greatest poets**. **His songs on nationalism and freedom helped to rally the masses to support the Indian Independence Movement in Tamil Nadu.** **A new age in Tamil literature began with his works.**

**Bharathi was essentially a lyrical poet.** **Most of his compositions are short lyrical outpourings on patriotic, devotional and mystic themes.** Named poems: ***Kannan Pattu*, *Nilavum Vanminum Katrum*, *Paanchali Sabadham*, *Kuyil Pattu***.

**He is considered a national poet because a number of his poems were of a patriotic flavour, through which he exhorted people to join the independence struggle and work vigorously for the liberation of the country. Instead of merely being proud of his country, he also outlined his vision for a free India.**

**He published the sensational *Sudesa Geethangal* in 1908.** **In order to proclaim its revolutionary ardour, he printed the weekly newspaper named *India* in red paper. It was the first paper in Tamil Nadu to publish political cartoons. He also published and edited a few other journals, for example *Vijaya*.**

Bharati is the single most complete case in this lesson because he occupies **four roles at once** - poet, journalist, freedom fighter and social reformer - and because the detail of the **red paper** and the **first political cartoons in Tamil Nadu** shows literature and journalism operating as the same activity. The observation that he **outlined a vision for a free India rather than merely expressing pride** is the sentence to use when a question asks what distinguished nationalist literature from patriotic sentiment.

### 6. Women writers across the modern corpus

Assembling the named women writers from this lesson and Lesson 19 gives a substantial, evidenced set rather than a general claim:

| Writer | Language | Record |
|---|---|---|
| **Mahadevi Verma** | Hindi | one of the most famous female writers of Hindi in the twentieth century; also a Chhayavad Yug poet |
| **Subhadrakumari Chauhan** | Hindi | Chhayavad Yug poet; author of the widely known poem on the Rani of Jhansi |
| **Amrita Pritam** | Punjabi | novels, short stories and poetry on the experience of women and the Partition; *Pinjar* |
| **Arupa Patangia** and **Parismita Singh** | Assamese | among the major modern Assamese writers |
| **Gulbadan Begum, Jahanara Begum, Zeb-un-Nissa** | Persian | historical, hagiographical and poetic authorship at the Mughal court (Lesson 17) |
| **Lakshmidevi, Mahadevi Dhiramati** | Sanskrit | Nibandha authors in the legal-digest tradition (Lesson 13) |
| **Avvaiyar, Nachchellaiyar, Kakkaipadiniyar, Andal** | Tamil | Sangam and Bhakti poets (Lesson 16) |
| **Janabai** | Marathi | the oldest known female writer in Marathi, 13th-14th century (Lesson 19) |
| **Lal Ded, Habba Khatoon** | Kashmiri | the first female poetess of Kashmiri, and the "Nightingale of Kashmir" (Lesson 19) |
| **Mirabai, Honnamma** | Braj / Kannada | bhakti poetry and bhajans; the first Kannada poetess of repute (Lessons 18 and 19) |
| Women poets in the **Gatha Saptashati** | Maharashtri Prakrit | verses by women poets in one of the earliest secular anthologies (Lesson 19) |

Eleven traditions, from Maharashtri Prakrit to twentieth-century Punjabi. An answer that cites even four of these rows has replaced a claim with a corpus.

### 7. What this session does not supply

Two headings in the source material for this period - **Nationalist Literature of India** and **Indian English Literature** - are presented as graphic panels whose content is not available in extractable text. This session therefore records the following openly:

- It supplies the **named nationalist instances** in section 4 and the **Bharati case** in section 5, all drawn from text it could read.
- It does **not** supply a consolidated account of **Indian English literature** - no authors, works or periodisation for that stream. Where an answer requires Indian English writing, the only records carried here are incidental: **Raja Ram Mohan Roy wrote in Bengali and English**; **Sir Syed Ahmad Khan wrote didactic and nationalistic texts in Urdu and English**; and **Balshastri Jambhekar's Darpan was a Marathi-English fortnightly**.
- A further recorded item stands alone without a supporting account: the translation of ancient Indian religious lyrics into English published as **"Songs from Prison"**, which appears in examination material and for which this session holds no further record and supplies no attribution.

Stating a gap is not a weakness in an answer; inventing an author list to fill it is a fatal one.

### Examination use

Objective items here attack the Chhayavaad attribution (Kamayani and Jaishankar Prasad), the authorship of well-known nationalist poems - the Rani of Jhansi poem is associated with **Subhadra Kumari Chauhan** and is regularly offered against Mahadevi Varma, Amrita Pritam and Balamani Amma - and the identification of Bankim's novels, where **Anandamath** is the one that portrays the three images of the Mother. Items also test which nineteenth-century Urdu novel is associated with **Nazir Ahmad Dehlvi**, for which this session holds the name **Mirat-ul-Uroos** from reproduced examination material but no independent account. For Mains, the causal chain in the opening visual and the instrument table in section 4 are the blocks that convert this material into an argument.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing that the movement to make Hindi a link language succeeded in creating a national language; no national language exists.
- Attributing Kamayani to the wrong movement or author; it is Jaishankar Prasad's, and it is the magnum opus of Chhayavaad.
- Confusing the four Adhunik kaal phases or their date ranges: Bhartendu Yug 1850-1900, Dwivedi Yug 1900-18, Chhayavad Yug 1918-37, Contemporary 1937 onwards.
- Treating Premchand as a Hindi-only writer; he wrote anthologies in Hindi and Urdu.
- Assuming print created Indian literature; the Mangal-Kavyas and much else existed before the nineteenth century but had not been widely published.
- Omitting the periodical press from an account of nationalist literature; Kesari, Darpan, India, Vijaya and Tahdhib-ul-Akhlaq are part of the record.
- Treating Bharati only as a poet; he was also a journalist who printed the weekly *India* on red paper and published the first political cartoons in Tamil Nadu.
- Filling the Indian English gap with invented names.

### Revision notes

1. Print technology developed in the 18th century and enlarged the reading public; electronic literature now occupies the same position.
2. Baptist Mission Press, Serampore, 1800, William Carey; a Bengali grammar and an English-Bengali dictionary; it inspired local Bengalis to open their own presses.
3. Adhunik kaal is the term for the modern period, used mostly for Hindi.
4. Bhartendu Yug 1850-1900: Radhakrishna Das, Pratapnarayan Mishra, Balkrishna Bhatta, Badrinarayan Chaudhuri, Sudhakar Dwivedi.
5. Dwivedi Yug 1900-18: Nathuram Sharma Sankar, Ram Naresh Tripathi, Ayodhya Sinha Upadhyay, Maithalisharan Gupt.
6. Chhayavad Yug 1918-37: Subhadrakumari Chauhan, Sumitranandan Pant, Mahadevi Verma, Jayashankar Prasad, Makhanlal Chaturvedi.
7. Contemporary Period 1937 onwards: Ramdhari Singh Dinkar, Bhagvati Charan Varma, Harivansh Rai Bachchan.
8. Bharatendu Harishchandra: Andher Nagari (1881), political satire; Bharat Durdasha; called the Father of Modern Hindu Literature.
9. Mahavir Prasad Dwivedi: an entire phase of Hindi writing is named after him.
10. Swami Dayanand spearheaded the movement to make Hindi a national link language though he wrote much in Gujarati; Satyartha Prakash, 1875.
11. Premchand, Nirala and Maithili Sharan Gupt questioned social orthodoxies; Premchand wrote in Hindi and Urdu; Godaan and Karmabhoomi.
12. Harivansh Rai Bachchan: Madhushala and Madhukalash. Mahadevi Verma: among the most famous female Hindi writers of the 20th century. Jaishankar Prasad: Kamayani (1936), the magnum opus of Chhayavaad.
13. Subramania Bharati (1882-1921): poet, journalist, freedom fighter, social reformer; Mahakavi Bharathiyar; a new age in Tamil literature; lyrical poet; Kannan Pattu, Nilavum Vanminum Katrum, Paanchali Sabadham, Kuyil Pattu; Sudesa Geethangal, 1908; the weekly *India* printed on red paper, the first paper in Tamil Nadu to publish political cartoons; also published and edited *Vijaya*.
14. Nationalist instruments: political satire on stage, the nationalist novel, the nationalist song, the regional newspaper, the reformist journal, the pioneering fortnightly, historical memory as resistance, the Partition novel, social-reform fiction.
15. Declared gap: this session supplies no consolidated account of Indian English literature; only incidental records of bilingual English writing are carried.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is "print technology enlarged the reading public" a stronger opening for a modern-literature answer than "the freedom struggle inspired Indian writers"?

**Model answer:** Because it identifies a **mechanism with named instances and dates** rather than a sentiment. The record states that print developed in the eighteenth century and **led more and more people to read and appreciate literature**; the Baptist Mission Press at Serampore was founded by **William Carey in 1800**, produced a Bengali grammar and an English-Bengali dictionary, and **inspired wealthy local Bengalis to open their own presses**. That chain explains the shift to prose, the rise of the novel and the essay, and the emergence of the periodical as a political instrument - Tilak's *Kesari*, Jambhekar's *Darpan* of 6 January 1832, Bharati's *India* on red paper, Sir Syed's *Tahdhib-ul-Akhlaq*. Nationalist content then enters through those forms. Beginning with the technology lets the nationalist argument be **derived** rather than asserted, and it explains why literature in this period looks structurally different from what preceded it.

**Misconception to avoid:** That print created Indian literature. It created the **public**: works such as the Bengali Mangal-Kavyas already existed but had not been widely published, and it was distribution rather than composition that the technology transformed.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Explain how print technology changed the form of Indian literature, and name the forms that resulted. (10 marks, 150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

**The change.** Print developed in the **eighteenth century** and **led more and more people to read and appreciate literature**. Its decisive Indian instance is the **Baptist Mission Press at Serampore, founded by William Carey in 1800**, which produced a **Bengali grammar** and an **English-Bengali dictionary** and, more importantly, **inspired wealthy local Bengalis to open their own presses**. Print did not create the literature - the **Mangal-Kavyas** already existed but **had not been widely published** - it created the **public**.

**The forms that resulted.** **Prose** became central, and with it the **essay, the novel and the political drama**; the **periodical** became a literary instrument - Tilak's **Kesari**, Jambhekar's **Darpan** of 6 January 1832, Bharati's **India** printed on red paper, Sir Syed's **Tahdhib-ul-Akhlaq**; and the **nationalist song and Partition novel** followed.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 10: **3** for the technology-to-public mechanism; **2** for the Serampore instance with its year and its knock-on effect on local presses; **2** for the distinction that print created the public and not the literature, evidenced by the Mangal-Kavyas; **3** for at least four resulting forms, of which the **periodical** must be one. Listing modern authors without the mechanism caps at 4.

## Lesson 21 - The manuscript as a physical object, and who looks after it now

Progress: 21/23 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: writing media, scribes, illustration, the oral-to-written passage, survival bias, and the institutions that survey, conserve and digitise manuscripts today

### Visual: the life of a manuscript, from speech to database
