### 1. The learner's actual problem

Manuscripts are usually treated as containers - the interesting thing is the text inside. But every question in this area is about the **container**: what it is made of, who copied it, why it survived, who holds it now and who has catalogued it. That is a different body of knowledge from the literary history of Lessons 12 to 20, and it is the part that connects to present-day policy.

### 2. The media, and what each implies

**A script is a standard for representing the parts of a spoken language by making specific marks on a medium** - and the definition names **paper, rocks, birch-bark** in that order. The manuscript record in this session adds **palm leaf**.

| Medium | Evidence in this session | What the medium implies |
|---|---|---|
| **Rock** (and pillar and copper plate) | the Ashokan edicts; Junagarh; Chebrolu; Kurgod; the Kollam Copper Plate; Nandinagari Sanskrit copper-plate inscriptions under Vijayanagara | durability and publicity; a fixed location; official or donative speech |
| **Palm leaf** | **Silappadikaram** is described as a palm leaf manuscript; **Vatteluttu** was used for centuries in inscriptions **and manuscripts** of South India | a southern and eastern manuscript tradition; fragile, requiring periodic recopying |
| **Birch bark** | named in the definition of a script's medium; and among the **three conservation streams** of the present mission in section 6 | a northern and north-western tradition; the Kharosthi manuscript record of Gandhara sits in this world |
| **Paper** | the later dominant medium; the Modi script's shorthand function presupposes rapid writing on a portable surface; the illustrated Hamzanama | volume, portability, illustration, and the possibility of a court atelier |

The analytical point is that **medium and region correlate**. Palm leaf is the southern manuscript material; birch bark the northern; paper the later and general one. So a manuscript's material is itself a piece of evidence about where and when it was made, before a word of it is read.

### 3. The scribe, visible in the script

Most of this session's material is about authors. This section is about the person who actually made the surviving object.

**Modi is a script used to write the Marathi language. It was developed in the 14th century AD as a shorthand variant of Devanagari by the scribes who were unable to write down the original Devanagari during rapid writing.**

Read that as evidence rather than as a fact about a script. It tells you that: scribes worked **under time pressure**; the pressure was severe enough to **generate a new writing system**; the system was adopted for **official use for Marathi until the twentieth century**; and it was also used to write **Urdu, Kannada, Gujarati, Hindi and Tamil**. A whole administrative working world is compressed into one sentence about a script's origin, and it is the best available evidence in this session that manuscripts were produced by **labouring copyists**, not only by authors.

Two further traces of the copying world appear elsewhere in this session. **Kaithi** was used in northern and eastern India for **legal, administrative and private records**, and was widely used during the Mughal period - a script defined by its documentary function. And the **Vedic charana** system of Lesson 12 is the pre-scribal equivalent: custody, specialisation, and a duty of exact reproduction, discharged by memory rather than by hand.

### 4. Illustration

**The Hamzanama - the story of the Persian hero Amir Hamza - is an illustrated manuscript commissioned by Akbar, showcasing Mughal art and narrative tradition.**

One record, but a rich one, because it establishes four things at once: that manuscripts could be **commissioned** by a patron as objects rather than as texts; that **illustration** was part of the production; that the illustrated manuscript is treated as a showcase of an **art tradition** and not only of a narrative; and that a **Persian story cycle** was the chosen subject in an Indian imperial atelier. Where an answer needs a single example of the manuscript as a made object, this is it. The painting tradition itself belongs to the painting topic and is routed there.

### 5. Survival bias, assembled

This session has accumulated, lesson by lesson, an unusually complete set of survival facts. Gathered together they support one of the strongest arguments available in this topic.

| Case | What was lost | Where it is recorded |
|---|---|---|
| **Sama Veda recensions** | over a thousand shakhas existed; **three** survive | Lesson 12 |
| **Rig Veda recensions** | 21 shakhas; **one complete** survivor and parts of a second | Lesson 12 |
| **Atharva Veda recensions** | 9 shakhas; **two** survive | Lesson 12 |
| **Katyayana's Varttika-sutra** | 1500 verses on Panini, **available only through references in Patanjali's work** | Lesson 3 |
| **Vishakhadatta's Devichandraguptam** | **survives only in fragments** | Lesson 14 |
| **Valaiapathi** | one of the five great Tamil epics, **now lost except for fragments** | Lesson 16 |
| **The first Sangam** | **no book has survived**; the assembly used Agattiyam as its grammar | Lesson 16 |
| **Avestan** | the language of the Avesta is **now extinct** | Lesson 15 |
| **Charyapada** | the oldest literary document of Bengali survived **in the Nepal Durbar Library** and was **recovered in 1916** | Lesson 19 |

```text
  THE ARGUMENT THESE CASES SUPPORT
  1. The surviving record is SMALL relative to what existed, and the
     loss is DOCUMENTED, not speculative.
  2. Loss is not random. It follows the failure of CUSTODIAL
     INSTITUTIONS - a charana ending, a court falling, a monastery
     closing, a language's speakers dispersing.
  3. Survival is often ACCIDENTAL and EXTERNAL: the Charyapada survived
     outside India; Katyayana survives inside his own commentator.
  4. THEREFORE: any statement about "what Indian literature contains"
     is a statement about what survived, and any conservation programme
     is an intervention in an ongoing process of loss - not a
     retrospective tidying-up.
  5. AND: the SAME LOGIC applies forward. A manuscript that is not
     surveyed, catalogued and conserved today joins the left-hand
     column of the table above.
```

Point five is the bridge to the current record, and it is the reason the current linkage in this session is placed in this lesson and nowhere else.

### 6. The present institutional stage - the single current linkage in this session

**Current record, verified 29 September 2026.** The **Gyan Bharatam Mission** is a Government of India mission for India's manuscript heritage, **announced in the Union Budget for 2025-26** and carried by the **Ministry of Culture**. Its own published statement of vision and objectives records the following:

| Element | Recorded content |
|---|---|
| **Vision** | to **preserve, digitize and disseminate India's vast manuscript heritage** and revive its civilizational knowledge traditions for future generations |
| **Key objectives** | **digitize and catalog over 1 crore manuscripts** across the country; establish a **National Digital Repository** of manuscripts; integrate **AI, OCR and blockchain** technologies for smart access, transcription and provenance |
| **Scholarly leadership** | led by domain experts in **Indology, philology and conservation science**; a full-time Director and a multidisciplinary core team |
| **Technological integration** | an **AI-assisted transcription platform**; **blockchain for provenance tracking**; cloud infrastructure for preservation; **APIs for integration with national and international manuscript archives** |
| **Conservation and capacity building** | **new conservation labs for palm leaf, birch bark and paper manuscripts**; a network of trained grassroots volunteers called **"Pandulipi Mitras"** across approximately **100 districts** |
| **Public engagement** | educational modules for schools and universities; immersive VR/AR experiences; **Manuscript Melas** and nationwide campaigns |
| **Timeline** | **2025-2031**, in three phases: institutional setup with pilot digitization and training rollout; national repository launch and technology platforms going live; outreach scaling and integration with national and international archives |

Four points connect this record directly to the teaching in this session, which is what makes it a genuine linkage rather than decoration.

1. **The three conservation labs are for palm leaf, birch bark and paper** - exactly the three manuscript media identified in section 2, and the same three named in the definition of a script's medium. The policy is organised around the material distinction this lesson teaches.
2. **Provenance tracking** addresses the problem of Lesson 11: a manuscript is mobile and its copy-history is usually unknown, so a chain-of-custody record is the modern answer to the oldest evidentiary weakness of the medium.
3. **Transcription** addresses the problem of Lesson 10: a manuscript may be written in a script that few can now read - Sarada, now rarely used except ceremonially by the Kashmiri Pandit community; Modi, replaced for Marathi in the twentieth century; Nandinagari, used in South India until the nineteenth century; Kaithi; Tirhuta. A holding that cannot be read is, in the terms of Lesson 8, a knowledge resource that is not yet available.
4. **Cataloguing at scale** addresses the survival-bias argument of section 5 prospectively: an unsurveyed manuscript is one whose loss will not even be recorded.

Two disciplines apply to using this record in an answer. **Cite the stable structural elements** - the ministry, the vision, the objectives, the conservation streams, the technology set, the volunteer network and the phased timeline to 2031 - which are the mission's own published description. **Do not cite progress totals.** Reported figures for manuscripts surveyed, digitised or published online differ across accounts and change continuously; this session deliberately records none of them, and an answer is stronger for saying "a phased programme running to 2031 with a stated target of over one crore manuscripts" than for quoting a number that may be wrong by the time it is read.

The predecessor institutional layer is routed rather than restated: **the National Mission for Manuscripts, the Indira Gandhi National Centre for the Arts and the National Archives** hold documentation and custody mandates, and the heritage and institutions topic owns their administrative history and their current status.

### 7. Comparative treatment: four eras of manuscript custody

| Era | Custodian | Mechanism | Failure mode |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Oral** | the *charana*, the bardic class, the Pir | specialised custody; prescribed accuracy in pronunciation, intonation and memorisation | catastrophic loss when a lineage ends |
| **Scribal** | monasteries, courts, temples, professional scribes | recopying onto palm leaf, birch bark, paper | gradual textual change; loss when the institution fails |
| **Colonial and early modern scholarly** | libraries, presses, individual scholars | printing, cataloguing, discovery - Carey's press, Haraprasad Shastri's recovery of the Charyapada in 1916, T. Ganapati Sastri's 1912 rediscovery of Svapnavasavadattam | dispersal; recovery dependent on individual scholarship and chance |
| **Digital and institutional** | a national mission and its repository | survey, catalogue, conservation labs by material, AI-assisted transcription, provenance tracking, public repository | dependence on sustained funding, standards and the readability of legacy scripts |

Row three is worth dwelling on because it contains two dated rediscoveries in this session - **1912** for a Sanskrit play and **1916** for the oldest Bengali literary document. Both were recovered by named scholars within four years of each other, which is a small but genuine illustration of how much of the surviving record is a product of early twentieth-century scholarship.

### Examination use

This lesson is the natural evidence base for any question on conserving India's documentary heritage, and it is one of the few places in this topic where a **current, citable government programme** attaches directly to a classical body of material. The answer shape that works is: the material distinction (palm leaf, birch bark, paper) - the scribal reality (Modi's origin) - the documented survival losses (the recension figures, the fragmentary texts) - the institutional response (the mission's objectives, conservation streams and timeline) - and a qualification about readability and provenance. Objective items in this area tend to attack the institutions rather than the manuscripts, and those belong to the heritage owner.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating conservation as a purely technical matter; the argument in section 5 shows loss is institutional, so the response must be institutional too.
- Quoting progress figures for the current mission; reported totals differ across accounts and change, and this session records none.
- Confusing the mission's target - over one crore manuscripts to be digitized and catalogued - with a claim about how many exist or have been completed.
- Forgetting that a manuscript may be unreadable because its script is obsolete, not because it is damaged.
- Treating "discovery" as composition; the Charyapada was composed in the 10th-11th centuries and recovered in 1916.
- Assuming all Indian manuscripts are on palm leaf; birch bark and paper are equally part of the record, and the conservation streams are organised on exactly that three-way distinction.
- Attributing the administrative history of manuscript institutions to this topic; it belongs to the heritage and institutions owner.

### Revision notes

1. A script's medium, as defined: paper, rocks, birch bark - and the manuscript record adds palm leaf.
2. Medium correlates with region: palm leaf in the south, birch bark in the north and north-west, paper later and general.
3. Silappadikaram is described as a palm leaf manuscript; Vatteluttu was used for centuries in inscriptions and manuscripts of South India.
4. Modi was developed in the 14th century as a shorthand variant of Devanagari by scribes unable to write full Devanagari during rapid writing - direct evidence of scribal working conditions.
5. Kaithi was used for legal, administrative and private records, widely in the Mughal period.
6. The Hamzanama is an illustrated manuscript commissioned by Akbar, showcasing Mughal art and narrative tradition.
7. Documented losses: over 1000 Sama Veda shakhas reduced to 3; 21 Rig Veda shakhas to 1 complete and parts of a second; 9 Atharva Veda shakhas to 2; Katyayana surviving only through references in Patanjali; Devichandraguptam and Valaiapathi surviving only in fragments; no book of the first Sangam surviving; Avestan extinct.
8. Survival can be external and accidental: the Charyapada survived in the Nepal Durbar Library and was recovered by Haraprasad Shastri in 1916; Svapnavasavadattam was rediscovered by T. Ganapati Sastri in 1912.
9. Loss follows the failure of custodial institutions, so it is not random and is therefore addressable.
10. Gyan Bharatam Mission: Ministry of Culture; announced in the Union Budget 2025-26; vision to preserve, digitize and disseminate India's manuscript heritage.
11. Its key objectives: digitize and catalog over 1 crore manuscripts; establish a National Digital Repository; integrate AI, OCR and blockchain for access, transcription and provenance.
12. Its pillars: scholarly leadership in Indology, philology and conservation science; AI-assisted transcription, blockchain provenance, cloud preservation and APIs linking national and international archives; new conservation labs for palm leaf, birch bark and paper; "Pandulipi Mitras" volunteers across about 100 districts; public engagement through educational modules, VR/AR and Manuscript Melas.
13. Its timeline runs 2025-2031 in three phases: institutional setup and pilot digitization; national repository and platform launch; outreach scaling and archive integration.
14. Discipline: cite the mission's structure and targets, not its changing progress totals. Route the National Mission for Manuscripts, IGNCA and the National Archives to the heritage owner.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is the survival record assembled in section 5 the strongest available argument for a manuscript conservation programme?

**Model answer:** Because it converts loss from a fear into a **documented, patterned and therefore addressable** phenomenon. The figures are specific: over a thousand Sama Vedic recensions reduced to three, twenty-one Rig Vedic recensions to one complete survivor and parts of a second, nine Atharva Vedic recensions to two, one of the five great Tamil epics surviving only in fragments, a major Sanskrit grammatical work surviving only as quotation inside its own commentary, and the oldest literary document of Bengali surviving outside India and recovered only in 1916. The pattern behind those figures is **institutional failure** - a custodial lineage, court, monastery or speech community ceasing - rather than random decay. If loss follows institutional failure, then institutional action is the appropriate remedy, which is exactly what a programme of survey, cataloguing, material-specific conservation, transcription and provenance tracking supplies. The argument also runs forward: an unsurveyed manuscript today is one whose eventual loss will not even be recorded.

**Misconception to avoid:** That conservation means preventing physical decay alone. On this evidence the binding constraints are also **cataloguing** - an unrecorded holding cannot be protected or used - and **readability**, since manuscripts written in Sarada, Modi, Nandinagari, Kaithi or Tirhuta may be intact and still unusable without transcription capacity.


### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Assess what the materials and production practices of Indian manuscripts reveal about the regions, institutions and working conditions that produced them. (20 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**Materials carry regional information.** The definition of a script names its media as **paper, rock and birch bark**, and the manuscript record adds **palm leaf**. The distribution is not random: **palm leaf** is the southern and eastern material - **Silappadikaram** is described as a palm leaf manuscript and **Vatteluttu** served southern manuscripts as well as inscriptions; **birch bark** belongs to the north and north-west, and the **Gilgit Manuscripts**, written on birch bark in Gupta and post-Gupta Brahmi, are its clearest survivor; **paper** is later and general. The **Rigveda** entry, preserved on **birch bark and paper**, shows one text crossing two materials. A manuscript's material is therefore evidence of place and period before a word is read.

**Production practices carry institutional information.** Custodians are named and specific - the **Asiatic Society, Kolkata** for the Vimalaprabha, the **L. D. Institute of Indology** for the Shantinatha Charitra, the **French Institute of Pondicherry** for some 11,000 Saiva manuscripts. Illustration marks a workshop: the **Tarikh-e-Khandan-e-Timuriyah** carries **133 Mughal miniatures** by named artists, and the **Hamzanama** was an illustrated manuscript commissioned by Akbar.

**Practices carry working conditions.** **Modi** was created in the fourteenth century as a **shorthand variant of Devanagari by scribes unable to write full Devanagari during rapid writing**, and **Kaithi** served legal, administrative and private records. A writing system generated by time pressure is direct evidence of the copyist's working day.

**Verdict.** Material, custody and script together let a manuscript be located, institutionally placed and socially read before its text is consulted.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 20: **6** for the material-to-region mapping with a named survivor for each of the three media; **5** for institutional evidence, requiring at least three named custodians or workshops; **5** for the working-conditions argument, of which 3 depend on the Modi scribal origin; **4** for a verdict that treats the physical object as readable evidence in its own right. Describing the materials without linking them to region, institution or labour caps at 9.

## Lesson 22 - Documentary-heritage recognition, and the two registers that are constantly conflated

Progress: 22/23 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the three levels of the Memory of the World programme, India's International Register inscriptions, India's Asia-Pacific Regional Register inscriptions, the composition-versus-inscription discipline, and the firewall between UNESCO programmes

CA found: Official record verified against the Ministry of Culture's Memory of the World page - the programme operates at international, regional and national levels; India's regional inscriptions are distinct from its International Register inscriptions. Recorded as a status verification, not as the session's single current linkage, which is reserved for Lesson 21

### Visual: one programme, three levels - and the error that ignores them

```text
        UNESCO MEMORY OF THE WORLD PROGRAMME (initiated 1992)
        to preserve documentary heritage and safeguard against
        "collective amnesia"
                              |
        +---------------------+---------------------+
        |                     |                     |
  INTERNATIONAL          REGIONAL              NATIONAL
  REGISTER               REGISTERS             REGISTERS
  globally significant   regional cultural     local heritage,
  documentary heritage   relevance             national committees
  nominations judged     for Asia-Pacific:     and grassroots
  by the INTERNATIONAL   MOWCAP - the Memory   participation
  ADVISORY COMMITTEE     of the World          |
  (IAC) and the          Committee for Asia    |
  REGISTER SUB-          and the Pacific       |
  COMMITTEE (RSC)        |                     |
        |                |                     |
  INDIA: inscriptions    INDIA: THREE          INDIA: nodal centre
  from 1997 onwards,     inscriptions,         for all levels is the
  most recently the      inscribed 2024,       IGNCA, designated in
  BHAGAVADGITA and       first submitted 2023  2014
  NATYASHASTRA (2025)    |
        |                RAMACARITAMANASA (illustrated manuscripts)
        |                SAHRDAYALOKA-LOCANA
        |                PANCATANTRA (15th-century manuscript)
        |
  THE ERROR THIS DIAGRAM PREVENTS
  Calling the three 2024 inscriptions "UNESCO Memory of the World
  Register" entries WITHOUT SAYING WHICH REGISTER. They are on the
  ASIA-PACIFIC REGIONAL REGISTER, not the INTERNATIONAL REGISTER.
  Register level is part of the fact, exactly as a date is.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Three errors, all common and all costly. The first is treating a **recognition year** as a **composition date** - saying the Rigveda "dates from 2007" is the extreme version, but the softer version, implying that more recently inscribed entries are newer texts, is just as wrong and much harder to spot. The second is treating **Memory of the World** as interchangeable with **World Heritage** or **Intangible Cultural Heritage**, which are separate programmes with different objects. The third is the one most candidates never even notice: treating **"the Memory of the World Register"** as a single list, when the programme runs **three registers at different levels**, and India holds inscriptions on two of them.

### 2. What the programme is, and how it is structured

**UNESCO initiated the Memory of the World Programme in 1992** to preserve documentary heritage and safeguard against **collective amnesia**. Its vision rests on three principles: **universal belonging, complete protection, and permanent accessibility** of documentary heritage.

It **operates at international, regional and national levels**:

- the **International Register** recognises **globally significant** documents; nominations are evaluated by the **International Advisory Committee (IAC)** and the **Register Subcommittee (RSC)**;
- **regional registers**, of which **MOWCAP** - the Memory of the World Committee for Asia and the Pacific - is the one relevant to India, identify documents with **regional cultural relevance**;
- **national registers** allow countries to preserve local heritage through dedicated committees.

India's engagement is led by the **Indira Gandhi National Centre for the Arts (IGNCA)**, designated the **Nodal Centre since 2014**, which facilitates nominations, conserves rare manuscripts and runs awareness and archival-management work.

Two features of the register itself follow. Its object is **documentary heritage** - a document, an archive, a manuscript collection - and **not a site or a practice**. And it is judged on significance rather than prestige, which is why it contains colonial administrative archives alongside sacred texts.

### 3. India on the **International** Register

The official Indian record itemises these inscriptions, with the institution that submitted or holds each. Learn the custodian alongside the entry: an item that pairs a manuscript with the wrong institution is as easy to set as one that pairs it with the wrong year.

| Entry | Year | Custodian or submitting institution, and what it is |
|---|---:|---|
| **I.A.S. Tamil Medical Manuscript Collection** | 1997 | Institute of Asian Studies, Chennai - **564 palm-leaf manuscripts**, mostly nineteenth century, on Siddha and Ayurveda: herbal remedies, disease treatments and ritual chants |
| **Saiva Manuscripts of Pondicherry** | 2005 | French Institute of Pondicherry - the largest collection of **Saiva Siddhanta** texts, about **11,000 manuscripts** collected between 1956 and 1990, housed at the Ecole Francaise d'Extreme-Orient, Puducherry |
| **The Rigveda** | 2007 | National Mission for Manuscripts, New Delhi, and the Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute, Pune - preserved **on birch bark and paper** |
| **Tarikh-e-Khandan-e-Timuriyah** | 2011 | Khuda Bakhsh Oriental Public Library, Patna - a sixteenth-century illustrated manuscript **commissioned by Akbar**, with **133 Mughal miniatures** by court artists including **Daswant, Basawan and Manohar** |
| **Vimalaprabha Laghukalacakratantrarajatika** | 2011 | Asiatic Society, Kolkata - a **palm-leaf** manuscript, the primary commentary on the **Kalachakra Tantra**; brought to India in **967 CE** and to Tibet in **1027 CE** |
| **Shantinatha Charitra** | 2013 | L. D. Institute of Indology, Ahmedabad - a **fourteenth-century Jain** manuscript in **Devanagari script**, with **10 Jain miniature paintings**, among the oldest known specimens |
| **Archives of the Dutch East India Company** | 2003 | National Archives of India with the **Netherlands, Indonesia, South Africa and Sri Lanka** - a **joint nomination**; records of trade, treaties and governance, **1602-1795 CE**; India's segment is located in Tamil Nadu |
| **The Gilgit Manuscripts** | 2017 | National Archives of India - the oldest surviving Buddhist texts, **discovered in 1931** in Gilgit on the Silk Route, written **on birch bark in Gupta and post-Gupta Brahmi**, fifth-sixth centuries CE |
| **The Maitreyavyakarana** | 2017 | Asiatic Society, Kolkata - a tenth-century Buddhist manuscript prophesying the future arrival of **Maitreya Buddha** |
| **The Abhinavagupta Manuscripts Collection** | 2023 | IGNCA - **248 manuscripts** on **palm leaf, birch and paper**, on philosophy, tantra, Kashmir Shaivism and aesthetics |
| **First Summit Meeting of the Non-Aligned Movement** | 2023 | IGNCA with **Algeria, Egypt, Indonesia and Serbia** - a **joint nomination**; textual files, photographs and films of the **1961 Belgrade Summit** |
| **Manuscript Collection of the Bhagavadgita** | 2025 | IGNCA; preserved at the Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute, Pune - **700 verses in 18 chapters**, embedded in the **Bhismaparva** of the Mahabharata |
| **Manuscript of the Natyasastra of Bharatamuni** | 2025 | IGNCA; preserved at the Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute, Pune - codified around the **second century BC**, an epitome of the **Natyaveda**, an oral body of performing-art knowledge of **36,000 verses** known as the **Gandharvaveda** |

**On the total.** The figure officially and widely reported after the April 2025 additions is **fourteen** Indian inscriptions on the International Register. The official itemised list above names **thirteen**. This session therefore **does not assert a total as a taught fact**: it teaches the **named entries with their years and custodians**, notes that the reported figure is fourteen, and records the difference between the reported figure and the itemised list as an **open item** in the ledger rather than filling it by guesswork. That is the same discipline as Lesson 7's treatment of a dated status, applied to a count.

### 4. India on the **Asia-Pacific Regional** Register

This is the section that corrects the most consequential error in the core source's table.

Nominations for the MOWCAP Regional Register opened in **2007**, but **until 2023 India submitted no documentary heritage to the regional register at all**. India's **first regional submissions were made in 2023** and were **successfully inscribed in 2024**. There are **three**:

| Entry | Inscribed | Submitted by, and what it is |
|---|---:|---|
| **The Illustrated Manuscripts of Ramacaritamanasa by Tulasidasa** | 2024 | IGNCA and the **National Museum** - an illustrated manuscript with intricate miniature paintings, preserving the literary and devotional essence of Tulsidas's Awadhi work |
| **The Manuscript of the Sahrdayaloka-Locana, a Text of Indian Poetics** | 2024 | IGNCA; at the Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute, Pune - a text of Indian poetics analysing aesthetics, literary theory and **rasa** |
| **The 15th Century Manuscript of the Pancatantra Fables** | 2024 | IGNCA; at the Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute, Pune - a rare fifteenth-century manuscript of the animal fables and moral stories |

**Three consequences for an answer.**

1. **Cite the register level.** "The Ramcharitmanas, the Panchatantra and the Sahrdayaloka-Locana were inscribed on the **Memory of the World Asia-Pacific Regional Register** in 2024" is correct; dropping "Asia-Pacific Regional" makes it wrong.
2. **The regional participation is recent and is itself the point.** India entered the regional register only in 2023-24, and the official framing describes this as marking India's **growing regional participation**. A question about India's documentary-heritage strategy is better answered with that fact than with a longer list of international entries.
3. **Do not upgrade a regional inscription.** Regional significance is the stated basis of the MOWCAP register; international significance is the stated basis of the International Register. Describing a regional entry as globally recognised misstates the decision that was actually taken.

### 5. Reading the core source's sixteen-row table correctly

The core art source prints one table headed as India's Memory of the World entries, states a total of **fourteen**, and lists **sixteen rows**. The official record explains most of that table, and the explanation is more useful than the arithmetic.

| What the source's table actually mixes | Rows |
|---|---|
| **International Register inscriptions** | Bhagavad Gita (2025); Natyashastra (2025); Abhinavagupta Manuscripts (2023); Gilgit Manuscript (2017); Maitreyavyakarana (2017); Shantinatha Charitra (2013); Tarikh-e-Khandan-e-Timuriyah (2011); Laghukalachakratantrarajatika Vimalaprabha (2011); Rigveda (2007); Shaiva Manuscript in Pondicherry (2005); Archives of the Dutch East India Company (2003); I.A.S. Tamil Medical Manuscript Collection (1997) |
| **Asia-Pacific Regional Register inscriptions, printed without the distinction** | Ramcharitmanas (2024); Panchatantra (2024); Sahrdayaloka-Locana (2024) |
| **Present in the official record but absent from the source's table** | the **First Summit Meeting of the Non-Aligned Movement** archives (2023), a joint nomination with Algeria, Egypt, Indonesia and Serbia |
| **Printed in the source's table but not traceable in the official Indian record** | a **"Saang Lok Collection" (2023)**, described there as materials on traditional folk art and performance. This session **does not teach it as an inscription**; it is recorded in the ledger as unverified |

```text
  HOW TO WRITE THIS IN AN ANSWER
  DO:    name the ENTRY + the REGISTER LEVEL + the YEAR.
         "The Rigveda was inscribed on the Memory of the World
          INTERNATIONAL Register in 2007; the 15th-century
          Pancatantra manuscript on the ASIA-PACIFIC REGIONAL
          Register in 2024."
  DO:    name the CUSTODIAN when the question is about conservation
         rather than about literature.
  DO NOT: write "UNESCO Memory of the World Register" with no level.
  DO NOT: assert a running total as a timeless fact.
  WHY:   a named entry with its register and year is verifiable and
         cannot go stale; a bare total is time-sensitive, and here the
         reported figure and the itemised official list do not match.
```

The general rule this episode teaches is the one that runs through the whole session: **when a printed aggregate cannot be reconciled with the itemised record, the named evidence is usable and the aggregate is not.**

### 6. The composition-versus-inscription discipline

The rule, with its evidence:

- **The Rigveda** is the oldest sacred text of the Vedic tradition, with the Vedic corpus estimated by historians at around 1500-1000 BCE (Lesson 12); its International Register inscription year is **2007**.
- **The Archives of the Dutch East India Company** are colonial-era records of 1602-1795; their inscription year is **2003** - **four years earlier** than the Rigveda's.
- **The manuscripts of the Bhagavadgita and the Natyasastra**, both ancient, were inscribed in **2025** - the **latest** recognitions for among the **oldest** texts.
- **The Gilgit Manuscripts** were written in the **fifth-sixth centuries CE**, **discovered in 1931**, and **inscribed in 2017** - three separate dates on one object.

Therefore: **the inscription-year sequence does not track composition chronology at all.** Any answer citing a register entry must give **composition era and inscription year separately** and must never imply that later recognition means later composition.

The same discipline has already appeared in this session in other forms: the **Chebrolu inscription** was issued in AD 207 and discovered in 2019 (Lesson 3), and the **Charyapada** was composed in the tenth-eleventh centuries and recovered in 1916 (Lesson 19). Issue, discovery, recovery, publication and recognition are five separate dates.

### 7. The generic range of India's documentary heritage

A second recurring error is to assume India's recognised documentary heritage consists of ancient religious texts. The official record refutes it directly, and naming two or three different types is what shows an answer has read the list rather than the famous entries.

| Register type | Entries, with register level |
|---|---|
| **Devotional and philosophical** | Bhagavadgita manuscripts (International, 2025); Rigveda (International, 2007); Ramacaritamanasa (Regional, 2024) |
| **Aesthetic and theoretical** | Natyasastra (International, 2025); Abhinavagupta Manuscripts (International, 2023); Sahrdayaloka-Locana (Regional, 2024) |
| **Didactic and narrative** | Pancatantra manuscript (Regional, 2024) |
| **Buddhist** | Gilgit Manuscripts, Maitreyavyakarana (International, 2017); Vimalaprabha Laghukalacakratantrarajatika (International, 2011) |
| **Jain** | Shantinatha Charitra (International, 2013) |
| **Shaiva sectarian** | Saiva Manuscripts of Pondicherry (International, 2005) |
| **Scientific and medical** | I.A.S. Tamil Medical Manuscript Collection (International, 1997) - Siddha and Ayurveda |
| **Political and dynastic chronicle** | Tarikh-e-Khandan-e-Timuriyah (International, 2011) - Persian |
| **Colonial administrative archive** | Archives of the Dutch East India Company (International, 2003) - joint nomination |
| **Twentieth-century diplomatic archive** | First Summit Meeting of the Non-Aligned Movement (International, 2023) - joint nomination, 1961 Belgrade Summit |

Ten types. The two that make the point most sharply are the **colonial administrative archive** and the **twentieth-century diplomatic archive**, because neither is a literary text at all - which shows that "documentary heritage" is a category defined by the document, not by the prestige of its contents. Note also that **two of the ten are joint nominations**, so India's recognised heritage includes documents it holds jointly with other states.

### 8. The programme firewall

Four recognitions are regularly confused, and the first of them has two levels.

| Programme, level or instrument | Object | What it recognises |
|---|---|---|
| **Memory of the World - International Register** | a document, archive or manuscript collection | documentary heritage of **global** significance; judged by the IAC and the Register Subcommittee |
| **Memory of the World - Asia-Pacific Regional Register (MOWCAP)** | the same kinds of object | documentary heritage of **regional** cultural relevance |
| **World Heritage** | a **place** - a site or property | cultural, natural or mixed heritage of outstanding universal value, under the **World Heritage Convention of 1972**, governed by the **World Heritage Committee** |
| **Intangible Cultural Heritage** | a **living practice** | safeguarding of practices, representations, expressions, knowledge and skills |
| **Geographical Indication** | a **product tied to an origin** | a domestic intellectual-property instrument, **not a UNESCO programme at all** |

The heritage and institutions topic owns the third, fourth and fifth of these in full, together with their current totals and administrative machinery. What this session owns is the **documentary** programme and both its registers - and the firewall itself, because a manuscript wrongly described as a World Heritage Site, or a regional inscription wrongly described as an international one, is a factual error inside a literature answer.

One further detail explains the difference in kind: the World Heritage system rests on a **treaty**, the World Heritage Convention of 1972, administered by an intergovernmental committee that also maintains a **List of World Heritage in Danger**. Memory of the World is a **register programme**, not a treaty-based property regime, and the two carry different legal consequences. The detail of that regime belongs to the heritage owner and is routed there.

### Examination use

Objective items here attack the inscription years of named entries, the distinction between the UNESCO programmes and - increasingly - the **register level**, since the three best-known recent Indian entries are regional rather than international. They can also attack the total, which is precisely why the named-entry-plus-level discipline in section 5 is the safe strategy. For Mains, this lesson supplies three high-value blocks: the **composition-versus-inscription** argument, which demonstrates chronological discipline in one sentence; the **generic-range** table, which converts "India has a rich manuscript heritage" into a claim evidenced by ten kinds of document at two register levels; and the **custodian column**, which lets a conservation answer name institutions rather than "the government". The best closing line for a documentary-heritage answer is that the value of the heritage lies as much in what has been catalogued, conserved and inscribed as in what was originally written.

### Traps introduced here

- **Naming a Memory of the World entry without its register level.** The Ramcharitmanas, Panchatantra and Sahrdayaloka-Locana manuscripts are on the **Asia-Pacific Regional Register**, inscribed 2024, not on the International Register.
- Treating an inscription year as a composition date, or as a discovery date.
- Implying that later-inscribed entries are later texts; the 2025 entries are among the oldest and the 2003 entry is among the most recent documents.
- Asserting a running total of Indian entries without a date, or reconciling a printed table with a reported figure by guesswork.
- Forgetting that two Indian International Register inscriptions are **joint nominations** - the Dutch East India Company archives and the Non-Aligned Movement summit archives.
- Describing India's entries as ancient religious texts; they include a colonial administrative archive, a Persian dynastic chronicle, a twentieth-century diplomatic archive and a medical compendium.
- Confusing Memory of the World with World Heritage, Intangible Cultural Heritage or Geographical Indication.
- Assigning the Ramcharitmanas to standard Hindi; it is Tulsidas's **Awadhi** work.
- Treating the Natyasastra's 2025 inscription as saying anything about the treatise's date; it is recorded as codified around the second century BC.
- Assuming India has always participated in the regional register; its first regional submissions were made in **2023** and inscribed in **2024**.

### Revision notes

1. The Memory of the World Programme was initiated by UNESCO in **1992** to preserve documentary heritage and guard against collective amnesia; its principles are universal belonging, complete protection and permanent accessibility.
2. It operates at **three levels**: the **International Register** (global significance; nominations judged by the **International Advisory Committee** and the **Register Subcommittee**), **regional registers** such as **MOWCAP** for Asia and the Pacific, and **national registers**.
3. India's nodal centre for the programme is the **IGNCA**, designated in **2014**.
4. India's **International Register** entries, with years and custodians: I.A.S. Tamil Medical Manuscript Collection (1997, Institute of Asian Studies, Chennai, 564 palm-leaf manuscripts); Saiva Manuscripts of Pondicherry (2005, French Institute of Pondicherry, about 11,000 manuscripts); Rigveda (2007, NMM and BORI Pune, on birch bark and paper); Tarikh-e-Khandan-e-Timuriyah (2011, Khuda Bakhsh Library Patna, commissioned by Akbar, 133 miniatures by Daswant, Basawan and Manohar); Vimalaprabha Laghukalacakratantrarajatika (2011, Asiatic Society Kolkata, palm leaf, to India 967 CE and Tibet 1027 CE); Shantinatha Charitra (2013, L. D. Institute of Indology, 14th century, Devanagari, 10 miniatures); Archives of the Dutch East India Company (2003, National Archives of India, joint with the Netherlands, Indonesia, South Africa and Sri Lanka, 1602-1795); Gilgit Manuscripts (2017, National Archives, discovered 1931, birch bark, Gupta and post-Gupta Brahmi); Maitreyavyakarana (2017, Asiatic Society, 10th century); Abhinavagupta Manuscripts Collection (2023, IGNCA, 248 manuscripts on palm leaf, birch and paper); First Summit Meeting of the Non-Aligned Movement (2023, joint with Algeria, Egypt, Indonesia and Serbia, 1961 Belgrade Summit); Manuscript Collection of the Bhagavadgita (2025, IGNCA, BORI Pune, 700 verses in 18 chapters in the Bhismaparva); Manuscript of the Natyasastra (2025, IGNCA, BORI Pune, codified around the 2nd century BC, 36,000 verses of the Gandharvaveda).
5. India's **Asia-Pacific Regional Register** entries, all **inscribed 2024** and first submitted in **2023**: the Illustrated Manuscripts of **Ramacaritamanasa** (IGNCA and the National Museum); the **Sahrdayaloka-Locana** (IGNCA, BORI Pune); and the **15th-century Pancatantra Fables** manuscript (IGNCA, BORI Pune). Regional nominations opened in 2007 and India submitted none until 2023.
6. **Two Indian International Register entries are joint nominations**: the Dutch East India Company archives and the Non-Aligned Movement summit archives.
7. The reported total of Indian International Register inscriptions after the April 2025 additions is **fourteen**; the official itemised list names **thirteen**. Cite named entries with register level and year rather than a total.
8. The core source prints one table of sixteen rows that mixes International and Regional inscriptions, omits the Non-Aligned Movement archives, and includes a "Saang Lok Collection" that is not traceable in the official Indian record.
9. Inscription years do not track composition chronology: Rigveda inscribed 2007, colonial archives 2003, and the oldest texts inscribed most recently in 2025.
10. Five separate dates recur: issue, discovery, recovery, publication and recognition. The Gilgit Manuscripts carry three at once - written 5th-6th centuries CE, discovered 1931, inscribed 2017.
11. The range covers devotional, aesthetic, didactic, Buddhist, Jain, Shaiva, medical, chronicle, colonial-administrative and twentieth-century diplomatic documentation.
12. Firewall: Memory of the World International Register (global documentary significance); MOWCAP Asia-Pacific Regional Register (regional relevance); World Heritage (places, 1972 Convention, World Heritage Committee, List of World Heritage in Danger); Intangible Cultural Heritage (living practices); Geographical Indication (a domestic intellectual-property instrument).
13. The heritage and institutions topic owns World Heritage, Intangible Cultural Heritage, Geographical Indications and their current totals.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "The Ramcharitmanas, the Panchatantra and the Sahrdayaloka-Locana were added to the UNESCO Memory of the World Register in 2024, taking India's tally higher." What is wrong, and what is the correct formulation?

**Model answer:** The year is right and the register level is missing, which makes the sentence wrong. All three were inscribed on the **Memory of the World Asia-Pacific Regional Register**, administered by **MOWCAP**, which recognises documentary heritage of **regional** cultural relevance - not on the **International Register**, which recognises documents of global significance and is judged by the International Advisory Committee and the Register Subcommittee. They were India's **first regional submissions**, made in 2023 and inscribed in 2024, after India had submitted nothing to the regional register since nominations opened in 2007. The correct formulation is: "In 2024 India's first three inscriptions on the Memory of the World **Asia-Pacific Regional Register** were made - the illustrated Ramacaritamanasa manuscripts, the Sahrdayaloka-Locana and a fifteenth-century Pancatantra manuscript - nominated by the IGNCA." The phrase "taking India's tally higher" should also be dropped, because it merges two separate registers into one count.

**Misconception to avoid:** That UNESCO recognition is a single undifferentiated honour. The programme runs international, regional and national registers with different significance thresholds, and the level at which a document is inscribed is part of the fact - as much as its year, its custodian and the difference between its composition date and its inscription date.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Explain the three-level structure of the Memory of the World programme and assess what India's pattern of participation at each level shows. (15 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**The structure.** The programme, initiated in **1992**, operates at **three levels**. The **International Register** recognises documentary heritage of **global** significance, with nominations evaluated by the **International Advisory Committee** and the **Register Subcommittee**. **Regional registers** - for Asia and the Pacific, **MOWCAP** - recognise documents of **regional cultural relevance**. **National registers** allow states to record local heritage. India's nodal centre for all three is the **IGNCA**, designated in **2014**.

**India's pattern.** Internationally, participation is **long and steady**: from the **I.A.S. Tamil Medical Manuscript Collection in 1997**, through the **Rigveda in 2007**, the **Gilgit Manuscripts and Maitreyavyakarana in 2017**, the **Abhinavagupta Manuscripts and the Non-Aligned Movement archives in 2023**, to the **Bhagavadgita and Natyasastra manuscripts in 2025**. Regionally, participation is **new**: MOWCAP nominations opened in **2007**, India submitted nothing until **2023**, and its first three inscriptions - the illustrated **Ramacaritamanasa**, the **Sahrdayaloka-Locana** and a fifteenth-century **Pancatantra** manuscript - were made in **2024**.

**Assessment.** Three things follow. India treated the **global** register as the only worthwhile arena for nearly three decades. The regional turn is **recent and deliberate**, and is described officially as marking growing regional participation. And the split matters for accuracy: three of India's best-known recent inscriptions are **regional**, so an answer that omits the level misstates what was granted.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 15: **5** for the three levels with the evaluating bodies and the IGNCA's role; **5** for the two participation patterns, requiring at least three dated International entries and all three Regional ones; **5** for the assessment, at 2 for the three-decade gap, 1 for the deliberate regional turn and 2 for the accuracy consequence. Any answer that treats "the Memory of the World Register" as one list caps at 6.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

By this point the material is in place. What remains is the discipline that keeps it from going wrong under examination pressure, where the temptation is always to complete a half-remembered pattern. Every one of the five controls exists because a specific, recurring error exists, and each control is a single question you can ask of your own sentence before you write it.

### 2. Control one: attribution

**The rule: never answer a work-author item by recognising a famous name. Recall the work's content, and check that the content matches the author's recorded profile.**

The planted confusion sets, gathered from across this session:

| Confusion set | The separation |
|---|---|
| **Charudatta and the clay cart** | **Bhasa** wrote **Daridra Charudatta**, a precursor; **Sudraka** wrote **Mrichchhakatika** |
| **Three Vasavadattas** | **Subandhu** - the prose romance **Vasavadatta**, in champu style; **Bhasa** - **Svapnavasavadattam**; **Sudraka** - **Vinavasavadatta** |
| **Two Somadevas** | **Somadeva Bhatta** of Kashmir - **Kathasaritsagara**; **Somadeva Suri** - the Jain **Upasakadyayana** and **Nitivakyamrita** |
| **Two epics of combat** | **Bharavi** - **Kiratarjuniyam** (Arjuna and Shiva as a hunter); **Magha** - **Shishupalavadha** (Krishna kills Shishupala) |
| **Two Firoz Shahi chronicles** | **Barani** - **Tarikh-e-Firoz Shahi**, history to Firoz Shah Tughlaq, plus the political treatise Fatwa-i-Jahandari; **Shams-i-Siraj Afif** - **Tarikh-i-Firoz Shahi**, on social, economic and welfare policies |
| **Two chronicles of Shah Jahan** | **Inayat Khan** - **Shahjahannama**; **Abdul Hamid Lahori** - **Padshahnama**, the official chronicle |
| **Two Alamgir histories** | **Khawas Khan** - **Alamgirnama**, continued by **Mirza Muhammad Kazim**; **Saqi Mustad Khan** - **Maasir-i-Alamgiri** |
| **Three Mahapuranas / Adipuranas** | **Pushpadanta** - Apabhramsha **Mahapurana**; **Jinasena** - **Mahapurana** and **Adipurana**; **Pampa** - the Kannada **Adipurana**; and **Gunabhadra** - **Adipurana** in the Rashtrakuta court ledger |
| **Two Ramcharitas** | **Sandhyakar Nandi's Ramcharita**, the Bengali *dvyasraya-kavya* on King Ramapala; and **Tulsidas's Ramcharitmanas**, the Awadhi retelling |
| **Two Kalpa Sutras** | **Bhadrabahu's Kalpa Sutra**, the Jain text of Tirthankara biographies; and **Kalpasutra** as one of the six **Vedangas**, on sacrificial practice |
| **Two Amarasimha contributions** | the five-subject scheme for the **Upapuranas**; and the **Amarakosha** compiled in the court of Chandragupta II |
| **Two Jayadevas' contexts** | **Jayadeva's Gita Govinda** as Sanskrit devotional kavya; and the same work in the Odia tradition, sung nightly at the Jagannatha temple in Odissi music style |

### 3. Control two: chronology

**The rule: name which of the eight possible dates you are giving.** Composition, oral transmission, redaction, earliest surviving manuscript, first inscription in the language, critical edition or publication, discovery or recovery, and formal recognition are eight separate events.

The worked cases in this session:

| Object | The dates it carries |
|---|---|
| **Chebrolu inscription** | issued **AD 207**; discovered **2019**; described as earliest datable South Indian Sanskrit inscription **"so far"** |
| **Rigveda** | Old Indo-Aryan develops around **1500 BC**; compilation estimated by historians at **1500-1200 BC**; Memory of the World inscription **2007** |
| **Charyapada** | composed **10th-11th centuries**; recovered in the Nepal Durbar Library **1916** |
| **Svapnavasavadattam** | composed by Bhasa, among the earliest Sanskrit dramatists; **rediscovered 1912** by T. Ganapati Sastri |
| **Shah Jo Risalo** | poems by Shah Abdul Latif, **17th century**; compiled by disciples; **published 1866** |
| **Sangam corpus** | mostly composed **and compiled** between **300 BC and 300 AD**; the Ettuthokai and Pattuppattu are compilations |
| **Manuscripts of the Bhagavad Gita** | ancient composition; **2025** recognition |

**Master chronology strip**, for ordering items. Each anchor carries its own hedge and none is offered as a precise year.

```text
 1936      KAMAYANI
 1963      OFFICIAL LANGUAGES ACT
 1967/1992/2003  Eighth Schedule expansions to 15, 18, 22
 2004      CLASSICAL LANGUAGE category created; Tamil 12 Oct 2004
 2005      criteria revised (Nov); Sanskrit 25 Nov 2005
 2008/2013/2014  Telugu and Kannada 31 Oct 2008; Malayalam 8 Aug
            2013; Odia 1 Mar 2014
 2024      criteria revised again; five languages added by Cabinet
            decision of 3 Oct 2024, to eleven
 1997-2025  India's Memory of the World INTERNATIONAL Register
            inscriptions; 2024 India's first three ASIA-PACIFIC
            REGIONAL Register inscriptions
 2025-2031  the current manuscript mission's phased timeline
```

### 4. Control three: region

**The rule: check the region claim separately from the family claim, because a pairing item evaluates them independently.**

| Planted regional confusion | The correction |
|---|---|
| Dravidian means southern | **Brahui** is Dravidian and is spoken in **Baluchistan**; Malto and Kurukh are in Bengal, Odisha, Bihar and Madhya Pradesh |
| Kharosthi is an Indian script generally | it belongs to **Gandhara** - present-day Afghanistan and Pakistan - in the **north-west** |
| Sarada is a general Sanskrit script | once more widespread, later **restricted to Kashmir**, now largely ceremonial among the Kashmiri Pandit community |
| Grantha is a Tamil-only script | used by Tamil speakers in **Tamil Nadu and Kerala**, to write **Sanskrit** and **Manipravalam** |
| Nandinagari is a northern Nagari variant | used in **South India** - southern Maharashtra, Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh, Tamil Nadu - from the 7th to the 19th centuries |
| Maharashtri Prakrit is confined to Maharashtra | used widely in **western and southern India**; the official language of the Satavahanas |
| Ahom is an Assamese language | a **Tai** language historically spoken in Assam, extinct in the subcontinent |
| Meitei is Indo-Aryan | **Sino-Tibetan**, the most important language of the Kuki-Garo-Bodo-Mikir-Naga sub-group, spoken mainly in **Manipur** |
| Romani is spoken in India | the only New Indo-Aryan language spoken **exclusively outside** the Indian subcontinent |

### 5. Control four: terminology

**The rule: family, language, dialect, register, script, calligraphic hand, genre, and each of the five statuses are distinct terms and none is a synonym for another.**

| Wrong formulation | Correct formulation |
|---|---|
| "Hindi is our national language" | no national language exists; Hindi in Devanagari script is the **official language of the Union** under Article 343(1) |
| "English is an associate national language" | at Union level, English has the status of **subsidiary official language** under the Official Languages Act, 1963 |
| "Ashokan edicts are in Sanskrit and Devanagari" | mostly **Prakrit** in **Brahmi**; **Kharosthi** in the north-west; **Greek and Aramaic** at Kandahar |
| "Prakrit is a dialect of Sanskrit" | Prakrit is the broad term for the **Middle Indo-Aryan** group and a **register** contrast to Sanskrit, not a local variety of it |
| "Nastaliq is the Urdu script" | Nastaliq is a **calligraphic hand** for the Perso-Arabic script; the Urdu script is a modification of the Persian alphabet |
| "Shahmukhi and Gurmukhi are variants of the same script" | **Shahmukhi** is the extended Urdu script used for Punjabi and Saraiki; **Gurmukhi** descends from **Sarada** and was standardised by Guru Angad |
| "All Indic scripts are abugidas" | **Ol Chiki** is a **true alphabet**, giving vowels equal representation |
| "Sanskrit is the mother of Indian languages" | within the **Indo-Aryan branch**, Old Indo-Aryan is ancestral; Dravidian, Austro-Asiatic and Sino-Tibetan have separate ancestries |
| "Brahmi is the mother of Indian scripts" (unqualified) | true of most Indian scripts and of all surviving Indic scripts in Southeast Asia, but the **Urdu script** derives from Arabic through Persian and **Santhali** and others use independent scripts |
| "Classical language" as a compliment | an **administrative recognition** granted from 2004 against published criteria, revised in November 2005 and again in 2024 |
| "A Memory of the World entry is a World Heritage Site" | documentary heritage, not a place; and state the register level - International or Asia-Pacific Regional - see the firewall in Lesson 22 |

### 6. Control five: status and currency

**The rule: every total in this topic is dated, and the date belongs in the sentence.**

| Total | The dated formulation |
|---|---|
| Eighth Schedule | **22 at present**, after expansions by the 21st (1967), 71st (1992) and 92nd (2003) Amendment Acts |
| Classical languages | **eleven**, following the recognitions of 2004, 2005, 2008, 2013, 2014 and **October 2024**; verified for this session as unchanged since |
| Memory of the World entries from India | the reported figure for the **International Register** after the 2025 additions is **fourteen**, while the official itemised list names thirteen; three further Indian entries sit on the **Asia-Pacific Regional Register** (2024). Cite **named entries with their register level and year** instead of a total |
| Languages endangered, vulnerable or extinct | **197** on the World Atlas of Languages - 81 vulnerable, 62 definitely endangered, 7 severely endangered, 42 critically endangered, 5 extinct |
| Manuscripts targeted by the current mission | a stated objective to digitize and catalog **over 1 crore**, in a programme phased to **2031**; progress totals deliberately not cited |

### 7. The directive and demand map

| If the question says | It is really testing | Do not write |
|---|---|---|
| *Language families and India's linguistic diversity* | that family, language, script, register and legal status are five separate classifications | treating all five as one |
| *Classical-language status* | what the status is, what it is not, the criteria **in force** and the dated list | quoting the superseded original-and-not-borrowed criterion, or confusing the status with the Eighth Schedule |
| *Sanskrit and Prakrit* | coexisting registers with different social functions | "Prakrit came after Sanskrit" |
| *The development of Indian scripts* | descent, direction, type, decipherment and the language-script separation | a list of script names |
| *Sangam literature as a historical source* | rich social and economic evidence bounded by the absence of connected political history | treating it as a dynastic chronicle |
| *The rise of vernacular literature* | expanded literary publics, with Sanskrit and Persian continuing | a zero-sum replacement story |
| *Bhakti and literature* | the mechanism - rejection of a restricted register in favour of the people's language - with named regional instances | a list of saints |
| *Persian literary sources and their age* | genre range, patronage, authorship including women, and the translation programme | a list of chronicles |
| *India's documentary heritage* | Memory of the World as a documentary programme with two register levels, and composition date separated from inscription year | treating manuscripts as World Heritage Sites, or omitting the register level |
| *Manuscript conservation* | survey, cataloguing, material-specific conservation, transcription, provenance and access | "manuscripts should be preserved" |
| *A dynasty's contribution to literature* | named author, named text, the patronage link and what the text evidences | "literature flourished under their patronage" |
| *Inscriptions as a source* | what the medium can and cannot prove, and the language-and-script choice it records | a retelling of the inscription's content |

### 8. Mark-scaled structures

| Marks | Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | thesis stated in one line -> the classification or claim stated precisely -> **two named texts or authors** with what each evidences -> **one caution** on dating, attribution or status -> a one-line verdict answering the directive |
| **15** | thesis -> the family, register or period frame -> **corpus-by-corpus or region-by-region evidence** -> the **mechanism** that explains the pattern (patronage, devotional turn, print, institutional custody) -> a dated status or recognition anchor -> verdict |
| **20** | all of the above -> **plus** script history and epigraphic evidence -> **plus** the translation and multilingual interaction dimension -> **plus** the documentary-heritage and institutional layer -> **plus** the dating and attribution discipline made visible -> verdict answering the exact wording |

### Examination use

This lesson is the answer-construction layer for everything above it. Its five controls are also a **self-marking checklist**: read your own paragraph and ask whether each named work has a verified author, each date has a named type, each region has been checked separately from its family, each term has been used precisely, and each total carries its date. A paragraph that survives all five is defensible even where the examiner knows more than the candidate.

### Traps introduced here

- Completing a half-remembered pattern instead of checking content against the author's profile.
- Giving a date without naming what the date is a date of.
- Checking a family claim and assuming the region claim is thereby settled.
- Using "national language", "associate language", "script" and "dialect" loosely.
- Quoting any total in this topic without its date.
- Writing a replacement narrative for the vernacular rise, or a coexistence narrative so absolute that it cannot accommodate the Kashmiri-Dogri and Vatteluttu-Grantha cases.
- Treating an answer's evidence as sufficient without a qualification; in this topic every strong claim has a documented counter-case, and naming it is what earns the higher band.

### Revision notes

1. Five controls: attribution, chronology, region, terminology, status.
2. Attribution rule: recall content, then check it against the author's recorded profile; never recognise a famous name.
3. Twelve planted confusion sets, from Charudatta and Mrichchhakatika to the two Kalpa Sutras and two Amarasimha contributions.
4. Chronology rule: eight possible dates - composition, oral transmission, redaction, earliest manuscript, first inscription, publication or critical edition, discovery or recovery, and recognition. Name which one you mean.
5. Worked date cases: Chebrolu (AD 207 / 2019); Rigveda (c. 1500-1200 BC / 2007); Charyapada (10th-11th c / 1916); Svapnavasavadattam (composed / rediscovered 1912); Shah Jo Risalo (17th c / published 1866); Sangam (composed and compiled 300 BC-AD 300); Bhagavad Gita manuscripts (ancient / 2025).
6. Region rule: check the region claim separately. Brahui in Baluchistan; Kharosthi in Gandhara; Sarada restricted to Kashmir; Grantha in Tamil Nadu and Kerala for Sanskrit and Manipravalam; Nandinagari in South India; Maharashtri across western and southern India; Ahom a Tai language; Meitei Sino-Tibetan; Romani only outside India.
7. Terminology rule: family, language, dialect, register, script, calligraphic hand, genre and the five statuses are distinct. No national language exists; English is the subsidiary official language of the Union; Nastaliq is a hand, not a script; Ol Chiki is a true alphabet.
8. Status rule: Eighth Schedule 22 after three amendment expansions; classical languages eleven after 3 October 2024 under the criteria revised in 2024; Memory of the World entries cited by name, **register level** and year rather than by total; 197 endangered, vulnerable or extinct languages; the manuscript mission's target of over one crore, phased to 2031.
9. Directive map: twelve question shapes with what each actually tests.
10. Mark-scaled structures: 10-mark thesis-classification-two texts-one caution-verdict; 15-mark adds the frame, corpus or region evidence, the mechanism and a dated anchor; 20-mark adds script and epigraphy, translation and multilingual interaction, the institutional layer, and visible dating discipline.
11. The higher band is reached by conceding a documented counter-case before the verdict, not by asserting the thesis more strongly.

### Concept check

**Question:** Apply all five controls to this sentence and rewrite it: "Ashokan edicts, written in Sanskrit using the Devanagari script, prove that Sanskrit was India's national language, and the Rigveda, which dates from 2007, was composed in the same tradition in southern India."

**Model answer:** **Attribution** is not at issue here, but the other four controls all fail. **Terminology:** the Ashokan record is mostly **Prakrit**, not Sanskrit, and it is in **Brahmi**, with **Kharosthi** in the north-west and **Greek and Aramaic** at Kandahar; Devanagari develops only in the **seventh century AD**. **Terminology again:** no **national language** exists in India; Hindi in Devanagari script is the official language of the Union under Article 343(1). **Chronology:** 2007 is the Rigveda's **Memory of the World inscription year**, not a composition date; the Vedic corpus is estimated by historians at around 1500-1000 BCE. **Region:** the Rigveda belongs to the Old Indo-Aryan tradition of the north, not to southern India. A corrected sentence: "The Ashokan edicts of 250-232 BCE are mostly in Prakrit written in Brahmi, with Kharosthi in the north-west and Greek and Aramaic at Kandahar; they record a multilingual, multi-script imperial practice rather than a single official language, since India has never had a national language. The Rigveda, belonging to the Old Indo-Aryan tradition, was compiled on historians' estimates around 1500-1200 BC and was inscribed on the Memory of the World Register in 2007."

**Misconception to avoid:** That one wrong element in a compound sentence can be corrected in isolation. Four of the five controls failed here, in different ways, and a candidate who fixes only the most obvious error still writes a sentence that would lose marks on three further counts.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Prompt.** *Examine the proposition that India's literary history is better described as a history of coexisting registers than as a sequence of one language replacing another. (20 marks, 250 words)*

**Answer, written to the 250-word ceiling.**

India's literary record supports coexistence rather than succession, though with regionally uneven intensity.

**The registers overlap by design.** Sanskrit was codified, orthodox and elite; the Prakrits were natural, casual and popular; the sources themselves state that the transition between varieties was slow and cannot be divided into strict chronological periods. Gupta-period drama proves simultaneity directly: within one play, high-varna characters speak Sanskrit while women and Shudras speak Prakrit. A coding device requires both registers to be current.

**Institutions carried several languages at once.** The Kushans used Greek, Kharoshthi and Brahmi scripts with Greek, Prakrit and Sanskritised registers. Pallava records are bilingual Tamil-Sanskrit from the seventh century. The ninth-century Kollam Copper Plate is Tamil in Vatteluttu with Grantha words and witness signatures in Arabic-Kufic, Pahlavi and Judaeo-Persian-Hebrew. The Kurgod inscription of the twelfth century carries Sanskrit, Prakrit and Kannada.

**The vernacular turn added publics rather than removing them.** Bhakti shifted composition into the people's language from the twelfth century, yet Sanskrit produced Kalhana's Rajatarangini and Somadeva's Kathasaritsagara, Persian produced a continuous chronicle tradition to the eighteenth century, and the Guru Granth Sahib was composed in Sant Bhasha, drawing on Punjabi, Apabhramsa, Hindi, Braj, Sanskrit, Khariboli and Persian.

**Qualification.** Replacement did occur locally: Kashmiri was eclipsed by Dogri after 1846, and Vatteluttu was replaced by Pallava-Grantha at court.

**Verdict.** Coexistence is the rule; displacement is the exception, and it follows political change rather than linguistic succession.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 20: **3** for answering *examine* by testing the proposition rather than asserting it; **8** for the four distinct evidence types at 2 each - a textual convention, dated inscriptions, continuing literatures and a composite scripture - with the multi-script Kollam plate required for the inscription band; **5** for the conceded counter-case, at 2.5 for each of the two dated displacement instances; **4** for a graded verdict distinguishing the rule from the exception. Most candidates can assert coexistence; the marks sit in arguing against oneself before concluding.

## The three owned Preliminary items

### 2021, Preliminary examination, General Studies Paper I - identification of three named figures

**Recorded as a demand description; response choices withheld under the reproduction policy above.**

**Exact stem.** "With reference to the history of ancient India, Bhavabhuti, Hastimalla and Kshemeshvara were famous" - followed by four single-category response choices, one of which is the correct professional category.

*(Stem read directly from the locally held official 2021 Preliminary General Studies Paper I.)*

**Key status.** **No official key for 2021 is held in this repository.** No answer letter is recorded or inferred here.

**Demand.** A **single-category identification** item across three names. It tests whether a candidate can assign three figures to one professional category, and it is set on the assumption that the candidate knows one of the three and guesses the rest.

**Why the response choices are withheld.** This session owns the discriminating material for the best-known of the three names.

**What this session supplies.** Lesson 14 records **Bhavabhuti** in the Sanskrit drama table with three works - **Uttararamacharita, Malatimadhava and Mahaviracharita** - noting that they are based on the Ramayana and explore emotion, ethics and duty, and that Uttararamacharita covers Rama's life after his coronation and his reunion with Sita. Lesson 19's patronage ledger records Bhavabhuti again as the court poet of **King Yashovarman of Kannauj**, with the same three works. **This session holds no record of Hastimalla or Kshemeshvara**, and supplies nothing towards those two names; that is declared in the gaps list below.

**What the item teaches beyond itself.** It is a pure **attribution-control** item of the kind Lesson 23 is built to defend against. The correct procedure is to identify the category from the name you do hold and to check that the category is consistent with what you know of that figure's work, rather than to reason from the two unfamiliar names.

### 2024, Preliminary examination, General Studies Paper I, question 63 - a work attributed to Bhasa

**Recorded as a demand description; response choices withheld under the reproduction policy above.**

**Exact stem.** "Which one of the following is a work attributed to playwright Bhasa?" - followed by four titles, of which one is attributed to Bhasa and the others to different authors and different genres.

*(Stem read directly from the locally held official 2024 Preliminary General Studies Paper I, Set A. The same item is reproduced in the core art source's literature-chapter objective list.)*

**Key status.** An **official** 2024 Set-A key is held locally. **It was not consulted, and no answer letter is recorded or inferred here.**

**Demand.** A **work-author attribution** item on a single named dramatist, with distractors drawn from adjacent and equally famous technical and dramatic literature. It tests the exact discipline of Lesson 23's attribution control.

**Why the response choices are withheld.** This session owns the attribution of every title that could appear in such an option set.

**What this session supplies.** Lesson 14 records **Bhasa's complete recorded corpus**: Pancharatra, Svapnavasavadattam, Urubhanga, Charudatta, Dootaghatotkacha and Dootavakyam in the drama table, and the fuller list of Pratima-nataka, Yagna-Phalam, Abhisheka-nataka, Panch-ratra, **Madhyama-vyayoga**, Duta-Ghattotkacha, Duta-Vakya, Urubhanga, Karnabharam, Harivamsa or Bala-charita and Daridra Charudatta. The same lesson separately records **Dandin's Kavyadarsha** as the foundational text on Sanskrit poetics, **Bharata Muni's Natyashastra** as the foundational text on drama, music and dance, and **Patanjali's Mahabhashya** as the great commentary on Panini's grammar. Lesson 23's attribution table records the Charudatta and Vasavadatta confusion sets built around Bhasa.

**What the item teaches beyond itself.** A four-option attribution item in this area is usually decided by **genre**, not by memory: if three of four options are a poetics treatise, a dramaturgical treatise and a grammatical commentary, the remaining option is the only play, and Bhasa is a playwright. Lesson 13's genre discipline therefore resolves an item that appears to test rote attribution.

### 2026, Preliminary examination, General Studies Paper I, question 7 - the place-value system

This item is **reproduced in full, with its response choices**, because this session does **not** own its discriminating material.

> **7.** Consider the following statements relating to the use of the place-value system in India:
>
> 1. The earliest epigraphic use of the place-value system in India is found in the Mankani plates from Gujarat (AD 595-596).
> 2. In the ninth century, place-values become general in inscriptions all over India.
> 3. The place-values have been found in Sanskrit inscriptions in South-east Asia as early as the seventh century.
>
> Which of the statements given above are correct?
>
> (a) 1 and 2 only
> (b) 1 and 3 only
> (c) 2 and 3 only
> (d) 1, 2 and 3

*(Stem and response choices read directly from the locally held official 2026 Preliminary General Studies Paper I, Set A.)*

**Key status.** A **provisional** 2026 Set-A key is held locally. **It was not consulted, and no answer letter is recorded or inferred here.**

**Demand.** A **three-statement epigraphic-chronology** item, requiring each statement to be evaluated independently against the record of place-value notation in Indian and Southeast Asian inscriptions.

**What this session supplies, and what it does not.** This session holds **no record of place-value notation in any inscription**, Indian or Southeast Asian, and therefore **supplies nothing towards statements 1, 2 or 3**. The item is **not resolved here, by design**. What this session does hold, and what an answer can legitimately use around the item, is the following:

- **Aryabhata's Aryabhatiya** is recorded in Lesson 14 as a seminal work in mathematics and astronomy **introducing zero, place value, trigonometry and planetary models** - a *textual* association with place value, not an epigraphic one.
- **All surviving Indic scripts in Southeast Asia are descendants of Brahmi** (Lesson 9). That is a fact about **script transmission only**; this session holds no evidence about what notation any Southeast Asian inscription carries, so it does not bear on the truth or falsity of the third statement.
- The evidentiary discipline of Lesson 11 applies directly: an **earliest datable epigraphic use** is a claim about the surviving public record, not about when a notation was first used.

**What the item teaches beyond itself.** It is a reminder that this topic's boundary with the history of science is real: the *script* in which a numeral system is written is owned here, while the *history of the numeral system itself* is not, and inventing an epigraphic record to complete a statement set is exactly the failure mode that Lesson 23's chronology control exists to prevent.
