| CSE 2022 - Yogavasistha | in whose reign the Yogavasistha was translated into Persian by Nizamuddin Panipati | adjacent | Lessons 14 and 17 |
| CSE 2022 - Jaina texts | which of four named texts are Jaina | adjacent; **largely owned** | Lesson 15 |
| CSE 2021 - Halbi, Ho and Kui | what the three terms pertain to | adjacent | Lessons 2 and 5 |
| CSE 2021 - Mother Language Day | two statements on the declaration of the day and on the demand for Bangla | adjacent | Lesson 5 |
| CSE 2021 - "Songs from Prison" | who is associated with this translation of ancient Indian religious lyrics into English | **not owned** | this session holds no record; declared in the gaps list |
| CSE 2016 - memorising chronicles | whose profession was the memorising of chronicles, dynastic histories and epic tales | **not owned** | this session records the *charana*, bardic and Pir custodial systems in Lessons 12, 16, 18 and 19 but holds no record of the specific term the item tests |
| CSE 2016 - a Sunga love story | which ancient book carries the love story of the son of the founder of the Sunga dynasty | adjacent; **owned in part** | Lesson 14 records **Malavikagnimitram** as Kalidasa's romantic drama between the Shunga king **Agnimitra** and Malavika, mentioning Pushyamitra's Rajasuya Yajna |
| CSE 2015 - recent classical status | which language was given classical-language status recently | **owned** | Lesson 7, the dated list of eleven; note that "recently" is time-stamped and its referent has changed since 2015 |
| CSE 2014 - classical declaration | which of three named languages have been declared Classical Languages by the Government | **owned** | Lesson 7, the dated list with years |
| CSE 2014 and earlier language items | family, script and Eighth Schedule identification | **owned** | Lessons 2, 6, 9 and 10 |

**Adjacent entries from other services' examinations**, read in full and recorded without reproduction: they concentrate on script and language classification (Brahmi's local variations, the largest linguistic group, the Harappan script, Khasi's branch, the Eighth Schedule combination, Romani and Gypsy origin, *Sant bhasha*, the decipherer of Brahmi and Kharosthi, Central Asian language families, and the principal languages of Jammu and Kashmir) and on literary attribution (Ramacharita as a *dvyasraya-kavya*, the Rani of Jhansi poem, Anandamath's three images of the Mother Goddess, Katyayana's chronological position, Zainavilas, Nuh Sipihr, Shivsankirttan, Kiratarjuniyam, Amuktamalyada's language, Rigarthadipika and Damayanti-Katha pairings, Nalayira Divyaprabandham, Sangitaraja, Kirtana-Ghosha, Bhanumati, Rana Kumbha's attributions, Krishnadevaraya's court poets, Chandimangala, the chronological ordering of Charaka Samhita, Natyashastra, Sangam literature and Ashtadhyayi, the Ashta-Chhapa's lineage, Mrichchhakatika, Tolkappiyam, Prayag Prashasti, Meghnad Badh Kabya, Mirat-ul-Uroos, the earliest literary reference to iron, Sursagar, Patanjali, Dashakumaracharita, the Rig Veda's ritual setting, Kitab-al Hind and Rehla, Rajatarangini, Ashtadhyayi's authorship, the Humayunnama, Silappadikaram's river and affiliation, Kalila wa Dimna, the Arthashastra's focus and daily royal timetable, Smriti property norms, Ashvaghosha's works, Madhyamika Karika, Panchatantra's character, Thayumanavar's Sittar poetry, Vraja Bhakti Vilasa, Tulsidas's works, Munis al Arwah, Faizi's Lilavati translation, Tarikh-i-Akbari, the Nibandha compiler, Manimekalai's author, Nalayira Divyaprabandham's composers, Ginan, and the Rig Veda's 10th Mandala marriage hymn). **Every one of these demands is taught in this session except the three declared gaps below.**

## The reproduced Main Examination items

Four entries appear in the core art source's literature-chapter Main Examination list. All four are reproduced in full; **none carries a solved model answer here**, in accordance with the practice of this session.

### 2021 - the nature of Bhakti literature

> Evaluate the nature of the Bhakti literature and its contribution to Indian culture. (Answer in 150 words)

**Ownership.** Routed in the ledger to the **medieval-history** owner for the Bhakti movement itself. **Cross-owned here for the literature half.**

**Directive.** *Evaluate* - form a judgement on worth and effect, not merely describe.

**Demand.** Two parts: the **nature** of Bhakti literature, and its **contribution** to Indian culture. An answer that describes only the saints has answered neither part.

**Answer approach.** Open with the **linguistic nature** from Lesson 18: Bhakti shunned Sanskrit as the Brahmins' language and turned to the language of the people, which is what gave Hindi its biggest boost and produced a sharp rise in Bengali, Hindi, Marathi and Gujarati from the twelfth century. Then establish the **pan-regional character** from Lessons 16 and 19 with named instances in at least four languages - the Tamil Alvars and Nayanars with the Nalayira Divyaprabandham and the Tevaram; Jnaneshwar, Namdev, Janabai and Tukaram in Marathi; Sankardev's Kirtana Ghosha in Assamese; Narsinh Mehta in Gujarati; Lal Ded in Kashmiri. Then turn to **contribution**: the creation of vernacular literary publics; the dialect-level achievement of Ramcharitmanas in Awadhi and the Braj corpus; social criticism, evidenced by Thayumanavar's protest against caste and temple-entry restrictions; and composite textual forms, evidenced by the Guru Granth Sahib in Sant Bhasha containing the compositions of Hindu and Muslim Bhagats. Close with the **evaluation**, using the qualification of Lesson 18: the contribution was an expansion of literary publics rather than a replacement of Sanskrit or Persian, both of which continued to produce major work.

### 2020 - Persian literary sources

> Persian literary sources of medieval India reflect the spirit of the age. Comment. (250 words)

**Ownership.** Routed to the **medieval-history** owner. **Cross-owned here for the literary-system half.**

**Directive.** *Comment* - give a considered opinion on the proposition, with support and qualification.

**Demand.** Whether the Persian corpus **reflects** its age. This requires both what the sources show and what they systematically omit.

**Answer approach.** Establish the **system** from Lesson 17: Persian arrived with the Turks and Mongols in the twelfth century and became the mode of communication of the court, producing five distinct genres - dynastic chronicle, political treatise, memoir, masnavi and Sufi discourse. Evidence the "spirit of the age" claim with genre-specific instances: Barani's *Fatwa-i-Jahandari* on ideal kingship for political thought; Afif's *Tarikh-i-Firoz Shahi* on social, economic and welfare policies for administrative life; *Fawaid-ul-Fuwad* for Chishti mysticism; the *Baburnama* and *Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri* for the ruler's own self-presentation; Amir Khusrau's *Nuh Sipihr* of 1318 for the court's appreciation of India's languages, arts and music; Jayasi's *Padmavat* of 1540 for the Sufi romance; and Jafar Zatalli's satirical *Kulliyat* for dissent. Add the **translation programme** at Fatehpur Sikri and Badauni's dissenting participation as evidence that the corpus was not a single official voice. Then **qualify**: these are overwhelmingly **court-generated** sources, so they reflect the spirit of a **political and literary elite**; the two name-collision pairs show competing official and semi-official accounts of the same reign; and the four women authors - Gulbadan Begum, Jahanara Begum, Zeb-un-Nissa - are exceptional rather than representative. Close with a graded verdict.

### 2016 - Krishnadevaraya

> Krishnadevaraya, the King of Vijayanagara, was not only an accomplished scholar himself but also a great patron of learning and literature. Discuss.

**Ownership.** **Cross-owned.** The Vijayanagara political record belongs to the medieval-history owner; the literary record is owned here.

**Directive.** *Discuss* - examine both halves of the proposition, which is structured as two claims.

**Demand.** Two separable claims - that he was **an accomplished scholar himself**, and that he was **a great patron**. An answer must evidence each separately.

**Answer approach.** For the first claim, use Lesson 19: he wrote ***Amuktamalyada*** in **Telugu**, composed while travelling via Vijayawada during his Kalinga campaign, a treatise stating the instance of Lord Vishnu in his dream; ***Krishnadevarayana Dinachari*** in **Kannada**; and four named **Sanskrit** treatises - *Madalasa Charita*, *Satyavadu Parinaya*, *Rasamanjari* and *Jambavati Kalyana*. For the second, use the **ashtadiggajas**, naming at least four with their works - Allasani Peddana's *Manu Charitra*, Nandi Thimmana's *Parijatapaharanamu*, Tenali Ramakrishna's *Panduranga Mahatmayam* and Pingali Surana's *Kalapurnodayam*, the first original poetic novel in Telugu - and then cross the language boundary: he patronised the **Kannada** poets Mallanarya, Chatu Vittal-anatha and Thimmana, had the Kannada saint **Vyasatirtha** as his Rajaguru, and patronised the **Tamil** poet Haridasa. Add the period characterisation: the Vijayanagara period is the **Golden Age of Telugu Literature**, also called the **Prabandha Age**. Close by noting what the evidence establishes beyond the question's own terms - a **four-language cultural programme** rather than patronage of a single literary tradition.

### 2013 - Sangam literature as a source

> Although not very useful from the point of view of a connected political history of South India, Sangam literature portrays the social and economic conditions of its time with remarkable vividness. Comment.

**Ownership.** Routed to the **Sangam-age** owner for the political and economic history. **Cross-owned here for the corpus and its evidentiary character.**

**Directive.** *Comment* - respond to a proposition that already contains its own two-sided structure.

**Demand.** The proposition is pre-balanced, so the answer must evidence **both** halves rather than arguing for one.

**Answer approach.** Take the second half first, because it carries the evidence. From Lesson 16: the **Pattinappalai's** record of customs officials at the seaport of **Puhar**; **Tolkappiyam's** fivefold division of lands by occupation and its **four castes**; the councils recorded in **Silappadikaram** - the five-member *Aimperunkulu* and the eight-member *Enperayam* - and the court called *avai*; the performing classes *Panar*, *Viraliyar* and *Kanigaiyar* and the popular entertainment *Koothu*; the warrior ritual *vatakkiruttal*; and the authorship fact that the poets included **men and women from different classes of society**. Then the first half: the corpus is organised **thematically** by *aham* and *puram* rather than by reign; its period of 300 BC to AD 300 is a **composition-and-compilation window**; the tradition's own three-Sangam framework has **no conclusive historical account for the first two**, which are widely considered legendary; and the surviving anthologies are compilations rather than annals. Close with the graded verdict of Lesson 16: rich and vivid on society, economy, material culture and values; bounded as a source for connected dynastic chronology - and add that stating the boundary is what makes the richness credible.

## The cross-owned Mains demand of 2024

> Estimate the contribution of Pallavas of Kanchi for the development of art and literature of South India. (Answer in 150 words)

**Ownership.** Routed to the **ancient-history** owner for the dynasty. **The literature half is cross-owned here**, and the architecture and sculpture halves belong to the architecture and sculpture owners.

**Directive.** *Estimate* - assess the size and significance of a contribution, which requires a judgement, not a list.

**Demand.** Art **and** literature in one answer, for one dynasty, in 150 words - so every sentence must carry evidence.

**Answer approach for the literature half.** Four elements, all recorded in this session and all datable or named. First, **institutional bilingualism**: bilingual **Tamil-Sanskrit Pallava inscriptions from the seventh century onwards** (Lesson 11), which is administrative evidence rather than assertion. Second, a **script decision**: **Vatteluttu was replaced by the Pallava-Grantha script in the Pallava court** (Lesson 10) - a concrete, institutional literary-cultural intervention. Third, **named literary patronage**: the Pallava kings **encouraged both Tamil and Sanskrit works and supported Bhakti saints and scholars**, and the Sanskrit prose **Dashakumara Charita by Dandin** is linked to their court (Lesson 16). Fourth, the **pre-existing Tamil literary culture** the Pallavas built on, evidenced by the earlier Tamil epics of Lesson 16. Route the architecture and painting material to their owners, and close with an estimate rather than a summary: Pallava literary patronage is **demonstrable rather than assumed**, because the court changed the script of its own records and issued its inscriptions in two languages.

## Adjacent Mains demands, 2026

| Item | Routed to | What this session supplies |
|---|---|---|
| The importance of **Ashokan inscriptions** for reconstructing Mauryan history | the ancient-history sources owner | Lesson 9 supplies the **language and script** record - mostly Prakrit in Brahmi, Kharosthi in the north-west, Greek and Aramaic at Kandahar, dated 250-232 BCE - and Lesson 11 supplies the **medium-based evidentiary limits**. The reconstruction of Mauryan administration, dhamma and society is **not** owned here |
| The **linguistic reorganization of Indian States** | the modern-history owner | Lesson 5's Bengali Language Movement and Lesson 6's four-box status architecture supply the vocabulary; the reorganisation itself is **not** owned here |

## Honest-nil statement

**This topic owns no Mains General Studies Paper I question and no Essay prompt in any window held in this repository from 2018 through 2026.** Its examined presence is at the **Preliminary** level, where it owns exactly **three** items - 2021 on the identification of three named figures, 2024 question 63 on a work attributed to Bhasa, and 2026 question 7 on the place-value system, for which it does not hold the discriminating material. At the Mains level it is a **cross-owned contributor**, supplying the literature half of the 2024 Pallava question and substantial material to four reproduced Main Examination items from 2013 to 2021. No question, year, marks, wording or answer has been invented to improve that position.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These checks run across lesson blocks rather than within one lesson. Each is answerable only by combining material taught in at least two places.

## Block A - classification and status (Lessons 1-8)

### Cumulative check 1

**Question:** Sanskrit, Pali and Bengali each hold a different combination of the statuses taught in this session. Set out the combination for each and state what the comparison establishes.

**Model answer:** **Sanskrit** holds **Eighth Schedule listing** - it was among the original fourteen - and **classical-language status**, recognised in **2005**. **Pali** holds **classical-language status**, recognised in **October 2024**, and is **not** in the Eighth Schedule. **Bengali** holds **Eighth Schedule listing** from the original fourteen and **classical-language status** from **October 2024**, and it additionally carries a **state official-language** history and, through the Bengali Language Movement, the origin of an **international observance** - UNESCO's declaration of 21 February as International Mother Language Day in 1999, recognised by the UN in 2002. The comparison establishes that the statuses are **independent and non-nested**: a language can be classical without being scheduled, scheduled without being classical, or both, and none of the statuses implies anything about the others or about the language's antiquity.

**Misconception to avoid:** That classical languages are a subset of scheduled languages. Pali and Prakrit refute it directly, and criterion four of the **2024** set, which permits a classical language to be discontinuous with later forms of its offshoots, is what makes recognition possible in the absence of a modern speech community.

### Cumulative check 2

**Question:** Using material from at least three lessons, show that a classification in this topic is always a claim by a particular authority and never a neutral description.

**Model answer:** **Linguistic classification** is made by scholars, and the sources show it contested: the derivation of Dravidian from Prakrit and Paisacha is recorded as the view of *some scholars*, while the same material treats Proto-Dravidian as the ancestor of twenty-one Dravidian languages (Lesson 2). **Constitutional classification** is made by Parliament through amendment: the Eighth Schedule grew from fourteen to twenty-two through the 21st, 71st and 92nd Amendment Acts, and the classification it produces disagrees with the descriptive one, since Maithili is both a Bihari dialect group and a scheduled language (Lessons 5 and 6). **Administrative classification** is made by the executive: classical-language status was created by a Government of India decision in 2004, its criteria were rewritten in November 2005 and again in 2024, and the list has been expanded in six separate years (Lesson 7). **International classification** is made by a treaty body: UNESCO's four-level endangerment scale and its documentary-heritage register (Lessons 8 and 22). Four authorities, four criteria sets, four different lists - and none of them can be used to answer a question posed under another's terms.

**Misconception to avoid:** That disagreement between classifications is an error to be resolved. The classifications answer different questions for different purposes, and an answer that names the authority behind each is more accurate than one that forces them into agreement.

## Block B - scripts and witnesses (Lessons 9-11)

### Cumulative check 3

**Question:** Trace, using three lessons, the complete chain by which a modern North Indian script such as Bengali descends from the oldest Indian writing system, and state one thing the chain does **not** establish.

**Model answer:** **Brahmi**, the oldest writing system used in the subcontinent in the final centuries BCE and the early centuries AD, passes through dynastic stages - **Ashokan, Kushana and Gupta or Northern Brahmi**. In the **late sixth century, Gupta Brahmi evolved into Siddhamatrika, also called Kutila**, which thrived between the sixth and thirteenth centuries and laid the groundwork for **Nagari, Eastern Nagari, Tirhuta, Odia and Nepalese**; from Siddhamatrika the modern North Indian scripts - **Bengali, Assamese, Odia and Maithili** - gradually emerged (Lessons 9 and 10). The chain **does not establish** anything about the descent of the **languages** those scripts write. Bengali the script descends from Brahmi; Bengali the language is a Modern Indo-Aryan language whose Middle Indo-Aryan antecedent is recorded separately as Ardhamagadhi (Lesson 4). Script descent and language descent are two different trees, and the four documented proofs in Lesson 10 - one script for many languages, one language across changing scripts, a court replacing a script by decision, and a single document carrying several script-language combinations - keep them apart.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a shared script implies a shared linguistic ancestry. Brahmi wrote Prakrit, Sanskrit and Tamil-Brahmi, and Tamil belongs to a different family altogether.

### Cumulative check 4

**Question:** The Kollam Copper Plate and the Charyapada are both objects, and both carry lessons about evidence. What different lesson does each carry?

**Model answer:** The **Kollam Copper Plate** of the ninth century is an **inscription**, and it teaches what the durable medium can show: a single legal instrument, mostly in **Tamil** and inscribed in **Vatteluttu** with a few words in **Grantha**, whose sixth plate carries witness signatures in **Arabic written in Kufic script, in Pahlavi, and in Judaeo-Persian written in Hebrew script**. It is therefore evidence of an ordinary multilingual, multi-script administrative and trading practice, fixed in place and time (Lessons 10 and 11). The **Charyapada** is a **manuscript** tradition, and it teaches what the perishable medium cannot guarantee: a collection of mystic songs of the tenth and eleventh centuries, considered the **oldest literary document of the Bengali language**, which survived in the **Nepal Durbar Library** and was **discovered by Haraprasad Shastri in 1916** (Lesson 19). The first teaches that a document can record a whole society at a moment; the second teaches that survival is contingent, often external, and that recovery is itself an event with a date and a named scholar.

**Misconception to avoid:** That the two kinds of witness can be used interchangeably. An inscription is strong on date, place, language and script and weak on breadth of content; a manuscript is strong on content and weak on original date and place, because every surviving copy is a copy of a copy.

## Block C - corpora (Lessons 12-16)

### Cumulative check 5

**Question:** Name four corpora taught in this session that are **layered** rather than single-author, and state the dating discipline each one requires.

**Model answer:** **The Vedic corpus**: orally handed down by generations of Brahmin families and then compiled, with historians estimating compilation around **1500-1000 BCE** and the Rig Veda around **1500-1200 BC**; the Brahmanas are usually dated **900-700 BC** with historians recorded as disagreeing. Distinguish oral transmission, compilation and the recension actually surviving (Lesson 12). **The epics and the Puranas**: long accretion histories, so core composition, redaction and surviving manuscripts must be dated separately; there are 18 Mahapuranas and about 19 Upapuranas, and the corpus was translated into vernacular languages over time (Lesson 13). **The Sangam corpus**: mostly composed **and compiled** between 300 BC and AD 300 - a window, not a date - preserved as the Ettuthokai and Pattuppattu compilations, with the three-Sangam framework being the tradition's own account and its first two assemblies widely regarded as legendary (Lesson 16). **The Jain Agamas**: originally compiled by the Ganadharas, **recompiled by the Svetambaras at Vallabhi in the mid-fifth century AD**, with the Digambaras rejecting that recompilation's authority - so the corpus has a compilation date that one of its two sects does not accept (Lesson 15). In each case the required discipline is to name which event the date refers to.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a compilation date dates the contents. A window such as 300 BC to AD 300 tells you when a corpus was assembled and largely composed, not when any individual poem within it was written.

### Cumulative check 6

**Question:** Using the Buddhist and Jain corpora, show that "canonical language" is a statement about transmission rather than about speech.

**Model answer:** The **Pali canon** - the Vinaya, Sutta and Abhidhamma Pitakas - is the canon of the **Theravada** school, and Pali is described as the **lingua franca of Theravada Buddhism**; but the same sources record that **the Buddha himself is believed not to have spoken in Pali**, and elsewhere that the Buddha and Mahavira **perhaps** spoke in Ardhamagadhi (Lessons 4 and 15). Meanwhile the Mahayana and Sarvastivada corpus - the Buddhacharita, Lalitavistara Sutra, Mulamadhyamakakarika and Mahavibhasa Sastra - is in **Sanskrit**, and the Mahavastu mixes **Sanskrit, Pali and Prakrit** in one text. On the Jain side, the Agamas are in **Ardhamagadhi**, the oldest Digambara text is in **Shauraseni**, the one text both sects accept is in **Sanskrit**, and Jain monks also wrote in Apabhramsha, Tamil, Kannada, Gujarati and Marathi depending on **era, region and patrons**. The pattern is consistent: canonical language tracks **school, region and patronage** - that is, the history of how a corpus was written and preserved - and not the speech of any founder.

**Misconception to avoid:** That the language of a religious canon tells you what its founder spoke. The sources hedge that question explicitly, and the hedge is the correct examination answer.

## Block D - medieval and modern (Lessons 17-20)

### Cumulative check 7

**Question:** Assemble three pieces of evidence from three different lessons that the vernacular rise from the twelfth century did **not** displace Sanskrit or Persian.

**Model answer:** First, **Sanskrit kept producing major work**: although Sanskrit was not so predominant in the medieval period, excellent works were composed in Rajasthan and Kashmir, including **Kalhana's Rajatarangini**, **Somadeva Bhatta's Kathasaritsagara** of the eleventh century and **Bilhana's Vikramankadevacharita** on Vikramaditya VI; the digest tradition also continued, with the oldest surviving Nibandha, the **Krityakalpataru**, composed in the early twelfth century (Lessons 13 and 14). Second, **Persian expanded rather than contracted**: arriving with the Turks and Mongols in the twelfth century, it became the mode of communication of the court and produced a continuous chronicle, treatise, memoir and Sufi-discourse tradition from Hasan Nizami to Saqi Mustad Khan (Lesson 17). Third, **the composite text**: the **Guru Granth Sahib**, expanded in 1678, is written in **Sant Bhasha**, which contains words from Punjabi, Apabhramsa, Hindi, Braj Bhasha, **Sanskrit**, Khariboli and **Persian** - a scripture of the vernacular devotional world that draws on both of the supposedly displaced languages (Lesson 15). The vernacular turn therefore **added publics and registers**; it did not subtract them.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a language's decline in one institutional role is a decline overall. Sanskrit's reduced dominance in court poetry coincided with continued production in history, narrative and legal digest, and with its incorporation into a new composite devotional register.

### Cumulative check 8

**Question:** Trace the Panchatantra across four lessons and state what its trajectory demonstrates.

**Model answer:** In **Lesson 13** it is a **didactic fable** by **Vishnu Sharma**, an ancient **non-religious** Sanskrit text of interrelated animal fables dated around **200 BC-300 AD**, concerned with the wise conduct of life and elsewhere described as teaching political science and statecraft; the parable-versus-fable distinction places it precisely, since its carriers are animals rather than humans. In **Lesson 17** it travels: translated into **old Persian in the sixth century** and into **Arabic in the eighth century** as ***Kalila wa Dimna***, a collection featuring two jackals, Kalila and Dimna, as narrators and protagonists. In **Lesson 22** a **fifteenth-century manuscript** of it, held at the Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute and nominated by the IGNCA, was inscribed in **2024 on the Memory of the World Asia-Pacific Regional Register** - a **regional**, not an international, inscription, and India's first regional submissions. In **Lesson 23** it is a case in the chronology control: composition around 200 BC-AD 300, translation in the sixth and eighth centuries, the surviving manuscript from the fifteenth century, and regional inscription in 2024 - four entirely separate dates. The trajectory demonstrates that a literary work carries **a composition history, a transmission history and a recognition history**, that these are independent, and that a text's international circulation can begin more than a thousand years before any modern act of recognition.

**Misconception to avoid:** That translation into other languages is a modern or colonial phenomenon. The Panchatantra reached Persian and Arabic centuries before the Mughal translation projects, which themselves preceded the National Translation Mission by four hundred years.

## Block E - materiality, recognition and answer construction (Lessons 21-23, and the synthesis)

### Cumulative check 9

**Question:** Lesson 21 argues that loss of texts follows institutional failure, and Lesson 22 shows that recognition operates at two register levels. Combine the two into a single claim about what determines whether an Indian manuscript is usable today, and name four pieces of evidence for it.

**Model answer:** The claim is that **usability is produced by a chain of institutional acts, not by the manuscript's own qualities**: a text must be **transmitted**, then **held**, then **catalogued and made legible**, and only then can it be **recognised**. Four pieces of evidence. First, **transmission**: over a thousand Sama Vedic recensions are reduced to three, because custody lay with *charanas* whose failure took the whole bundle with them (Lesson 12). Second, **holding**: the official record names a custodian for every recognised Indian document - the Institute of Asian Studies, Chennai for the Tamil medical manuscripts, the Asiatic Society, Kolkata for the Vimalaprabha and the Maitreyavyakarana, the Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute for the Bhagavadgita and Natyasastra manuscripts - so recognition presupposes an institution that already held the object (Lesson 22). Third, **legibility**: manuscripts in Sarada, Modi, Nandinagari, Kaithi or Tirhuta may be intact and still unreadable, which is why the current mission funds AI-assisted transcription (Lessons 10 and 21). Fourth, **recognition**: it comes last and at a stated level, and India's first Asia-Pacific Regional Register inscriptions were made only in 2024, decades after its International Register participation began in 1997.

**Misconception to avoid:** That recognition protects a manuscript. Recognition is a **downstream** act that presupposes survival, custody and cataloguing; an unrecorded manuscript cannot be nominated at all, which is why the survey and repository stages of Lesson 21 matter more to survival than the register does.

### Cumulative check 10

**Question:** Classical-language status and Memory of the World inscription are both "recognitions". Using Lessons 7 and 22, state four structural features they share and two respects in which they differ.

**Model answer:** **Shared.** (1) Both are granted by a **named authority** against **published tests** - the Union Cabinet on a Linguistic Experts Committee recommendation, and UNESCO through the International Advisory Committee and Register Subcommittee. (2) Both are **dated acts**, so any total must carry its date. (3) Both have **changed their own rules or scope** - the classical criteria were rewritten in November 2005 and again in 2024, and the Memory of the World programme runs registers at three levels with different thresholds. (4) Both are **evidence about a decision, not about antiquity**: Odia's 2014 recognition says nothing about the age of Odia literature, and the Rigveda's 2007 inscription says nothing about the age of the Rigveda. **Different.** (1) **Object**: classical status attaches to a **language**, Memory of the World to a **document, archive or collection** - so the Natyasastra manuscript is inscribed while Sanskrit is recognised, and the two facts are not interchangeable. (2) **Level**: Memory of the World has an explicit **international / regional / national** hierarchy, so the level is part of the fact; classical status has no tiers, only a growing list.

**Misconception to avoid:** That two recognitions of similar prestige can be cited interchangeably in an answer about "India's recognised heritage". One recognises a language and the other a physical documentary object at a stated register level; conflating them produces sentences such as "Sanskrit is on the Memory of the World Register", which is false as written.

### Cumulative check 11

**Question:** Apply the five controls of Lesson 23 to this sentence and rewrite it: "India's eleven classical languages were chosen because their literary traditions are original and not borrowed, and its sixteen Memory of the World entries, the latest being the Ramcharitmanas, prove the antiquity of that heritage."

**Model answer:** **Terminology and status currency fail first.** The *original and not borrowed* test belonged to the 2004 and 2005 criteria and was **dropped in the 2024 revision**, which substituted a requirement of **knowledge texts, especially prose in addition to poetry, with epigraphical and inscriptional evidence**; and the total of eleven must be dated to the Union Cabinet decision of **3 October 2024**. **Status currency fails again** on "sixteen Memory of the World entries": that is the row-count of one printed table, not a register total; the reported International Register figure is fourteen and the official itemised list names thirteen. **Terminology fails a third time** on register level: the **Ramcharitmanas** manuscripts were inscribed on the **Asia-Pacific Regional Register** in 2024, not the International Register, and the latest **International** inscriptions are the Bhagavadgita and Natyasastra manuscripts of 2025. **Chronology fails** on "prove the antiquity": an inscription year is a recognition date, not a composition date. **Attribution and region** are not engaged by this sentence. A corrected version: "Eleven languages hold classical status, the most recent five conferred by Union Cabinet decision on 3 October 2024 under criteria revised that year, which require knowledge texts and epigraphic evidence rather than an unborrowed tradition. Separately, India holds inscriptions on the Memory of the World International Register, most recently the Bhagavadgita and Natyasastra manuscripts in 2025, and three on the Asia-Pacific Regional Register, inscribed in 2024. None of those dates evidences the antiquity of the texts themselves."

**Misconception to avoid:** That a sentence containing several true-sounding facts can be repaired by correcting the most obvious one. Four of the five controls failed here in different ways, and an answer that fixes only the register level would still misstate the criteria, the totals and what a recognition date proves.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

Six further original questions follow. None reproduces or closely restates any verified question recorded in the PYQ section above, and none repeats a lesson-local prompt. Word ceilings are stated and observed: **10 marks - 150 words; 15 marks - 250 words; 20 marks - 250 words**, the difference between the last two lying in the number of dimensions carried and the depth of qualification, not in length. Each model ends with a **unique scoring note** giving a quantified mark split for that answer. With the twenty-three lesson-local models above, this file carries **twenty-nine** original Mains models in all.

## Original 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Prompt.** *Distinguish between a language family, a script and a literary register, with Indian examples. Explain why the distinction matters. (150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

A **language family** is a group of languages descended from a common ancestor predating recorded history: Indo-Aryan, Dravidian, Austro-Asiatic and Sino-Tibetan in India. A **script** is a standard for representing a spoken language by marks on a medium; it is independent of family. A **register** is a variety selected by social situation rather than locality.

The independence is documented. **Brahmi** wrote Prakrit, Sanskrit and Tamil-Brahmi - one script, two families. **Modi**, created for Marathi, was also used for Urdu, Kannada, Gujarati, Hindi and Tamil. **Maithili** moved from the Tirhuta script to Devanagari without changing language. Sanskrit and Prakrit coexisted as registers, the first codified and elite, the second natural and popular, and Gupta drama assigned them to characters by social position.

The distinction matters because conflating them produces claims the evidence forbids - that Ashokan edicts are Sanskrit in Devanagari, or that Dravidian descends from Prakrit.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 10: **3** for defining all three terms precisely rather than describing them; **3** for supplying a *separate* documented counter-example to each independence claim - Brahmi across two families, Modi across six languages, Maithili across two scripts - since one example doing all three jobs caps this band at 1; **2** for the register evidence from Gupta drama, the only textual proof offered; **2** for a closing sentence naming two specific false statements the distinction prevents. A definition-only answer scores 4 at most.

## Original 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Prompt.** *Examine the criteria and consequences of classical-language status in India, noting how the criteria have changed. (150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

Classical-language status is an **administrative recognition**, created by a Government of India decision in **2004** and revised twice.

**Criteria in force, revised 2024:** antiquity of early texts or recorded history over **1500-2000 years**; a body of ancient literature considered **a heritage** by generations of speakers; **knowledge texts, especially prose in addition to poetry, with epigraphical and inscriptional evidence**; and a classical language that **could be** distinct from the current form or discontinuous with later offshoots.

**What changed:** the 2004-2005 test that the literary tradition be **original and not borrowed** was **dropped** in 2024 and the evidence-based third criterion added.

**Consequences:** two annual international awards; a Centre of Excellence; the UGC **requested** to create chairs. Implementation includes three Sanskrit Central Universities (2020) and Centres of Excellence at Mysuru.

Eleven languages hold it, most recently five on **3 October 2024**.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 10: **2** for fixing the category as an administrative recognition in the opening line; **4** for quoting the **criteria in force** - and this band is forfeited entirely if the superseded *original and not borrowed* test is presented as current; **2** for stating what changed in 2024 and when; **1** for preserving the verb "requested" in the consequences; **1** for dating the total to 3 October 2024. In this area the *currency* of the criteria is marked before the prose.

## Original 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt.** *"The history of Indian scripts is a history of adoption, adaptation and replacement rather than of natural growth." Discuss. (250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

The proposition is largely sustained, though descent by gradual change also operates.

**Adoption.** Scripts were taken up for languages they were not designed for. **Brahmi** carried Prakrit, Sanskrit and Tamil-Brahmi. **Grantha**, used by Tamil speakers in Tamil Nadu and Kerala, wrote **Sanskrit and Manipravalam**. **Modi**, devised for Marathi, was used for Urdu, Kannada, Gujarati, Hindi and Tamil. The **Urdu script** was adopted from the Persian alphabet, itself from Arabic.

**Adaptation.** **Modi** was created in the fourteenth century as a **shorthand variant of Devanagari by scribes unable to write full Devanagari during rapid writing** - a working constraint producing a new system. **Gurmukhi**, developed from Sarada, has unique orthographic features because it grew **outside the ambit of Sanskrit tradition**. **Ol Chiki**, created in 1925 by Raghunath Murmu for Santali, broke the regional convention entirely by being a **true alphabet** rather than an abugida.

**Replacement.** **Vatteluttu was replaced by the Pallava-Grantha script in the Pallava court**. **Modi** was replaced for Marathi by the **Balbodh** style of Devanagari in the twentieth century. **Maithili** moved from **Tirhuta** to Devanagari.

**The qualification.** Gradual descent is equally documented: Gupta Brahmi evolved into **Siddhamatrika** in the late sixth century, from which Bengali, Assamese, Odia and Maithili emerged, and Sarada appeared in Kashmir in the same period.

**Verdict.** Indian script history combines slow palaeographic descent with repeated deliberate acts - courtly, scribal, standardising and inventive. The decisive changes were decisions.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 15: **3** for organising by the proposition's own three verbs - adoption, adaptation, replacement - rather than chronologically; **6** for evidence density at 2 per verb, requiring at least three named instances under each; **3** for the conceded descent counter-case with its own dated evidence, Gupta Brahmi to Siddhamatrika in the late sixth century; **3** for a verdict discriminating between deliberate acts and gradual descent instead of endorsing the proposition wholesale. An answer that lists scripts without sorting them by mechanism cannot exceed 7.

## Original 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt.** *Assess the manuscript as a material object, and the institutional character of the problem of its survival in India. (250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**The object.** A script is defined as marks on a medium - **paper, rock, birch bark** - and the manuscript record adds **palm leaf**. Medium correlates with region: palm leaf southern, evidenced by **Silappadikaram** described as a palm leaf manuscript and by Vatteluttu's use in southern manuscripts; birch bark northern; paper later and general. Production leaves traces: **Modi** exists because scribes could not write full Devanagari at speed, and the **Hamzanama** is an illustrated manuscript commissioned by Akbar.

**The losses, documented.** Over **a thousand Sama Vedic recensions** reduced to **three**; **twenty-one** Rig Vedic recensions to one complete survivor and parts of a second; **nine** Atharva Vedic recensions to two. **Katyayana's Varttika** survives only through references in Patanjali. **Devichandraguptam** and **Valaiapathi** survive in fragments. **No book of the first Sangam survives.**

**The character of the problem.** Loss is not random decay. It follows the failure of **custodial institutions** - a *charana* ending, a court falling, a speech community dispersing. Survival is often external and accidental: the **Charyapada** survived in the Nepal Durbar Library and was recovered in **1916**; **Svapnavasavadattam** was rediscovered in **1912**.

**The response, therefore institutional.** The Gyan Bharatam Mission, under the Ministry of Culture, targets **over one crore** manuscripts for digitisation and cataloguing, with a **National Digital Repository**, conservation labs for **palm leaf, birch bark and paper**, AI-assisted transcription, blockchain provenance and volunteers across about 100 districts, phased to **2031**.

**Verdict.** Because loss is institutional, conservation must be institutional; cataloguing and readability bind as tightly as physical repair.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 15: **3** for the material analysis - three media and the region correlation; **4** for documented survival ratios, at 1 each for the Sama, Rig and Atharva recension figures and the fragmentary texts, since a general claim about fragility earns none of it; **4** for the causal diagnosis that loss follows custodial failure, which is what makes the policy section derived rather than appended; **2** for naming the mission's material-specific conservation streams; **2** for a verdict naming cataloguing and readability as binding constraints. Quoting a progress figure for the mission loses currency credit rather than gaining evidence credit.

## Original 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Prompt.** *Evaluate the role of transmission - oral, scribal and institutional - in determining what survives of India's literary heritage. (250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

Transmission, not composition, determines survival.

**Oral transmission was engineered for exactness.** Each Veda had multiple recensions (*sakhas*) held by schools (*charanas*), family- or guru-based, each specialising in one recension with a duty of accurate **pronunciation, intonation and memorisation**, and each holding a complete set - Samhita, Brahmana, sometimes Aranyaka, and Upanishads. Parallel systems existed elsewhere: Tamil **Panar** and **Viraliyar** bards, Rajasthani bards singing ***virkavya***, and Pirs reciting ***ginans*** before they were written down.

**Its failure mode was catastrophic, not gradual.** When a lineage ended, its whole bundle went: Sama Veda, over a thousand recensions to three.

**Scribal transmission traded fidelity for reach.** Recopying on palm leaf, birch bark and paper allowed texts to travel and to be illustrated, but every copy is a copy of a copy, so a manuscript's date is the copy's date, not the text's.

**Institutional transmission decided the rest.** Court patronage from the Sungas to Awadh sustained production; the **Vallabhi** recompilation of the Jain Angas in the mid-fifth century produced a canon one sect **rejects**; monasteries, temples and the Serampore press of **1800** each created a distribution capacity that had not existed before.

**Recovery is itself institutional.** The Charyapada survived in a **Nepalese palace library** and was recovered in **1916**; Bhasa's Svapnavasavadattam was rediscovered in **1912**.

**Evaluation.** The surviving corpus is a record of institutions that lasted, not of works that deserved to. Any generalisation about "Indian literature" is therefore a generalisation about custodial success.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 20: **5** for treating oral, scribal and institutional transmission as a **causal system with distinct failure modes** rather than as a chronology; **5** for named mechanism detail - sakha and charana custody, the bardic and Pir carriers, the copy-of-a-copy problem; **4** for the Jain case in which transmission itself became **sectarian**, the Vallabhi recompilation being rejected by one sect; **3** for the two dated twentieth-century recoveries, 1912 and 1916, showing the process continues; **3** for an epistemological verdict that reframes every other claim in the topic. A narrative of "oral, then written" without failure modes cannot pass 9.

## Original 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Prompt.** *Examine the argument that formal recognition - constitutional, administrative and international - has become as consequential for India's linguistic and documentary heritage as creation itself. (250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

Recognition now allocates resources, visibility and survival prospects, but it is not equivalent to creation, and treating it so produces error.

**The case for consequence.** **Constitutional** recognition is contested and expanding: the Eighth Schedule grew from fourteen to twenty-two through the 21st, 71st and 92nd Amendment Acts, and its absence was worth a mass movement - the Bengali Language Movement, ending in official status in **1956** and UNESCO's **1999** Mother Language Day. **Administrative** recognition carries funding: classical status, created in **2004**, brings awards, a Centre of Excellence and chairs; five languages were added on **3 October 2024**. **International** recognition brings visibility at two levels: India's Memory of the World **International Register** inscriptions run from **1997** to **2025**, and its first three **Asia-Pacific Regional** inscriptions were made in **2024**.

**The limits.** Recognition is **revisable**: every total above has changed. It is **not evidence about antiquity**: the Rigveda was inscribed in 2007 and colonial archives in 2003, so the sequence does not track composition. It is **not self-executing**: the UGC is only "**requested**" to create chairs. And it is **not uniform**: Memory of the World runs distinct international and regional levels, so an inscription's **level** is part of what was granted.

**What actually protects heritage.** Survey, cataloguing, material-specific conservation, transcription and provenance - the functions the current manuscript mission targets - because an undocumented manuscript cannot be recognised at all.

**Verdict.** Recognition is powerful but derivative; documentation is the precondition, and antiquity is neither.

**Unique scoring note.** Out of 20: **6** for distinguishing the **three recognition authorities** - constitutional, administrative and international - with a dated instance each, at 2 apiece; **6** for the four limits at 1.5 each: revisability, silence about antiquity, non-self-execution, and the register-level distinction; **4** for identifying documentation as the **precondition** of recognition rather than its consequence; **4** for a verdict that **orders** documentation, recognition and antiquity instead of balancing them. Treating "UNESCO recognition" as one undifferentiated honour costs the whole first band.

# REMEDIATION

Fourteen recurring errors, each with the correction, the reason the error is attractive, and the drill that fixes it.

## Error set A - classification

**Error 1: "Sanskrit is the mother of all Indian languages."**
*Why it is attractive:* the sources themselves say Sanskrit is "the mother of many Indian languages", and the sentence feels culturally respectful.
*Correction:* within the **Indo-Aryan branch**, the Old Indo-Aryan stage is ancestral to the later Indo-Aryan layers. Dravidian descends from **Proto-Dravidian**; Austro-Asiatic and Sino-Tibetan have their own ancestries, and the Sino-Tibetan languages are described as **older than** the Indo-Aryan ones.
*Drill:* before writing any descent claim, name the **branch** it applies to. If you cannot name the branch, the claim is too broad.

**Error 2: "Dravidian emerged from Prakrit."**
*Why it is attractive:* it appears in the sources - as the view of *some scholars*.
*Correction:* carry the attribution or drop the claim. The standard treatment makes Dravidian an independent family from Proto-Dravidian, and the same material describes some Indian language groups as **indigenous to the subcontinent**.
*Drill:* search your own sentence for a hedge you have deleted. Hedges in this topic are load-bearing.

**Error 3: treating "dialect" as a rank.**
*Why it is attractive:* ordinary usage makes "dialect" pejorative.
*Correction:* a dialect is a form of a language spoken in a local area. **Maithili** is both a Bihari dialect group in the descriptive table and a **scheduled language** under the 92nd Amendment. **Ramcharitmanas**, the most famous work of Hindi devotional literature, is in **Awadhi**, a dialect, not in standard Hindi.
*Drill:* whenever you write "dialect", check whether the variety also holds an administrative status.

## Error set B - script

**Error 4: "Ashokan edicts were in Sanskrit, written in Devanagari."**
*Why it is attractive:* it fuses the most familiar Indian language with the most familiar Indian script.
*Correction:* mostly **Prakrit** in **Brahmi**, dated **250-232 BCE**; **Kharosthi** in the north-west; **Greek and Aramaic** at Kandahar. Devanagari develops in the **seventh century AD**.
*Drill:* answer language and script as two separate questions, always in that order.

**Error 5: the Indus script has been deciphered.**
*Why it is attractive:* the word "script" implies readability.
*Correction:* most inscriptions are **extremely short** and it is **unclear whether the symbols constitute a script used to record a language**. What is observable without reading is its **boustrophedon** direction.
*Drill:* for any claim about the Indus symbols, ask whether it could be established **without reading them**. If not, it is unsupportable.

**Error 6: attributing the decipherment or the Ashoka identification to the wrong person.**
*Why it is attractive:* two nineteenth-century achievements about the same inscriptions look like one.
*Correction:* **James Prinsep deciphered both Brahmi and Kharosthi in 1837**; **George Turnour** identified Piyadasi as Ashoka, using the Pali chronicle **Dipavamsa**.
*Drill:* rehearse the chain - decipherment, then a name in the text, then identification from an external Pali chronicle - as three steps by two people.

**Error 7: assuming every Indic script is an abugida and runs left to right.**
*Why it is attractive:* it is true of the most familiar ones.
*Correction:* **Kharosthi** is mostly right to left, though some inscriptions run left to right; the **Urdu script** is right to left; **Ol Chiki**, created in 1925, is a **true alphabet** giving vowels equal representation.
*Drill:* for each script, store three properties - direction, type and the languages it carries.

## Error set C - attribution and chronology

**Error 8: completing a famous-name pattern.**
*Why it is attractive:* one recognisable name in an option set feels like an answer.
*Correction:* recall the **content** and check it against the author's profile. Kiratarjuniyam is Arjuna and Shiva as a hunter - **Bharavi**; Shishupalavadha is Krishna killing Shishupala - **Magha**; Mrichchhakatika is **Sudraka's** while its precursor Daridra Charudatta is **Bhasa's**; the Kathasaritsagara is **Somadeva Bhatta's** and the Nitivakyamrita **Somadeva Suri's**.
*Drill:* say the plot before you say the author.

**Error 9: treating a discovery, recognition or publication year as a composition date.**
*Why it is attractive:* both are years attached to the same object.
*Correction:* the **Chebrolu inscription** was issued in **AD 207** and discovered in **2019**; the **Charyapada** was composed in the tenth-eleventh centuries and recovered in **1916**; **Shah Jo Risalo** collects a seventeenth-century poet and was **published in 1866**; the **Rigveda's** Memory of the World inscription is **2007**.
*Drill:* write the date, then write the noun it belongs to - issue, discovery, recovery, publication, recognition.

**Error 10: giving a single date to a layered corpus.**
*Why it is attractive:* a single date is easier to remember and to write.
*Correction:* the Vedic corpus was **orally handed down** and then **compiled**, with historians estimating **1500-1000 BCE**; the Sangam window of **300 BC-AD 300** covers composition **and** compilation; the Brahmanas' usual dating of **900-700 BC** carries a recorded disagreement among historians; and the transition between language stages is described as slow and **not separable into strict chronological periods**.
*Drill:* replace every bare year in a corpus sentence with a range plus its attribution.

## Error set D - status and recognition

**Error 11: "Hindi is India's national language."**
*Why it is attractive:* the phrase is culturally ubiquitous.
*Correction:* **there is no national language of India**, and neither the Constitution nor any Act defines the term. **Article 343(1)** makes Hindi in Devanagari script the **official language of the Union**, and the **Official Languages Act, 1963** gives English the status of **subsidiary official language**.
*Drill:* check the **label** in any statement before you evaluate its content; an item in this area usually swaps one status word into an otherwise correct clause.

**Error 12: quoting a total without its date.**
*Why it is attractive:* totals feel like stable facts.
*Correction:* every total here has changed: the Eighth Schedule from fourteen to twenty-two across 1967, 1992 and 2003; classical languages from six to eleven in **October 2024**; India's Memory of the World inscriptions from 1997 to 2025.
*Drill:* write the date in the same sentence as the number, or cite named instances instead of a total.

**Error 13: assuming classical status implies scheduling, or that either implies antiquity.**
*Why it is attractive:* all three feel like the same kind of honour.
*Correction:* **Pali and Prakrit** hold classical status without being scheduled; **Sanskrit** holds both; recognition years say nothing about a text's or a language's age, as the 2025 International Register inscription of the ancient Bhagavadgita manuscripts against the 2003 inscription of colonial archives demonstrates.
*Drill:* for any status claim, name the **authority**, the **instrument** and the **date**.

**Error 14: treating Memory of the World as World Heritage.**
*Why it is attractive:* both are UNESCO and both confer prestige.
*Correction:* Memory of the World recognises **documents**, at **two register levels** - the **International Register** for global significance and the **MOWCAP Asia-Pacific Regional Register** for regional relevance; World Heritage recognises **places** under the 1972 Convention; Intangible Cultural Heritage safeguards **living practices**; a Geographical Indication is a **domestic intellectual-property instrument**, not a UNESCO programme.
*Drill:* name the **object** of the recognition - a manuscript, a site, a practice or a product - and, for a manuscript, the **register level**, before naming the programme.

## Drill sets

**Drill set A - split the claim.** Take each sentence and name every separable assertion in it, then evaluate each: (1) "Pali is the language the Buddha spoke, and the Tripitaka preserves his words in it." (2) "Sangam poetry gives us the political history of the Cheras, Cholas and Pandyas." (3) "Urdu came to India from Persia in the thirteenth century." (4) "Brahmi is the ancestor of all Indian scripts and all Indian languages."

**Drill set B - name the date type.** For each, state which of the eight date types is being given: the Chebrolu inscription and 2019; the Rigveda and 2007; the Charyapada and 1916; Svapnavasavadattam and 1912; Shah Jo Risalo and 1866; the Sangam corpus and 300 BC-AD 300; the Jain Angas and the mid-fifth century AD; Gurmukhi and the sixteenth century.

**Drill set C - place the object.** For each, state the family, the script it is written in where recorded, the corpus it belongs to and the status it holds if any: Tolkappiyam; the Shatkhandagama; the Guru Granth Sahib; Amuktamalyada; the Gilgit Manuscript; the Kollam Copper Plate; Kavirajamarga; the Hamzanama.

**Drill set D - discriminate the near neighbours.** Separate each pair: Tarikh-e-Firoz Shahi and Tarikh-i-Firoz Shahi; Shahjahannama and Padshahnama; Adi Granth and Guru Granth Sahib; Dharmasutra and Dharmashastra; Bhasya and Nibandha; parable and fable; Gurmukhi and Shahmukhi; Nastaliq and the Urdu script; Alvars and Nayanars; Ettuthokai and Pattuppattu; Mahapurana and Upapurana; Siddhamatrika and Nandinagari.

**Drill set E - state the boundary.** For each demand, say which owner it belongs to and what this topic supplies: the significance of Ashokan inscriptions for Mauryan administration; the doctrine of the Upanishads; the linguistic reorganisation of States; the current total of India's World Heritage Sites; the administrative history of the National Archives; the Bhakti movement's social impact; Odissi dance as a performing art.

```text
   |      |         Kannada, Gujarati,    |        Sanskrit + MANIPRAVALAM)
   |      |         Hindi, Tamil)         |        -> MALAYALAM, TIGALARI,
   |      +-- SARADA (8th c, Kashmir;     |           SINHALA
   |      |    Sanskrit and Kashmiri;     |
   |      |    now ceremonial among       +-- NANDINAGARI (from Nagari;
   |      |    Kashmiri Pandits)          |    7th-19th c; South India;
   |      |     |                         |    NO CONNECTING HEADLINE;
   |      |     +-- GURMUKHI (standardised|    Vijayanagara Sanskrit
   |      |         16th c by GURU ANGAD; |    COPPER PLATES)
   |      |         Guru Granth Sahib)    |
   |      +-- SIDDHAM / SIDDHAMATRIKA (6th-13th c; "perfected mother";
   |           Sanskrit mantras; VAJRAYANA association)
   |            -> NAGARI, EASTERN NAGARI, TIRHUTA, ODIA, NEPALESE
   |            -> BENGALI, ASSAMESE, ODIA, MAITHILI
   +-- KAITHI (Brahmic; UP, Jharkhand, Bihar; LEGAL, ADMINISTRATIVE and
        PRIVATE RECORDS; widely used in the Mughal period)

  OUTSIDE THE BRAHMI DESCENT
  URDU SCRIPT  <- Persian alphabet <- Arabic alphabet; R->L; 13th-c
    origins; up to 40 letters, no letter cases; written in NASTALIQ
    (a calligraphic hand developed in Persia, 14th-15th c);
    extended form SHAHMUKHI for Punjabi and Saraiki
  OL CHIKI     created 1925 by PANDIT RAGHUNATH MURMU for SANTALI;
    a TRUE ALPHABET, not an abugida; the Constitution translated into it
  BHARATI      IIT Madras; a unified script proposed for NINE languages
```

```text
  COMPOSITION
      |

      |           exact pronunciation, intonation, memorisation)
      |           Panar & Viraliyar bards | Rajasthani virkavya bards
      |           Pirs reciting ginans
      |           FAILURE MODE: lineage ends -> whole bundle lost
      |           EVIDENCE: Sama Veda 1000+ -> 3 ; Rig 21 -> 1 + parts ;
      |                     Atharva 9 -> 2
      v
  COMMITTED TO WRITING on a MEDIUM
      +-- PALM LEAF (south; Silappadikaram; Vatteluttu manuscripts)
      +-- BIRCH BARK (north/north-west; named in the script definition)
      +-- PAPER (later, general; enables volume and illustration)
      |
  COPIED BY SCRIBES
      +-- evidence in the writing system itself: MODI as a shorthand
      |   created because scribes could not write full Devanagari at speed
      +-- documentary scripts: KAITHI for legal, administrative and

      |   recovered 1916

      +-- language itself lost: Avestan, extinct
      |
  SURVEYED -> CATALOGUED -> CONSERVED (labs by material: palm leaf,
  birch bark, paper) -> TRANSCRIBED (AI-assisted; legacy scripts such
  as Sarada, Modi, Nandinagari, Kaithi, Tirhuta) -> PROVENANCE TRACKED
```
