## Map 5 - four comparison matrices

**Matrix A - the two registers.**

| | Sanskrit | Prakrit |
|---|---|---|
| Character | orthodox, fixed rules | natural, original, casual, no strict rules |
| Users | learned people and elites, especially Brahmins | common people |
| Writing | early and prestigious | texts written relatively late by comparison |
| On stage | high-varna characters, in Gupta drama | women and Shudras, in Gupta drama |
| Span | Old Indo-Aryan from c. 1500 BC; classical form 300-200 BC | Middle Indo-Aryan, 600 BC-AD 1000 |

**Matrix B - the two witnesses.**

| | Inscription | Manuscript |
|---|---|---|
| Media | rock, pillar, copper plate | palm leaf, birch bark, paper |
| Survives by | durability | repeated copying |
| Date | usually of the act itself | of the copy, not the text |
| Place | fixed | mobile |
| Strong on | date, place, language, script | content, argument, literary form |
| Failure mode | selection by power | change through transmission |

**Matrix C - the five statuses.**

| Status | Authority | Instrument | Current position |
|---|---|---|---|
| Union official language | Constitution and Parliament | Article 343(1); Official Languages Act, 1963 | Hindi in Devanagari; English subsidiary official |
| Eighth Schedule | Parliament by amendment | the Schedule | 22, after 1967, 1992 and 2003 expansions |
| State official language | the State | State decision | need not be scheduled; Kokborok, French, Mizo |
| Classical language | Government of India | the 2004 decision; criteria revised November 2005 and 2024 | 11, most recently 3 October 2024 |
| Documentary-heritage inscription | UNESCO | Memory of the World: International Register, and the MOWCAP Asia-Pacific Regional Register | India's named International entries, 1997-2025; its first three Regional entries, 2024 |

**Matrix D - four religion-language relationships.**

| Tradition | Relationship |
|---|---|
| Buddhist | language tracks **school**: Pali for Theravada; Sanskrit for Mahayana and Sarvastivada; mixed in the Mahavastu |
| Jain | language tracks **region and patronage**: Ardhamagadhi, Shauraseni, Sanskrit, Apabhramsha, Tamil, Kannada, Gujarati, Marathi |
| Zoroastrian | language is **fixed then lost**: Avestan, now extinct; later works centuries after |
| Sikh | language is **deliberately composite**: Sant Bhasha from seven named sources, in a purpose-standardised script |

## Map 6 - the six answer spines

```text
 SPINE 1 - LINGUISTIC DIVERSITY
 four families + residual -> population shares -> the agglutinative /
 inflected contrast -> the dialect layer and the two counting systems ->
 the Linguistic Diversity Index -> endangerment (197; 5 extinct) ->
 the classification cautions -> verdict: layered, not merely plural

 SPINE 2 - SANSKRIT AND PRAKRIT
 codification (Panini -> Katyayana -> Patanjali) -> the register contrast
 -> the Gupta stage convention as documented evidence -> epigraphic
 anchors (Junagarh AD 150, Chebrolu AD 207) -> Apabhramsha and Bhamaha's
 three-fold classification -> verdict: coexistence, not succession

 SPINE 3 - SCRIPTS
 definition and media -> Indus (undeciphered) -> Brahmi and Kharosthi
 (type, direction, languages, Prinsep 1837, Turnour and the Dipavamsa)
 -> the descent tree -> the four proofs that script is not language ->
 the two non-Brahmi exceptions -> verdict: adoption and decision

 SPINE 4 - A LITERARY CORPUS AS EVIDENCE
 what the corpus is (structure, anthologies, authorship) -> what it
 records (material culture, institutions, classifications, values) ->
 what it cannot support (connected chronology; single dates) ->
 the transmission history -> verdict: bounded richness

 SPINE 5 - PATRONAGE AND MULTILINGUALISM
 the patronage ledger from the Sungas to Awadh -> rulers as authors ->
 multi-language courts (Krishnadevaraya's four; Pallava bilingualism) ->
 the translation economy (Fatehpur Sikri; Panchatantra outward) ->
 verdict: multilingual production was the normal condition

 SPINE 6 - HERITAGE AND RECOGNITION
 the material object -> documented survival losses -> loss as
 institutional failure -> survey, catalogue, conserve, transcribe,
 track provenance -> recognition as a separate dated act ->
 composition date against inscription year -> the programme firewall ->
 verdict: documentation precedes recognition; antiquity is neither
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## Classification vocabulary and the six separations

- Language family: languages descended from a common ancestor predating recorded history. Dialect: a local form of a language. Register: a variety selected by social situation. Script: a standard representing a spoken language by marks on a medium - paper, rock, birch bark. Abugida: consonant-based unit, secondary vowel notation except word-initially.
- Six separable claims in any sentence: family, language/register, script, corpus, material witness, status. A seventh - value - carries no marks.
- Lingua franca: a bridge language used between people not sharing a native language, typically a third language distinct from both. English now; Latin in the Roman empire; Pali for Theravada Buddhism; Persian at the Sultanate and Mughal courts.

## Language families of India

- Indo-Aryan ~74%, a branch of Indo-European; three sub-groups by period of origin. Dravidian ~25%, from Proto-Dravidian, 21 languages. Sino-Tibetan ~0.6%, "Kiratas" in old Sanskrit, older than Indo-Aryan. Austric (Austro-Asiatic), "Nisadas" in old Sanskrit. Plus "Others": Gondi, Oraon, Praji.
- Dravidian Northern 3: Brahui (Baluchistan), Malto, Kurukh. Central 11 including Telugu. Southern 7: Kannada, Tamil, Malayalam, Tulu, Kodagu, Toda, Kota. Majors: Telugu numerically largest, Tamil oldest, Kannada, Malayalam smallest and youngest. Tulu: pardana oral epics; Yakshagana. Telugu called the "Italian of the East" by Niccolò de' Conti, 15th century.
- Sino-Tibetan: Tibeto-Burman in four groups (Tibetan; Himalayan; North Assam; Kuki-Garo-Bodo-Mikir-Naga with Meitei most important) and Siamese-Chinese, represented by the extinct Ahom.
- Austric: Munda/Kol plus Mon-Khmer (Khasi, Nicobarese); Santhali most important; all Indian Austro-Asiatic languages except Khasi and Santhali are endangered.
- Grammar: Indo-Aryan inflected; Dravidian agglutinative. Root vocabulary also differs.
- Romani: the only New Indo-Aryan language spoken exclusively outside the subcontinent.
- Unsafe sentences: Sanskrit as mother of all Indian languages; Dravidian from Prakrit (an attributed minority view); Tamil as "purest" (evaluative).

## Indo-Aryan layers and registers

- Old Indo-Aryan from c. 1500 BC: Vedic Sanskrit; classical or "chaste" form 300-200 BC. Grammar: Panini's Ashtadhyayi (source date 400 BC) -> Katyayana's Varttika-sutra, 1500 verses, surviving only through references in Patanjali -> Patanjali's Mahabhashya, 250 BC. Katyayana lies between Panini and Patanjali.
- Sanskrit epigraphy: Junagarh/Girnar of Rudradaman I, c. AD 150; Chebrolu of Satavahana king Vijaya, AD 207, discovered 2019, earliest datable South Indian Sanskrit inscription so far.
- Sanskrit crosses religions: Mahavastu mixes Sanskrit, Pali and Prakrit; Lalitavistara Sutra and Ashvaghosha's Buddhacharita are Sanskrit.
- Middle Indo-Aryan, 600 BC-AD 1000. Prakrit: natural, casual, common tongue, no strict rules; writing in it relatively late. Sanskrit: orthodox, fixed rules, elites especially Brahmins.
- Pali: Magadha, 5th-1st century BC, origin in Ujjain or Taxila per some scholars; Tripitaka; written in Brahmi; lingua franca of Theravada Buddhism; the Buddha believed not to have spoken it.
- Ardhamagadhi: "most important kind of Prakrit"; court language of some Mahajanapadas and the Mauryas; Jain texts and Ashokan rock edicts; evolved into Bengali, Assamese, Odia, Maithili, Bhojpuri.
- Shauraseni: Dramatic Prakrit; Jain monks; the Digambara Shatkhandgama. Maharashtri: to the 9th century; predecessor of Marathi and Konkani; Satavahana official language; Gaha Kosha by Hala; Gaudavaho by Vakpati. Elu: old Sinhala. Paishachi: "Bhutabhasa"; Gunadhya's Brihatkatha; a chapter in the Prakrita Prakasha attributed to Vararuchi.
- Apabhramsha, 6th-7th century: "corrupt"; umbrella term; transition to Modern Indo-Aryan; Bhamaha divided poetry into Sanskrit, Prakrit and Apabhramsha; Dandin called it the dialect of the common folk; Pushpadanta's Mahapurana; Dhanapala's Bhavisayattakaha.
- Register as social marker: in Gupta plays, high-varna characters speak Sanskrit, women and Shudras Prakrit - a dramatic convention, not a transcript of speech.

## Dialects and the modern languages

- Modern Indo-Aryan after AD 1000: Hindi, Assamese, Bengali, Gujarati, Marathi, Punjabi, Rajasthani, Sindhi, Odia, Urdu.
- Hindi blocks: Western (Khari Boli standard; Braj Bhasha literary; Bundeli; Haryanvi; Kannauji); Eastern (Awadhi; Bagheli; Chhattisgarhi); Rajasthani (Marwari, Mewari, Jaipuri, Mewati, Malwi); Bihari (Bhojpuri, Magahi, Maithili); Pahari (Kumaoni, Garhwali); plus Bambaiya and Haflong Hindi.
- Suniti Kumar Chatterji's four Bengali dialects: Rarhi, Bangali, Kamrupi, Varendri. Punjabi's Majhi is standard. Tamil's Central dialect is standard; Madras Bashai is Chennai's. Maithili was written in Tirhuta, now mainly Devanagari. Urdu varieties: Dakhini (southern), Rekhta (older/poetic).
- Two counting systems: descriptive (thousands of varieties; world total near 7,000, around 359 spoken by millions) and administrative (22 scheduled; state languages outside the Schedule). Linguistic Diversity Index runs 0 to 1; no country is exactly either.

## Constitutional and administrative status

- Article 343(1): Hindi in Devanagari script as the official language of the Union. English was to cease for official purposes on 26 January 1965 unless Parliament decided otherwise; protests led to the Official Languages Act, 1963, which makes English the subsidiary official language.
- Eighth Schedule: original 14 - Assamese, Bengali, Gujarati, Hindi, Kannada, Kashmiri, Malayalam, Marathi, Odia, Punjabi, Sanskrit, Tamil, Telugu, Urdu; +Sindhi (21st Amendment, 1967); +Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali (71st, 1992); +Bodo, Maithili, Dogri, Santhali (92nd, 2003) = 22. English is not listed; Sanskrit is.
- States may adopt any official language, scheduled or not: Tripura-Kokborok (Sino-Tibetan), Puducherry-French, Mizoram-Mizo. Arunachal Pradesh and Nagaland have English as their only official language.
- There is no national language of India; neither the Constitution nor any Act defines the term.

## Classical-language recognition

- Created by a Government of India decision in 2004; criteria revised in November 2005 and again in 2024 on Linguistic Experts Committee recommendations. **Criteria in force (2024):** antiquity of early texts or recorded history over 1500-2000 years; a body of ancient literature considered **a heritage** by generations of speakers; **knowledge texts, especially prose texts in addition to poetry, epigraphical and inscriptional evidence**; and the classical language and literature **could be** distinct from the current form or discontinuous with later forms of its offshoots. **Dropped in 2024:** "original and not borrowed from another speech community" (a 2004 and 2005 criterion). The 2004 original set had three criteria only and set antiquity at **over a thousand years**.
- Eleven, with official dates: Tamil 12 October 2004 and Sanskrit 25 November 2005 (Ministry of Home Affairs); Telugu and Kannada 31 October 2008, Malayalam 8 August 2013, Odia 1 March 2014 (Ministry of Culture); Marathi, Pali, Prakrit, Assamese and Bengali by Union Cabinet approval on 3 October 2024. The Linguistic Experts Committee was established on 1 November 2004, and the 2024 Committee recommended the five as fulfilling the revised criteria.
- Benefits as promised: two annual international awards; a Centre of Excellence; the UGC to be requested to create professional chairs, at least in Central Universities to begin with. As implemented: three Central Universities for Sanskrit established in 2020 by an Act of Parliament; the Central Institute of Classical Tamil; Centres for Excellence in Classical Kannada, Telugu, Malayalam and Odia under the Central Institute of Indian Languages, Mysuru; National Awards, university chairs and centres through the Ministry of Education. NEP 2020 makes classical languages available in schools as options and names Persian among them, though Persian does not hold the status.
- Pali and Prakrit hold classical status without Eighth Schedule listing.

## Endangerment, extinction and revival

- Mechanism: migration for social and economic needs -> abandonment of the native language -> failure of transmission in the intergenerational period -> death and extinction.
- UNESCO's Atlas of the World Languages in Danger since 1996; the World Atlas of Languages documents language status as communicative tools and knowledge resources. Four levels between Safe and Extinct: Vulnerable, Definitely Endangered, Severely Endangered, Critically Endangered.
- India: 197 endangered, vulnerable or extinct - 81 vulnerable, 62 definitely endangered, 7 severely endangered, 42 critically endangered, 5 extinct. The highest number in danger of any country.
- Five extinct: Aka-Bo (last speaker 2010), Aka-Cari (2020), Ahom (Tai, Assam), Pazeh (Austronesian; last speaker died in Taiwan, 2010), Konda-Dora (Dravidian, Andhra Pradesh).
- Two 10,000-speaker rules: after the 1971 Census, Indian languages with fewer than 10,000 speakers were no longer considered in the List of Official Languages; UNESCO treats fewer than 10,000 speakers as potentially endangered.
- Initiatives: Adi Vaani (2025, AI, tribal languages, consortium led by IIT Delhi); BHASHINI (2022; Anuvaad, Vaanianuvaad, Chitraanuvaad); Bharatavani (CIIL; over 100 languages); DIKSHA (33 languages plus Indian Sign Language) and SWAYAM, with Anuvaadini used by AICTE and UGC; NEP 2020 mother-tongue instruction to at least Class 5, preferably Class 8; Bhasha Sangam (22 languages over 22 working days); National Translation Mission (higher-education texts into all 22 scheduled languages).

## Scripts: descent, type and decipherment

- Indus signs: short inscriptions; unclear whether they constitute a script recording a language; boustrophedon.
- Brahmi: oldest writing system of the subcontinent and Central Asia in the final centuries BCE and early centuries AD; origin debated between a Semitic script and the Indus script; wrote Prakrit, Sanskrit and Tamil-Brahmi; usually left to right; abugida; stages Ashokan, Kushana, Gupta/Northern; Gupta Brahmi -> Siddhamatrika/Kutila in the late 6th century -> Bengali, Assamese, Odia, Maithili; Sarada emerges in Kashmir at the same time; Northern angular, Southern circular; ancestor of all surviving Indic scripts in Southeast Asia. Best-known inscriptions: Ashoka's rock-cut edicts, 250-232 BCE.
- Kharosthi: 3rd century BCE-3rd century CE; Gandhara; Gandhari Prakrit and Sanskrit; sister script of Brahmi; abugida; numerals resembling Roman ones; mostly right to left though some inscriptions run left to right; named Bactrian Pahlavi by Prinsep after Bactrian coins. Bactrian was an extinct Eastern Iranian language and the official language of the Kushan empire.
- Both deciphered by James Prinsep in 1837; George Turnour identified Piyadasi as Ashoka using the Pali chronicle Dipavamsa.
- Regional scripts: Gupta (Sanskrit; parent of Nagari, Sarada, Siddham); Siddhamatrika 6th-13th c ("perfected mother", Vajrayana); Kadamba 4th-6th c (Kannada) -> Kannada-Telugu; Vatteluttu (Tamil and Malayalam; replaced by Pallava-Grantha in the Pallava court); Grantha 6th-20th c (Sanskrit and Manipravalam; parent of Malayalam, Tigalari, Sinhala); Sarada 8th c (Sanskrit and Kashmiri; now ceremonial); Gurmukhi (from Sarada; standardised 16th c by Guru Angad; Guru Granth Sahib; developed outside the ambit of Sanskrit tradition); Devanagari 7th c (47 characters: 14 vowels, 33 consonants); Modi 14th c (scribal shorthand; official for Marathi until the 20th c, then Balbodh; also used for Urdu, Kannada, Gujarati, Hindi, Tamil); Kaithi (legal, administrative and private records; Mughal period); Nandinagari (7th-19th c; South India; no connecting headline; Vijayanagara Sanskrit copper plates).
- Outside Brahmi descent: the Urdu script (Perso-Arabic, right to left, up to 40 letters, no letter cases, Nastaliq hand, extended form Shahmukhi for Punjabi and Saraiki); Ol Chiki (1925, Raghunath Murmu, Santali, a true alphabet; the Constitution translated into it). Bharati: an IIT Madras unified script for nine languages.
- Nastaliq is a calligraphic hand developed in Persia in the 14th-15th centuries; short verticals without serifs, long horizontal strokes.

## Inscription against manuscript

- Inscription: durable, fixed, usually official; strong on date, place, language and script; failure mode is selection. Manuscript: perishable, mobile, survives by recopying; strong on content and form; failure mode is transmission change.
- Earliest datable anchors: Sanskrit AD 150 (Junagarh) and AD 207 (Chebrolu, South India). Tamil becomes an important inscriptional language under the Pallavas, with bilingual Tamil-Sanskrit inscriptions from the 7th century; Chola and Pandya kings issued Tamil and bilingual Sanskrit-Tamil inscriptions.
- Two recorded internal conflicts: Kannada - earliest inscriptions in the late 6th/early 7th century AD, against the Halmidi inscription of the 5th century AD named as the oldest; Telugu - late 6th century Telugu Chola epigraphs as the beginning, against the Tippalar and Bhodampudu inscriptions of the 7th century AD as the oldest. Cite the named evidence and record the conflict.
- Multilingual documents: Kollam Copper Plate, 9th century - mostly Tamil in Vatteluttu with Grantha words; witness signatures in Arabic-Kufic, Pahlavi and Judaeo-Persian in Hebrew script. Kurgod inscription, Karnataka, 12th century - Sanskrit, Prakrit and Kannada.

## Vedic corpus and oral transmission

- Shruti: Vedas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas, Upanishads. Smriti: Vedanga, Smritis-Dharmashastras, Shad darshana, Puranas, Itihasa, Upavedas, Mahakavyas.
- Veda from vid, "to know"; believed Apaurusheya; orally transmitted then compiled, historians estimating 1500-1000 BCE; composed by rishis; all Samhitas give prominence to yagna.
- Rig Veda: oldest existing; 1028 hymns; 10 Mandalas; compiled c. 1500-1200 BC per historians; Hotri reciters; chief deity Indra; Usha, Prithvi and Vak among goddesses; Rigarthadipika by Venkata Madhava. Mandalas II-VII are the Gotra/Vamsa Mandalas or Kula Grantha; III holds the Gayatri Mantra; IX is entirely Soma Pavamana; X holds Nadistuti, Purush, Nasadiya and Surya Suktas and death hymns.
- Yajur Veda: "Yajus" = sacrifice; ritual guidebook; Shukla (Madhyandina, Kanva) and Krishna (Taittiriya, Kathaka, Maitrayani, Kapishthala); seems to know the metal iron.
- Sama Veda: named after Saman; 1875 hymns, historians arguing 75 are original, the rest from the Sakala branch of the Rig Veda; 16,000 ragas and raginis; book of chants; Udgatri reciters; Chandogya Upanishad attached.
- Atharva Veda: Brahma Veda; Atharvan and Angiras; Atharvangirasa; treatment for almost 99 diseases; black and white magic; book of magical formula.
- Traji/Trayi Vidya = Rig, Sama and Yajur.
- Sakha and charana: recensions held by family- or guru-based schools with a duty of oral transmission and accurate pronunciation, intonation and memorisation; each sakha carries its Samhita, Brahmana, sometimes an Aranyaka and one or more Upanishads. Survival: Rig 21 -> 1 complete + parts of Bashkala; Sama over 1000 -> Kauthuma, Jaiminiya (Talavakara), Ranayaniya; Atharva 9 -> Shaunaka, Paippalada.
- Brahmanas: one per Veda; usually 900-700 BC with historians disagreeing; Aitareya and Kaushitaki (Rig), Taittiriya and Shatapatha (Yajur), Tandya Mahabrahmana/Panchavimsha and Jaiminiya (Sama), Gopatha (Atharva); Shatapatha the largest and most philosophically rich.
- Aranyakas: "belonging to the forest"; taught by Munis; bridge Karma Marga and Gyan Marga.
- Upanishads: "to sit down near"; more than 200; Muktika canon of 108; principal ten - Isha, Kena, Katha, Prashan, Mundaka, Mandukya, Tattiriya, Aitareya, Chandogya, Brihadaranyaka; also called Vedanta; oldest Brihadaranyaka and Chandogya; Satyameva Jayate from Mundaka; transmitted within guru-shishya parampara.
- Six Vedangas: Siksha, Nirukta, Chhanda, Kalpasutra, Jyotisha, Vyakarana; later Sutras written on them, including the Ashtadhyayi.
- Five Upavedas: Dhanurveda (Yajur), Gandharvaveda (Sama), Ayurveda (Atharva), Arthasastra (Atharva), Sthapatyaveda (Yajur; the fifth).

## Epics, Puranas and the law-book tradition

- The epics are classical-category texts of religious importance and precursors of kavya, nataka and technical treatises.
- Puranas: "that which renews the old"; creation to destruction; folk tales, pilgrimages, astronomy, grammar, theology; focused on Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesh; 18 Mahapuranas; prominent - Bhagavata, Brahma, Vayu, Agni, Garuda, Padma, Vishnu, Matsya; Skanda longest, Markandeya shortest; supply geography, history and dynastic genealogies; translated into vernacular languages.
- About 19 Upapuranas on five subjects dictated by Amarasimha: Sarga, Pratisarga, Manvantara, Vamsa, Vamshanucharita.
- Dharmasutra (Vedic era, based on the Vedas) -> Dharmashastra (18 to 100 texts; poetic verses; mainly based on the Puranas) -> Bhasyas (commentaries interpreting with reasons; Bharuci and Medhatithi on the Manusmriti) -> Nibandhas (digests reconciling conflicts; oldest surviving Krityakalpataru, early 12th century, by Lakshmidhara of Kannauj).
- Four Dharmashastra texts: Manusmriti (2nd-3rd c AD, most studied, influence in Cambodia and Indonesia); Yajnavalkya Smriti (4th-5th c, best crafted); Naradasmriti (5th-6th c, juridical); Visnusmriti (7th c, bhakti).
- Property norms: paternal estate divided equally among sons; women could not claim a share; Stridhana retained by women and inheritable by their children without any claim of the husband.
- Nibandha authors by subject: Jimutavahana and Raghunandana on inheritance; Nanda-pandita on adoption; Todar Mal on the king's duties; Candesvara, Kamalakara-bhatta, Nilakantha and Mitra-misra on judicial process; Lakshmidevi's Vivadachandra and Mahadevi Dhiramati's Danavakyavali by women.
- Didactic/directive text persuades, coaxes and compels, used for political and moral issues, sermons and religious treatises; narrative text sustains interest, the most common prose type, used in story writing and novels. Parable: human character, spiritual/moral/religious lesson. Fable: animals, objects, mythical creatures and plants with human-like qualities.
- Panchatantra by Vishnu Sharma: didactic animal fables in Sanskrit, c. 200 BC-AD 300; ancient non-religious text; wise conduct of life, political science and statecraft. Hitopadesha by Narayan Pandit, c. 12th century.

## Classical Sanskrit

- Period 4th-12th century AD; bound by the rules of Panini's Ashtadhyayi; Patanjali's Mahabhashya and Katyayana's Varttika-sutra the other two famous grammars.
- Drama's purpose: Lokaranjana - diversion or entertainment.
- Four kavya styles: Mahakavya, Khandakavya, Gadya Kavya (Kadambari, Dashakumaracharita), Champu (Vasavadatta by Subandhu).
- Poetics vocabulary: alankara, rasa, dhvani, riti, vakrokti, gunas.
- Dramatists: Bharata Muni (Natyashastra, the Fifth Veda; Rasa, Bhava, stagecraft); Sudraka (Mrichchhakatika - courtesan heroine, non-mythological, realism, early Holi reference; Vinavasavadatta; Padmaprabhritaka); Vishakhadatta (Mudrarakshasa; Devi-Chandraguptam, fragmentary); Bhavabhuti (Uttararamacharita, Malatimadhava, Mahaviracharita); Bhasa (Svapnavasavadattam, rediscovered 1912 by T. Ganapati Sastri; Urubhanga's sympathetic Duryodhana; Pratima-nataka, Abhisheka-nataka, Panch-ratra, Madhyama-vyayoga, Duta-Ghattotkacha, Duta-Vakya, Karnabharam, Harivamsa/Bala-charita, Yagna-Phalam, Daridra Charudatta); Kalidasa (Abhijnanashakuntalam praised by Goethe; Malavikagnimitram; Vikramorvashiyam); Trivikrama Bhatta (Damayanti-Katha); Harshavardhana (Ratnavali, mentioning Holi; Priyadarsika; Nagananda); Maharana Kumbha (Sangita Raja, Sangita Mimamsa, Sudha Prabandha; a commentary on the Gita Govinda; four dramas said to use four provincial languages).
- Poets: Kalidasa (Kumarasambhavam; Raghuvamsha from Dileepa to Agnivarna; Meghaduta's Yaksha exiled by Kubera; Ritusamhara's six seasons); Jayadeva (Gita Govinda); Dandin (Kavyadarsha; Dashakumaracharita); Banabhatta (Kadambari; Harshacharita, the first historical biography in India); Harisena (Allahabad Pillar Inscription, champu style); Bharavi (Kiratarjuniyam); Magha (Shishupalavadha); Sriharsa (Naishadhiyacharita); Nayachandra Suri (Hammira Mahakavya); Somadeva Suri (Upasakadyayana, Nitivakyamrita); Subandhu (Vasavadatta); Bhatti (Bhattikavya/Ravanavadha); Bhartrhari (Vakyapadiya; Nitishataka); Vatsyayana (Kamasutra; Nyaya Sutra Bhashya).
- Science: Pingala, Charak, Sushruta, Madhava, Varahamihira (Pancha-Siddhantika; Brihat-Samhita), Aryabhata (Aryabhatiya - zero, place value, trigonometry, planetary models), Lagadha, Bhaskara II; Surya Siddhanta; Yuktidipika. Arthashastra names "Kautilya" or "Vishnugupta", argued to be aliases for Chanakya.
- Gupta period: Golden Age; Kalidasa in Chandragupta II's court; Amarakosha by Amarsimha; Narada Smriti; inscriptions including Prayag Prashasti, Bhittari, Junagadh and Udaygiri read as literature.
- Medieval Sanskrit: Kalhana's Rajatarangini; Somadeva Bhatta's Kathasaritsagara (11th c); Bilhana's Vikramankadevacharita. Yoga Vasistha Samhita: c. 7th-15th century AD; ~29,000 verses; six books; Advaita-like, drsti-srsti sub-school; repeatedly translated.

## Buddhist, Jain, Zoroastrian and Sikh corpora

- Buddhist canonical: Tripitaka in Pali - Vinaya (Suttavibhanga, Khandhaka, Parivara), Sutta (over 10,000 suttas; five Nikayas - Digha, Majjhima, Samyutta, Anguttara, Khuddaka, the last holding the Dhammapada, Jataka tales, Sutta Nipata and Udana), Abhidhamma (7 books including Dhammasangani and Patthana). Jatakas: 550+; last 10 depict the 10 paramitas; Nidanakatha as narrative frame.
- Buddhist Sanskrit: Buddhacharita (Ashvaghosha), Lalitavistara Sutra (ends with the first sermon at Sarnath), Mulamadhyamakakarika (Nagarjuna, Shunyata), Abhidharma Mahavibhasa Sastra (150 CE, Sarvastivada, basis of Vaibhasika). Mahavastu: Mahasamghika Lokottaravada, mixed Sanskrit-Pali-Prakrit. Milinda Panho (Menander and Nagasena). Visuddhimagga (Buddhaghosa, 5th c; sila, samadhi, prajna). Chronicles: Dipavamsa (oldest Sri Lankan, c. 3rd-4th c CE, Buddha's three visits, tooth relic), Mahavamsa (5th c, Mahanama, Mahinda), Mahavamsatika, Bodhi-Vamsa (12th c, Upatissa, Sanskritised Pali). Divya Vadana (2nd c CE, Dharmaraksita). Sanghabhuti's commentary on the Sarvastivada Vinaya.
- Jain: Agamas in Ardhamagadhi, 45 texts - 11 Angas, 12 Upangas, 10 Prakirnaka-sutras, 4 Mulasutras, 6 Chedasutras, 2 Chulikasutras; compiled by the Ganadharas; recompiled by the Svetambaras under Acharya Shraman Devardhigani at Vallabhi in the mid-5th century AD; the Digambaras reject that authority and hold the Karmaprabhrita/Shatkhandagama and Kashayaprabhrita sacred. Non-canonical Niryuktis and Samhitas by Bhadrabahu.
- Jain authors: Bhadrabahu (last Shrutkevali; teacher of Chandragupta Maurya; Shravanabelagola; Uvasaggaharam Stotra; Kalpa Sutra recited at Paryushana); Kundkund (Samayasara, Niyamasara); Samantabhadra (Ratnakaranda Sravakachara with 12 vratas; Aptamimansa introducing Syadvada); Umaswami/Umasvati (Tattvarthasutra, 2nd-5th c CE, in Sanskrit, the only text both sects accept, Seven Tattvas); Jinasena (Mahapurana, Adipurana); Haribhadra Suri (Saddarsanasamuccaya, Samaraiccakaha, anekantavada); Hemachandra Suri (Parishishtaparvan; Kalikalasarvajna; adviser to Kumarapala); Subhachandra (Pandavapurana); Tirutakkatevar (Civaka Cintamani); Nalatiyar's 400 quatrains; Valaiapathi, lost except fragments; Vagavatisutra; Nigantha Nikaya. Vallabhi and Kalinga as Jain centres; Pampa, Ponna and Ranna in Kannada, 9th-12th centuries.
- Zoroastrian: Mazdayasna; the Avesta in extinct Avestan, similar to Sanskrit, compiled finally under Sassanian rule, probably 4th century AD. Yasna, 72 chapters and also the chief ceremony; the Gathas, 5 chapters and 17 hymns, supposed to be by Zoroaster; Vendidad/Videvdad, laws against demons; Yashts, praise; Khordeh Avesta, daily prayers. Denkard, the Encyclopaedia of Zoroastrianism, 10th century, without divine status; Bundahishn, "Primal Creations", mostly 8th-9th centuries, Ahura Mazda against Angra Mainyu.
- Sikh: Adi Granth compiled by Bhai Gurdas under Guru Arjan Dev, 1604, with 15 Bhagats of the Bhakti and Sufi traditions; Guru Granth Sahib expanded 1678 under Guru Gobind Singh, the 11th and final spiritual authority, in Gurmukhi script and Sant Bhasha (Punjabi, Apabhramsa, Hindi, Braj Bhasha, Sanskrit, Khariboli, Persian), containing Ramananda, Namdev, Ravidas, Parmanand, Sain, Surdas and the Muslim Bhagats Kabir and Baba Farid; Dasam Granth, disputed attribution, mostly Braj Bhasha, some hymns recited at Nitnem; Janamsakhis (Bhai Bala, Miharban, Adi); Ardas in three parts; Five Banis - Japji Sahib, Jaap Sahib, Tav-Prasad Saaiye (morning), Benti Chaupai, Anand Sahib (evening).

## Sangam and the Tamil corpus

- "Sangam" = fraternity; assemblies organised by the Pandya kingdom; ~2381 poems, 473 named poets, 102 anonymous poets, ~30,000 lines; 300 BC-AD 300; poets included men and women of different classes.
- Ettuthokai (eight anthologies): Ainkurunuru, Akananuru, Purananuru, Kalittokai, Kuruntokai, Natrinai, Paripadal, Pathitrupathu. Pattuppattu (ten idylls) includes Tirumurukarruppatai and Pattinappalai.
- Two schools: Aham/Agam (inner field - love and sexual relations); Puram (outer field - social life, ethics, valour, customs).
- Three Sangams: First at Madurai under Agastya or Shiva, no book survived, used Agattiyam; Second at Kapatapuram under Agastya then Tolkappiyar, ~2000 poems compiled into Tolkappiyam; Third at Madurai under Nakkirar. The first two are widely considered legendary.
- Tolkappiyam: earliest surviving Tamil literary work; grammar of spoken and written Tamil; aham and puram; cultural history and geography; fivefold land division - Kurinji (hunting and honey), Mullai (cattle), Marudam (agriculture), Neydal (fishing and salt), Palai (robbery); four castes - Arasar, Anthanars, Vanigars, Vellalas.
- Five great epics: Silappadikaram (Ilango Adigal; palm leaf manuscript; Kannaki, Kovalan, Madhavi; the anklet; Cauvery and Poompuhar; 5th-6th c; Jain elements); Manimekalai (Satanar; ~6th c; anti-love story and sequel; Manimekalai a Mahayana Buddhist nun); Civaka Cintamani (Tirutakkatevar; Jain); Valayapathi (unknown author; Jain; fragments); Kundalakesi (Nathakuthanar; Buddhist).
- Poets: Kapilar the largest contributor; Nakkirar the commentator and author of Tirumurukarruppadai, the earliest Tamil devotional work; Avvaiyar; also Nachchellaiyar and Kakkaipadiniyar. Thiruvalluvar's Tirukkural, 1,330 couplets on aram, porul and inbam, traditionally linked to Sangam but probably post-Sangam.
- Patronage: Cheras (Silappatikaram; Kannagi/Pattini); Cholas (Karikala praised in Pattinappalai and Porunaraatrupadai); Pandyas (assemblies at Madurai; sponsored the Ettuthokai and Pattuppattu); Pallavas (Tamil and Sanskrit; Bhakti saints; Dandin's Dashakumara Charita linked to their court).
- Cultural practices: hereditary monarchy; the court called avai; Aimperunkulu of five ministers and Enperayam of eight officers in Silappadikaram; customs officials at Puhar in Pattinappalai; Seyon or Murugan as primary deity; Panar and Viraliyar bards; Kanigaiyar dancers; Koothu; vatakkiruttal, fasting till death facing north.
- Tamil Bhakti: 12 Alvars for Vishnu including Andal; Nayanars for Shiva. Nalayira Divyaprabandham, 4,000 hymns, the Tamil Veda; Tevaram by Appar, Sambandar and Sundarar; Tiruvachakam by Manikkavachakar; Periya Puranam by Sekkizhar on the 63 Nayanars under Kulottunga II, ranked the fifth Veda in Tamil; Kamba Ramayanam by Kambar with the patron named once every 1,000 verses; Tiruppavai by Andal, 30 hymns for Margazhi; Tirumandiram by Tirumular; Thayumanavar's Sittar poetry protesting caste and temple-entry restrictions.
- Evidentiary boundary: vivid on social and economic conditions; not very useful for a connected political history of South India.

## The Persianate system and translation

- Persian's roots are as old as Sanskrit; it arrived with the Turks and Mongols in the 12th century and became the mode of communication of the court.
- Sultanate: Hasan Nizami's Taj-ul-Ma'asir, the first Persian history of the early Sultanate; Minhaj-i-Siraj's Tabaqat-i-Nasiri; Amir Khusrau, "Tuti-e-Hind", serving seven rulers, pioneer of Qawwali - Divan, Nuh Sipihr (1318), Duval Rani-Khizr Khan; Barani's Tarikh-e-Firoz Shahi and Fatwa-i-Jahandari; Afif's Tarikh-i-Firoz Shahi; Isami's Futuh-us-Salatin; Fawaid-ul-Fuwad of Nizamuddin Auliya recorded by Amir Hasan Sijzi; Jayasi's Padmavat, 1540.
- Mughal: Baburnama, the earliest autobiography in the Islamic world; Gulbadan Begum's Humayun Nama; Abul Fazl's Akbarnama with the Ain-i-Akbari; Arif Qandhari's Tarikh-i-Akbari, the first chronicle of Akbar's early reign; Nizamuddin Ahmad's Tabaqat-i-Akbari; Badauni's Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh; Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khana in Persian and Hindi; the illustrated Hamzanama; Firishta's Tarikh-i-Firishta (Bijapur, c. 1600); Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri; Inayat Khan's Shahjahannama and Abdul Hamid Lahori's Padshahnama; Jahanara Begum's Munis-al-Arwah; Khawas Khan's Alamgirnama continued by Mirza Muhammad Kazim; Saqi Mustad Khan's Maasir-i-Alamgiri; Jafar Zatalli's satirical Kulliyat; Zeb-un-Nissa's Diwan-i-Makhfi, compiled posthumously.
- Translation: Akbar's projects at Fatehpur Sikri - Faizi on Lilavati, Badauni's Razmnama from the Mahabharata while opposing Akbar's religious policies, Haji Ibrahim Sirhindi on the Atharva Veda, Nizamuddin Panipati's Yogavasistha into Persian. Outward: the Panchatantra into old Persian in the 6th century and into Arabic in the 8th as Kalila wa Dimna, featuring the jackals Kalila and Dimna.

## Urdu and Hindi

- Urdu: linguistically Indo-Aryan, born in India, on the structure of Khari Boli; formation through Persian-Hindi interaction, especially in the barracks of the Turkish army, is a debated account; follows the grammar of Hindi and the form and script of Persian; initially also called Dakkani from its use by the Bahmani states of Ahmadnagar, Golconda, Bijapur and Berar; Amir Khusrau wrote in it at its nascent stage.
- Urdu figures: Mirza Ghalib (1797-1869) and his Diwan; Sauda, Dard, Mir Taqi Mir; Iqbal's Bang-e-Dara and "Sare Jahan se Achcha"; patronage by later Mughals including the poet-emperor Bahadur Shah Zafar and by the Nawabs of Awadh; Sir Syed Ahmad Khan's didactic and nationalistic texts and the journal Tahdhib-ul-Akhlaq propagating women's education and opposing purdah and polygamy.
- Genres: Qaseeda (single presiding subject, in praise of a king or noble; recited in Arabic, Persian or Tajik by Ismailis in Central Asia); Ginans (Nizari Ismaili Khoja devotional hymns; originally oral renditions by Pirs; based on verses from the Quran; in Gujarati, Urdu, Punjabi and Sindhi); Shahr Ashob (narrative poem of anguish over political and social shifts, widely used under the Mughals).
- Hindi: evolved between the 7th and 14th centuries from Apabhramsa, itself from Prakrit; its biggest boost came from the Bhakti movement, which shunned Sanskrit as the Brahmins' language; from the 12th century a sharp rise in Bengali, Hindi, Marathi and Gujarati; Prithviraj Raso by Chand Bardai was probably the first Hindi book.
- Bhakti poets: Kabir's dohas; Tulsidas's Ramcharitmanas in Awadhi plus Vinaya Patrika, Krishna Gitavali, Kavitavali, Gitavali, Sahitya Ratna, Dohavali, Vairagya Sandipani; Surdas's Sur Sagar; Rahim, Bhushan, Raskhan; Mirabai; Narayana Bhatta Goswami's 50+ bhakti-granthas and Vraja Bhakti Vilasa with 333 forests and holy places; Bihari's Satsai in Braj Bhasha; the Ashta-Chhapa, eight poet-disciples of Vallabhacharya in the Pushtimarg tradition.

## Regional literary publics

- Malayalam: Kokasandisan; Bhasa Kautilya, a commentary on the Arthashastra; Ramacharitam by Cheeraman, 12th century; Thirunizhalmala; the pattu genre; Ezhuthachan the Father of Malayalam; Vallathol Narayana Menon with Kumaran Asan and Ulloor S. Parameshwara Iyer; Mahakavya Chitrayogam.
- Telugu: Nannayya, 11th century, first poet, Mahabharata translation; Tippalar and Bhodampudu inscriptions, 7th century; Vijayanagara as the Golden Age and Prabandha Age; Uttaraharivamsam by Nachana Somanatha under Bukka I; Krishnadevaraya's Amuktamalyada; the ashtadiggajas, of whom seven are named in the sources, with their works; K. Veereshalingam Pantalu's Rajasekhara Charitramu, the first Telugu novel.
- Kannada: Jain beginnings with Madhava's Dharmanathapurana and Uritta Vilasa's Dharma Parikshe; the second oldest language in South India; Halmidi inscription, 5th century; Kavirajamarga by Amoghavarsha I, 9th century, who also wrote Prashnottara Ratnamalika in Sanskrit; Ratnatraya - Pampa the Father of Kannada (Adipurana in Champu; Vikramarjuna Vijaya/Pampa Bharatha; court of Chalukya Arikesari), Ponna (Shanti Purana; Ubhaya Kavichakravarthi), Ranna (Ajitha Purana; Sahasa Bhima Vijaya/Gada Yuddha); Keshiraja's Shabdamanidarpana; Narahari's Torave Ramayana; Lakshmisha's Jaimini Bharata; Sarvajna's tripadi; Honnamma's Hadibadeya Dharma; Andayya's Madana Vijaya/Kabbigara Kava, the first pure Kannada text without a Sanskrit word.
- Bengali: Charyapada, 10th-11th century, oldest literary document, discovered by Haraprasad Shastri in the Nepal Durbar Library in 1916; the Mangalkavya set; Shivasankirttan by Rameshwar Bhattacharya; Bhanumati by Chakrapanidatta; Ramcharita by Sandhyakar Nandi; Baptist Mission Press at Serampore, William Carey, 1800; Raja Ram Mohan Roy; Vidyasagar and Akshay Kumar Dutta; Bankim Chandra Chatterjee's Anandamath with Vande Mataram; Tagore's Gitanjali Nobel of 1913 and both national anthems; Michael Madhusudan Dutt's Meghnad Badh Kavya (1861) and Tilottama Sambhava Kabya (1860), the first Bengali blank verse; Sarat Chandra Chatterjee, Qazi Nazrul Islam, R.C. Dutt.
- Assamese: buranjis; Sankardev's Kirtana Ghosha; Padmanath Gohain Baruah, Lakshminath Bezbaroa, Jyoti Prasad Agarwalla, Arupa Patangia, Parismita Singh.
- Odia: Sarala Das, 15th century; Upendra Bhanja, early 18th century; Jayadeva's Gita Govinda sung nightly at the Jagannatha temple in Odissi style; Panchasakha - Jagannath Das, Balaram Das, Achyutanda Das, Yasovanta Das, Ananta Das; Radhanath Ray and Fakir Mohan Senapati.
- Gujarati: Narsinh Mehta, 15th century; Narmad; Govardhanram Madhavram Tripathi's Saraswatichandra; K.M. Munshi's Prithivivallabh, 1928.
- Rajasthani: Dingal and Pingal; Dhola Maru; Mirabai in Braj; bards singing virkavya.
- Sindhi: influence of Rajasthan, Gujarat, Islam and Sufism; Shah Abdul Latif, 17th century, compiled as Shah Jo Risalo, published 1866; Dewan Kauromal; Mirza Kalich Beg; Pir Nooruddin's ginans.
- Kashmiri: Rajanaka Shiti Kantha's Mahanaya-Prakasha; Kalhana's Rajatarangini in Sanskrit; Lal Ded; Sufi Ghulam Mohammad, Zinda Kaul, Mahjoor; Shrivara's Zainavilas on Zain-ul-Abidin; Habba Khatoon, the "Nightingale of Kashmir", who introduced loal; Noor Din/Nund Rishi; eclipse by Dogri after 1846.
- Punjabi: Nath Yogi era, 9th-14th centuries; Persian and Gurmukhi scripts; the Adi Granth as a composite text; Guru Gobind Singh's Savaiye; Sohni-Mahiwal, Sassi-Punnu and Waris Shah's Heer-Ranjha; Kafis of Baba Farid and Bulleh Shah; Bhagat Singh's "Rang de Basanti Chola"; Bhai Vir Singh's Rana Surat Singh; Mohan Singh, Puran Singh; Amrita Pritam's Pinjar.
- Marathi: Yadav beginnings; Mahanubhav Panth founded by Chakradhar Swami, 1100-1200, accepting all castes; Warkari Sampradaya; Nath Panth influence; Mhaimbhat's Leela Charitra, 1278, the first Marathi biography; Jnaneshwar's kirtan and Gita commentary; Namdev's Abhangas; Janabai the oldest known female writer; first period 1200-1350; a gap to the early 16th century; Eknath born 1518; the third and most brilliant period from the 17th century to the close of Peshwa rule - Ramdas's Dasbodh, Tukaram's Abhanga and Tukaramgatha with the Pravritti-Nivritti discussion, Mukteshwar's Mahabharata translation; Tilak's Kesari; Jambhekar's Darpan, 6 January 1832; Hari Narayan Apte, V.S. Chiplunkar, Kesavasut; M.G. Ranade, G.T. Madkholkar, K.T. Telang.

## Patronage ledger

- Post-Mauryan: Sungas (Patanjali's Mahabhashya; Bhasa's plays); Satavahanas (Hala's Gatha Saptashati, 700 Prakrit love poems in Maharashtri including verses by women poets); Indo-Greeks (Milindapanho); Kushans under Kanishka (Ashvaghosha's Buddhacharita and Saundarananda; the revised Charaka Samhita).
- Court-poet pairings: Chandragupta Maurya-Kautilya; Samudragupta-Harisena; Chandragupta II-Kalidasa; Harshavardhana-Bana; Yashovarman-Bhavabhuti; Pulakeshin II-Ravikirti; Arikesari II-Pampa; Vikramaditya VI-Bilhana; Amoghavarsha I-Jinasena and Gunabhadra; Krishna III-Ponna; Gurjara Pratiharas-Rajashekhara; Raja Bhoja-Dhananjaya and Padmagupta; Hoysalas-Raghavanka; Krishnadevaraya-the ashtadiggajas; Wodeyar-Tirumalarya; Serfoji II-Vedanayagam Sastriar; Delhi Sultans-Amir Khusro; Lakshmana Sena-Jayadeva; Kumarapala-Hemachandra; Shivaji-Parmanand; Baliar Singh-Gangadhar Mishra; Durlabh Narayan-Hema Saraswati; Kulottunga Chola I-Jayamkondar; Akbar-Abu'l Fazl; Jahangir-Mutamad Khan and Muhammad Hadi; Shah Jahan-Abdul Hamid Lahori; Aurangzeb-Saqi Mustad Khan; Ranjit Singh-Rattan Singh Bhangu; Wajid Ali Shah-Agha Hasan Amanat; Krishna Chandra of Nadia-Ramprasad Sen.

## Modern literature and the nationalist turn

- Print from the 18th century enlarged the reading public; electronic literature now occupies the same position. Serampore 1800; Carey's grammar and dictionary; local presses followed.
- Adhunik kaal phases: Bhartendu Yug 1850-1900; Dwivedi Yug 1900-18; Chhayavad Yug 1918-37; Contemporary 1937 onwards, with their named writers.
- Bharatendu Harishchandra's Andher Nagari (1881) and Bharat Durdasha; Father of Modern Hindu Literature. Mahavir Prasad Dwivedi. Swami Dayanand's Satyartha Prakash, 1875, and the movement for Hindi as a link language - a movement, not an achieved status. Premchand's Godaan and Karmabhoomi in Hindi and Urdu; Nirala; Maithili Sharan Gupt; Harivansh Rai Bachchan's Madhushala and Madhukalash; Mahadevi Verma; Jaishankar Prasad's Kamayani (1936), the magnum opus of Chhayavaad.
- Subramania Bharati (1882-1921): Mahakavi Bharathiyar; poet, journalist, freedom fighter, social reformer; Kannan Pattu, Nilavum Vanminum Katrum, Paanchali Sabadham, Kuyil Pattu; Sudesa Geethangal, 1908; the weekly India printed on red paper, the first Tamil Nadu paper to publish political cartoons; also Vijaya.
- Nationalist instruments across languages: stage satire, the novel, the song, the regional newspaper, the reformist journal, the pioneering fortnightly, historical memory, the Partition novel and social-reform fiction.
- Women writers across the session: Mahadevi Verma, Subhadrakumari Chauhan, Amrita Pritam, Arupa Patangia, Parismita Singh, Gulbadan Begum, Jahanara Begum, Zeb-un-Nissa, Lakshmidevi, Mahadevi Dhiramati, Avvaiyar, Nachchellaiyar, Kakkaipadiniyar, Andal, Janabai, Lal Ded, Habba Khatoon, Mirabai, Honnamma, and the women poets in the Gatha Saptashati.

## Manuscript materiality and custody

- Media: paper, rock, birch bark in the definition of a script; palm leaf in the manuscript record. Medium correlates with region.
- Scribal evidence: Modi created because scribes could not write full Devanagari at speed; Kaithi for legal, administrative and private records. Illustration: the Hamzanama commissioned by Akbar.
- Survival losses: Sama Veda 1000+ recensions to 3; Rig Veda 21 to 1 complete and parts of a second; Atharva Veda 9 to 2; Katyayana only through references; Devichandraguptam and Valaiapathi fragmentary; no book of the first Sangam; Avestan extinct. External survival: the Charyapada recovered in 1916; Svapnavasavadattam rediscovered in 1912.
- Loss follows custodial failure, so the remedy is institutional.
- Gyan Bharatam Mission, Ministry of Culture, announced in the Union Budget 2025-26: vision to preserve, digitize and disseminate manuscript heritage; objectives to digitize and catalog over 1 crore manuscripts, establish a National Digital Repository, and integrate AI, OCR and blockchain; scholarly leadership in Indology, philology and conservation science; AI-assisted transcription, blockchain provenance, cloud preservation, APIs to national and international archives; conservation labs for palm leaf, birch bark and paper; "Pandulipi Mitras" across about 100 districts; educational modules, VR/AR and Manuscript Melas; phased 2025-2031. Progress totals deliberately not cited.

## Documentary-heritage recognition

- The Memory of the World Programme was initiated in 1992 and runs **three levels** - International Register (global significance, judged by the International Advisory Committee and the Register Subcommittee), regional registers such as **MOWCAP** for Asia and the Pacific, and national registers. India's nodal centre is the **IGNCA**, designated 2014. It recognises documents, not sites or practices.
- India's **International Register** entries, with custodians: I.A.S. Tamil Medical Manuscript Collection 1997 (Institute of Asian Studies, Chennai); Saiva Manuscripts of Pondicherry 2005 (French Institute of Pondicherry); Rigveda 2007 (NMM and BORI Pune); Tarikh-e-Khandan-e-Timuriyah 2011 (Khuda Bakhsh Library, Patna) and Vimalaprabha Laghukalacakratantrarajatika 2011 (Asiatic Society, Kolkata); Shantinatha Charitra 2013 (L. D. Institute of Indology); Archives of the Dutch East India Company 2003 (National Archives of India; joint); Gilgit Manuscripts 2017 (National Archives) and Maitreyavyakarana 2017 (Asiatic Society); Abhinavagupta Manuscripts Collection 2023 and the First Summit Meeting of the Non-Aligned Movement 2023 (IGNCA; the latter joint); Manuscript Collection of the Bhagavadgita 2025 and Manuscript of the Natyasastra 2025 (IGNCA, BORI Pune).
- India's **Asia-Pacific Regional Register (MOWCAP)** entries, all inscribed 2024 and first submitted 2023: the illustrated Ramacaritamanasa manuscripts (IGNCA and the National Museum); the Sahrdayaloka-Locana; and the 15th-century Pancatantra Fables manuscript.
- The reported International Register figure after the 2025 additions is **fourteen**; the official itemised list names **thirteen**; two Indian International entries are **joint nominations**. Cite named entries with **register level** and year, not a total.
- The core source prints one sixteen-row table that mixes International and Regional inscriptions, omits the Non-Aligned Movement archives, and includes an unverified "Saang Lok Collection".
- The inscription-year sequence does not track composition chronology: the Rigveda inscribed 2007 against colonial archives inscribed 2003, and ancient texts inscribed most recently in 2025.
- Ten register types represented, including a Persian dynastic chronicle, a colonial administrative archive, a twentieth-century diplomatic archive and a medical compendium.
- Firewall: Memory of the World International Register (global documentary significance) - MOWCAP Asia-Pacific Regional Register (regional relevance) - World Heritage (places, 1972 Convention, World Heritage Committee, List of World Heritage in Danger) - Intangible Cultural Heritage (living practices) - Geographical Indication (a domestic intellectual-property instrument).

## Answer-writing discipline

- Five controls: attribution (content before name), chronology (name which of eight dates), region (checked separately from family), terminology (family, language, dialect, register, script, hand, genre, status), and status currency (date every total).
- Twelve planted confusion sets, from Charudatta and Mrichchhakatika to the two Kalpa Sutras.
- Mark-scaled structures: 10 marks - thesis, precise claim, two named texts, one caution, verdict; 15 marks - adds the frame, corpus or region evidence, the mechanism and a dated anchor; 20 marks - adds script and epigraphy, translation and multilingual interaction, the institutional layer and visible dating discipline.
- Where the marks sit: every model in this file carries a quantified split, and across the twenty-nine models the largest single band is always **argument or assessment**, never enumeration. Evidence bands are awarded per named instance, so one example doing several jobs is scored once.
- The higher band comes from conceding a documented counter-case before the verdict.

## Bounded-research declaration

This session was generated under the bounded-research rule. The substantive base is the complete core owner, the complete enrichment owner and one core OCR art and culture source covering both the languages-and-scripts chapter and the literature chapter. External work was confined to three declared purposes.

**(a) PYQ verification and routing.** The routing ledgers and the locally held official Preliminary papers named in the manifest above. The official 2024 Set-A key and the provisional 2026 Set-A key were **not consulted**, and the reproducing source's own two answer keys, encountered during extraction, were **not carried into this session**.

**(b) One bounded material-fact pass.** The list of items was fixed before searching and is reproduced here in full, with the outcome of each.

| Item | Why it was on the list | Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| The criteria for classical-language status actually in force, and whether any language has been added since October 2024 | the core source prints the 2005-era criteria, and a superseded criterion taught as current is a substantive error; the total is also date-sensitive | **Resolved from the official Ministry of Culture explainer.** The criteria were set in 2004 (three criteria, antiquity over a thousand years), revised in **November 2005** (four criteria; Sanskrit declared on that revision) and revised again in **2024**, when the *original and not borrowed* test was **dropped** and a **knowledge-texts, prose and epigraphical-evidence** criterion added. Official recognition dates and notifying ministries were taken from the same record. **No language has been added** after the Union Cabinet decision of **3 October 2024**; the total stands at **eleven** |
| Whether India has any Memory of the World inscription later than the 2025 additions | the same status control, and the enrichment owner records an explicit open caution on this count | **Resolved in the negative.** No Indian International Register inscription later than the 2025 additions of the Bhagavadgita and Natyasastra manuscripts is reported |
| What the core source's sixteen-row Memory of the World table actually contains, given its stated total of fourteen | the discrepancy is internal to the source and affects a directly examinable set of entries | **Substantially resolved from the official Ministry of Culture record.** The table **mixes two registers**: twelve of its rows are **International Register** inscriptions, while **Ramcharitmanas, Panchatantra and Sahrdayaloka-Locana (2024) are Asia-Pacific Regional Register** inscriptions made through MOWCAP. The table also **omits** the **First Summit Meeting of the Non-Aligned Movement** archives (2023, joint nomination), which the official record includes, and prints a **"Saang Lok Collection" (2023)** that is **not traceable** in the official Indian record. Two residual items remain open and are recorded below: the difference between the **reported** International Register figure of fourteen and the **thirteen** entries the official list itemises, and the status of the unverified row |
| The particulars of the current manuscript mission - ministry, objectives, conservation streams and timeline | the topic's manuscript-institutions demand requires a genuine current anchor, and the core source carries none | **Resolved from the ministry's own published page.** Vision, key objectives, strategic pillars, conservation labs for palm leaf, birch bark and paper, the volunteer network and the 2025-2031 three-phase timeline are recorded in Lesson 21 |
| Reported progress totals for that mission - manuscripts surveyed, digitised and published | they would have made the current linkage more concrete | **Not resolved to a single reliable figure.** Reported totals differ materially across accounts and change continuously. The session records **none** of them and says so explicitly, and no claim in the session depends on them |

**(c) One bounded current linkage.** Exactly one is claimed, file-wide, in **Lesson 21**, and it is the Gyan Bharatam Mission record above. Every other pre-teach checklist in this session records the absence of a genuine linkage truthfully, and the two status verifications in Lessons 7 and 22 are recorded as verifications rather than as current linkages.

## Discrepancies recorded rather than resolved

| # | Discrepancy | How this session handles it |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | The core source prints a single sixteen-row Memory of the World table under a stated total of **14**, without distinguishing register levels | Lesson 22 separates the two registers from the official record: it teaches the **International Register** entries with custodians and years, teaches the three **Asia-Pacific Regional Register** entries of 2024 separately, records the **omitted** Non-Aligned Movement archives and the **unverified** "Saang Lok Collection", and instructs that entries be cited with **register level and year** rather than by total |
| 1a | The **reported** figure for India's International Register inscriptions after April 2025 is **fourteen**, while the official itemised list names **thirteen** | Recorded as an **open item**. Lesson 22 states both, teaches the itemised entries, and declines to assert a total as a taught fact |
| 1b | The core source dates the **Archives of the Dutch East India Company** to **2003**; the Ministry of Culture's own page dates the same inscription to **2013** | Lesson 22 uses **2003**, which is the year independently reported for that joint inscription, and the variance is recorded here rather than silently resolved |
| 2 | Kannada's earliest inscription is given both as the **late 6th / early 7th century AD** and as the **Halmidi inscription of the 5th century AD** | Lesson 11 prints both statements, prefers the specific named evidence for citation and records the conflict |
| 3 | Telugu's inscriptional beginning is given both as the **late 6th century** Telugu Chola epigraphs and as the **Tippalar and Bhodampudu inscriptions of the 7th century AD** | Lesson 11 prints both, offers a plausible reconciliation - beginning of use against named oldest inscriptions - **as a suggestion**, and does not assert it |
| 4 | **Gaha Kosha** is described in one place as a drama and in another, in detail, as an anthology of **700 Prakrit love poems** compiled by King Hala | Lesson 4 uses the detailed description, states the safe formulation and records the genre-label conflict; no argument depends on the label |
| 5 | The Rig Veda mandala table labels both **Mandala I and Mandala X** as "youngest" while applying a single "oldest and shortest" label to Mandalas II-VII, and both I and X carry 191 hymns | Lesson 12 prints the table as given, reads the labels as a broad three-way grouping rather than a fine ranking, and notes that no argument depends on them |
| 6 | The core source states flatly that the Buddha **did not speak Pali** but preached in Ardhamagadhi, and separately that the Buddha and Mahavira **perhaps** spoke in Ardhamagadhi | Lesson 4 prints both statements side by side, adopts the hedged formulation and states the safe examination position |
| 7 | The derivation of **Dravidian from Prakrit and Paisacha** is recorded as the view of *some scholars* and sits in tension with the same source's statement that Proto-Dravidian gave rise to the Dravidian languages | Lesson 2 marks it as an attributed minority view, supplies the safe formulation and instructs that the attribution be carried |
| 8 | **Ahom** appears both as the Indian representative of the Siamese-Chinese group and, in the extinct-languages list, as a **Tai** language of Assam | Lessons 2 and 8 record both placements; the classification label is noted as an older descriptive convention and is not resolved |
| 9 | The Sangam-era practice appears as ***vatakkiruttal*** in the source's own text and as ***vattakirutal*** in a reproduced examination item | Lesson 16 and the PYQ section record both spellings and state that the variant is recorded, not resolved |
| 10 | **Maitreyavyakarana** in the enrichment owner and in the official record appears as **Maitreyayvarakarana** in the core source's table | Lesson 22 uses the official spelling **Maitreyavyakarana** and the variant is recorded here |
| 11 | The core source's evaluative descriptions - Tamil as "purest", Paishachi as "an unimportant dialect", Apabhramsha as "corrupt" | Lessons 2 and 4 separate descriptive content, which is used as evidence, from evaluative content, which is reported as the tradition's or the source's judgement and never reproduced as evidence |
| 12 | The sources state that **eight** poets, the *ashtadiggajas*, were attached to Krishnadevaraya's court but name only **seven**, introducing them as those who "deserve special attention"; a past examination demand separately names **Dhurjati** and **Siddheshvara** in connection with that court | Lesson 19 states the count and the naming gap explicitly, prints the seven recorded names and instructs that the recorded list be used rather than an expanded one; the eighth name is listed among the declared gaps |

## Declared gaps - material a candidate may expect that this session does not supply

1. **No record of Hastimalla or Kshemeshvara.** The 2021 Preliminary item names three figures; this session supplies the full record for Bhavabhuti only and supplies nothing towards the other two.
2. **No eighth name for the *ashtadiggajas*.** The sources state that eight poets were attached to Krishnadevaraya's court and name only seven; the eighth is not supplied and is not inferred from external lists.
3. **No epigraphic record of place-value notation**, Indian or Southeast Asian. The 2026 Preliminary item is reproduced in full and is deliberately not resolved.
4. **No consolidated account of Indian English literature.** The two source headings for nationalist literature and Indian English literature are presented as graphic panels whose content is not available in extractable text; only incidental bilingual-English records are carried.
5. **No record of the translation published as "Songs from Prison"**, which appears in reproduced examination material; no attribution is supplied or inferred.
6. **No record of the term for the profession of memorising chronicles, dynastic histories and epic tales**, which appears in reproduced examination material; the *charana*, bardic and Pir custodial systems are taught, but not that specific term.
7. **No independent account of Nazir Ahmad Dehlvi's novel**; the title recorded in reproduced examination material is carried without a supporting account.
8. **No earliest datable Tamil inscription.** The sources record Tamil's rise as an inscriptional language under the Pallavas but give no dated earliest Tamil epigraph; the gap is stated rather than filled.
9. **No stated total for India's Memory of the World International Register inscriptions.** The reported figure is fourteen and the official itemised list names thirteen; the difference was not established, so named entries with their register level and year are taught instead of a total.
10. **No verification of the "Saang Lok Collection"** printed in the core source's table as a 2023 entry. It is not traceable in the official Indian record and is not taught as an inscription.
11. **No progress figures for the current manuscript mission.** Reported totals differ across accounts and are deliberately omitted.
12. **No administrative history, founding dates or current status for the National Mission for Manuscripts, the Indira Gandhi National Centre for the Arts or the National Archives.** Routed to the heritage and institutions owner.
13. **No current totals for World Heritage Sites, Intangible Cultural Heritage elements or Geographical Indications.** Routed to the heritage and institutions owner, which holds its own verification date.
14. **No manuscript folio counts, colophon readings or palaeographic datings** for any named manuscript; none is invented.
15. **No doctrinal exposition** of the Upanishads, the Tripitaka, the Agamas, the Avesta or the Guru Granth Sahib. Those texts are taught here as corpora; their doctrine belongs to the religion and philosophy owners.
16. **No political chronology** for any dynasty named in the patronage ledger. Routed to the ancient and medieval history owners.

## Source-use statement

Canonical Markdown supplied the topic's ownership boundaries, the essential vocabulary, the classification and status firewalls, the trap set, the answer architecture with its directive-and-demand map and mark-scaled structures, the evidence banks for script and documentary heritage, and the factual-risk and dynamic-status controls. The enrichment owner supplied the language-as-social-marker thesis, the composition-versus-inscription discipline, the manuscript-ecology and text-layer concepts, the comparative inscription-year framework and its own open caution on the entry count. The core OCR art and culture source supplied the paragraph-level record for the complete language classification, the constitutional and classical-language sections, the endangerment and initiatives material, the full script inventory, the entire Vedic, epic, Puranic, law-book, classical Sanskrit, Buddhist, Jain, Zoroastrian, Sikh, Sangam, Persianate, Urdu, Hindi, regional and modern literary corpora, the patronage tables, the Memory of the World table and both reproduced question lists. The PYQ ledgers and the locally held official papers supplied ownership, exact wording and key status. The single current linkage supplied one dated institutional record for the manuscript-conservation demand. Two further bounded checks, against official government records, corrected two status blocks that the core source carries in a superseded or undifferentiated form: the **classical-language criteria**, where the official explainer supplies the 2004, November 2005 and 2024 sets, the official recognition dates and the notifying ministries; and the **Memory of the World** material, where the official record supplies the programme's three-level structure, the IGNCA's nodal role, the itemised International Register inscriptions with their custodians, and the three Asia-Pacific Regional Register inscriptions of 2024 that the core source prints without distinguishing them. Where those records leave a figure unreconciled, the session teaches named entries rather than a total.

No artifact under the permanently excluded package directory was read, searched, cited or relied upon at any stage of this generation, and no learner-v2 artifact was used. Qdrant was not required and did not block or delay this session.
